## Nice Girl with 5 Husbands / 有五个丈夫的好姑娘
===Original===

To be given paid-up leisure and find yourself unable to create is unpleasant for any artist. To be stranded in a cluster of desert cabins with a dozen lonely people in the same predicament only makes it worse. So Tom Dorset was understandably irked with himself and the Tosker-Brown Vacation Fellowships as he climbed with the sun into the valley of red stones. He accepted the chafing of his camera strap against his shoulder as the nagging of conscience. He agreed with the disparaging hisses of the grains of sand rutched by his sneakers, and he wished that the occasional breezes, which faintly echoed the same criticisms, could blow him into a friendlier, less jealous age.
He had no way of knowing that just as there are winds that blow through space, so there are winds that blow through time. Such winds may be strong or weak. The strong ones are rare and seldom blow for short distances, or more of us would know about them. What they pick up is almost always whirled far into the future or past.
This has happened to people. There was Ambrose Bierce, who walked out of America and existence, and there are thousands of others who have disappeared without a trace, though many of these may not have been caught up by time tornadoes and I do not know if a time gale blew across the deck of the *Marie Celeste*.
Sometimes a time wind is playful, snatching up an object, sporting with it for a season and then returning it unharmed to its original place. Sometimes we may be blown about by whimsical time winds without realizing it. Memory, for example, is a tiny time breeze, so weak that it can ripple only the mind.
A very few time winds are like the monsoon, blowing at fixed intervals, first in one direction, then the other. Such a time wind blows near a balancing rock in a valley of red stones in the American Southwest. Every morning at ten o’clock, it blows a hundred years into the future; every afternoon at two, it blows a hundred years into the past.
Quite a number of people have unwittingly seen time winds in operation. There are misty spots on the sea’s horizon and wavery patches over desert sands. There are mirages and will o’ the wisps and ice blinks. And there are dust devils, such as Tom Dorset walked into near the balancing rock.

===Chinese===

得了带薪的闲暇却发现自个儿创作不出来，对任何艺术家都是件不痛快的事。被困在一簇沙漠小木屋里，跟一打处境相同的孤独的人做伴，只会更糟。所以汤姆·多塞特（Tom Dorset）顶着太阳爬进红石谷时，他对自己、对托斯克-布朗度假奖学金都憋着一股火，这可以理解。他把相机背带磨肩膀的刺痛当作良心的唠叨。他认同被他的帆布鞋碾过的沙粒发出的轻蔑嘶声，他但愿那偶尔刮起、隐隐重复着同样批评的微风，能把他吹进一个更友善、不那么爱吃醋的时代。
他无从知道，正如有吹过太空的风，也有吹过时间的风。这样的风可强可弱。强的罕见，且很少短距离地刮，不然我们当中会有更多人知道它们。它们卷起的东西，几乎总被用到久远的未来或过去。
这种事在人身上发生过。有安布罗斯·比尔斯，走出美国、走出存在；还有成千上万消失得无影无踪的人——尽管其中许多也许并不是被时间龙卷风卷走的，我也不知道有没有一场时间狂风扫过“玛丽·赛勒斯特号”的甲板。
有的时间风顽皮，抓起一件东西，耍上一季，又完好无损地把它放回原处。有时我们可能被异想天开的时间风吹得团团转而浑然不觉。比如记忆，就是一阵微小的时间微风，微弱到只能吹皱心湖。
极少数时间风像季风，定时而吹，先朝一个方向，再朝另一个。这样一阵时间风，就刮在美国西南部红石谷里一块平衡石附近。每天早晨十点，它向未来刮一百年；每天下午两点，它向过去刮一百年。
不少人无意中见过时间风运作。海平线上有朦胧的斑点，沙漠上有波动的斑块。有海市蜃楼、鬼火、冰反。还有尘卷风——比如汤姆·多塞特在平衡石附近走进去的那一个。

===Original===

It seemed to him no more than a spiteful upgust of sand, against which he closed his eyes until the warm granules stopped peppering the lids. He opened them to see the balancing rock had silently fallen and lay a quarter buried﻿—no, that couldn’t be, he told himself instantly. He had been preoccupied; he must have passed the balancing rock and held its image in his mind.
---
Despite this rationalization he was quite shaken. The strap of his camera slipped slowly down his arm without his feeling it. And just then there stepped around the giant bobbin of the rock an extraordinarily pretty girl with hair the same pinkish copper color.
She was barefoot and wearing a pale blue playsuit rather like a Grecian tunic. But most important, as she stood there toeing his rough shadow in the sand, there was a complete naturalness about her, an absence of sharp edges, as if her personality had weathered without aging, just as the valley seemed to have taken another step toward eternity in the space of an instant.
She must have assumed something of the same gentleness in him, for her faint surprise faded and she asked him, as easily as if he were a friend of five years’ standing, “Tell now, do you think a woman can love just one man? All her life? And a man just one woman?”
Tom Dorset made a dazed sound.
His mind searched wildly.
“I do,” she said, looking at him as calmly as at a mountain. “I think a man and woman can be each other’s world, like Tristan and Isolde or Frederic and Catherine. Those old authors were wise. I don’t see why on earth a girl has to spread her love around, no matter how enriching the experiences may be.”
“You know, I agree with you,” Tom said, thinking he’d caught her idea﻿—it was impossible not to catch her casualness. “I think there’s something cheap about the way everybody’s supposed to run after sex these days.”
“I don’t mean that exactly. Tenderness is beautiful, but﻿—” She pouted. “A big family can be vastly crushing. I wanted to declare today a holiday, but they outvoted me. Jock said it didn’t chime with our mood cycles. But I was angry with them, so I put on my clothes﻿—”
“Put on﻿—?”
“To make it a holiday,” she explained bafflingly. “And I walked here for a tantrum.” She stepped out of Tom’s shadow and hopped back. “Ow, the sand’s getting hot,” she said, rubbing the grains from the pale and uncramped toes.
“You go barefoot a lot?”

===Chinese===

在他看来那不过是一阵恶狠狠的扬沙，他闭上眼睛，直到温热的颗粒不再噼啪打在眼皮上。他睁开眼，看见平衡石已经无声地倒了，埋进沙里四分之一——不，不可能，他立刻告诉自己。他方才心不在焉；准是已经走过平衡石了，脑子里还留着它的影像。
---
尽管这样自我开解，他还是相当震动。相机的背带顺着他的胳膊慢慢滑落，他都没有感觉。就在那时，从那块绕线杆似的巨岩后面，转出来一个漂亮得出奇的姑娘，头发是同样的粉铜色。
她赤着脚，穿一身淡蓝色的运动服，颇像希腊的束腰长衣。但最重要的是，她站在那儿，脚尖踢着他投在沙地上的粗砺影子时，周身有一种彻底的自然，没有一点锋棱，仿佛她的人格风化过却没有衰老，就像这山谷仿佛在一瞬间又向永恒迈了一步。
她一定也在他身上看出了几分同样的温柔，因为她淡淡的惊讶褪去了，随即问他，轻松得像面对一个交往了五年的朋友：“说实话，你觉得一个女人能只爱一个男人吗？一辈子？一个男人只爱一个女人？”
汤姆·多塞特发出一声迷糊的声音。
他的脑子疯狂地搜索。
“我觉得能，”她说，望着他，平静得像望一座山。“我想一个男人和一个女人可以互为对方的世界，像特里斯坦与伊索尔德，或者弗雷德里克与凯瑟琳。那些老作家是有智慧的。我不明白一个姑娘干吗非得把爱撒开到处给，不管那些经历多么让人长见识。”
“你知道吗，我同意你的看法，”汤姆说，心想自己抓住了她的意思——她的随意劲儿让人不可能不跟着随意。“我觉得如今人人都被指望去追着性跑，这里面有种廉价的东西。”
“我不完全是那个意思。温情是美的，可是——”她撅起嘴。“一大家子人能压死人。我今天本想宣布放假，可他们投票否决了我。乔克说那跟我们的情绪周期不合拍。可我生了他们的气，所以我就穿上了衣服——”
“穿上了——？”
“好放假呀，”她解释得让人摸不着头脑。“我就走到这儿来发一顿脾气。”她跨出汤姆的影子，蹦了回去。“哎哟，沙子烫起来了，”她说着，从苍白的、没有挤夹痕迹的脚趾上搓下沙粒。
“你常光脚？”

===Original===

Tom guessed.
“No, mostly digitals,” she replied and took something shimmering from a pocket at her hip and drew it on her foot. It was a high-ankled, transparent moccasin with five separate toes. She zipped it shut with the speed of a card trick, then similarly gloved the other foot. Again the metal-edged slit down the front seemed to close itself.
“I’m behind on the fashions,” Tom said, curious. They were walking side by side now, the way she’d come and he’d been going. “How does that zipper work?”
“Magnetic. They’re on all my clothes. Very simple.” She parted her tunic to the waist, then let it zip together.
“Clever,” Tom remarked with a gulp. There seemed no limits to this girl’s naturalness.
“I see you’re a button man,” she said. “You actually believe it’s possible for a man and woman to love just each other?”
---
His chuckle was bitter. He was thinking of Elinore Murphy at Tosker-Brown and a bit about cold-faced Miss Tosker herself. “I sometimes wonder if it’s possible for anyone to love anyone.”
“You haven’t met the right girls,” she said.
“Girl,” he corrected.
She grinned at him. “You’ll make me think you really are a monogamist. What group do you come from?”
“Let’s not talk about that,” he requested. He was willing to forego knowing how she’d guessed he was from an art group, if he could be spared talking about the Vacation Fellowships and those nervous little cabins.
“My group’s very nice on the whole,” the girl said, “but at times they can be nefandously exasperating. Jock’s the worst, quietly guiding the rest of us like an analyst. How I loathe that man! But Larry’s almost as bad, with his shamefaced bumptiousness, as if we’d all sneaked off on a joyride to Venus. And there’s Jokichi at the opposite extreme, forever scared he won’t distribute his affection equally, dividing it up into mean little packets like candy for jealous children who would scream if they got one chewy less. And then there’s Sasha and Ernest﻿—”
“Who are you talking about?” Tom asked.
“My husbands.” She shook her head dolefully. “To find five more difficult men would be positively Martian.”
Tom’s mind backtracked frantically, searching all conversations at Tosker-Brown for gossip about cultists in the neighborhood. It found nothing and embarked on a wider search. There were the Mormons (was that the word that had sounded like Martian?)

===Chinese===

汤姆猜。
“不，多半穿趾套鞋，”她回答，从胯部的口袋里掏出一样闪光的东西套在脚上。那是一只高帮的、透明的鹿皮鞋，有五个分开的脚趾。她以纸牌戏法的速度拉上它，又同样给另一只脚套上。鞋面前缘那道金属边的开口似乎自己合拢了。
“我在时尚上落伍了，”汤姆说，很好奇。此刻他们并肩走着，朝着她来的、他本来要去的方向。“那拉链是怎么回事？”
“磁力。我所有衣服上都有。很简单。”她把上衣敞到腰，又让它自己拉拢。
“聪明，”汤姆咽了口口水说。这姑娘的自然似乎没有边。
“我看你是个‘钮扣派’，”她说。“你真的相信一男一女可以只爱彼此？”
---
他的笑是苦笑。他想着托斯克-布朗的埃莉诺·墨菲，还有冷脸的托斯克小姐本人。“我有时候怀疑有没有可能任何人爱任何人。”
“你是没遇上对的姑娘们，”她说。
“一个姑娘，”他纠正。
她冲他咧嘴一笑。“你再这样我要相信你真是一夫一妻派了。你是哪个群组的？”
“咱们别谈这个，”他请求。只要能免谈度假奖学金和那些神经兮兮的小木屋，他情愿不去追究她怎么猜出他来自一个艺术群组。
“我的群组总的来说很不错，”姑娘说，“可有的时候他们能气人得无以复加。乔克最坏，像个精神分析师似的悄悄引导我们其他人。我多恨那个人啊！可拉里也差不多坏，他那副羞答答的自大，仿佛我们全都偷偷溜去金星兜了趟风。城吉则走另一个极端，永远害怕自己不能把感情均分，把它分成一小包一小包的糖，发给一群眼红的孩子，谁少一块软糖就要尖叫。还有萨沙和欧内斯特——”
“你在说谁呀？”汤姆问。
“我的丈夫们。”她哀愁地摇头。“再找出五个更难伺候的男人，那就真是火星人了。”
汤姆的脑子拼命倒带，在托斯克-布朗的所有闲谈里搜罗关于附近有什么教团的八卦。一无所获，于是扩大搜索范围。有摩门教徒（刚才听起来像“火星人”的是不是这个词来着？）

===Original===

but it wasn’t the Mormon husbands who were plural. And then there was Oneida (weren’t husbands and wives both plural there?) but that was 19th century New England.
“Five husbands?” he repeated. She nodded. He went on, “Do you mean to say five men have got you alone somewhere up here?”
“To be sure not,” she replied. “There are my kwives.”
“Kwives?”
“Co-wives,” she said more slowly. “They can be fascinerously exasperating, too.”
---
Tom’s mind did some more searching. “And yet you believe in monogamy?”
She smiled. “Only when I’m having tantrums. It was civilized of you to agree with me.”
“But I actually do believe in monogamy,” he protested.
She gave his hand a little squeeze. “You are nice, but let’s rush now. I’ve finished my tantrum and I want you to meet my group. You can fresh yourself with us.”
As they hurried across the heated sands, Tom Dorset felt for the first time a twinge of uneasiness. There was something about this girl, more than her strange clothes and the odd words she used now and then, something almost﻿—though ghosts don’t wear digitals﻿—spectral.
They scrambled up a little rise, digging their footgear into the sand, until they stood on a long flat. And there, serpentining around two great clumps of rock, was a many-windowed adobe ranch house with a roof like fresh soot.
“Oh, they’ve put on their clothes,” his companion exclaimed with pleasure. “They’ve decided to make it a holiday after all.”
Tom spotted a beard in the group swarming out to meet them. Its cultish look gave him a momentary feeling of superiority, followed by an equally momentary apprehension﻿—the five husbands were certainly husky. Then both feelings were swallowed up in the swirl of introduction.
He told his own name, found that his companion’s was Lois Wolver, then smiling faces began to bob toward his, his hands were shaken, his cheeks were kissed, he was even spun around like blind man’s bluff, so that he lost track of the husbands and failed to attach Mary, Rachel, Simone and Joyce to the right owners.
He did notice that Jokichi was an Oriental with a skin as tight as enameled china, and that Rachel was a tall slim Negro girl. Also someone said, “Joyce isn’t a Wolver, she’s just visiting.”
He got a much clearer impression of the clothes than the names. They were colorful, costly-looking, and mostly Egyptian and Cretan in inspiration.

===Chinese===

但摩门教里多的不是丈夫。然后有奥奈达（那儿不是丈夫妻子都成群吗？）可那是19世纪的新英格兰。
“五个丈夫？”他重复。她点头。他继续：“你是说，五个男人把你一个人藏在这上头什么地方？”
“当然不是，”她回答。“还有我的共妻们。”
“共妻们？”
“一起的老婆，”她放慢了说。“她们也能气人得迷人。”
---
汤姆的脑子又搜了一轮。“可你还相信一夫一妻？”
她笑了。“只在我发脾气的时候。你同意我的看法，很文明。”
“可我是真的相信一夫一妻，”他抗议。
她捏了捏他的手。“你人真好，不过咱们快走吧。我脾气发完了，我要你见见我的群组。你可以跟我们一起清爽一下。”
匆匆穿过滚烫的沙地时，汤姆·多塞特头一回感到一阵不安。这姑娘身上有什么东西，不止是她的奇装异服和时不时冒出的怪词，某种几乎——虽然鬼魂不穿趾套鞋——幽灵似的东西。
他们手脚并用爬上一道小坡，鞋底抠进沙里，站上一片长长的平地。就在那儿，绕着两大丛巨岩蜿蜒而行的，是一座多窗的土坯牧场宅子，屋顶像新鲜的煤烟。
“哦，他们把衣服穿上了，”他的同伴欣喜地喊。“他们到底还是决定放假了。”
汤姆在涌出来迎接的人群里看见一部大胡子。那副教派气给了他一瞬间的优越感，紧接着又是一瞬间的忧惧——五个丈夫个个壮实。随后两种感觉都被介绍的旋涡吞没了。
他报了自己的名字，得知同伴叫洛伊丝·沃尔弗（Lois Wolver），然后一张张笑脸朝他晃过来，他的手被握，他的脸颊被亲，他甚至被推着转圈像玩摸瞎子，以至于丈夫们谁是谁没记住，玛丽、瑞秋、西蒙娜和乔伊斯没能各归其主。
他确实注意到城吉是个东方人，皮肤紧得像上釉的瓷器，瑞秋是个高挑纤细的黑人姑娘。还有人说：“乔伊斯不是沃尔弗家的人，她只是来做客的。”
名字没记清，衣服倒给了他深刻得多的印象。花花绿绿，看着昂贵，灵感多半来自埃及和克里特。

===Original===

Some of them would have been quite immodest, even compared to Miss Tosker’s famous playsuits, except that the wearers didn’t seem to feel so.
“There goes the middle-morning rocket!” one of them eagerly cried.
Tom looked up with the rest, but his eyes caught the dazzling sun. However, he heard a faint roaring that quickly sank in volume and pitch, and it reminded him that the Army had a rocket testing range in this area. He had little interest in science, but he hadn’t known they were on a daily schedule.
“Do you suppose it’s off the track?” he asked anxiously.
“Not a chance,” someone told him﻿—the beard, he thought. The assurance of the tones gave him a possible solution. Scientists came from all over the world these days and might have all sorts of advanced ideas. This could be a group working at a nearby atomic project and leading its peculiar private life on the side.
---
As they eddied toward the house he heard Lois remind someone, “But you finally did declare it a holiday,” and a husband who looked like a gay pharaoh respond, “I had another see at the mood charts and I found a subtle surge I’d missed.”
Meanwhile the beard (a black one) had taken Tom in charge. Tom wasn’t sure of his name, but he had a tan skin, a green sarong, and a fiercely jovial expression. “The swimming pool’s around there, the landing spot’s on the other side,” he began, then noticed Tom gazing at the sooty roof. “Sun power cells,” he explained proudly. “They store all the current we need.”
Tom felt his idea confirmed. “Wonder you don’t use atomic power,” he observed lightly.
The beard nodded. “We’ve been asked that. Matter of esthetics. Why waste sunlight or use hard radiations needlessly? Of course, you might feel differently. What’s your group, did you say?”
“Tosker-Brown,” Tom told him, adding when the beard frowned, “the Fellowship people, you know.”
“I don’t,” the beard confessed. “Where are you located?”
Tom briefly described the ranch house and cabins at the other end of the valley.
“Comic, I can’t place it.” The beard shrugged. “Here come the children.”
A dozen naked youngsters raced around the ranch house, followed by a woman in a vaguely African dress open down the sides.
“Yours?” Tom asked.
“Ours,” the beard answered.
“*C’est un homme!*”
“*Regardez des vêtements!*”
“No need to practice, kids; this is a holiday,” the beard told them.

===Chinese===

其中有些本该相当不体面，哪怕跟托斯克小姐那些出名的运动服相比——只是穿的人似乎并不觉得。
“上午中的火箭起飞了！”其中一个热切地喊。
汤姆跟大家一样抬头，眼睛被晃眼的太阳刺了一下。不过他听见一阵低低的轰鸣，音量和音高很快沉下去，这让他想起军方在这片区域有个火箭试验场。他对科学没多大兴趣，可不知道火箭还按每日时刻表发射。
“你说它会不会偏了轨道？”他担心地问。
“绝不可能，”有人告诉他——他以为是那部大胡子。这语气的笃定给了他一个可能的解答。如今科学家来自世界各地，可能有各种先进思想。这也许是一群在附近原子项目工作的人，顺带过着他们独特的私生活。
---
随着人群朝宅子打着旋涌去，他听见洛伊丝提醒谁：“可你到底还是宣布放假了。”一个长得像快活的法老的丈夫回答：“我又看了一遍情绪图表，发现了一道我漏掉的细微涌动。”
与此同时那部大胡子（黑胡子）接管了汤姆。他的名字汤姆还没弄清，但他皮肤晒成棕色，围着绿纱笼，一脸热烈的欢快。“游泳池在那边，着陆坪在另一头，”他开头说，随即注意到汤姆在盯那煤烟色的屋顶。“太阳能电池，”他骄傲地解释。“我们需要的电全存得下。”
汤姆觉得他的猜想被证实了。“奇怪你们不用原子能，”他随口说。
大胡子点头。“有人问过。美学问题。干吗浪费阳光，或者没必要的硬辐射？当然，你可能想法不同。你的群组是做什么的，你方才说？”
“托斯克-布朗，”汤姆告诉他，见大胡子皱眉又补了一句，“奖学金那边的人，你知道的。”
“我不知道，”大胡子承认。“你们在哪儿？”
汤姆简单描述了山谷另一头的牧场宅子和木屋。
“有意思，我想不起来有那么个地方。”大胡子耸耸肩。“孩子们来了。”
一打光着身子的小孩绕着宅子跑过来，后面跟着一个穿侧面开衩的非洲风长裙的女人。
“你的？”汤姆问。
“我们的，”大胡子回答。
“*C'est un homme!*”
“*Regardez des vêtements!*”
“不用练了，孩子们；今天放假，”大胡子告诉他们。

===Original===

“Tom, Helen,” he said, introducing the woman with the air-conditioned garment. “Her turn today to companion *die Kinder*.”
---
One of the latter rapped on the beard’s knee. “May we show the stranger our things?” Instantly the others joined in pleading. The beard shot an inquiring glance at Tom, who nodded. A moment later the small troupe was hurrying him toward a spacious lean-to at the end of the ranch house. It was chuckful of strange toys, rocks and plants, small animals in cages and out, and the oddest model airplanes, or submarines. But Tom was given no time to look at any one thing for long.
“See my crystals? I grew them.”
“Smell my mutated gardenias. Tell now, isn’t there a difference?” There didn’t seem to be, but he nodded.
“Look at my squabbits.” This referred to some long-eared white squirrels nibbling carrots and nuts.
“Here’s my newest model spaceship, a DS-57-B. Notice the detail.” The oldest boy shoved one of the submarine affairs in his face.
---
Tom felt like a figure that is being tugged about in a rococo painting by wide pink ribbons in the chubby hands of naked cherubs. Except that these cherubs were slim and tanned, fantastically energetic, and apparently of depressingly high I.Q. (What these scientists did to children!) He missed Lois and was grateful for the single little girl solemnly skipping rope in a corner and paying no attention to him.
The odd lingo she repeated stuck in his mind: “Gik-lo, I-o, Rik-o, Gis-so. Gik-lo, I-o﻿ ﻿…”
Suddenly the air was filled with soft chimes. “Lunch,” the children shouted and ran away.
Tom followed at a soberer pace along the wall of the ranch house. He glanced in the huge windows, curious about the living and sleeping arrangements of the Wolvers, but the panes were strangely darkened. Then he entered the wide doorway through which the children had scampered and his curiosity turned to wonder.
A resilient green floor that wasn’t flat, but sloped up toward the white of the far wall like a breaking wave. Chairs like giants’ hands tenderly cupped. Little tables growing like mushrooms and broad-leafed plants out of the green floor. A vast picture window showing the red rocks.
Yet it was the wood-paneled walls that electrified his artistic interest. They blossomed with fruits and flowers, deep and poignantly carved in several styles. He had never seen such work.

===Chinese===

“汤姆，海伦，”他介绍那位穿空调裙装的女人。“今天轮到她陪*孩子们*。”
---
其中一个敲敲大胡子的膝盖。“我们可以给陌生人看我们的东西吗？”其他孩子立刻加入恳求。大胡子询问地看了汤姆一眼，汤姆点头。片刻之后，这支小队伍就催着他奔向宅子尽头一座宽敞的披棚。里面塞满了古怪的玩具、石头和植物、关着和放养的小动物，还有最古怪的模型飞机，或者潜艇。可汤姆连一件东西都来不及细看。
“看我的晶体？我种的。”
“闻闻我的变种栀子花。说实话，是不是不一样？”似乎没什么不一样，但他点了头。
“看我的松鼠兔。”这指的是几只哨着胡萝卜和坚果的白毛长耳松鼠。
“这是我最新的宇宙飞船模型，DS-57-B。注意细节。”最大的男孩把那潜艇样子的东西之一杵到他脸前。
---
汤姆觉得自己像画里的人物，被光屁股小天使胖乎乎的手拽着一条条粉红缎带拖来拖去。只不过这些小天使又瘦又晒得黑，精力旺盛得吓人，智商显然高得让人泄气。（这些科学家把孩子怎么搞的！）他想念洛伊丝，并且感激那个独自在角落里一本正经跳绳、完全不搭理他的小女孩。
她反复念叨的怪调子印进了他脑子：“Gik-lo, I-o, Rik-o, Gis-so. Gik-lo, I-o……”
忽然空中响起柔和的钟琴声。“午饭，”孩子们喊着一哄而散。
汤姆迈着沉稳些的步子沿宅子的墙走。他朝大窗户里张望，好奇沃尔弗一家的起居安排，可窗玻璃暗得出奇。随后他从孩子们蹦跳而入的宽门洞走进去，好奇心变成了惊叹。
一片有弹性的绿地面，不是平的，朝远处白墙斜斜涌起，像一道卷起的浪。椅子像温柔合拢的巨人的手掌。小桌子像蘑菇和阔叶植物一样从绿地面里长出来。一扇巨大的观景窗框着红岩。
然而真正让他的艺术细胞过电的，是木板墙。墙上开满了果实与花朵，以好几种风格深刻而锥心地雕成。他从没见过这样的活儿。

===Original===

He became aware of a silence and realized that his hosts and hostesses were smiling at him from around a long table. Moved by a sudden humility, he knelt and unlaced his sneakers and added them to the pile of sandals and digitals by the door. As he rose, a soft and comic piping started and he realized that beyond the table the children were lined up, solemnly puffing at little wooden flutes and recorders. He saw the empty chair at the table and went toward it, conscious for the moment of nothing but his dusty feet.
He was disappointed that Lois wasn’t sitting next to him, but the food reminded him that he was hungry. There was a charming little steak, striped black and brown with perfection, and all sorts of vegetables and fruits, one or two of which he didn’t recognize.
“Flown from Africa,” someone explained to him.
These sly scientists, he thought, living behind their security curtain in the most improbable world!
When they were sitting with coffee and wine, and the children had finished their concert and were busy at another table, he asked, “How do you manage all this?”
Jock, the gay pharaoh, shrugged. “It’s not difficult.”
Rachel, the slim Negro, chuckled in her throat. “We’re just people, Tom.”
He tried to phrase his question without mentioning money. “What do you all do?”
“Jock’s a uranium miner,” Larry (the beard) answered, briskly taking over. “Rachel’s an algae farmer. I’m a rocket pilot. Lois﻿—”
---
Although pleased at this final confirmation of his guess, Tom couldn’t help feeling a surge of uneasiness. “Sure you should be telling me these things?”
Larry laughed. “Why not? Lois and Jokichi have been exchange-workers in China the last six months.”
“Mostly digging ditches,” Jokichi put in with a smile.
“﻿—and Sasha’s in an assembly plant. Helen’s a psychiatrist. Oh, we just do ordinary things. Now we’re on grand vacation.”
“Grand vacation?”
“When all of us have a vacation together,” Larry explained. “What do you do?”
“I’m an artist,” Tom said, taking out a cigaret.
“But what else?” Larry asked.
Tom felt an angry embarrassment. “Just an artist,” he mumbled, cigaret in mouth, digging in his pockets for a match.
“Hold on,” said Joyce beside him and pointed a silver pencil at the tip of the cigaret. He felt a faint thrill in his lips and then started back, coughing. The cigaret was lighted.
“Please mutate my poppy seeds, Mommy.”

===Chinese===

他察觉到一阵安静，意识到主人们和主妇们正围着一张长桌朝他微笑。受一阵突来的谦卑驱使，他跪下解开帆布鞋的鞋带，把鞋添到门边那堆凉鞋和趾套鞋旁。他站起身时，响起一阵轻快滑稽的管乐，他看见桌子那头孩子们排成一列，一本正经地吹着小木笛和竖笛。他看见桌旁那张空椅子，朝它走去，那一刻只意识到自己满是尘土的脚。
洛伊丝没坐在他旁边，他有点失望，可食物提醒了他肚子饿。有一块讨人喜欢的小牛排，煎得棕黑条纹恰到好处，还有各色蔬菜水果，其中一两种他认不得。
“从非洲空运来的，”有人对他解释。
这些狡猾的科学家，他想，躲在安全帷幕后面，过着这么个最不可思议的世界！
等大家喝着咖啡和酒坐下，孩子们结束了音乐会、在另一张桌子忙活起来，他问：“这一切你们是怎么打理的？”
乔克，那个快活的法老，耸耸肩。“不难。”
瑞秋，那个纤细的黑人姑娘，喉咙里咕咕地笑。“我们只是普通人，汤姆。”
他试着不提钱来措辞。“你们都是干什么的？”
“乔克是铀矿矿工，”拉里（大胡子）回答，麻利地接了过去。“瑞秋是藻类农场主。我是火箭驾驶员。洛伊丝——”
---
尽管这最后一锤定音地证实了他的猜测，汤姆还是禁不住涌起一阵不安。“你们确定该告诉我这些？”
拉里笑了。“有什么不行？洛伊丝和城吉过去六个月在中国做交换工。”
“主要在挖沟，”城吉笑着插话。
“——萨沙在一家装配厂。海伦是精神科医生。哦，我们干的都是平常事。现在我们在放大假。”
“大假？”
“我们所有人一起休假的时候，”拉里解释。“你是做什么的？”
“我是艺术家，”汤姆说着掏出一支烟。
“可还做什么呢？”拉里问。
汤姆感到一阵恼怒的窘迫。“就是个艺术家，”他咕哝，烟叨在嘴上，在口袋里摸火柴。
“等等，”旁边的乔伊斯说着，把一支银铅笔对准烟头。他嘴唇上感到微微一麻，随后一惊，咳起来。烟点着了。
“请给我变种一下罂粟籽，妈妈。”

===Original===

A little girl had darted to Joyce from the children’s table.
“You’re a very dirty little girl,” Joyce told her without reproof. “Hold them out.” She briefly directed the silver pencil at the clay pellets on the grimy little palm. The little girl shivered delightedly. “I love ultrasonics, they feel so funny.” She scampered off.
Tom cleared his throat. “I must say I’m tremendously impressed with the wood carvings. I’d like to photograph them. Oh, Lord!”
“What’s the matter?” Rachel asked.
“I lost my camera somewhere.”
“Camera?” Jokichi showed interest. “You mean one for stills?”
“Yes.”
“What kind?”
“A Leica,” Tom told him.
Jokichi seemed impressed. “That is interesting. I’ve never seen one of those old ones.”
“Tom’s a button man,” Lois remarked by way of explanation, apparently. “Was the camera in a brown case? You dropped it where we met. We can get it later.”
“Good, I’d really like to take those pictures,” Tom said. “Incidentally, who did the carvings?”
“We did,” Jock said. “Together.”
Tom was grateful that the scamper of the children out of the room saved him from having to reply. He couldn’t think of anything but a grunt of astonishment.
The conversation split into a group of chats about something called a psych machine, trips to Russia, the planet Mars, and several artists Tom had never heard of. He wanted to talk to Lois, but she was one of the group gabbling about Mars like children. He felt suddenly uneasy and out of things, and neither Rachel’s deprecating remarks about her section of the wood carvings nor Joyce’s interesting smiles helped much. He was glad when they all began to get up. He wandered outside and made his way to the children’s lean-to, feeling very depressed.
---
Once again he was the center of a friendly naked cluster, except for the same solemn-faced little girl skipping rope. A rather malicious but not very hopeful whim prompted him to ask the youngest, “What’s one and one?”
“Ten,” the shaver answered glibly. Tom felt pleased.
“It could also be two,” the oldest boy remarked.
“I’ll say,” Tom agreed. “What’s the population of the world?”
“About seven hundred million.”
Tom nodded noncommittally and, grabbing at the first long word that he thought of, turned to the eldest girl. “What’s poliomyelitis?”
“Never heard of it,” she said.
The solemn little girl kept droning the same ridiculous chant: “Gik-lo, I-o, Rik-o, Gis-so.”

===Chinese===

一个小女孩从孩子们的桌子边窜到乔伊斯跟前。
“你是个小脏姑娘，”乔伊斯对她说，并无责备之意。“摊开手。”她把银铅笔对准脏兮兮小手掌上的泥丸照了一下。小女孩开心地一哆嗦。“我爱超声波，痒痒的真好受。”她蹦跳着跑了。
汤姆清清嗓子。“我得说，那些木雕给我极深的印象。我想拍下来。哦，天哪！”
“怎么了？”瑞秋问。
“我的相机不知丢哪儿了。”
“相机？”城吉来了兴趣。“你说静态的那种？”
“是。”
“什么样的？”
“徕卡，”汤姆告诉他。
城吉似乎被触动了。“那确实有意思。那些老式的我一台都没见过。”
“汤姆是钮扣派，”洛伊丝说，显然算作解释。“相机是不是在棕色的套子里？你掉在咱们碰头的地方了。回头可以去拿。”
“好，我真的很想拍那些照片，”汤姆说。“顺便问一句，雕那些东西的是谁？”
“我们大家，”乔克说。“一起。”
孩子们窜出屋子，正好免了他非回答不可，他心存感激。他只想得出一声惊异的咕噜。
谈话分裂成几攦闲聊：关于一种叫“心理机”的东西、去俄国的旅行、火星，还有好几位汤姆从没听说过的艺术家。他想跟洛伊丝说话，可她正在那堆像孩子一样叽叽喳喳谈火星的人里。他突然感到不安、感到被排除在外，瑞秋对她那部分木雕的自谦、乔伊斯意味深长的微笑都帮不上什么忙。大家起身时他松了口气。他晃到外面，朝孩子们的披棚走去，情绪很低落。
---
又一次，他被一圈友好的光屁股围在中间，除了那个仍然一本正经跳绳的严肃小女孩。一阵有点恶毒却也并不抱什么希望的冲动，促使他问最年幼的：“一加一是几？”
“十，”小家伙脱口而出。汤姆感到满意。
“也可以是二，”最大的男孩说。
“可不，”汤姆同意。“全世界人口是多少？”
“七亿左右。”
汤姆不置可否地点点头，抓起脑子里蹦出的第一个长词，转向最大的女孩。“小儿麻痹症是什么？”
“没听说过，”她说。
那个严肃的小女孩还在哼那支荒唐的调子：“Gik-lo, I-o, Rik-o, Gis-so。”

===Original===

His ego eased, Tom went outside and there was Lois.
“What’s the matter?” she asked.
“Nothing,” he said.
She took his hand. “Have we pushed ourselves at you too much? Has our jabbering bothered you? We’re a loud-mouthed family and I didn’t think to ask if you were loning.”
“Loning?”
“Solituding.”
“In a way,” he said. They didn’t speak for a moment. Then, “Are you happy, Lois, in your life here?” he asked.
Her smile was instant. “Of course. Don’t you like my group?”
He hesitated. “They make me feel rather no good,” he said, and then admitted, “but in a way I’m more attracted to them than any people I’ve ever met.”
“You are?” Her grip on his hand tightened. “Then why don’t you stay with us for a while? I like you. It’s too early to propose anything, but I think you have a quality our group lacks. You could see how you fit in. And there’s Joyce. She’s just visiting, too. You wouldn’t have to lone unless you wanted.”
Before he could think, there was a rhythmic rush of feet and the Wolvers were around them.
“We’re swimming,” Simone announced.
Lois looked at Tom inquiringly. He smiled his willingness, started to mention he didn’t have trunks, then realized that wouldn’t be news here. He wondered whether he would blush.
Jock fell in beside him as they rounded the ranch house. “Larry’s been telling me about your group at the other end of the valley. It’s comic, but I’ve whirled down the valley a dozen times and never spotted any sort of place there. What’s it like?”
“A ranch house and several cabins.”
---
Jock frowned. “Comic I never saw it.” His face cleared. “How about whirling over there? You could point it out to me.”
“It’s really there,” Tom said uneasily. “I’m not making it up.”
“Of course,” Jock assured him. “It was just an idea.”
“We could pick up your camera on the way,” Lois put in.
The rest of the group had turned back from the huge oval pool and the dark blue and flashing thing beyond it, and stood gay-colored against the pool’s pale blue shimmer.
“How about it?” Jock asked them. “A whirl before we bathe?”
Two or three said yes besides Lois, and Jock led the way toward the helicopter that Tom now saw standing beyond the pool, its beetle body as blue as a scarab, its vanes flashing silver.
The others piled in. Tom followed as casually as he could, trying to suppress the pounding of his heart. “Wonder you don’t go by rocket,” he remarked lightly.

===Chinese===

自尊缓过劲来，汤姆走到外面，洛伊丝就在那儿。
“怎么了？”她问。
“没什么，”他说。
她拉起他的手。“是我们对你逼得太紧了？我们的叽叽喳喳烦着你了？我们家都是大嗓门，我没想过问问你是不是想独处。”
“独处？”
“一个人待着。”
“有点，”他说。两人沉默了片刻。然后他问：“洛伊丝，你在这儿的生活幸福吗？”
她的微笑立刻绽开。“当然。你不喜欢我的群组？”
他迟疑。“他们让我觉得自己挺没用的，”他说，随即又承认，“可在某种意义上，他们比我见过的任何人都更吸引我。”
“是吗？”她握他的手更紧了。“那你干吗不跟我们住一阵子？我喜欢你。现在提什么还为时过早，可我觉得你有我们群组缺的一种素质。你可以看看合不合得来。还有乔伊斯呢。她也只是来做客。你用不着独处，除非你想。”
他还没来得及想，一阵有节奏的脚步声涌来，沃尔弗一家已经围住了他们。
“我们去游泳，”西蒙娜宣布。
洛伊丝询问地望着汤姆。他笑着表示愿意，正想说没带泳裤，随即意识到在这儿这不算新闻。他不知道自己会不会脸红。
绕过宅子时乔克跟他并排走。“拉里跟我讲了你们在山谷那头的群组。真逗，我顺着山谷跑十几回了，从没发现那儿有任何地方。什么样子的？”
“一座牧场宅子，加几间小屋。”
---
乔克皱眉。“真逗，我从没看见过。”他的脸晴了。“飞过去看看怎么样？你可以指给我看。”
“它真的在，”汤姆不安地说。“不是我编的。”
“当然，”乔克让他放心。“就是随便一想。”
“路上还能捎上你的相机，”洛伊丝插话。
其他人已从巨大的椭圆泳池和池那边一样深蓝闪光的东西那儿折回来，站在池水淡蓝的微光前，一个个色彩鲜亮。
“怎么样？”乔克问大家。“洗澡前来一圈？”
除了洛伊丝还有两三个说好，乔克领路走向那架汤姆这才看见的、停在池子那边的直升机，甲虫似的机身蓝得像一只圣甲虫，旋翼闪着银光。
其他人陆续钻进去。汤姆尽量若无其事地跟上，努力压住心跳。“奇怪你们不坐火箭，”他轻松地说。

===Original===

Jock laughed. “For such a short trip?”
The vanes began to thrum. Tom sat stiffly, gripping the sides of the seat, then realized that the others had sunk back lazily in the cushions. There was a moment of strain and they were falling ahead and up. Looking out the side, Tom saw for a moment the sooty roof of the ranch house and the blue of the pool and the pinkish umber of tanned bodies. Then the helicopter lurched gently around. Without warning a miserable uneasiness gripped him, a desire to cling mixed with an urge to escape. He tried to convince himself it was fear of the height.
He heard Lois tell Jock, “That’s the place, down by that rock that looks like a wrecked spaceship.”
The helicopter began to fall forward. Tom felt Lois’ hand on his.
“You haven’t answered my question,” she said.
“What?” he asked dully.
“Whether you’ll stay with us. At least for a while.”
He looked at her. Her smile was a comfort. He said, “If I possibly can.”
“What could possibly stop you?”
“I don’t know,” he answered abstractedly.
“You’re strange,” Lois told him. “There’s a weight of sadness in you. As if you lived in a less happy age. As if it weren’t 2050.”
“Twenty?” he repeated, awakening from his thoughts with a jerk. “What’s the time?” he asked anxiously.
“Two,” Jock said. The word sounded like a knell.
“You need cheering,” Lois announced firmly.
Amid a whoosh of air rebounding from earth, they jounced gently down. Lois vaulted out. “Come on,” she said.
Tom followed her. “Where?” he asked stupidly, looking around at the red rocks through the settling sand cloud stirred by the vanes.
“Your camera,” she told him, laughing. “Over there. Come on, I’ll race you.”
He started to run with her and then his uneasiness got beyond his control. He ran faster and faster. He saw Lois catch her foot on a rock and go down sprawling, but he couldn’t stop. He ran desperately around the rock and into a gust of up-whirling sand that terrified him with its suddenness. He tried to escape from the stinging, blinding gust, but there was the nightmarish fright that his wild strides were carrying him nowhere.
Then the sand settled. He stopped running and looked around him. He was standing by the balancing rock. He was gasping. At his feet the rusty brown leather of the camera case peeped from the sand. Lois was nowhere in sight. Neither was the helicopter.

===Chinese===

乔克笑了。“这么短的路？”
旋翼开始嗡鸣。汤姆僵坐着，抓紧座椅两侧，随即发现其他人已懒洋洋陷进垫子里。一阵短暂的紧绷，他们向前上方跌出去。朝侧面望了一眼，汤姆一时看见宅子煤烟色的屋顶、泳池的蓝色和晒熟的身体那粉棕的褐色。然后直升机轻轻一侧转。毫无预兆，一阵难受的不安攫住了他，一种想抓住什么又想逃开的混合冲动。他试着说服自己这只是恐高。
他听见洛伊丝对乔克说：“就是那儿，在那块像失事飞船的岩石旁边下去。”
直升机开始向前下降。汤姆感到洛伊丝的手放在他手上。
“你还没回答我的问题，”她说。
“什么？”他木然地问。
“要不要跟我们留下来。至少住一阵。”
他看着她。她的微笑是一种安慰。他说：“只要我可能，我就留下。”
“能有什么事拦住你？”
“不知道，”他心不在焉地答。
“你这个人真怪，”洛伊丝对他说。“你身上有一份沉甸甸的悲伤。好像你活在一个不那么幸福的年代。好像现在不是2050年似的。”
“二十？”他一激灵从思绪里醒来。“几点了？”他着急地问。
“两点，”乔克说。这个词听起来像丧钟。
“你需要打打气，”洛伊丝断然宣布。
在一阵从地面反弹回来的气流呼啸中，他们轻轻颠了颠落了地。洛伊丝翻身跳出去。“来，”她说。
汤姆跟着她。“去哪儿？”他傻乎乎地问，环顾旋翼搅起的落沙云外的红岩。
“你的相机呀，”她笑着说。“在那边。来，我跟你赛跑。”
他刚跟她跑起来，那阵不安就挣脱了他的控制。他越跑越快。他看见洛伊丝的脚绊在一块石头上，扑倒了，可他停不下来。他不顾一切地绕过那块岩石，冲进一阵向上打旋的扬沙，那猝不及防让他心惊。他想逃出这阵刺人眼睛的尘风，可有一种梦魇般的恐惧：他狂乱的步子哪儿也带不去他。
然后沙落定了。他停下脚步环顾四周。他站在平衡石旁。他喘着气。脚边，相机套锈棕色的皮革从沙里露出一点。洛伊丝不见了。直升机也不见了。

===Original===

The valley seemed different, rawer﻿—one might almost have said younger.
Hours after dark he trailed into Tosker-Brown. Curtained lights still glowed from a few cabins. He was footsore, bewildered, frightened. All afternoon and through the twilight and into the moonlit evening that turned the red rocks black, he had searched the valley. Nowhere had he been able to find the soot-roofed ranch house of the Wolvers. He hadn’t even been able to locate the rock like a giant bobbin where he’d met Lois.
During the next days he often returned to the valley. But he never found anything. And he never happened to be near the balancing rock when the time winds blew at ten and two, though once or twice he did see dust devils. Then he went away and eventually forgot.
In his casual reading he ran across popular science articles describing the binary system of numbers used in electronic calculating machines, where one and one make ten. He always skipped them. And more than once he saw the four equations expressing Einstein’s generalized theory of gravitation:
Γi = 0
Rik = 0
He never connected them with the little girl’s chant: “Gik-lo, I-o, Rik-o, Gis-so.”

===Chinese===

山谷似乎不一样了，更生涩——简直可以说更年轻了。
天黑后好几个小时，他拖着一双脚走回托斯克-布朗。几间小屋还亮着帘后的灯。他脚痛，困惑，害怕。整个下午、穿过暮色、直到把红岩照成黑色的月光夜，他搜遍了山谷。哪儿也找不到沃尔弗家那座煤烟色屋顶的宅子。他连遇见洛伊丝的那块绕线杆似的巨岩都找不到了。
之后几天他常回山谷来。可他再没找到任何东西。十点和两点时间风刮起的时候，他也从没碰巧在平衡石附近，虽然有一两回确实看见了尘卷风。后来他走了，最终忘了。
在随便翻阅中他撞见过一些科普文章，讲电子计算机用的二进制记数法，在那里一加一等于十。他总是跳过去不读。还不止一次，他见过表示爱因斯坦广义引力理论的四个方程：
Γi = 0
Rik = 0
他从未把它们跟小女孩的吟唱联系起来：“Gik-lo, I-o, Rik-o, Gis-so。”

### I / 一
===Original===

Silently, so as not to shock anyone with illusions about well dressed young women, Sandra Lea Grayling cursed the day she had persuaded the *Chicago Space Mirror* that there would be all sorts of human interest stories to be picked up at the first international grandmaster chess tournament in which an electronic computing machine was entered.
Not that there weren’t enough humans around, it was the interest that was in doubt. The large hall was crammed with energetic dark-suited men of whom a disproportionately large number were bald, wore glasses, were faintly untidy and indefinably shabby, had Slavic or Scandinavian features, and talked foreign languages.
They yakked interminably. The only ones who didn’t were scurrying individuals with the eager-zombie look of officials.
Chess sets were everywhere﻿—big ones on tables, still bigger diagram-type electric ones on walls, small peg-in sets dragged from side pockets and manipulated rapidly as part of the conversational ritual and still smaller folding sets in which the pieces were the tiny magnetized disks used for playing in free-fall.
There were signs featuring largely mysterious combinations of letters: F.I.D.E., W.B.M., U.S.C.F., U.S.S.F., U.S.S.R. and U.N.E.S.C.O. Sandra felt fairly sure about the last three.
The many clocks, bedside table size, would have struck a familiar note except that they had little red flags and wheels sprinkled over their faces and they were all in pairs, two clocks to a case. That Siamese-twin clocks should be essential to a chess tournament struck Sandra as a particularly maddening circumstance.
---
Her last assignment had been to interview the pilot pair riding the first American manned circum-lunar satellite﻿—and the five alternate pairs who hadn’t made the flight. This tournament hall seemed to Sandra much further out of the world.
Overheard scraps of conversation in reasonably intelligible English were not particularly helpful. Samples:
“They say the Machine has been programmed to play nothing but pure Barcza System and Indian Defenses﻿—and the Dragon Formation if anyone pushes the King Pawn.”
“Hah! In that case﻿ ﻿…”
“The Russians have come with ten trunkfuls of prepared variations and they’ll gang up on the Machine at adjournments. What can one New Jersey computer do against four Russian grandmasters?”

===Chinese===

桑德拉·莉·格雷林（Sandra Lea Grayling）心里咒骂着，嘴上不出声，免得吓着那些对衣着光鲜的年轻女性抱有美好幻想的人——她咒骂自己当初说服《芝加哥太空镜报》的那一天：让她相信这第一届有电子计算机参加的国际象棋特级大师赛，能捞到各种人情味故事。
不是人不夠——成问题的是“人情味”。大厅里挤满了精力充沛、深色西装的男人，其中不成比例的一大摊秃顶、戴眼镜、有点儿温逼不整、说不出的寒酸，长着斯拉夫或斯堪的纳维亚面孔，说着外国话。
他们喋喋不休。唯一不说的，是那些带着官员特有的热切僵尸神色、跑来跑去的人。
棋具到处都是——桌上摆着大的，墙上挂着更大的电演示盘，衣兜里拖出插钉式小棋盘、作为谈话仪式的一部分飞快摆弄，还有更小的折叠盘，棋子是自由落体对局用的微型磁片。
招牌上尽是神秘的字母组合：F.I.D.E.、W.B.M.、U.S.C.F.、U.S.S.F.、U.S.S.R.和U.N.E.S.C.O.。后三个桑德拉还有几分把握。
那许多床头柜大小的时钟本该给人熟悉感，要不是钟面上撒满了小红旗和小转轮，而且一律成对，一个壳子两只钟。连体婴钟竟是一场象棋比赛的必需品，这一点在桑德拉看来尤其让人抓狂。
---
她的上一个任务是采访美国第一颗载人绕月卫星的飞行员夫妇——外加没轮上飞行的五对候补夫妇。可在桑德拉眼里，这个比赛大厅比那还要远离人间。
偶尔飘进耳朵的几段勉强听得懂的英语也不特别有用。样本：
“他们说那台机器被编好了程序，只会下纯粹的巴尔扎体系和印度防御——谁挺王前兵，就上龙式阵形。”
“哈！那样的话……”
“俄国人带了十箱子准备好的变着来，封棋的时候会一起围剿机器。一台新泽西计算机，怎么对抗四个俄国特级大师？”

===Original===

“I heard the Russians have been programmed﻿—with hypnotic cramming and somno-briefing. Votbinnik had a nervous breakdown.”
“Why, the Machine hasn’t even a *Haupturnier* or an intercollegiate won. It’ll over its head be playing.”
“Yes, but maybe like Capa at San Sebastian or Morphy or Willie Angler at New York. The Russians will look like potzers.”
“Have you studied the scores of the match between Moon Base and Circum-Terra?”
“Not worth the trouble. The play was feeble. Barely Expert Rating.”
Sandra’s chief difficulty was that she knew absolutely nothing about the game of chess﻿—a point that she had slid over in conferring with the powers at the *Space Mirror*, but that now had begun to weigh on her. How wonderful it would be, she dreamed, to walk out this minute, find a quiet bar and get pie-eyed in an evil, ladylike way.
---
“Perhaps mademoiselle would welcome a drink?”
“You’re durn tootin’ she would!” Sandra replied in a rush, and then looked down apprehensively at the person who had read her thoughts.
It was a small sprightly elderly man who looked like a somewhat thinned down Peter Lorre﻿—there was that same impression of the happy Slavic elf. What was left of his white hair was cut very short, making a silvery nap. His pince-nez had quite thick lenses. But in sharp contrast to the somberly clad men around them, he was wearing a pearl-gray suit of almost exactly the same shade as Sandra’s﻿—a circumstance that created for her the illusion that they were fellow conspirators.
“Hey, wait a minute,” she protested just the same. He had already taken her arm and was piloting her toward the nearest flight of low wide stairs. “How did you know I wanted a drink?”
“I could see that mademoiselle was having difficulty swallowing,” he replied, keeping them moving. “Pardon me for feasting my eyes on your lovely throat.”
“I didn’t suppose they’d serve drinks here.”
“But of course.” They were already mounting the stairs. “What would chess be without coffee or schnapps?”
“Okay, lead on,” Sandra said. “You’re the doctor.”
“Doctor?” He smiled widely. “You know, I like being called that.”
“Then the name is yours as long as you want it﻿—Doc.”
---
Meanwhile the happy little man had edged them into the first of a small cluster of tables, where a dark-suited jabbering trio was just rising. He snapped his fingers and hissed through his teeth. A white-aproned waiter materialized.

===Chinese===

“我听说俄国人自己也被编了程——用催眠填鸭和睡眠简报。沃特宾尼克都精神崩溃了。”
“曝，那台机器连个*顶级联赛*或校际冠军都没赢过。这可完全是超水平发挥。”
“是，可也许像圣塞巴斯蒂安的卡帕，或者墨菲，或者纽约的威利·安格勒。俄国人会显得像群废物。”
“你研究过月球基地和环地站那场对抗赛的棋谱吗？”
“不值得费那个劲。下得太弱。勉强专家级。”
桑德拉的主要难处在于她对国际象棋一窍不通——这一点她在跟《太空镜报》高层面谈时含糊过去了，现在却开始压在心上。她梦想着，要是这会儿能走出去、找间安静的酒吧、以淑女的邪恶方式喝个烂醉，多美妙。
---
“小姐也许愿意来一杯？”
“那还用说！”桑德拉脱口而出，然后带着点不安低头看那个读出她心事的人。
他是个矮小活泼的老头，像瘦了一圈彼得·洛尔——同样是那种快乐的斯拉夫小精灵的印象。剩下的白发剪得很短，像一层银色的绒。他的夹鼻眼镜镜片颇厚。但跟周围一身阴沉的男人截然相反，他穿一身珠灰色的西装，色调几乎与桑德拉的一模一样——这让她产生了一种错觉：两人是同谋。
“嘿，等一下，”她照样抗议。他已经挽起她的手臂，往最近的一道低宽台阶导航。“你怎么知道我想喝酒？”
“我看得出小姐吞咽有困难，”他答道，脚下不停。“原谅我饱览了您可爱的喉咙。”
“我原以为这儿不供酒。”
“当然供。”他们已经在上台阶了。“没有咖啡和白兰地，象棋算什么？”
“行，带路吧，”桑德拉说。“听医生的。”
“医生？”他笑得很开。“您知道吗，我喜欢这个称呼。”
“那这名字就归您了，想用多久用多久——医生。”
---
这时那个快乐的小老头已经把他们挤进一小片桌子中的第一张，一桌吹吹喳喳的深色西装三人正起身。他打了個响指，从牙缝里嘶了一声。一个系白围裙的侍者现了形。

===Original===

“For myself black coffee,” he said. “For mademoiselle rhine wine and seltzer?”
“That’d go fine.” Sandra leaned back. “Confidentially, Doc, I was having trouble swallowing﻿ ﻿… well, just about everything here.”
He nodded. “You are not the first to be shocked and horrified by chess,” he assured her. “It is a curse of the intellect. It is a game for lunatics﻿—or else it creates them. But what brings a sane and beautiful young lady to this 64-square madhouse?”
Sandra briefly told him her story and her predicament. By the time they were served, Doc had absorbed the one and assessed the other.
“You have one great advantage,” he told her. “You know nothing whatsoever of chess﻿—so you will be able to write about it understandably for your readers.” He swallowed half his demitasse and smacked his lips. “As for the Machine﻿—you *do* know, I suppose, that it is not a humanoid metal robot, walking about clanking and squeaking like a late medieval knight in armor?”
“Yes, Doc, but﻿ ﻿…” Sandra found difficulty in phrasing the question.
“Wait.” He lifted a finger. “I think I know what you’re going to ask. You want to know why, if the Machine works at all, it doesn’t work perfectly, so that it always wins and there is no contest. Right?”
Sandra grinned and nodded. Doc’s ability to interpret her mind was as comforting as the bubbly, mildly astringent mixture she was sipping.
He removed his pince-nez, massaged the bridge of his nose and replaced them.
“If you had,” he said, “a billion computers all as fast as the Machine, it would take them all the time there ever will be in the universe just to play through all the possible games of chess, not to mention the time needed to classify those games into branching families of wins for White, wins for Black and draws, and the additional time required to trace out chains of key-moves leading always to wins. So the Machine can’t play chess like God. What the Machine can do is examine all the likely lines of play for about eight moves ahead﻿—that is, four moves each for White and Black﻿—and then decide which is the best move on the basis of capturing enemy pieces, working toward checkmate, establishing a powerful central position and so on.”
---
“That sounds like the way a man would play a game,” Sandra observed. “Look ahead a little way and try to make a plan. You know, like getting out trumps in bridge or setting up a finesse.”

===Chinese===

“我要黑咖啡，”他说。“小姐来莱茵酒兑苏打水？”
“那挺好。”桑德拉向后一靠。“说实话，医生，我正吞咽困难……咳，这儿的一切都咽不下去。”
他点头。“您不是第一个被象棋吓得魂飞魄散的人，”他让她放心。“这是理智的诅咒。这是疯子的游戏——要不就是它制造疯子。可一位健全又美丽的年轻小姐，怎么会被带到这座六十四格的疯人院来？”
桑德拉简单讲了她的来由和处境。等到酒水上来，“医生”已经听完了前者、掂量完了后者。
“您有一个大优势，”他对她说。“您对象棋一无所知——所以您能为读者写出他们看得懂的报道。”他咣下半小杯咖啡，咂咂嘴。“至于机器——您总该如何道，它不是那种人形金属机器人，不是那种像中世纪晚期披甲骑士一样走来走去哗啦作响、吱吱叫的？”
“知道，医生，可是……”桑德拉觉得问题不好措辞。
“等等。”他竖起一根手指。“我想我知道您要问什么。您想知道，既然机器居然能下棋，它为什么不完美到永远赢、让比赛毫无悬念。对吧？”
桑德拉咧嘴点头。“医生”读心的本事，跟她嘴里那杯气泡汩汩、微微收敛的混合液一样让人安心。
他摘下夹鼻眼镜，揉揉鼻梁，又戴上。
“假如您有，”他说，“十亿台跟机器一样快的计算机，让它们只把所有可能的对局都打一遍，也得用完宇宙里未来会有的一切时间，遑论再把这些对局分门别类归入白胜、黑胜、和棋的分支家族，遑论再追踪那些始终通向胜利的关键着链。所以机器没法像上帝那样下棋。机器能做的，是把大约八步棋以内——也就是白黑各四步——所有像样的变例都检视一遍，然后根据吃对方子、向将死推进、构筑强大中心等标准，决定哪一步最好。”
---
“这听着像人下棋的路数，”桑德拉说。“往前看一小段，试着订个计划。你知道，像桥牌里清将牌，或者布置一个飞牌。”

===Original===

“Exactly!” Doc beamed at her approvingly. “The Machine *is* like a man. A rather peculiar and not exactly pleasant man. A man who always abides by sound principles, who is utterly incapable of flights of genius, but who never makes a mistake. You see, you are finding human interest already, even in the Machine.”
Sandra nodded. “Does a human chess player﻿—a grandmaster, I mean﻿—ever look eight moves ahead in a game?”
“Most assuredly he does! In crucial situations, say where there’s a chance of winning at once by trapping the enemy king, he examines many more moves ahead than that﻿—thirty or forty even. The Machine is probably programmed to recognize such situations and do something of the same sort, though we can’t be sure from the information World Business Machines has released. But in most chess positions the possibilities are so very nearly unlimited that even a grandmaster can only look a very few moves ahead and must rely on his judgment and experience and artistry. The equivalent of those in the Machine is the directions fed into it before it plays a game.”
“You mean the programming?”
“Indeed yes! The programming is the crux of the problem of the chess-playing computer. The first practical model, reported by Bernstein and Roberts of I.B.M. in 1958 and which looked four moves ahead, was programmed so that it had a greedy worried tendency to grab at enemy pieces and to retreat its own whenever they were attacked. It had a personality like that of a certain kind of chess-playing dub﻿—a dull-brained woodpusher afraid to take the slightest risk of losing material﻿—but a dub who could almost always beat an utter novice. The W.B.M. machine here in the hall operates about a million times as fast. Don’t ask me how, I’m no physicist, but it depends on the new transistors and something they call hypervelocity, which in turn depends on keeping parts of the Machine at a temperature near absolute zero. However, the result is that the Machine can see eight moves ahead and is capable of being programmed much more craftily.”
“A million times as fast as the first machine, you say, Doc? And yet it only sees twice as many moves ahead?” Sandra objected.
“There is a geometrical progression involved there,” he told her with a smile.

===Chinese===

“正是！”“医生”赞许地冲她微笑。“机器*确实*像一个人。一个相当古怪、也说不上讨喜的人。一个永远恪守稳妥原则的人，完全发不出天才的灵光，可也从不犯错。您瞧，您已经在找到人情味了，连机器身上都有。”
桑德拉点头。“人类棋手——我是说特级大师——下棋时会往前看八步吗？”
“当然会！在关键时刻，比如说有机会困住对方老王立刻取胜，他往前看的远不止八步——甚至三四十步。机器多半也被编了程序来识别这类局面、做出类似的事，不过世界商业机器公司公布的资料不足以让我们确信。但在绝大多数局面里，可能性多到近乎无限，连特级大师也只能看很少几步，必须依靠他的判断、经验和艺术。机器里与这些等价的，是开赛前喂给它的那套指令。”
“你是说程序？”
“正是！程序是下棋计算机问题的核心。第一台实用型号，I.B.M.的伯恩斯坦和罗伯茨1958年报告的，往前看四步，编程编得它有一种贪婪而紧张的倾向：见子就吃，自己的子一被攻就缩。它的性格像某一类棋篓子——一个怕丢一兵一卒、不敢冒丝毫风险的呆脑推木头的——不过这种篓子几乎总能赢彻底的门外汉。大厅里这台W.B.M.机器，运行速度大约快一百万倍。别问我怎么快的，我不是物理学家，但它靠的是新晶体管和某种他们称为“超高速”的东西，而那又靠把机器的一部分保持在接近绝对零度的温度。总之，结果是机器能看八步棋，且能够被编程得狡猾得多。”
“你说比第一台机器快一百万倍，医生？可它只多看一倍的步数？”桑德拉抗议。
“这里头有个几何级数，”他笑着对她说。

===Original===

“Believe me, eight moves ahead is a lot of moves when you remember that the Machine is errorlessly examining every one of thousands of variations. Flesh-and-blood chess masters have lost games by blunders they could have avoided by looking only one or two moves ahead. The Machine will make no such oversights. Once again, you see, you have the human factor, in this case working for the Machine.”
“Savilly, I have been looking all over the place for you!”
A stocky, bull-faced man with a great bristling shock of black, gray-flecked hair had halted abruptly by their table. He bent over Doc and began to whisper explosively in a guttural foreign tongue.
---
Sandra’s gaze traveled beyond the balustrade. Now that she could look down at it, the central hall seemed less confusedly crowded. In the middle, toward the far end, were five small tables spaced rather widely apart and with a chessboard and men and one of the Siamese clocks set out on each. To either side of the hall were tiers of temporary seats, about half of them occupied. There were at least as many more people still wandering about.
On the far wall was a big electric scoreboard and also, above the corresponding tables, five large dully glassy chessboards, the White squares in light gray, the Black squares in dark.
One of the five wall chessboards was considerably larger than the other four﻿—the one above the Machine.
Sandra looked with quickening interest at the console of the Machine﻿—a bank of keys and some half-dozen panels of rows and rows of tiny telltale lights, all dark at the moment. A thick red velvet cord on little brass standards ran around the Machine at a distance of about ten feet. Inside the cord were only a few gray-smocked men. Two of them had just laid a black cable to the nearest chess table and were attaching it to the Siamese clock.
Sandra tried to think of a being who always checked everything, but only within limits beyond which his thoughts never ventured, and who never made a mistake﻿ ﻿…
“Miss Grayling! May I present to you Igor Jandorf.”
She turned back quickly with a smile and a nod.
“I should tell you, Igor,” Doc continued, “that Miss Grayling represents a large and influential Midwestern newspaper. Perhaps you have a message for her readers.”
The shock-headed man’s eyes flashed. “I most certainly do!” At that moment the waiter arrived with a second coffee and wine-and-seltzer.

===Chinese===

“相信我，八步已经很多了，如果你记得机器是在毫无差错地检视几千个变例里的每一个。血肉之躯的象棋大师们，输过的棋里有只要往前看一两步就能避免的失着。机器不会出这种纰漏。您瞧，您又一次碰到了人的因素——这回是替机器干活的。”
“萨维利，我到处找你！”
一个敦实的、公牛脸的男人在他们的桌旁猛地站住，一头黑发夹杂灰白、茂密倒竖。他俯向“医生”，开始用一种喉音很重的外语爆炸性地耳语。
---
桑德拉的目光越过栏杆。能从上往下看了，中央大厅就不显得那么乱糟糟地拥挤。中部靠远端，五张小桌彼此隔得挺开，每张桌上摆着一副棋、一盒棋子和一对连体婴钟。大厅两侧是临时搭的一排排座位，坐了大约一半。至少还有同样多的人仍在四处游荡。
远墙上是一块大型电子计分牌，还有在对应桌子上方的五面大而发暗的演示棋盘，白格浅灰，黑格深黑。
五面墙棋里有一面明显比其余四面大——机器上方那面。
桑德拉兴致渐浓地打量机器的控制台——一排键，加六面嵌板，嵌板上是一行行微型指示灯，此刻全灭着。一条厚重的红天鹅绒绳，架在小铜柱上，在离机器约十英尺处绕了一圈。绳内只有几个穿灰罩衫的男人。其中两个刚把一条黑色电缆铺到最近的棋桌，正往连体婴钟上接。
桑德拉试着去想这样一个存在：永远核对一切，但只在一定的限度之内，思想从不越过那限度，而且从不犯错……
“格雷林小姐！请允许我向您介绍伊戈尔·詹多夫。”
她带着笑和点头很快转回来。
“我得告诉你，伊戈尔，”“医生”继续说，“格雷林小姐代表一家有影响的中西部大报。也许你有话要对她的读者说。”
倒竖头发的男人眼睛一亮。“当然有！”正在此时侍者端来了第二杯咖啡和又一杯酒加苏打。

===Original===

Jandorf seized Doc’s new demitasse, drained it, set it back on the tray with a flourish and drew himself up.

“Tell your readers, Miss Grayling,” he proclaimed, fiercely arching his eyebrows at her and actually slapping his chest, “that I, Igor Jandorf, will defeat the Machine by the living force of my human personality! Already I have offered to play it an informal game blindfold﻿—I, who have played 50 blindfold games simultaneously! Its owners refuse me. I have challenged it also to a few games of rapid-transit﻿—an offer no true grandmaster would dare ignore. Again they refuse me. I predict that the Machine will play like a great oaf﻿—at least against *me*. Repeat: I, Igor Jandorf, by the living force of my human personality, will defeat the Machine. Do you have that? You can remember it?”
“Oh yes,” Sandra assured him, “but there are some other questions I very much want to ask you, Mr. Jandorf.”
“I am sorry, Miss Grayling, but I must clear my mind now. In ten minutes they start the clocks.”
While Sandra arranged for an interview with Jandorf after the day’s playing session, Doc reordered his coffee.
“One expects it of Jandorf,” he explained to Sandra with a philosophic shrug when the shock-headed man was gone. “At least he didn’t take your wine-and-seltzer. Or did he? One tip I have for you: don’t call a chess master Mister, call him Master. They all eat it up.”
“Gee, Doc, I don’t know how to thank you for everything. I hope I haven’t offended Mis﻿—Master Jandorf so that he doesn’t﻿—”
“Don’t worry about that. Wild horses couldn’t keep Jandorf away from a press interview. You know, his rapid-transit challenge was cunning. That’s a minor variety of chess where each player gets only ten seconds to make a move. Which I don’t suppose would give the Machine time to look three moves ahead. Chess players would say that the Machine has a very slow sight of the board. This tournament is being played at the usual international rate of 15 moves an hour, and﻿—”
“Is that why they’ve got all those crazy clocks?” Sandra interrupted.
“Oh, yes. Chess clocks measure the time each player takes in making his moves. When a player makes a move he presses a button that shuts his clock off and turns his opponent’s on. If a player uses too much time, he loses as surely as if he were checkmated.

“告诉你的读者，格雷林小姐，”他宣称着，朝她凶猛地扬起眉毛，还真拍了拍自己的胸口，“我，伊戈尔·詹多夫，将以我人类人格的鲜活力量击败机器！我早已提出跟它下一盘非正式的盲棋——我，下过50盘盲棋同时赛的人！它的主人们拒绝了我。我还向它挑战过几盘快棋——真正的特级大师绝不敢轻视的提议。他们又拒绝了我。我预言，机器会下得像个大傻瓜——至少对*我*。重复一遍：我，伊戈尔·詹多夫，将以我人类人格的鲜活力量击败机器。记下了吗？记得住吗？”
“记得，”桑德拉向他保证，“不过还有些别的问题我很想问您，詹多夫先生。”
“很抱歉，格雷林小姐，可我现在必须澄清心绪。十分钟后他们开钟。”
桑德拉与詹多夫安排好当日赛程后的采访，“医生”重新点了咖啡。
“詹多夫就是这路人，”倒竖头发的男人走后，他带着哲学式的耸肩对桑德拉解释。“至少他没端走你的酒加苏打。是吧？给你一条提示：别叫象棋大师“先生”，要叫“大师”。他们个个吃这一套。”
“天哪，医生，真不知道怎么谢您才好。我希望我没得罪詹——詹多夫大师，免得他不肯——”
“那不用担心。野马也拉不住詹多夫离开一次新闻采访。您知道吗，他那个快棋挑战挺狡猾。那是象棋的一个小变种，每人每步只有十秒钟。我想那不会给机器留出看三步棋的时间。棋手们会说，机器的棋盘视觉很慢。本届比赛按通常的国际赛率每小时走15步进行，而且——”
“所以他们才搞了那一堆疯子钟？”桑德拉插嘴。
“对。象棋钟计量每方走棋用的时间。棋手走一步，按一下钮，关掉自己的钟、开动对手的。谁用时太多，就跟被将死一样输棋。

“Gosh, I don’t know. Say, Doc, is it really true that Master Jandorf has played 50 games at once blindfolded? I can’t believe that.”

“Of course not!” Doc assured her. “It was only 49 and he lost two of those and drew five. Jandorf always exaggerates. It’s in his blood.”

Doc chuckled. “Not exactly,” he said gently. “He is originally a Pole and now he has Argentinian citizenship. You have a program, don’t you?”

“Cripes, Doc, they all sound like they were Russians,” Sandra said after a bit. “Except this Willie Angler. Oh, he’s the boy wonder, isn’t he?”
Doc nodded. “Not such a boy any longer, though. He’s﻿ ﻿… Well, speak of the Devil’s children.﻿ ﻿… Miss Grayling, I have the honor of presenting to you the only grandmaster ever to have been ex-chess-champion of the United States while still technically a minor﻿—Master William Augustus Angler.”
A tall, sharply-dressed young man with a hatchet face pressed the old man back into his chair.
“How are you, Savvy, old boy old boy?” he demanded. “Still chasing the girls, I see.”
“Please, Willie, get off me.”
“Can’t take it, huh?” Angler straightened up somewhat. “Hey waiter! Where’s that chocolate malt? I don’t want it *next* year. About that *ex-*, though. I was swindled, Savvy. I was robbed.”
“Willie!” Doc said with some asperity. “Miss Grayling is a journalist. She would like to have a statement from you as to how you will play against the Machine.”
---
Angler grinned and shook his head sadly. “Poor old Machine,” he said. “I don’t know why they take so much trouble polishing up that pile of tin just so that I can give it a hit in the head. I got a hatful of moves it’ll burn out all its tubes trying to answer. And if it gets too fresh, how about you and me giving its low-temperature section the hotfoot, Savvy? The money W.B.M.’s putting up is okay, though. That first prize will just fit the big hole in my bank account.”
“I know you haven’t the time now, Master Angler,” Sandra said rapidly, “but if after the playing session you could grant me﻿—”
“Sorry, babe,” Angler broke in with a wave of dismissal. “I’m dated up for two months in advance. Waiter! I’m here, not there!” And he went charging off.
Doc and Sandra looked at each other and smiled.
“Chess masters aren’t exactly humble people, are they?” she said.
Doc’s smile became tinged with sad understanding. “You must excuse them, though,” he said. “They really get so little recognition or recompense. This tournament is an exception. And it takes a great deal of ego to play greatly.”
“I suppose so. So World Business Machines is responsible for this tournament?”
“Correct. Their advertising department is interested in the prestige. They want to score a point over their great rival.”
“But if the Machine plays badly it will be a black eye for them,” Sandra pointed out.

“医生”点头。“不过不再那么童了。他……呃，说曹操曹操到……格雷林小姐，我荣幸地向您介绍唯一一位在法律上尚未成年时就拿过美国象棋冠军头衔的特级大师——威廉·奥古斯塔斯·安格勒大师。”

安格勒咧嘴一笑，又难过地摇头。“可怜的老机器，”他说。“我不明白他们干吗费那么大劲打磨那一堆洋铁皮，就为让我给它脑袋上来一下。我有一帽子的着法，够它把真空管全烧了去应。它要是太放肆——萨维，你我去给它低温段来个鞭炮怎么样？不过W.B.M.出的钱倒是不错。那笔头奖刚好能堵上我银行账户的大窟窿。”
“我知道您现在没时间，安格勒大师，”桑德拉赶紧说，“不过要是赛后的时段您能赏我——”
“抱歉，宝贝，”安格特勒一挥手打断。“我两个月的档期都排满了。侍者！我在这儿，不在那儿！”说着冲出去了。
“医生”和桑德拉对视一笑。
“象棋大师们不太算谦虚的人吧？”她说。
“医生”的笑染上一层伤感的谅解。“不过您得原谅他们，”他说。“他们得到的承认和报酬实在太少。这次比赛是个例外。而要把棋下到伟大，需要很大很大的自我。”
“大概吧。所以这届比赛是世界商业机器公司主办的？”

“True,” Doc agreed thoughtfully. “W.B.M. must feel very sure﻿ ﻿… It’s the prize money they’ve put up, of course, that’s brought the world’s greatest players here. Otherwise half of them would be holding off in the best temperamental-artist style. For chess players the prize money is fabulous﻿—$35,000, with $15,000 for first place, and all expenses paid for all players. There’s never been anything like it. Soviet Russia is the only country that has ever supported and rewarded her best chess players at all adequately. I think the Russian players are here because U.N.E.S.C.O. and F.I.D.E. (that’s Federation Internationale des Echecs﻿—the international chess organization) are also backing the tournament. And perhaps because the Kremlin is hungry for a little prestige now that its space program is sagging.”
“But if a Russian doesn’t take first place it will be a black eye for them.”
Doc frowned. “True, in a sense. *They* must feel very sure﻿ ﻿… Here they are now.”

Four men were crossing the center of the hall, which was clearing, toward the tables at the other end. Doubtless they just happened to be going two by two in close formation, but it gave Sandra the feeling of a phalanx.
“The first two are Lysmov and Votbinnik,” Doc told her. “It isn’t often that you see the current champion of the world﻿—Votbinnik﻿—and an ex-champion arm in arm. There are two other persons in the tournament who have held that honor﻿—Jal and Vanderhoef the director, way back.”
“Will whoever wins this tournament become champion?”
“Oh no. That’s decided by two-player matches﻿—a very long business﻿—after elimination tournaments between leading contenders. This tournament is a round robin: each player plays one game with every other player. That means nine rounds.”
“Anyway there *are* an awful lot of Russians in the tournament,” Sandra said, consulting her program. “Four out of ten have U.S.S.R. after them. And Bela Grabo, Hungary﻿—that’s a satellite. And Sherevsky and Krakatower are Russian-sounding names.”
“The proportion of Soviet to American entries in the tournament represents pretty fairly the general difference in playing strength between the two countries,” Doc said judiciously. “Chess mastery moves from land to land with the years. Way back it was the Muslims and the Hindus and Persians. Then Italy and Spain. A little over a hundred years ago it was France and England.

“可要是俄国人拿不到第一，对他们就是一记黑眼圈。”
医生皱眉。“从某种意义上说，是的。*他们*必定非常有把握……喏，他们来了。”

四个人正穿过正在清空的大厅中央，朝另一头的桌子走去。他们八成只是碰巧两人一组、紧挨着走，但桑德拉看着像一方阵。
“前两个是利斯莫夫和沃特宾尼克，”医生告诉她。现任世界冠军——沃特宾尼克——和一位前冠军手挽手，这可不常见。本届比赛里还有两个人得过那个荣誉——贾尔，还有主任范德胡夫，那都是早年的事了。”
“这届比赛的胜者会成为世界冠军吗？”
“哦不。那要靠两人对抗赛决定——很长的一套——先在主要竞争者之间打淘汰赛。本届是循环赛：每人跟其他每人都下一盘。也就是九轮。”
“反正比赛里俄国人也实在多得吓人，”桑德拉看着秩序册说。“十个人里四个后面印着U.S.S.R.。还有贝拉·格拉博，匈牙利——那是卫星国。谢列夫斯基和克拉卡托沃听着也像俄国名字。”
“比赛里苏方与美方参赛人的比例，相当公允地代表了两国总体棋力差距，”医生评判道。“棋艺的宗主随年月流转。早年是穆斯林、印度人和波斯人。然后是意大利和西班牙。一百多年前是法国和英国。

===Original===

Then Germany, Austria and the New World. Now it’s Russia﻿—including of course the Russians who have run away from Russia. But don’t think there aren’t a lot of good Anglo-Saxon types who are masters of the first water. In fact, there are a lot of them here around us, though perhaps you don’t think so. It’s just that if you play a lot of chess you get to looking Russian. Once it probably made you look Italian. Do you see that short bald-headed man?”
“You mean the one facing the Machine and talking to Jandorf?”
“Yes. Now that’s one with a lot of human interest. Moses Sherevsky. Been champion of the United States many times. A very strict Orthodox Jew. Can’t play chess on Fridays or on Saturdays before sundown.” He chuckled. “Why, there’s even a story going around that one rabbi told Sherevsky it would be unlawful for him to play against the Machine because it is technically a *golem*﻿—the clay Frankenstein’s monster of Hebrew legend.”
Sandra asked, “What about Grabo and Krakatower?”
---
Doc gave a short scornful laugh. “Krakatower! Don’t pay any attention to *him*. A senile has-been, it’s a scandal he’s been allowed to play in this tournament! He must have pulled all sorts of strings. Told them that his lifelong services to chess had won him the honor and that they had to have a member of the so-called Old Guard. Maybe he even got down on his knees and cried﻿—and all the time his eyes on that expense money and the last-place consolation prize! Yet dreaming schizophrenically of beating them all! Please, don’t get me started on Dirty Old Krakatower.”
“Take it easy, Doc. He sounds like he would make an interesting article? Can you point him out to me?”
“You can tell him by his long white beard with coffee stains. I don’t see it anywhere, though. Perhaps he’s shaved it off for the occasion. It would be like that antique womanizer to develop senile delusions of youthfulness.”
“And Grabo?” Sandra pressed, suppressing a smile at the intensity of Doc’s animosity.
Doc’s eyes grew thoughtful. “About Bela Grabo (why are three out of four Hungarians named Bela?) I will tell you only this: That he is a very brilliant player and that the Machine is very lucky to have drawn him as its first opponent.”
He would not amplify his statement. Sandra studied the Scoreboard again.
“This Simon Great who’s down as programming the Machine. He’s a famous physicist, I suppose?”

===Chinese===

然后是德国、奥地利和新大陆。如今是俄国——当然也包括从俄国跑出来的俄国人。不过别以为就没有许多一流盘格鲁-撒克逊血统的大师。其实，我们周围就有一大批，虽然您也许不觉得。只是，象棋下多了，人就长得像俄国人。早年下多了大概长得像意大利人。您看见那个矮个子秃顶了吗？”
“正对着机器、在跟詹多夫说话的那个？”
“对。这位可是人情味十足。摩西·谢列夫斯基。多次美国冠军。非常严守教规的犹太正教徒。星期五和星期六日落前不能下棋。”他咯咯笑了。“喏，甚至有传言说，有位拉比告诉谢列夫斯基，跟机器对弈是不合教法的，因为严格说它是个*泥人*——希伯来传说里那种泥造的怪物。”
桑德拉问：“格拉博和克拉卡托沃呢？”
---
医生短促而轻蔑地笑了一声。“克拉卡托沃！*他*你一个字也别信。一个老糊涂的过时货，让他进这届比赛是丑闻！他准是拉遍了所有的线。跟他们说，他对象棋的终身功勋赢得了这份荣誉，说他们必须有一位所谓老卫队的成员。也许还跪下哭了一场——眼睛从头到尾盯着那笔津贴和末名安慰奖！居然还精神分裂地梦想把他们全都赢了！求求您，别让我提起老脏鬼克拉卡托沃。”
“别激动，医生。他听着倒像能写出一篇有趣的文章？您能指给我看吗？”
“认他靠那条带咖啡渍的白长胡子。不过我哪儿也没看见。也许他为这场合剃了。那老风流倒干得出这种事——上了年纪还妄想自己年轻。”
“格拉博呢？”桑德拉追问，忍着笑看医生这股仇气的劲儿。
医生的眼神变得沉思。“关于贝拉·格拉博（为什么四个匈牙利人里仨叫贝拉？）我只告诉您这一句：他是个非常辉煌的棋手，而机器第一轮就抽到他，实在走运。”
他不肯多说。桑德拉又研究起计分牌。
“这个给机器编程的西蒙·格雷特。想必是著名物理学家？”

===Original===

“By no means. That was the trouble with some of the early chess-playing machines﻿—they were programmed by scientists. No, Simon Great is a psychologist who at one time was a leading contender for the world’s chess championship. I think W.B.M. was surprisingly shrewd to pick him for the programming job. Let me tell you﻿—No, better yet﻿—”
Doc shot to his feet, stretched an arm on high and called out sharply, “Simon!”
A man some four tables away waved back and a moment later came over.
“What is it, Savilly?” he asked. “There’s hardly any time, you know.”
---
The newcomer was of middle height, compact of figure and feature, with graying hair cut short and combed sharply back.
Doc spoke his piece for Sandra.
Simon Great smiled thinly. “Sorry,” he said, “but I am making no predictions and we are giving out no advance information on the programming of the Machine. As you know, I have had to fight the Players’ Committee tooth and nail on all sorts of points about that and they have won most of them. I am not permitted to re-program the Machine at adjournments﻿—only between games (I did insist on that and get it!) And if the Machine breaks down during a game, its clock keeps running on it. My men are permitted to make repairs﻿—if they can work fast enough.”
“That makes it very tough on you,” Sandra put in. “The Machine isn’t allowed any weaknesses.”
Great nodded soberly. “And now I must go. They’ve almost finished the countdown, as one of my technicians keeps on calling it. Very pleased to have met you, Miss Grayling﻿—I’ll check with our P.R. man on that interview. Be seeing you, Savvy.”
The tiers of seats were filled now and the central space almost clear. Officials were shooing off a few knots of lingerers. Several of the grandmasters, including all four Russians, were seated at their tables. Press and company cameras were flashing. The four smaller wallboards lit up with the pieces in the opening position﻿—white for White and red for Black. Simon Great stepped over the red velvet cord and more flash bulbs went off.
“You know, Doc,” Sandra said, “I’m a dog to suggest this, but what if this whole thing were a big fake? What if Simon Great were really playing the Machine’s moves? There would surely be some way for his electricians to rig﻿—”
Doc laughed happily﻿—and so loudly that some people at the adjoining tables frowned.
“Miss Grayling, that is a wonderful idea!

===Chinese===

“完全不是。早年的几台下棋机器毛病就出在这——都是科学家编的程。不，西蒙·格雷特是个心理学家，一度还是世界象棋冠军的主要竞争者。我认为W.B.M.挑他做编程，精明得叫人吃惊。让我告诉您——不，这样更好——”
医生倏地站起来，高举一只手臂，尖声喊道：“西蒙！”
四张桌子外一个男人挥手回应，片刻后走了过来。
“什么事，萨维利？”他问。“时间不多了，你知道。”
---
来者中等个子，身材和五官都很紧凑，花白的头发剪得短，利落地向后梳。
医生替桑德拉说明了来意。
西蒙·格雷特淡淡一笑。“抱歉，”他说，“我不做预测，我们也不发布机器编程的任何事先信息。您知道，为了这类问题我跟棋手委员会寸土必争，他们赢下了大部分。我不被允许在封棋时给机器改程序——只能在两盘棋之间（这一点我坚持住了，赢到了！）而如果机器在一盘棋中出故障，它的钟照走不误。我的人可以修——如果他们修得够快。”
“这对你太苛刻了，”桑德拉插话。“机器一点弱点都不许有。”
格雷特严肃地点头。“现在我得走了。‘倒计时’快完了——我的一个技师总这么叫。很高兴认识您，格雷林小姐——采访的事我跟我们的公关确认。回头见，萨维。”
看台坐满了，中央场地也空了。工作人员在驱散几小撮流连的人。几位特级大师，包括全部四个俄国人，已在各自桌前落座。新闻界和公司的相机闪个不停。四面小墙盘亮起开局阵形的棋子——白方白色，黑方红色。西蒙·格雷特跨过红丝绒绳，又有闪光灯炸响。
“我说，医生，”桑德拉开口，“我提这个可太冱了——可万一整件事是个大骗局呢？万一机器的棋其实都是西蒙·格雷特在走？他的电工们肯定有法子装个机关——”
医生开心地笑了——笑得那么响，邻桌的人都皱起眉。
“格雷林小姐，这主意妙极了！

===Original===

I will probably steal it for a short story. I still manage to write and place a few in England. No, I do not think that is at all likely. W.B.M. would never risk such a fraud. Great is completely out of practice for actual tournament play, though not for chess-thinking. The difference in style between a computer and a man would be evident to any expert. Great’s own style is remembered and would be recognized﻿—though, come to think of it, his style was often described as being machinelike﻿ ﻿…” For a moment Doc’s eyes became thoughtful. Then he smiled again. “But no, the idea is impossible. Vanderhoef as Tournament Director has played two or three games with the Machine to assure himself that it operates legitimately and has grandmaster skill.”
“Did the Machine beat him?” Sandra asked.
---
Doc shrugged. “The scores weren’t released. It was very hush-hush. But about your idea, Miss Grayling﻿—did you ever read about Maelzel’s famous chess-playing automaton of the 19th Century? That one too was supposed to work by machinery (cogs and gears, not electricity) but actually it had a man hidden inside it﻿—your Edgar Poe exposed the fraud in a famous article. In *my* story I think the chess robot will break down while it is being demonstrated to a millionaire purchaser and the young inventor will have to win its game for it to cover up and swing the deal. Only the millionaire’s daughter, who is really a better player than either of them﻿ ﻿… yes, yes! Your Ambrose Bierce too wrote a story about a chess-playing robot of the clickety-clank-grr kind who murdered his creator, crushing him like an iron grizzly bear when the man won a game from him. Tell me, Miss Grayling, do you find yourself imagining this Machine putting out angry tendrils to strangle its opponents, or beaming rays of death and hypnotism at them? I can imagine﻿ ﻿…”
While Doc chattered happily on about chess-playing robots and chess stories, Sandra found herself thinking about him. A writer of some sort evidently and a terrific chess buff. Perhaps he was an actual medical doctor. She’d read something about two or three coming over with the Russian squad. But Doc certainly didn’t sound like a Soviet citizen.
He was older than she’d first assumed. She could see that now that she was listening to him less and looking at him more. Tired, too. Only his dark-circled eyes shone with unquenchable youth.

===Chinese===

我说不定要把它偷去写短篇。我在英国还能写几篇、卖出去几篇。不，我认为那完全不可能。W.B.M.绝不会冒那种诈骗的险。格雷特对真正的比赛实战彻底生了，虽然象棋思维没生。计算机和人的风格差异，任何行家都看得出来。格雷特本人的棋风人们记得，认得出来——不过话说回来，他的棋风倒常被说成机器一样……”有一瞬间医生的眼神若有所思。随即他又笑了。“不，不可能。比赛主任范德胡夫亲自跟机器下过两三盘，确信它运作合法、具有特级大师棋力。”
“机器赢了他吗？”桑德拉问。
---
医生耸了耸肩。“棋谱没公布。非常保密。不过说到您的点子，格雷林小姐——您读到过19世纪梅尔策那台著名的下棋自动机吗？那台也被说成全靠机械（齿轮齿簧，不是电），实际上里头藏着一个人——你们的埃德加·坡在一篇名文里揭穿过。在我的小说里，我想让那台象棋机器人在给百万富翁买家演示时出了故障，年轻发明人只好替它把棋赢下来遮丑、成文交易。只是那百万富翁的女儿，其实比他们俩都下得好……对，对！你们的安布罗斯·比尔斯也写过一篇下棋机器人的小说，咔哒咔哒-吱嘎那种，谋杀了自己的创造者——那人赢了一盘棋，它就像铁灰熊一样把他抱碎了。告诉我，格雷林小姐，您有没有忍不住想象这台机器伸出愤怒的触须勒死对手，或者朝他们放射死亡和催眠光线？我都能想象……”
医生喋喋不休地聊着下棋机器人和象棋小说，桑德拉发现自己开始想他这个人。显然是个作家，兼狂热的棋迷。也许还真是个医学博士。她读到过俄国代表团带了两位三位医生来。可医生的口气完全不像苏联公民。
他比她起初以为的年纪大。现在她听他说话少了、看他多了，看得出来。也累了。只有他那双黑眼圈的眼睛，闪着扑不灭的年轻。

===Original===

A useful old guy, whoever he was. An hour ago she’d been sure she was going to muff this assignment completely and now she had it laid out cold. For the umpteenth time in her career Sandra shied away from the guilty thought that she wasn’t a writer at all or even a reporter, she just used dime-a-dozen female attractiveness to rope a susceptible man (young, old, American, Russian) and pick his brain﻿ ﻿…
She realized suddenly that the whole hall had become very quiet.
Doc was the only person still talking and people were again looking at them disapprovingly. All five wallboards were lit up and the changed position of a few pieces showed that opening moves had been made on four of them, including the Machine’s. The central space between the tiers of seats was completely clear now, except for one man hurrying across it in their direction with the rapid yet quiet, almost tiptoe walk that seemed to mark all the officials. *Like morticians’ assistants*, she thought. He rapidly mounted the stairs and halted at the top to look around searchingly. His gaze lighted on their table, his eyebrows went up, and he made a beeline for Doc. Sandra wondered if she should warn him that he was about to be shushed.
The official laid a hand on Doc’s shoulder. “Sir!” he said agitatedly. “Do you realize that they’ve started your clock, Dr. Krakatower?”
---
Sandra became aware that Doc was grinning at her. “Yes, it’s true enough, Miss Grayling,” he said. “I trust you will pardon the deception, though it was hardly one, even technically. Every word I told you about Dirty Old Krakatower is literally true. Except the long white beard﻿—he never wore a beard after he was 35﻿—that part was an out-and-out lie! Yes, yes! I will be along in a moment! Do not worry, the spectators will get their money’s worth out of me! And W.B.M. did not with its expense account buy my soul﻿—that belongs to the young lady here.”
Doc rose, lifted her hand and kissed it. “Thank you, mademoiselle, for a charming interlude. I hope it will be repeated. Incidentally, I should say that besides﻿ ﻿… (Stop pulling at me, man!﻿—there can’t be five minutes on my clock yet!)﻿ ﻿… that besides being Dirty Old Krakatower, grandmaster emeritus, I am also the special correspondent of the *London Times*. It is always pleasant to chat with a colleague.

===Chinese===

管他是谁，一个有用的老头。一小时前她还确信这次任务要彻底搞砸，现在已经被安排得明明白白。职业生涯里第无数次，桑德拉躲开那个负罪的念头：她根本不是作家，连记者都不算——她不过是拿一毛钱一打的女性魅力，套住一个易感男人（年轻的、年老的、美国的、俄国的），掏他的脑子……
她忽然意识到整个大厅安静了下来。
医生是唯一还在说话的人，人们又朝他们投来不满的目光。五面墙盘全亮了，几个棋子位置的变动显示其中四面已经完成了开局头几步，包括机器那面。看台之间的中央场地现在彻底空了，只有一个人正快步穿过、朝他们这边来，那种又快又轻、几乎踮着脚的步子，似乎标记着所有工作人员的身份。像殡仪馆的助手，她想。他迅速登上台阶，在顶上站住，探询地环顾。目光落在他们的桌上，眉毛扬起，径直奔向医生。桑德拉琢磨着要不要提醒他，他就要被“嘘”了。
那工作人员把手搭在医生肩上。“先生！”他激动地说。“您意识到他们已经开您的钟了吗，克拉卡托沃博士？”
---
桑德拉发觉医生正冲她咧嘴笑。“是的，千真万确，格雷林小姐，”他说。“我相信您会原谅这个欺骗，虽然严格说来它算不上。我关于老脏鬼克拉卡托沃说的每一个字都字字属实。除了长白胡子——他35岁以后就不留胡子——那部分是彻头彻尾的谎！是，是！我马上就来！别担心，观众们从我身上能得到他们票价的价值！还有，W.B.M.的那点津贴并没有买走我的灵魂——那属于这位小姐。”
医生站起身，捧起她的手吻了吻。“谢谢您，小姐，这一段插曲很迷人。希望能再有一次。顺便说一句，我应该说明，除了……（别拽我，伙计！——我钟上肯定还没走满五分钟！）……除了身为老脏鬼克拉卡托沃、荣誉特级大师之外，我还是《伦敦时报》的特派记者。跟同行聊天总是愉快的。

===Original===

Please do not hesitate to use in your articles any of the ideas I tossed out, if you find them worthy﻿—I sent in my own first dispatch two hours ago. Yes, yes, I come! Au revoir, mademoiselle!”
He was at the bottom of the stairs when Sandra jumped up and hurried to the balustrade.
“Hey, Doc!” she called.
He turned.
“Good luck!” she shouted and waved.
He kissed his hand to her and went on.
People glared at her then and a horrified official came hurrying. Sandra made big frightened eyes at him, but she couldn’t quite hide her grin.

===Chinese===

我抛出的这些想法，您要是觉得有价值，尽管拿去用在您的文章里——我自己的第一篇电讯两小时前就发出去了。是，是，来了！回头见，小姐！”
他到台阶底下时，桑德拉跳起来奔到栏杆边。
“嘿，医生！”她喊。
他回头。
“祝好运！”她大声喊，挥着手。
他吻了吻自己的手心给她，走了。
人们瞪她，一个吓坏了的工作人员匆匆赶来。桑德拉朝他睁大惊恐的眼睛，却没能完全藏住嘴角的笑。

## A Bad Day for Sales / 推销的倒霉日
===Original===

The big bright doors of the office building parted with a pneumatic *whoosh* and Robie glided onto Times Square. The crowd that had been watching the fifty-foot-tall girl on the clothing billboard get dressed, or reading the latest news about the Hot Truce scrawl itself in yard-high script, hurried to look.
Robie was still a novelty. Robie was fun. For a little while yet, he could steal the show. But the attention did not make Robie proud. He had no more emotions than the pink plastic giantess, who dressed and undressed endlessly whether there was a crowd or the street was empty, and who never once blinked her blue mechanical eyes. But she merely drew business while Robie went out after it.
For Robie was the logical conclusion of the development of vending machines. All the earlier ones had stood in one place, on a floor or hanging on a wall, and blankly delivered merchandise in return for coins, whereas Robie searched for customers. He was the demonstration model of a line of sales robots to be manufactured by Shuler Vending Machines, provided the public invested enough in stocks to give the company capital to go into mass production.
The publicity Robie drew stimulated investments handsomely. It was amusing to see the TV and newspaper coverage of Robie selling, but not a fraction as much fun as being approached personally by him. Those who were usually bought anywhere from one to five hundred shares, if they had any money and foresight enough to see that sales robots would eventually be on every street and highway in the country.
---
Robie radared the crowd, found that it surrounded him solidly, and stopped. With a carefully built-in sense of timing, he waited for the tension and expectation to mount before he began talking.
“Say, Ma, he doesn’t look like a robot at all,” a child said. “He looks like a turtle.”
Which was not completely inaccurate. The lower part of Robie’s body was a metal hemisphere hemmed with sponge rubber and not quite touching the sidewalk. The upper was a metal box with black holes in it. The box could swivel and duck.
A chromium-bright hoopskirt with a turret on top.
“Reminds me too much of the Little Joe Paratanks,” a legless veteran of the Persian War muttered, and rapidly rolled himself away on wheels rather like Robie’s.
His departure made it easier for some of those who knew about Robie to open a path in the crowd.

===Chinese===

办公楼那扇明亮的大门气动人声地分开，罗比（Robie）滑上了时代广场。原本在看服装广告牌上那个五十英尺高的姑娘穿衣服的人群，或读着“热停战”最新消息用一码高的字迹在新闻屏上爬行的人群，都涌过来看。
罗比还是件新鲜玩意。罗比好玩。还有一小阵子，他能抢尽风头。但这份关注不让罗比骄傲。他不比那个粉色塑料女巨人更有情绪——那巨人穿衣脱衣永无休止，不管有没有人群、街上空不空，蓝色的机械眼睛一次也不眨。只是她坐等生意，而罗比是追出去找生意的。
因为罗比是售货机发展的逻辑终点。此前所有售货机都立在一个地方，立在地板上或挂在墙上，收了硬币就木然交货，而罗比搜寻顾客。他是舒勒自动售货机公司销售机器人系列的演示样机——前提是公众把股票买够，让公司有资本投入量产。
罗比吸引来的公众关注漂亮地刺激了投资。看电视和报纸报道罗比卖东西挺有趣，但远不及被他亲自找上门来有趣。被找上的人通常都会买上一到五百股，只要他们有钱、又有足够的远见，看得出销售机器人早晚要遍布全国的每条街道和公路。
---
罗比对人群做了雷达扫描，发现自己被团团围住，便停下了。凭着精心内建的时机感，他等紧张和期待攒够了，才开口说话。
“咦，妈，他根本不像机器人嘛，”一个孩子说。“他像只乌龟。”
这不算全错。罗比身体的下半部是一个金属半球，镶着海绵橡胶边，不怎地碰着人行道。上半部是一个带黑孔的金属箱。箱子能转能低头。
一条镀铬发亮的箍裙，顶上一座炮塔。
“太让我想起小乔空降坦克了，”一位没了腿的波斯战争老兵咕哝着，飞快地滚开了，他那轮子倒和罗比的挺像。
他的离开，让一些了解罗比的人更容易在人群里开出一条道。

===Original===

Robie headed straight for the gap. The crowd whooped.
Robie glided very slowly down the path, deftly jogging aside whenever he got too close to ankles in skylon or sockassins. The rubber buffer on his hoopskirt was merely an added safeguard.
The boy who had called Robie a turtle jumped in the middle of the path and stood his ground, grinning foxily.
Robie stopped two feet short of him. The turret ducked. The crowd got quiet.
“Hello, youngster,” Robie said in a voice that was smooth as that of a TV star, and was, in fact, a recording of one.
The boy stopped smiling. “Hello,” he whispered.
“How old are you?” Robie asked.
“Nine. No, eight.”
“That’s nice,” Robie observed. A metal arm shot down from his neck, stopped just short of the boy.
The boy jerked back.
“For you,” Robie said.
The boy gingerly took the red polly-lop from the neatly fashioned blunt metal claws, and began to unwrap it.
“Nothing to say?” asked Robie.
“Uh﻿—thank you.”
After a suitable pause, Robie continued. “And how about a nice refreshing drink of Poppy Pop to go with your polly-lop?” The boy lifted his eyes, but didn’t stop licking the candy. Robie waggled his claws slightly. “Just give me a quarter and within five seconds﻿—”
A little girl wriggled out of the forest of legs. “Give me a polly-lop, too, Robie,” she demanded.
“Rita, come back here!” a woman in the third rank of the crowd called angrily.
Robie scanned the newcomer gravely. His reference silhouettes were not good enough to let him distinguish the sex of children, so he merely repeated, “Hello, youngster.”
“Rita!”
“Give me a polly-lop!”
Disregarding both remarks, for a good salesman is single-minded and does not waste bait, Robie said winningly, “I’ll bet you read *Junior Space Killers*. Now I have here﻿—”
“Uh-uh, I’m a girl. *He* got a polly-lop.”
---
At the word “girl,” Robie broke off. Rather ponderously, he said, “I’ll bet you read *Gee-Gee Jones, Space Stripper*. Now I have here the latest issue of that thrilling comic, not yet in the stationary vending machines. Just give me fifty cents and within five﻿—”
“Please let me through. I’m her mother.”
A young woman in the front rank drawled over her powder-sprayed shoulder, “I’ll get her for you,” and slithered out on six-inch platform shoes. “Run away, children,” she said nonchalantly.

===Chinese===

罗比径直朝那道口子滑去。人群欢呼。
罗比顺着人路缓缓滑行，每当太逼近穿着斯凯纶或袜靴的脚踝就灵巧地侧身让开。箍裙上的橡胶缓冲圈只是多一道保险。
那个说罗比像乌龟的男孩跳到路中央，站定不动，狡黠地咧嘴笑。
罗比在离他两英尺处停住。炮塔低了低头。人群静下来。
“你好，小家伙，”罗比用一种平滑得像电视明星的声音说——事实上那就是一位明星的录音。
男孩不笑了。“你好，”他小声说。
“你几岁了？”罗比问。
“九岁。不，八岁。”
“真好，”罗比说。一条金属臂从他颈下帜地探出，停在男孩跟前。
男孩猛地缩回。
“给你的，”罗比说。
男孩小心翼翼地从那副做工精致的钝金属爪里接过红色棒棒糖，剥起糖纸来。
“不说点什么吗？”罗比问。
“呃——谢谢。”
停顿得恰到好处后，罗比继续。“配着棒棒糖，来一瓶清爽的波比汽水怎么样？”男孩抬起眼，但没停下属糖。罗比轻轻摇了摇爪子。“只要给我两毛五，五秒之内——”
一个小女孩从腿的森林里扭出来。“也给我一根棒棒糖，罗比，”她要求。
“丽塔，回来！”人群第三排一个女人怒喊。
罗比严肃地扫描新来者。他的参考剪影不够好，分不出孩子的性别，于是只是重复：“你好，小家伙。”
“丽塔！”
“给我棒棒糖！”
两句都不理——好的推销员心无旁鹜，不浪费饵——罗比动听地说：“我打赌你看《少年太空杀手》。我这儿正好有——”
“嗯-嗯，我是女孩。*他*得了棒棒糖。”
---
听到“女孩”二字，罗比刹住。他慢吞吞地说：“我打赌你看《太空艳星吉吉·琼斯》。我这儿正好有那本惊险漫画的最新一期，固定售货机还没上架的。只要给我五毛，五秒之内——”
“请让让。我是她妈妈。”
前排一个年轻女人从扑了粉的肩膀上方拖着腔说：“我去给你把她拎回来，”然后踩着六英寸厚底鞋滑了出去。“跑开，孩子们，”她不经意地说。

===Original===

Lifting her arms behind her head, she pirouetted slowly before Robie to show how much she did for her bolero half-jacket and her form-fitting slacks that melted into skylon just above the knees. The little girl glared at her. She ended the pirouette in profile.
---
At this age-level, Robie’s reference silhouettes permitted him to distinguish sex, though with occasional amusing and embarrassing miscalls. He whistled admiringly. The crowd cheered.
Someone remarked critically to a friend, “It would go over better if he was built more like a real robot. You know, like a man.”
The friend shook his head. “This way it’s subtler.”
No one in the crowd was watching the newscript overhead as it scribbled, “Ice Pack for Hot Truce? Vanadin hints Russ may yield on Pakistan.”
Robie was saying, “… in the savage new glamor-tint we have christened Mars Blood, complete with spray applicator and fit-all fingerstalls that mask each finger completely except for the nail. Just give me five dollars﻿—uncrumpled bills may be fed into the revolving rollers you see beside my arm﻿—and within five seconds﻿—”
“No, thanks, Robie,” the young woman yawned.
“Remember,” Robie persisted, “for three more weeks, seductivizing Mars Blood will be unobtainable from any other robot or human vendor.”
“No, thanks.”
Robie scanned the crowd resourcefully. “Is there any gentleman here﻿ ﻿…” he began just as a woman elbowed her way through the front rank.
“I told you to come back!” she snapped at the little girl.
“But I didn’t get my polly-lop!”
“… who would care to﻿ ﻿…”
“Rita!”
“Robie cheated. Ow!”
---
Meanwhile, the young woman in the half bolero had scanned the nearby gentlemen on her own. Deciding that there was less than a fifty percent chance of any of them accepting the proposition Robie seemed about to make, she took advantage of the scuffle to slither gracefully back into the ranks. Once again the path was clear before Robie.
He paused, however, for a brief recapitulation of the more magical properties of Mars Blood, including a telling phrase about “the passionate claws of a Martian sunrise.”
But no one bought. It wasn’t quite time. Soon enough silver coins would be clinking, bills going through the rollers faster than laundry, and five hundred people struggling for the privilege of having their money taken away from them by America’s first mobile sales robot.

===Chinese===

她双臂举到脑后，在罗比面前缓缓旋身，展示她为那件波莱罗半短上衣、那条贴身长裤付出了多少——裤子在膝上没入斯凯纶。小女孩瞪她。她以侧影结束旋转。
