有人挑剔地对同伴说：“他要造得更像真正的机器人才叫好。你知道，像个人的样子。”
同伴摇头。“这样才更妙。”
没有人抬头看头顶的新闻屏正在爬写：“热停战要结冰？瓦纳丁暗示俄国人可能在巴基斯坦问题上让步。”
罗比正在说：“……用的是我们命名为‘火星之血’的狂野新魅彩，附喷涂敷器和不挑指型的全套指套，把每根手指完全罩住，只露指甲。只要给我五块——平整的纸币可以喂进我手臂旁那对滚辊——五秒之内——”
“不要，谢了，罗比，”年轻女人打了个哈欠。

“不要，谢了。”
罗比随机应变地扫描人群。“这儿有没有哪位先生……”他刚开始，一个女人挤过前排。

与此同时，那穿半短上衣的年轻女人自己也扫描了一下附近的先生们。判断出他们接受罗比似乎要提的提议的机会不足五成，她趁乱优雅地滑回了队列。罗比面前又空出一条路。
但他停了停，把火星之血更神奇的特性简短重述了一遍，包括一句点睛的“火星日出的热烈之爪”。
可是没人买。时候还没到。很快银币就会叮当作响，纸币过辊快得像过洗衣机，五百人争着享受把钱交给美国第一台移动销售机器人收走的特权。

===Original===

But there were still some tricks that Robie had to do free, and one certainly should enjoy those before starting the more expensive fun.
So Robie moved on until he reached the curb. The variation in level was instantly sensed by his under-scanners. He stopped. His head began to swivel. The crowd watched in eager silence. This was Robie’s best trick.
Robie’s head stopped swiveling. His scanners had found the traffic light. It was green. Robie edged forward. But then the light turned red. Robie stopped again, still on the curb. The crowd softly *ahhed* its delight.
It was wonderful to be alive and watching Robie on such an exciting day. Alive and amused in the fresh, weather-controlled air between the lines of bright skyscrapers with their winking windows and under a sky so blue you could almost call it dark.
(But way, way up, where the crowd could not see, the sky was darker still. Purple-dark, with stars showing. And in that purple-dark, a silver-green something, the color of a bud, plunged down at better than three miles a second. The silver-green was a newly developed paint that foiled radar.)
Robie was saying, “While we wait for the light, there’s time for you youngsters to enjoy a nice refreshing Poppy Pop. Or for you adults﻿—only those over five feet tall are eligible to buy﻿—to enjoy an exciting Poppy Pop fizz. Just give me a quarter or﻿—in the case of adults, one dollar and a quarter; I’m licensed to dispense intoxicating liquors﻿—and within five seconds﻿ ﻿…”
But that was not cutting it quite fine enough. Just three seconds later, the silver-green bud bloomed above Manhattan into a globular orange flower. The skyscrapers grew brighter and brighter still, the brightness of the inside of the Sun. The windows winked blossoming white fire-flowers.
The crowd around Robie bloomed, too. Their clothes puffed into petals of flame. Their heads of hair were torches.
---
The orange flower grew, stem and blossom. The blast came. The winking windows shattered tier by tier, became black holes. The walls bent, rocked, cracked. A stony dandruff flaked from their cornices. The flaming flowers on the sidewalk were all leveled at once. Robie was shoved ten feet. His metal hoopskirt dimpled, regained its shape.
The blast ended. The orange flower, grown vast, vanished overhead on its huge, magic beanstalk. It grew dark and very still. The cornice-dandruff pattered down.

===Chinese===

不过罗比还有些戏法得白送，开演收费的重头戏之前，当然该先享受这些。
于是罗比继续走，直到抵达路缘。高度的变化立刻被他的下扫描器感知。他停下。脑袋开始旋转。人群屏息热望地看着。这是罗比最拿手的戏法。
罗比的脑袋停止旋转。他的扫描器找到了红绿灯。是绿的。罗比朝前蹭。可灯变红了。罗比又停下，仍在路缘上。人群轻轻地*啊*出声，满心欢喜。
活在这样的好日子里、看着罗比，真叫人快活。在明亮摩天楼夹峙的新鲜气候控制空气里活得开心、看得开心——那些楼的窗户眨着眼——头顶的天蓝得几乎可以叫作深蓝。
（可是在很高很高的地方，人群看不见的地方，天还要深。紫深，露出星星。而在那片紫深里，一个银绿色的什么东西，花蕾的颜色，以每秒三英里多的速度扎下来。那银绿是一种新开发的、骗过雷达的涂料。）
罗比正说着：“等灯的工夫，你们这些小家伙正好来一瓶清爽的波比汽水。或者你们大人——只有身高超过五英尺的才有资格购买——来一瓶刺激的波比气泡酒。只要给我两毛五，或者——大人是一块二毛五；我有卖酒执照——五秒之内……”
可这还不够抽点。仅仅三秒之后，那朵银绿色的花蕾在曼哈顿上空绽成一朵球状的橙花。摩天楼越来越亮、亮上加亮，太阳内部的那种亮。窗户眨出绽放的白色火焰之花。
罗比周围的人群也开了花。他们的衣服鼓成一瓣瓣火焰。他们的头发成了火炬。
---
橙花连梗带朵地长大。冲击波来了。眨眼的窗一层层碎掉，变成黑洞。墙弯了、晃了、裂了。石屑从檐口剥落。人行道上的火焰之花被一齐吹平。罗比被推出去十英尺。金属箍裙凹了一下，又弹回原形。
冲击波过去了。长得巨大的橙花，顺着一根硕大的魔豆藤在头顶消失。天黑下来，非常静。檐口的石屑簌簌落下。

A few small fragments rebounded from the metal hoopskirt.
Robie made some small, uncertain movements, as if feeling for broken bones. He was hunting for the traffic light, but it no longer shone either red or green.
He slowly scanned a full circle. There was nothing anywhere to interest his reference silhouettes. Yet whenever he tried to move, his under-scanners warned him of low obstructions. It was very puzzling.
The silence was disturbed by moans and a crackling sound, as faint at first as the scampering of distant rats.
A seared man, his charred clothes fuming where the blast had blown out the fire, rose from the curb. Robie scanned him.
“Good day, sir,” Robie said. “Would you care for a smoke? A truly cool smoke? Now I have here a yet-unmarketed brand﻿ ﻿…”
But the customer had run away, screaming, and Robie never ran after customers, though he could follow them at a medium brisk roll. He worked his way along the curb where the man had sprawled, carefully keeping his distance from the low obstructions, some of which writhed now and then, forcing him to jog. Shortly he reached a fire hydrant. He scanned it. His electronic vision, though it still worked, had been somewhat blurred by the blast.
“Hello, youngster,” Robie said. Then, after a long pause, “Cat got your tongue? Well, I have a little present for you. A nice, lovely polly-lop.
“Take it, youngster,” he said after another pause. “It’s for you. Don’t be afraid.”
His attention was distracted by other customers, who began to rise up oddly here and there, twisting forms that confused his reference silhouettes and would not stay to be scanned properly. One cried, “Water,” but no quarter clinked in Robie’s claws when he caught the word and suggested, “How about a nice refreshing drink of Poppy Pop?”
The rat-crackling of the flames had become a jungle muttering. The blind windows began to wink fire again.
---
A little girl marched, stepping neatly over arms and legs she did not look at. A white dress and the once taller bodies around her had shielded her from the brilliance and the blast. Her eyes were fixed on Robie. In them was the same imperious confidence, though none of the delight, with which she had watched him earlier.
“Help me, Robie,” she said. “I want my mother.”
“Hello, youngster,” Robie said. “What would you like? Comics? Candy?”
“Where is she, Robie? Take me to her.”

几块小碎片从金属箍裙上弹开。
罗比做了几个小小的、迟疑的动作，像在摸有没有骨折。他在找红绿灯，可它既不再亮红也不再亮绿。

寂静被呻吟和一种噼啪声搅扰，起初微弱得像远处老鼠的奔窜。

“日安，先生，”罗比说。“来支烟怎么样？真正清凉的一支烟？我这儿正好有一种还没上市的新牌子……”
可顾客尖叫着跑掉了，而罗比从不追着顾客跑，尽管他能以中速轻快地滚着跟上。他沿着那人倒卧过的路缘一路行进，小心地跟低矮障碍物保持距离——其中一些不时扭动，逼得他绕行。不久他到了一个消防栓前。他扫描它。他的电子视觉虽然还能用，但被爆炸弄得有点模糊。

火焰的老鼠噼啪变成了丛林的咕哝。瞎了的窗又开始眨火。

一个小女孩迈步走来，利落地跨过她看都不看的手臂和腿。一条白裙子和她周围曾经更高的身体替她挡住了强光和冲击波。她的眼睛盯着罗比。那眼里还是先前看他的那种专断的笃信，只是没有了欢喜。
“帮我，罗比，”她说。“我要我妈妈。”

“气球？

===Original===

Would you like to watch me blow up a balloon?”
The little girl began to cry. The sound triggered off another of Robie’s novelty circuits, a service feature that had brought in a lot of favorable publicity.
“Is something wrong?” he asked. “Are you in trouble? Are you lost?”
“Yes, Robie. Take me to my mother.”
“Stay right here,” Robie said reassuringly, “and don’t be frightened. I will call a policeman.” He whistled shrilly, twice.
Time passed. Robie whistled again. The windows flared and roared. The little girl begged, “Take me away, Robie,” and jumped onto a little step in his hoopskirt.
“Give me a dime,” Robie said.
The little girl found one in her pocket and put it in his claws.
“Your weight,” Robie said, “is fifty-four and one-half pounds.”
“Have you seen my daughter, have you seen her?” a woman was crying somewhere. “I left her watching that thing while I stepped inside﻿—*Rita!*”
“Robie helped me,” the little girl began babbling at her. “He knew I was lost. He even called the police, but they didn’t come. He weighed me, too. Didn’t you, Robie?”
But Robie had gone off to peddle Poppy Pop to the members of a rescue squad which had just come around the corner, more robotlike in their asbestos suits than he in his metal skin.

想看我吹个气球吗？”
小女孩哭起来。哭声触发了罗比另一条新奇电路，一项带来过大量好评的服务功能。
“出什么事了吗？”他问。“你遇到麻烦了？你迷路了？”

可罗比已经滚远了，去给刚从拐角过来的一队救援人员推销波比汽水——他们穿着石棉服，比穿金属皮肤的他还更像机器人。

## A Hitch in Space / 太空差事出奌子
===Original===

Once when I was doing a hitch with the Shaulan Space Guard out Scorpio way, my partner Jeff Bogart developed just about the most harmless psychosis you could imagine: he got himself an imaginary companion.
And the imaginary companion turned out to be me.
Well, I’m a pretty nice guy and so having two of me in the ship didn’t seem a particularly bad idea. At first. In fact there’d be advantages of it, I thought. For instance, Jeff liked to talk a weary lot﻿ ﻿… and the imaginary Joe Hansen could spell me listening to him, while I projected a book or just harkened to the wheels going around in my own head against the faint patter of starlight on the hull.
I met Jeff first at a space-rodeo, oddly enough, but now the two of us were out on a servicing check of the orbital beacons and relays and rescue depots of the five planets of the Shaulan system. A completely routine job, its only drawback that it was lengthy. Our ship was an ionic jeep that looked like a fancy fountain pen, but was very roomy for three men﻿—one of them imaginary.
I caught on to Jeff’s little mania by overhearing him talking to me. I’d be coming back from the head or stores or linear accelerator or my bunk, and I’d hear him yakking at me. It embarrassed me the first time, how to go back into the cabin when the other me was there. But I just swam in, and without any transition-strain at all that I could observe Jeff looked around at me, smiling sort of glaze-eyed, and said warmly, “Joe. My buddy Joe. Am I glad they paired us.”
If Jeff had a major fault, as opposed to a species of nuttiness, it was that he was strictly a speak-only-good, positive-thinking guy who always deferred to me. Even idolized me, if you can imagine that. He’d give me such fulsome praise I’d be irked ten times an orbit.
Another thing that helped me catch on was that he always called the other me Joseph.
---
At first I thought the whole thing might be a gag, or maybe a deliberate way of letting off steam against me without violating his always-a-sweet-guy code﻿—like happy husbands cursing in the bathroom﻿—but then came the scrambled eggs.

===Chinese===

当年我在沙乌兰太空卫队当差、驻天蝎座那边的时候，我的搭档杰夫·博加特（Jeff Bogart）得上了一种你能想出的最无害的精神病：他给自己弄出了一个想象中的同伴。
而那个想象中的同伴，就是我。
喏，我这人挺不错，所以船上有两个我似乎不算什么坏主意。起初不算。其实还有好处呢，我想。比方说，杰夫爱说话爱得吓人……想象中的乔·汉森（Joe Hansen）可以替我听着，而我呢，放一本书的投影，或者光听听自己脑子里的转轮，衬着星光打在船壳上的轻响。
我跟杰夫是在一场太空竞技赛上认识的，说来也怪，如今我们俩出来做例行维护，检查沙乌兰系统五颗行星的轨道信标、中继站和救援库。一份彻底的例行差事，唯一的缺点是长。我们的船是一艘离子吉普，样子像一支花哨的钢笔，坐三个人绰绰有余——其中一个还是想象出来的。
我是偷听他跟我说话才发觉杰夫这点小毛病的。我从厕所、储藏室、直线加速器或者铺位回来，就听见他在朝我哇啦哇啦。头一回我挺尴尬：另一个我在舱里，我怎么进舱呢。可我就那么游了进去，没有一点我能察觉的过渡紧张，杰夫转头看我，笑得眼神有点发直，热情地说：“乔。我的好哥们乔。把咱俩编在一起，我可真高兴。”
如果说杰夫有个大毛病——相对于那种疯劲儿而言——那就是他是个只说好话、正面思维的严格分子，对我永远恭恭敬敬。甚至崇拜我，你能想象吗。他那些谄媚的夸奖，每一圈轨道都得把我腻着十回。
帮我识破的另一件事是：他管另一个我总是叫约瑟夫。
---
起初我以为整件事是个玩笑，或者是他一种不动声色的出气法子——冲我撒气又不违反他那套永远做好人的守则——就像快活的丈夫躲进卫生间骂娘——可后来出了炒鸡蛋的事。

===Original===

I’d slept late and when I squinted into the cabin there was Jeff hovering over a plate of yellow fluff and shaking his finger at my empty seat and saying, “Dammit, Joseph, eat your scrambled eggs, I cooked ’em ’specially for you,” and when he crawfished out toward the galley a couple seconds later he was saying, “Now you start on those eggs, Joseph, before I get back.”
I thought for a bit and then I slid into my place and polished them off.
When he floated in with the coffee he gave me another of those glaze-eyed God-fearing looks﻿—but just a mite disappointed, I thought﻿—and said, “Dammit, Joe, you’re perfect! You always clean your plate.”
Apparently when I was there, Joseph just didn’t exist for Jeff. And vice versa. It was sort of eerie, especially with the hum of space in my ears like a seashell and nobody else for five million miles.
Beginning with the scrambled eggs, I discovered that Jeff didn’t exactly idolize Joseph﻿—or even take with him the attitude of “My buddy can do no wrong,” like he did with me. I overheard him criticizing Joseph. Reasonably at first; then I heard him chewing him out﻿—next bullying him.
It made me wistful, that last, thinking how good it would feel to be full-bloodedly cursed to my face once in a while instead of all the sweetness and light. And right there I got the idea for some amateur therapy, Shaula-Deva help me.
I waited for a moment when we were both relaxed and then I said, “Jeff, the trouble with you is you’re too nice. You ought to criticize things more. For a starter, criticize me. Tell me my faults. Go ahead.”
He flushed a little and said, “Dammit, Joe, how can I? You’re perfect!”
“No man is perfect, Jeff,” I told him solemnly, feeling pretty foolish.
“But you’re my buddy I always can trust,” he protested, squirming a bit. “I wish you wouldn’t talk this way.”
---
“Jeff, you can’t trust anybody too far,” I said. “Even good guys can do bad things. When I was a boy there was a kid named Harry I practically worshipped. We lived on a pioneer world of Fomalhaut that had good snow, and we’d hitch rides with our sleds off little airscrew planes taking off. We’d each have a long white line on his sled and loop it beforehand around the plane’s tail-gear and back to the sled. Then we’d hide.

===Chinese===

那天我睡了个懒觉，眯眼往舱里一眕，杰夫正悬在一盘黄澄澄的松软东西上，指着我那张空座位的方向晃手指：“该死，约瑟夫，把你的炒鸡蛋吃了，我特意给你炒的。”几秒后他朝厨房倒退出去时还在说：“现在就开始吃那些蛋，约瑟夫，趁我还没回来。”
我想了想，然后滑进我的位子，把它们吃了个精光。
他端着咖啡飘进来时，又给了我一个那种眼神发直、敬畏上帝的注视——不过我想，带着一点点失望——说：“该死，乔，你太完美了！你总是把盘子吃干净。”
显然，我在的时候，约瑟夫对杰夫就不存在。反过来也一样。这有点瘆人，尤其耳朵里是海螺似的太空嗡鸣，方圆五百万英里再没有别人。
从炒鸡蛋开始，我发现杰夫对约瑟夫并不真是崇拜——甚至连对我那种“我的好哥们不会做错事”的态度都没有。我偷听到他批评约瑟夫。起初还算讲理；后来我听见他在骂他——再后来是欺负他。
最后那种让我怅然，想着偶尔被人当面血淋淋地臭骂一顿该多痛快，好过这没完没了的光明和甜。就在那儿我起了业余心理治疗的念头，沙乌拉-德瓦保佑。
我等到我们俩都放松的时刻，然后说：“杰夫，你的毛病是太好了。你应该多批评点东西。先从批评我开始。说说我的缺点。来吧。”
他有点脸红，说：“该死，乔，我怎么说得出？你完美！”
“没人完美，杰夫，”我郑重地告诉他，自觉相当蠢。
“可你是我永远信得过的好哥们，”他抗议，有点扭动。“你别这么说话。”
---
“杰夫，谁都不能信太深，”我说。好人也会做坏事。我小时候有个叫哈里的孩子，我差不多崇拜他。我们住在北落师门一个拓荒星球上，那儿雪好，我们常挂着雪橇蹭小螺旋桨飞机起飞的顺风。我们各在雪橇上拴一根长长的白绳，事先绕过飞机的尾轮再回到雪橇上。然后我们就躲起来。

===Original===

As soon as the pilot got aboard we’d jump on our sleds and each grab the free end of his line and have one comet of a ride, until the plane took off. Then we’d quick let go.
“Well, one frosty morning I let go and nothing happened, except I started to rise. Harry had tied the free end of my line tight to my sled.
“I could have just rolled off, I suppose, but I didn’t want to lose my sled or my line either. Luckily I had a sheath knife handy and I used it. I even made a whizeroo of a landing. But ever afterwards my feelings toward Harry﻿—”
“Stop it, please, Joe!” Jeff interrupted, very red in the face and shaking a little. “That boy Harry was utterly evil. And I don’t want to hear any more about this, or anything like it, ever again. Understand?”
I told him sure I did. Heck, I could see I’d gone the wrong way about it. I even begged his pardon.
After that I just sweated it out. But I found I couldn’t spend much time on books or my thoughts, I’d keep listening for what Jeff was saying to Joseph. And sometimes when he’d pause for Joseph’s reply I’d catch myself waiting for the imaginary me to make one. So I took to staying in the same cabin as Jeff as much as I could.
That seemed to make him uncomfortable after a while, though he pretended to glory in it. He’d ask me questions like, “Tell me about life, Joe. So I’ll know how to handle myself if we’re ever parted.”
But the weariest things come to an end, even duty orbits around Shaula. And so the time came when we were servicing our last beacon﻿—outside the planet Shaula-by, it was. Next step would be a fast interplanetary orbit for Base at Shaula-near.
I was out working﻿—on a safety line of course, but suit-jetting around more than I needed to, just for the pure joy of it, so that my suit tank was almost dry. I’d switched my suit radio off for a bit, because, working in space, Jeff had taken to just gabbling to me nervously all the time﻿—maybe because he figured there couldn’t be room for Joseph with him in his suit.
I finished up and paused for a last look at the ship. She was sweetly slim from her conical living quarters to the taper-tail of her ionic jet, but she had more junk on her than an amateur asteroid prospector hangs on his suit the first time out. Every duty orbit, fifty scientists come with permission from the Commandant to hang some automatic research gadget on the hull.

===Chinese===

飞行员一上飞机，我们就跳上雪橇，各抓住自己绳子的活头，来一发彗星似的飞驰，直到飞机离地。然后我们赶紧撒手。
“喏，一个霜冻的早晨，我撒了手，却什么也没发生，只除了我开始上升。哈里把我绳子的活头紧紧拴在了我的雪橇上。”
“我本可以干脆滚下去，我想，可我不想丢雪橇也不想丢绳子。幸好手边有把鞘刀，我用上了。甚至还来了个漂亮的着陆。可打那以后我对哈里的感情——”
“别说啦，求你，乔！”杰夫打断我，脸通红，有点发抖。“那个哈里坏透了。这事我不想再听了，任何这样的事，永远不。明白吗？”
我说明白。唉，我看得出我搞反了方向。我甚至求了他原谅。
那以后我就硬熬。可我发现书和自己的念头都安不下心，总在听杰夫在对约瑟夫说什么。有时他停下来等约瑟夫回话，我会发现自己也在等那个想象中的我回一句。于是我尽量跟杰夫待在同一个舱里。
这让他过了一阵不太自在，虽然他假装荣耀得很。他会问我这样的问题：“给我讲讲人生吧，乔。万一咱们分开了，我也好知道怎么自处。”
可再累人的事也有到头的时候，连绕沙乌拉的值班轨道也不例外。于是到了我们维护最后一座信标的时候——在沙乌拉-伴星外面。下一步就是快车道星际轨道、回沙乌拉-近星基地。
我在外面干活——当然系着安全绳，不过喷来喷去超出了必要，纯粹为了好玩，结果宇航服的气罐快干了。我把服内电台关了一小会儿，因为在太空干活时，杰夫变得会一直紧张地冲我絮叨——也许他觉得穿着宇航服，舱里塞不下约瑟夫。
我干完活，停下来最后看一眼船。从锥形的生活舱到离子喷口那渐细的尾巴，她苗条得可爱，可挂的破烂比小行星探矿新手头一回出门挂在宇航服上的还多。每个值班轨道，五十个科学家拿着指挥官的批条来，往船壳上挂某种自动研究玩意。

===Original===

The craziest one this time was a huge flattened band of gold-plated aluminum, little more than foil-thick, attached crosswise just in front of the tail and sticking out twenty feet on each side. I don’t know what it was there for﻿—maybe to measure the effects of space on a Moebius strip﻿—but it looked like a wedding ring that had been stepped on. So Jeff and I called it Trompled Love.
But in spite of the junk, the ship looked mighty sweet against the saffron steppes and baby-blue seas of Shaula-by with Shaula herself, old Lambda Scorpii, flaming warm and wildly beyond, and with “United States” standing out big as life on the ship’s living quarters. United States of Shaula, of course.
---
I was almost dreaming out there, thinking how it hadn’t been such a terrible duty after all, when I saw the ship begin to slide past Shaula.
Poking out of her tail, ghostlier than the flame over a café royale, was the evil blue glow of her jet. In an instant I’d guessed exactly what had happened and was beating myself on the head for not having anticipated it. Joseph had swum into the cabin right after Jeff. And Jeff had yelled at him. “It’s about time, you lazy lunkhead! Everything secure? Okay, I’m switching on the beam!” And I’d probably brought the whole thing about by telling him that damfool sled story﻿—and then sticking to him so close he just had to get rid of me, so as to be with Joseph.
Meanwhile the ship was gathering speed in her sneaky way and the wavy safety line between me and the airlock was starting to straighten.
As you know, an ionic jet’s only good space-to-space. It’s not for heavy-G work; ours could deliver only one-half G at max and was doing less than one-quarter now. Which meant the ship was starting off slower than most ground cars.
But the beam would fire for hours, building up to a terminal velocity of fifteen miles a second and carrying the ship far, far away from lonely Joe Hansen.
Except that we were tied together, of course.
I was very grateful then for the weeks I’d practiced space-roping, though I’d never won any prizes with it, because without thinking I started to whip my line very carefully. And on the third try, just as it was getting pretty straight, I managed to settle it in a notch in one outside end of Trompled Love.

===Chinese===

这回最疯的是一条镀金铝制的扁平大带子，比锡箔厚不了多少，横着绑在尾部靠前一点，两边各伸出去二十英尺。我不知道它干嘛用的——也许测太空对莫比乌斯环的效应——可它看着像一枚被人踩过的结婚戒指。于是杰夫和我管它叫“踩扁的爱”。
可尽管破烂不少，衬着沙乌拉-伴星的橙黄草原和浅蓝海洋，衬着沙乌拉本尊——老天蝎座λ星——在远处火热狂放地燃烧，这船还是秀气极了，“合众国”三个字在生活舱上顶天立地。当然，沙乌兰合众国。
---
我在外面都快出神了，想着这趟差事到底也不算太糟，这时我看见船开始从沙乌拉旁边滑走。
从她尾部探出来的，比皇家咖啡上的火苗还幽，是喷口那邪恶的蓝光。一瞬间我就全猜到了，直敲自己的脑袋怎么没预见到。约瑟夫就在杰夫后面游进了舱。杰夫朝他嚷了。“你可算来了，你这懒大个！都固定好了？好，我开喷了！”而这件事八成是我自己招的——讲了那个雪橇蠢故事——然后又贴他贴得那么紧，害他只好甩掉我，好跟约瑟夫在一起。
与此同时，船正用它那鬼鬼祟祟的方式加速，我和气闸之间那根波状安全绳开始绷直。
你知道，离子喷口只适合太空到太空。它干不了高G的活；我们这台满打满算只能给出半个G，眼下还不到四分之一。也就是说，船起步比大多数地面汽车还慢。
可这道喷流会烧上好几个钟头，累积到每秒十五英里的末速，把船带得远远、远远地离开孤零零的乔·汉森。
当然，除非我们俩拴在一起。
那一刻我特别感激这几周练过的太空套索，虽然从没赢过奖，因为我不假思索就开始非常小心地甩我的绳子。第三下，正当它绷得相当直的时候，我把它稳稳套进了“踩扁的爱”一端外侧的一个凹口里。

===Original===

After that I took up strain on the line as gradually as I could, letting it friction through my gloves for as long as I could before putting all my mass on it﻿—because although one-quarter G isn’t much, it piles up in a few seconds to quite a jerk. I spread that jerk into several little ones.
Well, the last jerk came and the line didn’t part and Trompled Love didn’t crumple much, though the Shaula-light showed me several very nasty-looking wrinkles in it. And there I was trailing along after the ship, though out to one side, and feeling about as much strain on the line as if I were hanging from a cliff on the moon, and knowing I was going about five feet a second faster every second.
---
My idea wanting to be out to the side (and bless my impulses for realizing it was the one important thing!) was to keep my line and myself out of the beam. An ionic jet doesn’t look hot from the side. But from straight on it’s a lot brighter than an arc light﻿—it’s almost as tight as a laser beam﻿—and I didn’t want to think about what it would do to me, even trailing as I was a hundred yards aft.
Though of course long before it had ruined me, it would have disintegrated my line.
My being out to the side was putting the ship off balance on its jet and presumably throwing its course toward base and Shaula-near little by little into error. But that was the least of my worries, believe me.
I thought for a bit and remembered I could talk to Jeff over my suit radio. I decided to try it, not without misgivings.
I tongued it on and said, “Jeff. Oh, Jeff. I’m out here. You forgot me.”
I was going to say some more, but just then he broke in, angry and so loud it made my helmet ring, with, “Joseph! Did you hear anything then?” A pause, then, “Well, clean the wax out of your ears, stupid, because I did! I think we got an enemy out there!”
Another and longer pause, while my blood curdled a bit thicker, then, “Well, okay, Joseph, I’ll go along with you this time. But if I hear the enemy once more, I’m going to suit up and take a rifle and sit in the airlock door until I’ve potted him.”
I tongued the radio off quick, fearful I’d sneeze or something. I had only one faint consolation: Joseph seemed to be a bit on my side, or maybe he was just lazy.

===Chinese===

然后我开始尽可能渐进地给绳子加力，让它先从手套上摩过去尽量久、再吃上我全部的质量——因为四分之一G虽然不大，几秒钟里也攒得出相当的一拽。我把那一拽摊成了好几小拽。
喏，最后一拽来了，绳子没断，“踩扁的爱”也没怎么塌，虽然沙乌拉的光照出上面几道很难看的皱。我就在船后头拖着走了，虽然偏在一侧，绳上的张力跟吊在月亮的悬崖上差不多，而且我知道自己每秒都快五英尺。
---
我想待在侧面的主意（保佑我的直觉，让它意识到这是头等大事！）是不让我的绳子和我自己进喷流。离子喷流从侧面看不烫。可从正对着看，它比弧光灯亮得多——几乎跟激光束一样紧——我不想去想它对我干得出什么，哪怕我拖在一百码后。
当然，早在它毁掉我之前，它就会先让我的绳子解体。
我偏在一侧，让船在喷流上失了衡，多半也在一点点把朝基地和沙乌拉-近星的航线甩偏。可相信我，那是我最不操心的。
我想了想，记起我可以用服内电台跟杰夫说话。我决定试试，不是没有顾虑。
我用舌头抵开电台，说：“杰夫。喂，杰夫。我在外面呢。你把我忘了。”
我还要往下说，他插进来，火气大得震得我头盔嗡嗡响：“约瑟夫！你刚才听见什么了吗？”停了停，然后，“那把你耳朵里的耳屎揧揧干净，蒞货，我听见了！我看外面来了个敌人！”
又停了更长一段，我的血凝得更稠了一点，然后，“好吧，约瑟夫，这回我依你。可我要再听见那敌人一回，我就穿上宇航服、拿一支步枪、坐在气闸门口，直到把他打下来。”
我飞快抵灭了电台，生怕自己打个喷嚏什么的。我只有一点微弱的安慰：约瑟夫似乎略微站在我这边，要不就是他懒。

===Original===

I thought some more, a mite frantic-like now, and after a while I said to myself, *Been going five minutes now, so I’m doing about a quarter of a mile a second﻿—that’s fifteen miles a minute, wow!﻿—but out here velocities are purely relative. My suit does a little better than a quarter G full on. Okay. I’ll jet to the ship.*
No sooner said than acted on﻿—I was beginning to rely too much on impulse now. The suit jet killed my false weight at once and I was off, mighty careful to aim myself along my line or a little outside it, so as not to wander over into the beam.
Pretty soon the tail and Trompled Love were getting noticeably bigger.
Then a lot bigger.
Then my suit fuel ran out.
---
I’d built up enough velocity so that I was still gaining on the ship for a few seconds. In fact, I almost made it. My gauntlet was about to close on Trompled Love when the ship started slowly to pull away. Oh, it was frustrating!
I remembered then what I should have a lot earlier, and grabbed for the ship-end of my line so as not to lose the distance I’d gained﻿—and in my haste I knocked it away from me. The only good thing was that I didn’t knock it out of the notch.
Now I was losing space to the ship faster and faster. Yet all I could do was reel in the me-end of the line as fast as I could. Suddenly the whole line straightened and gave me a bigger jerk than I’d intended. I could see Trompled Love crumple a little. And I was swinging just a bit, like a pendulum.
I used a glove-friction to spread the rest of the jerk, but still I was at the end of my line and Trompled Love had crumpled a bit more before I was coasting along with the ship again.
My side of Trompled Love was bent back maybe twenty degrees. The eye of the beam shone at me from the tail like a pale blue moon. For quite a while it brightened and dimmed as I tick-tock swung.
Meanwhile I was beating my skull for not having thought earlier of the obvious slow-but-safe way of doing it, instead of that lunatic suit-jetting. I once heard a psychologist say we’re mental slaves to power-machinery and I guess he had something.
Clearly all I had to do was climb handover-hand up the line to the ship. At moon gravity that would be easy. If I should get tired I only had to clamp on and rest.
So I waited for my emotions to settle a bit, and then I reached along the line and gave a smooth, medium-strength heave.

===Chinese===

我又想了想，这会儿有点慌了，过了一阵我对自己说：*已经走了五分钟，那我大概是每秒四分之一英里——一分钟十五英里，哇！——可在这儿速度纯属相对。我的服喷全开比四分之一G强一点。行。我喷到船上去。*
说到做到——这会儿我开始过分依赖冲动了。服喷一下子杀死了我的假重量，我出发了，非常小心地让自己顺着绳子或者偏外一点矄准，免得晃进喷流。
很快，船尾和“踩扁的爱”明显变大。
然后大了很多。
然后我的服燃料用光了。
---
我攒下的速度还够再追近船几秒。其实，我差点就成了。我的护手眼看要抓住“踩扁的爱”，船开始慢慢拉开距离。哦，真气人！
我这才记起早该记起的事，去抓绳子的船头那端，免得丢掉追来的距离——慌里慌张反倒把它从身边拍开了。唯一的好处是没把它拍出凹口。
现在我离船越来越快地失距。可我能做的只有尽快收绳子的我这头。突然整根绳子绷直，给了我一下比预想更大的猛拽。我看见“踩扁的爱”又塌了一点。我还微微荡起来，像个钟摆。
剩下的那股冲力我用手套摩擦摊开了，可我还是被吊在了绳子尽头，等我重新贴着船滑行时，“踩扁的爱”又多塌了一点。
我这侧的“踩扁的爱”向后折了大约二十度。喷口的眼睛从船尾照着我，像一轮淡蓝的月亮。好一阵子，它随着我的滴答摆动忽明忽暗。
同时我猛敲自己的脑壳，怎么没早点想到那个显然的又慢又稳的法子，而不是那种玩命的服喷。我听过一位心理学家说，我们是动力机械的精神奴隶，我看他说到点子上了。
显然我该做的就是沿着绳子一把一把攀上船。月球重力下那不难。累了只消扣住歇歇。
于是我等情绪稳了稳，然后顺着绳子伸手，来了一次平滑的、中等力气的引体。

===Original===

Maybe there is something to E.S.P.﻿—at least in a devilish sort of way﻿—because I picked the exact moment when Jeff decided to feed the beam more juice.
There was a *big* jerk and I saw Trompled Love crumple a lot, so that it was pointing more than forty-five degrees aft.
Now there was a steady pull on the line like I was hanging from a cliff on Mars. And the eye of the beam was a blue moon not so pale﻿—in fact more like a sizzling blue sun seen through a light fog.
After that I just didn’t have the heart to try the climb again. Once I started to draw myself up, very cautious, but on the first handhold I seemed to feel along the line Trompled Love crumpling some more and I quit for good.
I figured that at this boost Jeff would be up to proper speed for Shaula-near in less than two hours. Well, I had suit-oxy and refrigeration for longer than that.
Of course if Jeff decided not to cut the beam on schedule, maybe with the idea of eloping with Joseph to the next solar system﻿—well, I’d discover then whether suit-oxy running out would stimulate me to try the climb again alongside the beam.
(Or I could wait until he got her up near the speed of light, when by the General Theory of Relativity the line ought to be shortened enough so that I could hop aboard if I were sudden enough about it﻿ ﻿… *No, Joe Hansen, you quit that*, I told myself, *you don’t want to die with the gears in your head all stripped*.)
Thinking about the beam got me wondering exactly how close I was to it. I unshipped my suit-antenna and pulled it out to full length﻿—about eight feet﻿—and fished around with it in the direction of the beam.
Nothing seemed to happen to it. It didn’t glow or anything; but I suddenly got a little electric shock, and when I drew it back I could see three inches of the tip were gone and the next couple inches were pitted. So much for curiosity.
Next I reattached the antenna to my suit﻿—which turned out to be a lot more troublesome job than unshipping it﻿—and tongued on the radio with the idea of listening in on Jeff.
---
Right away I heard him say, “Wake up, Joseph! I’m going to tell you your faults again. I got a new way of cataloguing them﻿—chronologically. Begin with childhood. You hitched sled-rides on airplanes. That was bad, Joseph, that was against the law.

===Chinese===

也许心灵感应真有那么回事——至少以一种促狭的方式——因为我挑的那个瞬间，恰是杰夫决定给喷流加料的时候。
来了*好大*一拽，我看见“踩扁的爱”塌了一大块，朝后指着超过四十五度。
现在绳上是稳定的拖力，像我吊在火星的悬崖上。喷口的眼睛成了一轮不那么淡的蓝月亮——其实更像隔着一层薄雾看到的一颗滋滋响的蓝太阳。
那以后我再没心思试着攀了。有一回我开始往上引体，非常小心，可第一个把手刚搭上，我似乎顺着绳子感觉到“踩扁的爱”又塌了一些，我就彻底死心了。
我算计着，按这个加速，杰夫不到两小时就能达到去沙乌拉-近星的正经速度。喏，我的服氧和制冷撑得比那久。
当然，要是杰夫决定不按时刻关喷流——也许存心带着约瑟夫私奔到下一个太阳系——那我就到时候看看，服氧耗尽会不会刺激我沿着喷流旁边再攀一回。
（要么我就等他把船推到接近光速，到那时按广义相对论，绳子该缩短到我一跳就能上船——只要我跳得够突然……*不，乔·汉森，打住*，我告诉自己，*你可不想死的时候脑子里齿轮全崩了牙。*）
想着喷流，我开始琢磨自己到底离它多近。我卸下服天线，拉到全长——大约八英尺——朝喷流的方向探了探。
它似乎没怎么样。不发光什么的；可我突然挨了一下小小的电击，收回来一看，尖端没了三英寸，往下两英寸麻点累累。好奇心到此为止。
接着我把天线重新装回宇航服——结果比卸下来麻烦得多——然后抵开电台，打算听听杰夫。
---
马上就听见他说：“醒醒，约瑟夫！我再给你数数你的毛病。我找着了个编目的新法子——按年份。从童年开始。你蹭飞机的雪橇顺风。那是坏的，约瑟夫，那是犯法的。

===Original===

If the man had caught you doing it, if he’d seen you whizzing along there back of him, he’d have had every right to shoot you down in cold blood. Life is hard, Joseph, life is merciless﻿ ﻿…”
Right then I felt a tickle in my throat.
I tried quick to shut off the radio, but it is remarkably difficult to tongue anything when you have a cough coming. It came out finally in a series of squeaky glubs.
“Snap to, Joseph, and listen hard,” I heard Jeff say. “It’s started again. Animal noises this time. You know if they make spacesuits for black panthers, Joseph?”
I tongued off the radio quick, before the followup cough came.
I didn’t have anything left to do now but think. So I thought about Jeff﻿—how there seemed to be one Jeff who hated my guts and another Jeff who idolized me and another Jeff sneaking around in a jungle of sabertooth tigers and﻿ ﻿… heck, there was probably a good twenty Jeffs sitting around inside his skull, some in light, some in darkness, but all of them watching each other and arguing together all the time. It was an odd way to think of a personality﻿—a sort of perpetual Kaffeeklatsch﻿—but it had its points. Maybe some of the little guys weren’t Jeffs at all, but his father and mother and a caveman ancestor or two and maybe some great-great-grandchild butting in now and then from the future﻿ ﻿…
Well, I saw that speculation was getting out of hand so, taking a tip from Jeff, I began to count my own sins.
It took quite a while. Some of them were pretty interesting reading, almost enough to take my mind off my predicament, but I tired of it finally.
Then I began to count the stars.
It was really the longest two hours plus I ever spent, except maybe the time my first big girl disappeared. But I don’t know. The experiences are hard to compare.
I was about halfway through the stars when I went weightless. For an awful instant I thought the line had parted at last, but then I looked toward the ship and saw the bright little moon was gone.
---
Right away I gave a couple of tugs on the line and began to close slowly with the tail. No trouble at all﻿—actually my only difficulty was resisting the temptation to build up more momentum, which would have resulted in a crash landing.
I softed-in on Trompled Love okay, except there was a big spark. The beam must have charged me good. Then I worked my way to the true hull. After that there were handholds.

===Chinese===

要是那人逮住你干这事，看见你在他背后噝噝地飞，他完全有权把你当场击落。人生是艰难的，约瑟夫，人生是无情的……”
就在那时我喉咙痒了。
我赶紧想关电台，可咳嗽将至时舌头什么都抵不动。它终于出来了，一串尖细的咕噜。
“精神点，约瑟夫，仔细听，”我听见杰夫说。“又开始了。这回是野兽叫。你知道他们给黑豹做宇航服吗，约瑟夫？”
后续的咳嗽上来之前，我抵灭了电台。
现在我没剩什么可做的，只有想。于是我想杰夫——似乎有一个恨我入骨的杰夫，一个崇拜我的杰夫，还有一个在剑齿虎的丛林里鬼鬼祟祟的杰夫，还有……唉，他脑壳里八成坐着足足二十个杰夫，有的在光里，有的在黑暗里，可全都互相盯着、无时不刻拌着嘴。这么想一个人格挺怪的——一场永不散场的咖啡茶话会——不过也有它的道理。也许有些小家伙根本不是杰夫，而是他爹、他妈、一两个穴居人老祖宗，兴许还有个曾曾孙不时从未来插一杠子……
好嘛，我看这胡思乱想要出圈，于是学杰夫一招，开始数我自己的罪。
数了好一阵。有些读起来相当带劲，几乎够我忘掉眼下的处境，可我终于数腻了。
然后我开始数星星。
那真是我度过的最长的两个多小时，兴许除了我第一个大姑娘消失的那回。可也难说。这两种经历不好比。
星星数到一半，我失重了。有那么可怕的一瞬我以为绳子终于断了，可我朝船一看，那颗亮亮的小月亮没了。
---
我马上拽了两下绳子，开始朝船尾慢慢合拢。毫无麻烦——其实我唯一的困难是抵制攒更多动量的诱惑，那会以一次撞击着陆收场。
我软软地贴上了“踩扁的爱”，只炸了个大火花。喷流一定把我充了个饱。然后我挪到真正的船壳上。再往后就有把手了。

===Original===

Finally I got to a porthole in the living quarters, and I looked in, and there was Jeff jawing away at my empty seat. I put my helmet against the hull and very faintly I heard him say, “Joseph, I’m still worried about the enemy. I keep thinking I hear him or it. I’m going to make us some coffee, so we’ll stay real alert. You break out the guns.”
I don’t suppose anyone ever moved quite so quietly *and* so quickly in a spacesuit as I did then. I got in the airlock, I got her up to pressure, I got unsuited﻿—and all in less than five minutes, I’m sure. Maybe less than four.
I swam to the cabin. It was empty. I slid into my seat just as Jeff floated in with the coffee.
He went real pale when he spotted me. I saw there might be some trouble this time with the Joseph-Joe transition. But I knew the only way to play it was real cool. I nested there in my seat as if I hadn’t a worry or urge in the world﻿—though my nerves and throat were just screaming for a squirt of that coffee.
“Joe!” he squeaked at last. “Migod, you gave me an awful scare. I thought you’d done a bunk, I thought, you’d spaced yourself, I kept picturing you outside the ship.”
“Why no, Jeff,” I answered quietly. “One way or another, I’ve been in this seat ever since takeoff.”
His brow wrinkled as he thought about that.
I looked at the board and noticed that our terminal trip-velocity read fifteen miles a second. My, my.
Finally Jeff said, “That’s right, you have.” And then, just a shade unhappily, “I might have known. You always tell the truth, Joe﻿—you’re perfect.”

===Chinese===

最后我到了生活舱的一扇舷窗前，往里看，杰夫正对着我那张空座位唠叨。我把头盔贴上船壳，隐隐听见他说：“约瑟夫，我还是担心那个敌人。我总觉得听见他了，或者它。我去给咱们煮点咖啡，好保持高度警惕。你把枪拿出来。”
我猜从没有人穿着宇航服行动得像我当时那样又轻又快。我进了气闸，压了气压，脱了服——我肯定前后不到五分钟。也许不到四。
我游进舱。空的。杰夫端着咖啡飘进来时，我正好滑进自己的座位。
他看见我，脸刷地白了。我看这回约瑟夫-乔的换档怕要出麻烦。可我知道唯一的打法就是绝对冷静。我窝在座位里，仿佛世上没有一丝牵挂或冲动——虽然我的神经和喉咙都在尖叫着想呷一口那咖啡。
“乔！”他终于尖声喊。“老天，你把我吓死了。我以为你开溜了，我以为你把自己丢太空了，我一直在想你在船外面。”
“哪有，杰夫，”我平静地答。“不知怎么的，起飞以来我就一直在这个座位上。”
他皱起眉头琢磨这话。
我看看仪表板，注意到我们的末段行程速度写着每秒十五英里。啧啧。
杰夫终于说：“对，你是在。”然后，隐约有点不快地，“我本该知道的。你总是说实话，乔——你完美。”

## A Pail of Air / 一桶空气
===Original===

Pa had sent me out to get an extra pail of air. I’d just about scooped it full and most of the warmth had leaked from my fingers when I saw the thing.
You know, at first I thought it was a young lady. Yes, a beautiful young lady’s face all glowing in the dark and looking at me from the fifth floor of the opposite apartment, which hereabouts is the floor just above the white blanket of frozen air. I’d never seen a live young lady before, except in the old magazines﻿—Sis is just a kid and Ma is pretty sick and miserable﻿—and it gave me such a start that I dropped the pail. Who wouldn’t, knowing everyone on Earth was dead except Pa and Ma and Sis and you?
Even at that, I don’t suppose I should have been surprised. We all see things now and then. Ma has some pretty bad ones, to judge from the way she bugs her eyes at nothing and just screams and screams and huddles back against the blankets hanging around the Nest. Pa says it is natural we should react like that sometimes.
When I’d recovered the pail and could look again at the opposite apartment, I got an idea of what Ma might be feeling at those times, for I saw it wasn’t a young lady at all but simply a light﻿—a tiny light that moved stealthily from window to window, just as if one of the cruel little stars had come down out of the airless sky to investigate why the Earth had gone away from the Sun, and maybe to hunt down something to torment or terrify, now that the Earth didn’t have the Sun’s protection.
I tell you, the thought of it gave me the creeps. I just stood there shaking, and almost froze my feet and did frost my helmet so solid on the inside that I couldn’t have seen the light even if it had come out of one of the windows to get me. Then I had the wit to go back inside.
Pretty soon I was feeling my familiar way through the thirty or so blankets and rugs Pa has got hung around to slow down the escape of air from the Nest, and I wasn’t quite so scared. I began to hear the tick-ticking of the clocks in the Nest and knew I was getting back into air, because there’s no sound outside in the vacuum, of course. But my mind was still crawly and uneasy as I pushed through the last blankets﻿—Pa’s got them faced with aluminum foil to hold in the heat﻿—and came into the Nest.
---
Let me tell you about the Nest. It’s low and snug, just room for the four of us and our things.

===Chinese===

爸打发我出去多弄一桶空气。我刚舀满，指尖上的暖气差不多漏光了，就在那时我看见了那东西。
你知道，起初我以为那是一位年轻女士。对，一张年轻漂亮女士的脸，在黑暗里发着光，从对面公寓的五楼望着我——在这一带，五楼就是冻空气白毯子上头刚好一层。我从没见过活的年轻女士，除了旧杂志上——妹妹还是个小不点，妈病得厉害、蒞得厉害——吓得我一松手把桶都掉了。换了谁不掉呢，明知道地球上除了爸妈、妹妹和你，人人都死了？
就算这样，我按理也不该惊讶。我们谁都会时不时看见点什么。妈就有些很吓人的，从她冲着什么也没有的地方瞪眼、就叫啊叫啊、缩回挂在小巢四周的毯子里那样子判断。爸说，我们有时那样反应是自然的。
等我捡回桶、再看对面公寓时，我多少明白了妈那些时候的感受，因为我看清了那根本不是年轻女士，只是一点光——一小点光，在窗与窗之间悄悄地挪，就好像一颗狠毒的小星星，从没有空气的天上下来了，要查一查地球为什么离开了太阳，也许还要搜出个什么来折磨折磨、吓唬吓唬，如今地球没有了太阳的保护。
我告诉你，这念头让我浑身发毛。我就那么站着直抖，脚都快冻掉了，头盔里面结的霜也结得死死的，哪怕那光真从哪扇窗里出来抓我，我也看不见它了。后来我才想起回里面去。
很快，我摸着熟悉地穿过爸挂的三十来条毯子和地毯——用来减缓空气从小巢逃走——就没那么怕了。我开始听见小巢里钟的滴答声，知道我回到空气里了，因为外面真空里当然没有声音。可等我推开最后几条毯子——爸在它们朝外那面贴了铝箔保热——走进小巢时，心里还是毛毛的、不安生。
---
让我给你讲讲小巢吧。它又低又暖和，刚够我们四个加我们的东西。

===Original===

The floor is covered with thick woolly rugs. Three of the sides are blankets, and the blankets roofing it touch Pa’s head. He tells me it’s inside a much bigger room, but I’ve never seen the real walls or ceiling.
Against one of the blanket-walls is a big set of shelves, with tools and books and other stuff, and on top of it a whole row of clocks. Pa’s very fussy about keeping them wound. He says we must never forget time, and without a sun or moon, that would be easy to do.
The fourth wall has blankets all over except around the fireplace, in which there is a fire that must never go out. It keeps us from freezing and does a lot more besides. One of us must always watch it. Some of the clocks are alarm and we can use them to remind us. In the early days there was only Ma to take turns with Pa﻿—I think of that when she gets difficult﻿—but now there’s me to help, and Sis too.
It’s Pa who is the chief guardian of the fire, though. I always think of him that way: a tall man sitting cross-legged, frowning anxiously at the fire, his lined face golden in its light, and every so often carefully placing on it a piece of coal from the big heap beside it. Pa tells me there used to be guardians of the fire sometimes in the very old days﻿—vestal virgins, he calls them﻿—although there was unfrozen air all around then and you didn’t really need one.
He was sitting just that way now, though he got up quick to take the pail from me and bawl me out for loitering﻿—he’d spotted my frozen helmet right off. That roused Ma and she joined in picking on me. She’s always trying to get the load off her feelings, Pa explains. He shut her up pretty fast. Sis let off a couple of silly squeals too.
Pa handled the pail of air in a twist of cloth. Now that it was inside the Nest, you could really feel its coldness. It just seemed to suck the heat out of everything. Even the flames cringed away from it as Pa put it down close by the fire.
Yet it’s that glimmery white stuff in the pail that keeps us alive. It slowly melts and vanishes and refreshes the Nest and feeds the fire. The blankets keep it from escaping too fast. Pa’d like to seal the whole place, but he can’t﻿—building’s too earthquake-twisted, and besides he has to leave the chimney open for smoke.
Pa says air is tiny molecules that fly away like a flash if there isn’t something to stop them.

===Chinese===

地上铺着厚厚的毛绒地毯。三面是毯子，顶上的毯子贴着爸的头。他告诉我这是在一间大得多的屋子里，可我从没见过真正的墙和天花板。
靠着一面毯墙是一个大棚架，摆着工具、书和别的零碎，顶上一整排钟。爸上发条上得可紧了。他说我们绝不能忘了时间，没有了太阳和月亮，忘了是很容易的。
第四面墙挂满毯子，只留着壁炉周围，炉里有火，永远不许灭。它保我们不冻死，还有好多别的用处。我们当中必须永远有人看着它。有几只钟是闹钟，可以拿来提醒。早年间只有妈跟爸轮班——她犯脾气的时候我就想想这个——现在有我帮忙了，妹妹也算一个。
不过爸才是火的主守。我一向是那样想他的：一个高个子男人盘腿坐着，着急地皱着眉看火，有皱纹的脸在火光里金黄金黄的，隔一阵就小心地从旁边那大煤堆上拣一块煤添进去。爸告诉我，在很老很老的年头，有时候也有守火的——他管他们叫圣火贞女——虽说那时候四面八方都是不冻的空气，其实用不着守。
他这会儿就那么坐着，不过还是飞快地起来接过我的桶，骂我磨蹭——他一眼就瞅见我冻住的头盔了。这一嗓子把妈惊动了，她也加入进来数落我。她总想把心里的闷气往外倒，爸解释说。他很快让她住了嘴。妹妹也趁机傻乐了两声。
爸拿一截布抒着，拎那桶空气。这会儿它进了小巢，你才真感觉得出它的冷。它简直要把所有东西的热都吸走。爸把它放在火边上时，连火苗都躲着它。
可就是桶里那种闪着微光的白东西养活了我们。它慢慢化掉、变没，让小巢换上新气，喂火。毯子不让它跑得太快。爸想把整个地方封死，可封不了——楼让地震抒得太厉害了，再说烟囱还得开着走烟。
爸说，空气是很小很小的分子，没有东西拦着，一眨眼就飞没了。

===Original===

We have to watch sharp not to let the air run low. Pa always keeps a big reserve supply of it in buckets behind the first blankets, along with extra coal and cans of food and other things, such as pails of snow to melt for water. We have to go way down to the bottom floor for that stuff, which is a mean trip, and get it through a door to outside.
You see, when the Earth got cold, all the water in the air froze first and made a blanket ten feet thick or so everywhere, and then down on top of that dropped the crystals of frozen air, making another white blanket sixty or seventy feet thick maybe.
Of course, all the parts of the air didn’t freeze and snow down at the same time.
First to drop out was the carbon dioxide﻿—when you’re shoveling for water, you have to make sure you don’t go too high and get any of that stuff mixed in, for it would put you to sleep, maybe for good, and make the fire go out. Next there’s the nitrogen, which doesn’t count one way or the other, though it’s the biggest part of the blanket. On top of that and easy to get at, which is lucky for us, there’s the oxygen that keeps us alive. Pa says we live better than kings ever did, breathing pure oxygen, but we’re used to it and don’t notice. Finally, at the very top, there’s a slick of liquid helium, which is funny stuff. All of these gases in neat separate layers. Like a pussy caffay, Pa laughingly says, whatever that is.
---
I was busting to tell them all about what I’d seen, and so as soon as I’d ducked out of my helmet and while I was still climbing out of my suit, I cut loose. Right away Ma got nervous and began making eyes at the entry-slit in the blankets and wringing her hands together﻿—the hand where she’d lost three fingers from frostbite inside the good one, as usual. I could tell that Pa was annoyed at me scaring her and wanted to explain it all away quickly, yet could see I wasn’t fooling.
“And you watched this light for some time, son?” he asked when I finished.
I hadn’t said anything about first thinking it was a young lady’s face. Somehow that part embarrassed me.
“Long enough for it to pass five windows and go to the next floor.”
“And it didn’t look like stray electricity or crawling liquid or starlight focused by a growing crystal, or anything like that?”
He wasn’t just making up those ideas.

===Chinese===

我们得盯紧了，不能让空气用低了。爸总在头几条毯子后面存着一大桶一大桶的备用气，还有多余的煤、成箱的罐头和别的，比如化水用的雪。那些东西得下到底层去拿，那段路可赖了，还得经过一道通外面的门。
你知道，地球变冷的时候，空气里所有的水先冻了，到处铺了层十来英尺厚的毯子，然后那上面又落下来冻空气的结晶，又盖了层白毯子，兴许六七十英尺厚。
当然，空气的各部分不是同时冻了落下来的。
先掉下来的是二氧化碳——铲雪取水的时候，得留神别铲得太高，混进那玩意儿，它会让你睡着，兴许一睡不醒，还会把火弄灭。再往下是氮，那个好坏都不相干，虽说毯子里数它那层最厚。最上面那层、好拿的那层——我们运气好——是养活我们的氧。爸说我们日子过得比国王们还阔，吸的是纯氧，不过我们吸惯了，没觉出什么。最顶上，还有薄薄一层液氦，那玩意儿才逗呢。这些气一层一层码得整整齐齐。像一杯“猫咪咖啡冻”，爸笑着说，管它那是什么呢。
---
我憋着要讲我看见的东西，所以刚一摘下头盔、人还在从外头衣服里往外爬，我就放开了。妈立刻紧张起来，开始朝毯子的入口缝使眼色，抒着两只手——把冻掉了三根手指的那只手包在好的那只里，跟平常一样。我看得出来，爸恼我吓着她，想赶紧把这事解释没了，可他也看得出骗不过我。
“你看着那光有一阵子了，儿子？”我讲完他问。
我没提起初以为那是张年轻女士的脸。不知怎么，那段让我难为情。
“够它走过五扇窗、上了一层楼。”
“它不像乱跑的电，不像爬着的液体，也不像让长起来的晶体聚的星光，不像这些？”
他不是随口编的这些念头。

===Original===

Odd things happen in a world that’s about as cold as can be, and just when you think matter would be frozen dead, it takes on a strange new life. A slimy stuff comes crawling toward the Nest, just like an animal snuffing for heat﻿—that’s the liquid helium. And once, when I was little, a bolt of lightning﻿—not even Pa could figure where it came from﻿—hit the nearby steeple and crawled up and down it for weeks, until the glow finally died.
“Not like anything I ever saw,” I told him.
He stood for a moment frowning. Then, “I’ll go out with you, and you show it to me,” he said.
Ma raised a howl at the idea of being left alone, and Sis joined in, too, but Pa quieted them. We started climbing into our outside clothes﻿—mine had been warming by the fire. Pa made them. They have plastic headpieces that were once big double-duty transparent food cans, but they keep heat and air in and can replace the air for a little while, long enough for our trips for water and coal and food and so on.
Ma started moaning again, “I’ve always known there was something outside there, waiting to get us. I’ve felt it for years﻿—something that’s part of the cold and hates all warmth and wants to destroy the Nest. It’s been watching us all this time, and now it’s coming after us. It’ll get you and then come for me. Don’t go, Harry!”
Pa had everything on but his helmet. He knelt by the fireplace and reached in and shook the long metal rod that goes up the chimney and knocks off the ice that keeps trying to clog it. Once a week he goes up on the roof to check if it’s working all right. That’s our worst trip and Pa won’t let me make it alone.
“Sis,” Pa said quietly, “come watch the fire. Keep an eye on the air, too. If it gets low or doesn’t seem to be boiling fast enough, fetch another bucket from behind the blanket. But mind your hands. Use the cloth to pick up the bucket.”
Sis quit helping Ma be frightened and came over and did as she was told. Ma quieted down pretty suddenly, though her eyes were still kind of wild as she watched Pa fix on his helmet tight and pick up a pail and the two of us go out.
---
Pa led the way and I took hold of his belt. It’s a funny thing, I’m not afraid to go by myself, but when Pa’s along I always want to hold on to him. Habit, I guess, and then there’s no denying that this time I was a bit scared.
You see, it’s this way.

===Chinese===

在一个冷得不能再冷的世界里，怪事有的是，你以为物质都冻死了，它偏偏又活出一种怪的新生命。一种黏糊糊的东西朝小巢爬过来，活像一头嗅热的野兽——那就是液氦。还有一回，我还没长大，一道闪电——连爸都琢磨不出它打哪儿来——劈中附近的教堂尖顶，在上头爬上爬下好几个星期，辉光才终于熄掉。
“我见过的什么都不像，”我告诉他。
他皱着眉站了一会儿。然后说：“我跟你出去，你指给我看。”
一听要被留单独待着，妈嚎了起来，妹妹也跟着起哄，可爸把她们镇住了。我们开始套外头衣服——我的一直在火边烘着。爸做的。头盔是塑料的，原先是大号的两用透明食品罐，可它们保热保气，还能换一小会儿气，够我们出去取水、取煤、取吃的什么的一趟。
妈又开始鸣咽：“我早就知道外面有个什么东西，等着抓我们。我好些年都觉出来了——一样属于寒冷的东西，恨一切温热，想毁掉小巢。它一直看着我们，这会儿它来了。它先抓你，再来抓我。别去，哈里！”
爸什么都穿好了就差头盔。他跪在壁炉边，伸手进去，晃了晃那根通进烟囱、把老想堵烟囱的冰敲掉的长金属杆。他一星期上一次房顶，检查它好不好使。那是我们最险的一趟，爸不让我一个人去。
“妹妹，”爸平静地说，“来看火。也盯着点空气。要是低了，或者开得不够欢，就去毯子后头再拎一桶。当心手。用布拿桶。”
妹妹不帮妈害怕了，走过来照办。妈消停得出奇地快，虽然看着爸扣紧头盔、拎起桶、我们俩出去时，她眼里还有点野。
---
爸头前带路，我抓住他的腰带。说来怪，我一个人出去倒不怕，爸一跟着，我就总想抓着他。习惯吧，我猜，再说这一回我确实有点怕。
你知道，是这么回事。

===Original===

We know that everything is dead out there. Pa heard the last radio voices fade away years ago, and had seen some of the last folks die who weren’t as lucky or well-protected as us. So we knew that if there was something groping around out there, it couldn’t be anything human or friendly.
Besides that, there’s a feeling that comes with it always being night, *cold* night. Pa says there used to be some of that feeling even in the old days, but then every morning the Sun would come and chase it away. I have to take his word for that, not ever remembering the Sun as being anything more than a big star. You see, I hadn’t been born when the dark star snatched us away from the Sun, and by now it’s dragged us out beyond the orbit of the planet Pluto, Pa says, and taking us farther out all the time.
I found myself wondering whether there mightn’t be something on the dark star that wanted us, and if that was why it had captured the Earth. Just then we came to the end of the corridor and I followed Pa out on the balcony.
I don’t know what the city looked like in the old days, but now it’s beautiful. The starlight lets you see it pretty well﻿—there’s quite a bit of light in those steady points speckling the blackness above. (Pa says the stars used to twinkle once, but that was because there was air.) We are on a hill and the shimmery plain drops away from us and then flattens out, cut up into neat squares by the troughs that used to be streets. I sometimes make my mashed potatoes look like it, before I pour on the gravy.
Some taller buildings push up out of the feathery plain, topped by rounded caps of air crystals, like the fur hood Ma wears, only whiter. On those buildings you can see the darker squares of windows, underlined by white dashes of air crystals. Some of them are on a slant, for many of the buildings are pretty badly twisted by the quakes and all the rest that happened when the dark star captured the Earth.
Here and there a few icicles hang, water icicles from the first days of the cold, other icicles of frozen air that melted on the roofs and dripped and froze again. Sometimes one of those icicles will catch the light of a star and send it to you so brightly you think the star has swooped into the city. That was one of the things Pa had been thinking of when I told him about the light, but I had thought of it myself first and known it wasn’t so.

===Chinese===

我们知道外面一切都死了。爸几年前听着最后的无线电声音一个个远去，也亲眼见过最后一批没我们这么走运、没这么受保护的人死掉。所以我们知道，要是真有什么东西在外面摸索，那绝不会是人，也不会是善茬。
再说了，永远的黑夜、*冷*黑夜，本身自带一种感觉。爸说老日子里也有几分那种感觉，可每天早晨太阳一来就把它赶跑了。这我只能信他的话，因为我记着的太阳从来就只是一颗大点的星。你知道，黑星把我们从太阳身边抢走的时候我还没出生，到如今它已经把我们拖出了冥王星的轨道，爸说，而且一直把我们往更远处拖。
我不由琢磨，黑星上会不会有什么东西想要我们，它抓地球是不是就为这个。正在这时我们走到了走廊尽头，我跟着爸上了阳台。
我不知道这城市老日子里什么样，可现在它很美。星光让你看得挺清楚——头顶黑色里撒的那些不动的亮点，光还挺足的。（爸说星星从前会眨眼，那是因为有空气。）我们在一座小山上，微光的平原从我们脚下铺开、再摊平，让原先当街道用的沟槽切成一格一格整齐的方块。我有时候把土豆泥捏成那样，浇肉汁之前。
几座高些的楼从羽绒似的平原里戳出来，顶上扣着空气结晶凝成的圆帽，像妈戴的毛兜帽，只是更白。那些楼上看得见一块块发暗的窗，底下衬着空气结晶的白杠杠。有几座是斜的，好多楼让地震抒得相当不成样子，还有黑星抓走地球那阵子的种种别的。
到处挂着几根冰凌，有开头的日子冻下的水冰凌，也有屋顶上化过、滴下来又冻上的空气冰凌。有时哪根冰凌接住一颗星的光，亮亮地递到你眼睛里，你还当那颗星扑进了城。我跟爸讲那光的时候，他想到的念头里就有这一条，可我自己先想到过了，知道不是。

===Original===

He touched his helmet to mine so we could talk easier and he asked me to point out the windows to him. But there wasn’t any light moving around inside them now, or anywhere else. To my surprise, Pa didn’t bawl me out and tell me I’d been seeing things. He looked all around quite a while after filling his pail, and just as we were going inside he whipped around without warning, as if to take some peeping thing off guard.
I could feel it, too. The old peace was gone. There was something lurking out there, watching, waiting, getting ready.
Inside, he said to me, touching helmets, “If you see something like that again, son, don’t tell the others. Your Ma’s sort of nervous these days and we owe her all the feeling of safety we can give her. Once﻿—it was when your sister was born﻿—I was ready to give up and die, but your Mother kept me trying. Another time she kept the fire going a whole week all by herself when I was sick. Nursed me and took care of the two of you, too.”
---
“You know that game we sometimes play, sitting in a square in the Nest, tossing a ball around? Courage is like a ball, son. A person can hold it only so long, and then he’s got to toss it to someone else. When it’s tossed your way, you’ve got to catch it and hold it tight﻿—and hope there’ll be someone else to toss it to when you get tired of being brave.”
His talking to me that way made me feel grown-up and good. But it didn’t wipe away the thing outside from the back of my mind﻿—or the fact that Pa took it seriously.
---
It’s hard to hide your feelings about such a thing. When we got back in the Nest and took off our outside clothes, Pa laughed about it all and told them it was nothing and kidded me for having such an imagination, but his words fell flat. He didn’t convince Ma and Sis any more than he did me. It looked for a minute like we were all fumbling the courage-ball. Something had to be done, and almost before I knew what I was going to say, I heard myself asking Pa to tell us about the old days, and how it all happened.
He sometimes doesn’t mind telling that story, and Sis and I sure like to listen to it, and he got my idea. So we were all settled around the fire in a wink, and Ma pushed up some cans to thaw for supper, and Pa began. Before he did, though, I noticed him casually get a hammer from the shelf and lay it down beside him.

===Chinese===

他把他的头盔贴上我的，好说话省劲，让我把那几扇窗指给他。可这会儿里面没有一点光在动，哪儿都没有。让我意外的是，爸没有骂我、说我看见幻象了。他舀满桶之后四面看了好一阵，就在我们要进去的当口，他毫无预兆地猛一转身，像要逮个正探头探脑的东西个措手不及。
我也感觉得到。原来那份安宁没了。有什么东西潜伏在外面，看着，等着，预备着。
到了里面，他贴着头盔对我说：“你要再看见那样的东西，儿子，别告诉其他人。你妈这阵子神经紧张，欠她的那份安全感，我们能给多少给多少。有一回——你妹妹出生那会儿——我都准备认输等死了，是你妈撑着我没垮。还有一回我病了，她一个人把火守了整整一星期。还护理我，还带你们俩。”
---
“你知道我们有时玩的那个游戏吧，坐在小巢的一格里，把一个球抛来抛去？勇气就像那个球，儿子。一个人只能握它那么久，然后就得抛给别人。轮到抛给你，你就得接住、攒紧——并且盼着等你当英雄当累了，还有别人可以抛。”
他这样跟我说话，让我觉得自己长大了、挺好的。可它没能把外面的那东西从我脑子后头抹掉——也没抹掉爸把它当真这一事实。
---
这种事的感情是藏不住的。我们回到小巢脱掉外衣，爸把它全当笑话讲，说没什么，还取笑我想象力太丰富，可他的话落了空。他没说服妈和妹妹，也没说服我。有一分钟，看着就像我们都在把勇气球往地上掉。总得干点什么，我几乎还没想好要说什么，就听见自己在求爸给我们讲老日子，讲那一切是怎么发生的。
他有时候不介意讲那个故事，妹妹和我可爱听了，他也明白了我的意思。于是一眨眼我们全围着火坐好，妈推上来几筒罐头化着当晚饭，爸开讲了。不过开讲之前，我注意到他随手从架子上拿了把锤子，搁在手边。

===Original===

It was the same old story as always﻿—I think I could recite the main thread of it in my sleep﻿—though Pa always puts in a new detail or two and keeps improving it in spots.
He told us how the Earth had been swinging around the Sun ever so steady and warm, and the people on it fixing to make money and wars and have a good time and get power and treat each other right or wrong, when without warning there comes charging out of space this dead star, this burned out sun, and upsets everything.
You know, I find it hard to believe in the way those people felt, any more than I can believe in the swarming number of them. Imagine people getting ready for the horrible sort of war they were cooking up. Wanting it even, or at least wishing it were over so as to end their nervousness. As if all folks didn’t have to hang together and pool every bit of warmth just to keep alive. And how can they have hoped to end danger, any more than we can hope to end the cold?
Sometimes I think Pa exaggerates and makes things out too black. He’s cross with us once in a while and was probably cross with all those folks. Still, some of the things I read in the old magazines sound pretty wild. He may be right.
---
The dark star, as Pa went on telling it, rushed in pretty fast and there wasn’t much time to get ready. At the beginning they tried to keep it a secret from most people, but then the truth came out, what with the earthquakes and floods﻿—imagine, oceans of *unfrozen* water!﻿—and people seeing stars blotted out by something on a clear night. First off they thought it would hit the Sun, and then they thought it would hit the Earth. There was even the start of a rush to get to a place called China, because people thought the star would hit on the other side. But then they found it wasn’t going to hit either side, but was going to come very close to the Earth.
Most of the other planets were on the other side of the Sun and didn’t get involved. The Sun and the newcomer fought over the Earth for a little while﻿—pulling it this way and that, like two dogs growling over a bone, Pa described it this time﻿—and then the newcomer won and carried us off. The Sun got a consolation prize, though. At the last minute he managed to hold on to the Moon.
That was the time of the monster earthquakes and floods, twenty times worse than anything before.

===Chinese===

还是那个老故事——我想我睡着了都能背出它的主线——不过爸总会添上一两个新细节，时不时这里那里再润色一把。
他讲地球原本绕着太阳转，又稳又暖和，上面的人忙着赚钱、打仗、找乐子、抓权、彼此相待不论对错，冷不丁地，从太空里冲出来这颗死星，这颗烧空的太阳，把一切全搅了。
你知道，我很难相信那些人的感受，就像很难相信他们有那么多那么多人。想想吧，人居然在准备他们鼓捣的那种可怕的战争。甚至想要它，或者至少盼它快来了结，好结束他们的提心吊胆。好像大家不是必须抱团、把每一点热凑在一起才能活命似的。他们又怎么能指望终结危险呢？我们不也终结不了寒冷吗？
有时候我想爸夸张了，把事情说得太黑。他时不时对我们发脾气，当年大概也冲那些人发过。不过，我在旧杂志里读到的一些东西也确实挺邪乎。他也许没说错。
---
黑星，爸接着讲，冲进来得很快，没多少时间准备。起初他们还想对大多数人保密，可后来真相瞒不住了，又是地震又是洪水——想想吧，一洋一洋*不冻的*水！——又是人们在晴夜里看见星星被什么挡掉。起先他们以为它会撞上太阳，后来以为它会撞上地球。甚至刚开始还涌着往一个叫中国的地方跑，因为人们以为那星会撞另一面。可后来他们发现它哪面都不撞，只会贴着地球很近地过。
其他行星多半在太阳另一边，没沾上事。太阳和新来的那颗为地球斗了一小会儿——你拉我拽，像两条狗就着一根骨头扯，爸这回是这么讲的——后来新来的赢了，把我们带走了。不过太阳得了个安慰奖。最后一刻它死死拽住了月亮。
那就是大地震和大洪水的年月，比以前任何一次都凶二十倍。

===Original===

It was also the time of the Big Jerk, as Pa calls it, when all Earth got yanked suddenly, just as Pa has done to me once or twice, grabbing me by the collar to do it, when I’ve been sitting too far from the fire.
---
You see, the dark star was going through space faster than the Sun, and in the opposite direction, and it had to wrench the world considerably in order to take it away.
The Big Jerk didn’t last long. It was over as soon as the Earth was settled down in its new orbit around the dark star. But it was pretty terrible while it lasted. Pa says that all sorts of cliffs and buildings toppled, oceans slopped over, swamps and sandy deserts gave great sliding surges that buried nearby lands. Earth was almost jerked out of its atmosphere blanket and the air got so thin in spots that people keeled over and fainted﻿—though of course, at the same time, they were getting knocked down by the Big Jerk and maybe their bones broke or skulls cracked.
We’ve often asked Pa how people acted during that time, whether they were scared or brave or crazy or stunned, or all four, but he’s sort of leery of the subject, and he was again tonight. He says he was mostly too busy to notice.
You see, Pa and some scientist friends of his had figured out part of what was going to happen﻿—they’d known we’d get captured and our air would freeze﻿—and they’d been working like mad to fix up a place with airtight walls and doors, and insulation against the cold, and big supplies of food and fuel and water and bottled air. But the place got smashed in the last earthquakes and all Pa’s friends were killed then and in the Big Jerk. So he had to start over and throw the Nest together quick without any advantages, just using any stuff he could lay his hands on.
I guess he’s telling pretty much the truth when he says he didn’t have any time to keep an eye on how other folks behaved, either then or in the Big Freeze that followed﻿—followed very quick, you know, both because the dark star was pulling us away very fast and because Earth’s rotation had been slowed in the tug-of-war, so that the nights were ten old nights long.
Still, I’ve got an idea of some of the things that happened from the frozen folk I’ve seen, a few of them in other rooms in our building, others clustered around the furnaces in the basements where we go for coal.

===Chinese===

那也是“大猛拽”的年月——爸就这么叫它——整个地球猛一下被拽走，就像爸有一两回对我干的那样，揪住我后领子一拽，因为我坐得离火太远。
---
你知道，黑星在太空里跑得比太阳快，方向还相反，它得把地球相当厉害地一拧，才带得走。
大猛拽没持续多久。地球在新轨道上围着黑星安顿下来，它就完了。可它持续着的那会儿相当吓人。爸说，各种各样的悬崖和楼都倒了，海洋泼了出来，沼泽和沙地掀起大片滑坡，把近处的土地埋了。地球差点被拽出它那床大气毯子，有些地方的空气稀得人直栽跟头晕过去——不过当然，同时他们还被大猛拽撞着，没准骨头断了、脑壳裂了。
我们常问爸，那阵子人们都是什么样子，是害怕、勇敢、发疯还是发懵，还是四样全占，可他对这个话题有点犯怵，今晚又是。他说他忙得根本顾不上看。
你知道，爸和他的几个科学家朋友早猜到了要发生的事的一部分——他们知道我们会被捕获、我们的空气会冻上——他们拼了命地赶出一处地方，墙壁门扇不透风，隔冷，备下大量的食品、燃料、水和瓶装空气。可那地方在最后的大地震里砸了，爸的朋友全死在那阵子和那次大猛拽里。所以他只好从头再来，毫无便利地飞快拼出这个小巢，能用上手边的什么就用什么。
我猜他说的是实情：不管是那阵子还是随后的大冻结，他都没工夫看别人怎么行事——那冻结来得很快，你知道，一来因为黑星拽我们拽得飞快，二来地球的自转在那场拉锯里慢了下来，一夜顶过去十夜。
不过，从我看过的那些冻住的人，我多少能想出发生过的一些事：有几个在我们楼里别的房间，另一些聚在我们下去取煤的那些地下室的炉子边。

===Original===

In one of the rooms, an old man sits stiff in a chair, with an arm and a leg in splints. In another, a man and woman are huddled together in a bed with heaps of covers over them. You can just see their heads peeking out, close together. And in another a beautiful young lady is sitting with a pile of wraps huddled around her, looking hopefully toward the door, as if waiting for someone who never came back with warmth and food. They’re all still and stiff as statues, of course, but just like life.
Pa showed them to me once in quick winks of his flashlight, when he still had a fair supply of batteries and could afford to waste a little light. They scared me pretty bad and made my heart pound, especially the young lady.
---
Now, with Pa telling his story for the umpteenth time to take our minds off another scare, I got to thinking of the frozen folk again. All of a sudden I got an idea that scared me worse than anything yet. You see, I’d just remembered the face I’d thought I’d seen in the window. I’d forgotten about that on account of trying to hide it from the others.
What, I asked myself, if the frozen folk were coming to life? What if they were like the liquid helium that got a new lease on life and started crawling toward the heat just when you thought its molecules ought to freeze solid forever? Or like the electricity that moves endlessly when it’s just about as cold as that? What if the ever-growing cold, with the temperature creeping down the last few degrees to the last zero, had mysteriously wakened the frozen folk to life﻿—not warm-blooded life, but something icy and horrible?
That was a worse idea than the one about something coming down from the dark star to get us.
Or maybe, I thought, both ideas might be true. Something coming down from the dark star and making the frozen folk move, using them to do its work. That would fit with both things I’d seen﻿—the beautiful young lady and the moving, starlike light.
The frozen folk with minds from the dark star behind their unwinking eyes, creeping, crawling, snuffing their way, following the heat to the Nest.
I tell you, that thought gave me a very bad turn and I wanted very badly to tell the others my fears, but I remembered what Pa had said and clenched my teeth and didn’t speak.
We were all sitting very still. Even the fire was burning silently. There was just the sound of Pa’s voice and the clocks.

===Chinese===

有一间房里，一个老头直挺挺坐在椅子上，一条胳膊一条腿上着夹板。另一间里，一男一女挤在一张床上，身上压着一摞被子。刚看得见两颗脑袋探出来，紧紧挨着。还有一间里，一位年轻漂亮的女士坐着，裹着一大堆衣物，满怀希望地望着门口，像在等一个再没带着温热和吃食回来的人。他们全都一动不动、僵得像雕像，当然，可又活灵活现。
爸给我看过他们一回，用手电很快地眨了几下——那会儿他电池还挺富余，浪费得起一点光。他们把我吓得够呛，心怦怦跳，尤其是那位年轻女士。
---
这会儿，爸把他的故事第无数遍讲开来、好让我们忘掉又一场惊吓，我又想起那些冻住的人了。突然冒出一个念头，比之前什么都吓人。你知道，我刚记起我在窗口以为看见的那张脸。为着瞄住其他人，我把它忘了。
要是，我问自己，冻住的人活过来了呢？要是他们像液氦那样——你以为它的分子该永远冻死了，它偏偏又领了新生命，朝热气爬起来了呢？或者像那种电，冷到那份上还动个没完？要是越长越大的冷——温度再爬低最后几度、爬到最后那个零度——神秘地唤醒了冻住的人，不是热血的活，而是某种冰冷的、可怕的东西呢？
这比“黑星上有什么东西下来抓我们”那个念头还糟。
要不，我想，两个念头也许都是真的。黑星上有什么东西下来，让冻住的人动起来，拿他们当差遣。这跟我看见的两样东西全都对得上——漂亮的年轻女士，和那点移动的、星星似的光。
冻住的人，一双不眨的眼睛后面装着黑星的心思，悄悄地、爬着、嗅着，循着热气摸到小巢来。
我告诉你，这念头让我大大地不好受了一场，我特别想把我怕的告诉他们，可我想起爸说过的话，咬紧牙关没做声。
我们都坐得静静的。连火都烧得没声。只有爸的声音和钟。

===Original===

And then, from beyond the blankets, I thought I heard a tiny noise. My skin tightened all over me.
Pa was telling about the early years in the Nest and had come to the place where he philosophizes.
“So I asked myself then,” he said, “what’s the use of going on? What’s the use of dragging it out for a few years? Why prolong a doomed existence of hard work and cold and loneliness? The human race is done. The Earth is done. Why not give up, I asked myself﻿—and all of a sudden I got the answer.”
Again I heard the noise, louder this time, a kind of uncertain, shuffling tread, coming closer. I couldn’t breathe.
“Life’s always been a business of working hard and fighting the cold,” Pa was saying. “The earth’s always been a lonely place, millions of miles from the next planet. And no matter how long the human race might have lived, the end would have come some night. Those things don’t matter. What matters is that life is good. It has a lovely texture, like some rich cloth or fur, or the petals of flowers﻿—you’ve seen pictures of those, but I can’t describe how they feel﻿—or the fire’s glow. It makes everything else worth while. And that’s as true for the last man as the first.”
And still the steps kept shuffling closer. It seemed to me that the inmost blanket trembled and bulged a little. Just as if they were burned into my imagination, I kept seeing those peering, frozen eyes.
“So right then and there,” Pa went on, and now I could tell that he heard the steps, too, and was talking loud so we maybe wouldn’t hear them, “right then and there I told myself that I was going on as if we had all eternity ahead of us. I’d have children and teach them all I could. I’d get them to read books. I’d plan for the future, try to enlarge and seal the Nest. I’d do what I could to keep everything beautiful and growing. I’d keep alive my feeling of wonder even at the cold and the dark and the distant stars.”
But then the blanket actually did move and lift. And there was a bright light somewhere behind it. Pa’s voice stopped and his eyes turned to the widening slit and his hand went out until it touched and gripped the handle of the hammer beside him.
---
In through the blanket stepped the beautiful young lady. She stood there looking at us the strangest way, and she carried something bright and unwinking in her hand.

===Chinese===

然后，从毯子外面，我觉得我听见了一点很小的声音。我浑身的皮都收紧了。
爸正讲到小巢的早年，讲到他发议论的那一段。
“于是我那时问自己，”他说，“活下去有什么用？多拖几年有什么用？干吗要拖着一段注定的日子，苦熬、寒冷、孤单？人类完事了。地球完事了。干吗不放弃，我问自己——突然间我得了答案。”
我又听见了那声音，这回响些，一种拿不准的、拖沓的脚步，越来越近。我啹不上气。
“生活从来就是苦干加抗寒的事，”爸说着。“地球从来就是个孤单的地方，离下一颗行星几百万英里。人类就算活得再久，末日总有哪个晚上要来。这些都不打紧。打紧的是生活是好的。它有一种可爱的质地，像某种厚实的布或毛皮，像花瓣——你见过照片，可我说不出它们摸着什么样——或者像火的光。它让其余一切都值。这对最后一个人和第一个人一样真。”
脚步还在拖拖拉拉地逼近。我觉得最里层那条毯子抖了抖、鼓起来一点。就像烙进了我的想象，我眼前老是那双双窥探的、冻住的眼睛。
“所以就在那时那地，”爸继续说，这时我听得出他也听见脚步了，故意大声说话好让我们也许听不见，“就在那时那地，我告诉自己，我要照着我们面前还有全部永恒那样过下去。我要生孩子，把我会的都教给他们。我要让他们读书。我要为将来打算，设法扩大小巢、封死小巢。我要尽力让一切美好、生长。哪怕对着寒冷、黑暗和遥远的星星，我也要守住我的惊奇感。”
可毯子真的动了、掀起来了。它后面什么地方有一道亮光。爸的声音停了，他的眼睛转向那道越裂越大的缝，他的手伸出去，摸到了、攒住了身边锤子的柄。
---
那位年轻漂亮的女士从毯子里跨了进来。她站在那儿用顶奇怪的样子看着我们，手里拿着一样亮亮的、不眨眼的东西。

===Original===

And two other faces peered over her shoulders﻿—men’s faces, white and staring.
Well, my heart couldn’t have been stopped for more than four or five beats before I realized she was wearing a suit and helmet like Pa’s homemade ones, only fancier, and that the men were, too﻿—and that the frozen folk certainly wouldn’t be wearing those. Also, I noticed that the bright thing in her hand was just a kind of flashlight.
The silence kept on while I swallowed hard a couple of times, and after that there was all sorts of jabbering and commotion.
They were simply people, you see. We hadn’t been the only ones to survive; we’d just thought so, for natural enough reasons. These three people had survived, and quite a few others with them. And when we found out *how* they’d survived, Pa let out the biggest whoop of joy.
They were from Los Alamos and they were getting their heat and power from atomic energy. Just using the uranium and plutonium intended for bombs, they had enough to go on for thousands of years. They had a regular little airtight city, with airlocks and all. They even generated electric light and grew plants and animals by it. (At this Pa let out a second whoop, waking Ma from her faint.)
But if we were flabbergasted at them, they were double-flabbergasted at us.
One of the men kept saying, “But it’s impossible, I tell you. You can’t maintain an air supply without hermetic sealing. It’s simply impossible.”
That was after he had got his helmet off and was using our air. Meanwhile, the young lady kept looking around at us as if we were saints, and telling us we’d done something amazing, and suddenly she broke down and cried.
They’d been scouting around for survivors, but they never expected to find any in a place like this. They had rocket ships at Los Alamos and plenty of chemical fuel. As for liquid oxygen, all you had to do was go out and shovel the air blanket at the top *level*. So after they’d got things going smoothly at Los Alamos, which had taken years, they’d decided to make some trips to likely places where there might be other survivors. No good trying long-distance radio signals, of course, since there was no atmosphere to carry them around the curve of the Earth.
Well, they’d found other colonies at Argonne and Brookhaven and way around the world at Harwell and Tanna Tuva.

===Chinese===

另外两张脸从她肩膀后面探出来——男人的脸，煞白，瞪着眼。
唉，我的心跳停了顶多四五拍，就认清了她穿着一套带头盔的衣服，跟爸自制的那套像，只是讲究些，那两个男人也是——冻住的人断不会穿那些。还有，我注意到她手里那个亮东西就是一截手电。
沉默接着延续，我使劲咽了两口，随后就是没完没了的叽里咕噜和大呼小叫。
他们就是人，你知道。活下来的不是只有我们；我们只是那么以为，理由也够自然。这三个人活了下来，还带着不少人。等我们弄清他们*怎么*活下来的，爸发出了最大的一声欢呼。
他们来自洛斯阿拉莫斯，热和电都取自原子能。光是用掉那些本来造炸弹的铀和钚，就够他们用上几千年。他们有一座规规矩矩的不漏气的小城，气闸什么的都齐。他们甚至还发电灯，靠它种植物养动物。（听到这儿爸又欢呼了一声，把晕过去的妈吵醒了。）
可我们对他们目瞪口呆，他们对我们是双倍的目瞪口呆。
其中一个男人直念叨：“可这不可能，我告诉你。没有密封，你维持不了空气。这根本不可能。”
那是他摘下头盔、正用我们的空气说话的时候。与此同时，那位年轻女士不停地打量我们，把我们当圣徒似的，说我们做成了一件不得了的事，说着说着她突然垮下来哭了。
他们一直在四下侦查幸存者，可压根没指望在这种地方找到。洛斯阿拉莫斯有火箭，化学燃料有的是。至于液氧，只消出去在最顶上一层铲空气毯子就行。所以在洛斯阿拉莫斯把一切理顺之后——那花了好些年——他们决定跑几趟可能还有幸存者的地方。长距离无线电信号当然没用，因为没有大气帮它绕过地球的弯。
喏，他们在阿贡、布鲁克海文，还有绕到世界那一头的哈韦尔、唐努图瓦，找到了别的聚居点。

===Original===

And now they’d been giving our city a look, not really expecting to find anything. But they had an instrument that noticed the faintest heat waves and it had told them there was something warm down here, so they’d landed to investigate. Of course we hadn’t heard them land, since there was no air to carry the sound, and they’d had to investigate around quite a while before finding us. Their instruments had given them a wrong steer and they’d wasted some time in the building across the street.
---
By now, all five adults were talking like sixty. Pa was demonstrating to the men how he worked the fire and got rid of the ice in the chimney and all that. Ma had perked up wonderfully and was showing the young lady her cooking and sewing stuff, and even asking about how the women dressed at Los Alamos. The strangers marveled at everything and praised it to the skies. I could tell from the way they wrinkled their noses that they found the Nest a bit smelly, but they never mentioned that at all and just asked bushels of questions.
In fact, there was so much talking and excitement that Pa forgot about things, and it wasn’t until they were all getting groggy that he looked and found the air had all boiled away in the pail. He got another bucket of air quick from behind the blankets. Of course that started them all laughing and jabbering again. The newcomers even got a little drunk. They weren’t used to so much oxygen.
Funny thing, though﻿—I didn’t do much talking at all and Sis hung on to Ma all the time and hid her face when anybody looked at her. I felt pretty uncomfortable and disturbed myself, even about the young lady. Glimpsing her outside there, I’d had all sorts of mushy thoughts, but now I was just embarrassed and scared of her, even though she tried to be nice as anything to me.
I sort of wished they’d all quit crowding the Nest and let us be alone and get our feelings straightened out.
And when the newcomers began to talk about our all going to Los Alamos, as if that were taken for granted, I could see that something of the same feeling struck Pa and Ma, too. Pa got very silent all of a sudden and Ma kept telling the young lady, “But I wouldn’t know how to act there and I haven’t any clothes.”
The strangers were puzzled like anything at first, but then they got the idea. As Pa kept saying, “It just doesn’t seem right to let this fire go out.”

===Chinese===

这回他们来看了看我们的城市，并没真指望找到什么。可他们有一样仪器，能察觉最微弱的热波，它告诉他们这底下有什么热的东西，所以他们降落下来查。我们当然没听见他们降落，因为没有空气传声，他们也得转悠好一阵才找到我们。他们的仪器指歪了一回，在对街那栋楼里耗了些工夫。
---
到这会儿，五个大人全聊得像开了闸。爸给那两个男人演示他怎么生火、怎么除烟囱里的冰，等等。妈的精神头好了不起，领着年轻女士看她的锅碗针线，甚至打听洛斯阿拉莫斯的女人们穿什么。生客们对样样东西啧啧称奇，夸上了天。从他们皱鼻子的样子我看得出，他们觉得小巢有点味儿，可他们只字不提，光问成桶成桶的问题。
其实，说话和兴奋得太多，爸把事都忘了，直到大伙都有点发晕，他一看，桶里的空气全化光跑光了。他赶紧从毯子后面又拎来一桶。这当然又招得大家一阵大笑大嚷。生客们甚至有点醉了。他们不习惯这么多氧。
不过怪的是——我根本没怎么说话，妹妹一直吊在妈身上，谁一看她就把脸埋起来。我自己也觉得相当别扭、相当乱，连对那位年轻女士也是。在外面瞥见她那会儿，我有过各式各样黏糊糊的念头，可这会儿我只是发窘、怕她，虽然她对我好得没法再好。
我多少希望他们全都别挤着小巢，让我们单独待会儿，把心思捋顺。
等生客们谈起我们全都搬去洛斯阿拉莫斯、好像那是理所当然的时候，我看得出，几分同样的感觉也击中了爸妈。爸突然非常沉默，妈不停地对年轻女士说：“可我到那儿不知道该怎么做人，我也没有衣裳。”
生客们起先纳闷得不行，后来懂了。就像爸不停说的：“让这炉火灭了，总觉得不像话。”

===Original===

---
Well, the strangers are gone, but they’re coming back. It hasn’t been decided yet just what will happen. Maybe the Nest will be kept up as what one of the strangers called a “survival school.” Or maybe we will join the pioneers who are going to try to establish a new colony at the uranium mines at Great Slave Lake or in the Congo.
Of course, now that the strangers are gone, I’ve been thinking a lot about Los Alamos and those other tremendous colonies. I have a hankering to see them for myself.
You ask me, Pa wants to see them, too. He’s been getting pretty thoughtful, watching Ma and Sis perk up.
“It’s different, now that we know others are alive,” he explains to me. “Your mother doesn’t feel so hopeless any more. Neither do I, for that matter, not having to carry the whole responsibility for keeping the human race going, so to speak. It scares a person.”
I looked around at the blanket walls and the fire and the pails of air boiling away and Ma and Sis sleeping in the warmth and the flickering light.
“It’s not going to be easy to leave the Nest,” I said, wanting to cry, kind of. “It’s so small and there’s just the four of us. I get scared at the idea of big places and a lot of strangers.”
He nodded and put another piece of coal on the fire. Then he looked at the little pile and grinned suddenly and put a couple of handfuls on, just as if it was one of our birthdays or Christmas.
“You’ll quickly get over that feeling son,” he said. “The trouble with the world was that it kept getting smaller and smaller, till it ended with just the Nest. Now it’ll be good to have a real huge world again, the way it was in the beginning.”
I guess he’s right. You think the beautiful young lady will wait for me till I grow up? I’ll be twenty in only ten years.

===Chinese===

---
喏，生客们走了，可他们还要回来。到底怎么办还没定。也许小巢会照一个生客的说法保留下去，当一所“生存学校”。也许我们会加入开拓者，去大奴湖或刚果的铀矿建新的聚居点。
当然，生客们一走，我就老想着洛斯阿拉莫斯和那些别的大得不得了的聚居点。我自己也馋着去看看。
要我说，爸也想看。他一直变得挺爱琢磨，看着妈和妹妹一天天缓过来。
“现在不一样了，知道别人还活着，”他对我解释。“你妈不那么绝望了。就我说，我也一样，不用一个人扛着让人类延续下去的整个担子了，可以说。那怪吓人的。”
我环顾毯墙、火、沸着跑光的空气桶、睡在暖和与闪烁火光里的妈和妹妹。
“离开小巢不会容易，”我说，有点想哭。“它这么小，又只有我们四个。一想到大地方、一大堆生人，我就害怕。”
他点点头，往火里又添了一块煤。然后他看看那一小堆煤，忽然咧嘴一笑，又抓了两把添上，就好像我们谁过生日，或者到了圣诞节。
“你很快就会过掉那股劲的，儿子。这世界的毛病就在于它越变越小，小到最后只剩小巢。往后又会再有一个真正的大世界，像起初那样，那才好呢。”
我猜他说得对。你觉得那位年轻漂亮的女士会等我长大吗？再过十年我就二十了。

## Appointment in Tomorrow / 明日之约
===Original===

The first angry rays of the sun﻿—which, startlingly enough, still rose in the east at 24 hour intervals﻿—pierced the lacy tops of Atlantic combers and touched thousands of sleeping Americans with unconscious fear, because of their unpleasant similarity to the rays from World War III’s atomic bombs.
They turned to blood the witch-circle of rusty steel skeletons around Inferno in Manhattan. Without comment, they pointed a cosmic finger at the tarnished brass plaque commemorating the martyrdom of the Three Physicists after the dropping of the Hell Bomb. They tenderly touched the rosy skin and strawberry bruises on the naked shoulders of a girl sleeping off a drunk on the furry and radiantly heated floor of a nearby roof garden. They struck green magic from the glassy blot that was Old Washington. Twelve hours before, they had revealed things as eerily beautiful, and as ravaged, in Asia and Russia. They pinked the white walls of the Colonial dwelling of Morton Opperly near the Institute for Advanced Studies; upstairs they slanted impartially across the Pharoahlike and open-eyed face of the elderly physicist and the ugly, sleep-surly one of young Willard Farquar in the next room. And in nearby New Washington they made of the spire of the Thinkers’ Foundation a blue and optimistic glory that outshone White House, Jr.
It was America approaching the end of the Twentieth Century. America of jukebox burlesque and your local radiation hospital. America of the mask-fad for women and Mystic Christianity. America of the off-the-bosom dress and the New Blue Laws. America of the Endless War and the loyalty detector. America of marvelous Maizie and the monthly rocket to Mars. America of the Thinkers and (a few remembered) the Institute. “Knock on titanium,” “Whadya do for blackouts,” “Please, lover, don’t think when I’m around,” America, as combat-shocked and crippled as the rest of the bomb-shattered planet.
Not one impudent photon of the sunlight penetrated the triple-paned, polarizing windows of Jorj Helmuth’s bedroom in the Thinker’s Foundation, yet the clock in his brain awakened him to the minute, or almost. Switching off the Educational Sandman in the midst of the phrase, “… applying tensor calculus to the nucleus,” he took a deep, even breath and cast his mind to the limits of the world and his knowledge.

===Chinese===

太阳最初那些愤怒的光线——足以让人吃一惊的是，它仍每24小时从东方升起一次——刺穿大西洋卷浪的蕾丝顶，以无意识的恐惧触碰成千上万沉睡的美国人，因为它们太像第三次世界大战原子弹的射线了。
它们把曼哈顿“地狱”周围那圈生锈钢铁骨架组成的巫术圆环照成血色。它们不加评论地，把一根宇宙的手指指向那块发暗的黄铜牌，纪念三位物理学家在“地狱炸弹”投下之后的殉难。它们温柔地抚过一个姑娘被酒意浸透的裸肩上玫瑰色的皮肤和草莓似的淤青——她睡在附近一座屋顶花园毛茸茸的辐射供暖地板上。它们从“老华盛顿”那片玻璃似的斑迹中击出绿色的魔法。十二小时前，它们在亚洲和俄国也揭示过同样鬼魅般美丽、同样疮痍的景象。它们把莫顿·奥珀利在高等研究院附近的殖民地式住宅的白墙染成粉红；楼上，它们不偏不倚地斜过老物理学家那张法老似的、睁着眼的脸，也斜过隔壁房间里年轻的威拉德·法夸尔那张难看的、被睡意惹恼的脸。而在附近的新华盛顿，它们把思想家基金会的尖塔造成一座蓝色的、乐观主义的辉煌，盖过了小白宫。
这是临近二十世纪末的美国。点唱机滑稽剧和你家附近辐射医院的美国。女人戴面具的时髦和神秘基督教的美国。露胸裙和新蓝色法令的美国。无休止战争和忠诚检测器的美国。了不起的梅姬和每月一班火星火箭的美国。思想家和（少数人还记得的）研究院的美国。“敲钛木避邪”“停电了咋整”“求你了亲爱的，我在旁边时你别思考”——同这颗被炸弹震碎的星球上其他地方一样战斗休克、遍体伤残的美国。
没有一个不识趣的太阳光子穿透乔吉·赫尔穆特在思想家基金会卧室里那三层的偏振窗，可他脑子里的钟还是把他叫醒了，准点到分，或者差不多。他在这句正说到一半时关掉教育睡仙——“……把张量微积应用于原子核”——深深地、均匀地吸了一口气，把心智投向世界和他的知识的边界。

===Original===

It was a somewhat shadowy vision, but, he noted with impartial approval, definitely less shadowy than yesterday morning.
Employing a rapid mental scanning technique, he next cleared his memory chains of false associations, including those acquired while asleep. These chores completed, he held his finger on a bedside button, which rotated the polarizing window panes until the room slowly filled with a muted daylight. Then, still flat on his back, he turned his head until he could look at the remarkably beautiful blonde girl asleep beside him.
---
Remembering last night, he felt a pang of exasperation, which he instantly quelled by taking his mind to a higher and dispassionate level from which he could look down on the girl and even himself as quaint, clumsy animals. Still, he grumbled silently, Caddy might have had enough consideration to clear out before he awoke. He wondered if he shouldn’t have used his hypnotic control of the girl to smooth their relationship last night, and for a moment the word that would send her into deep trance trembled on the tip of his tongue. But no, that special power of his over her was reserved for far more important purposes.
Pumping dynamic tension into his 20-year-old muscles and confidence into his 60-year-old mind, the 40-year-old Thinker rose from bed. No covers had to be thrown off; the nuclear heating unit made them unnecessary. He stepped into his clothing﻿—the severe tunic, tights and sockassins of the modern business man. Next he glanced at the message tape beside his phone, washed down with ginger ale a vita-amino-enzyme tablet, and walked to the window. There, gazing along the rows of newly planted mutant oaks lining Decontamination Avenue, his smooth face broke into a smile.
It had come to him, the next big move in the intricate game making up his life﻿—and mankind’s. Come to him during sleep, as so many of his best decisions did, because he regularly employed the time-saving technique of somno-thought, which could function at the same time as somno-learning.
He set his who?-where? robot for “Rocket Physicist” and “Genius Class.” While it worked, he dictated to his steno-robot the following brief message:

===Chinese===

那是一个多少有点朦胧的图景，不过，他以不偏不倚的赞许注意到，确实比昨天早晨少朦胧了一点。
用一套快速心理扫描技术，他接着清理了记忆链上的错误关联，包括睡着时习得的那些。这些杂务完成，他按住床头的一个钮，它旋转偏振窗玻璃，直到房间慢慢充满柔和的天光。然后，还仰面躺着，他转头去看身边睡着的那个美貌惊人的金发姑娘。
---
想起昨夜，他感到一阵恼怒的刺痛，他立刻把它压下去，方法是把自己带到更高、更超然的层面，从那里俯看姑娘甚至他自己，看作两头古怪的、笨拙的动物。不过，他还是默默地抱怨，卡蒂本来可以体谅一点，赶在他醒来之前清场。他琢磨自己昨晚是不是该用他对姑娘的催眠控制来抹平他们的关系，有一会儿，那个会让她进入深度恍惚的词在他舌尖上颤动。可是不，他对她那项特别的权力要留给重要得多的事情。
往二十岁的肌肉里泵入动力张力，往六十岁的心智里泵入自信，这位四十岁的思想家从床上起来。不用掀被子；核供热装置让被子成了多余。他跨进衣服——现代生意人那种笔挺的束腰外衣、紧身裤和袜靴。接着他瞥了一眼电话旁的留言带，用姜汁汽水送下一片维他命-氨基酸-酶片，走到窗前。在那儿，望着净化大道两旁新栽的突变橡树行列，他光滑的脸绽开笑容。
它来了，那构成他一生——也是人类一生——的复杂棋局中的下一步大棋。在睡梦中来到他这里，就像他许多最好的决策一样，因为他经常使用节省时间的睡眠思维技术，可以和睡眠学习同时进行。
他把“谁？-在哪？”机器人设定为“火箭物理学家”和“天才级”。趁它工作，他向速记机器人口授了下面这封短信：

===Original===

> Dear Fellow Scientist:
> A project is contemplated that will have a crucial bearing on man’s future in deep space. Ample nonmilitary Government funds are available. There was a time when professional men scoffed at the Thinkers. Then there was a time when the Thinkers perforce neglected the professional men. Now both times are past. May they never return! I would like to consult you this afternoon, three o’clock sharp, Thinkers’ Foundation I.
> Jorj Helmuth

===Chinese===

> 亲爱的科学家同仁：
> 现正筹划一项对人类未来在深空具有关键意义的项目。政府有充足的非军用经费可用。曾几何时，专业人士对思想家们嗤之以鼻。后来又有那么一段，思想家们不得不冷落专业人士。如今两段都过去了。愿它们永不回头！我想今天下午向您请教，三点整，思想家基金会一号楼。
> 乔吉·赫尔穆特

===Original===

Meanwhile the who?-where? had tossed out a dozen cards. He glanced through them, hesitated at the name “Willard Farquar,” looked at the sleeping girl, then quickly tossed them all into the addresso-robot and plugged in the steno-robot.
