The Mystery was abruptly solved one sunny Sunday afternoon. Going into the bathroom in her stocking feet, Kitty-Come-Here saw Gummitch apparently trying to drown himself in the toilet. His hindquarters were on the seat but the rest of his body went down into the bowl. Coming closer, she saw that his forelegs were braced against the opposite side of the bowl, just above the water surface, while his head thrust down sharply between his shoulders. She could distinctly hear rhythmic lapping.
To tell the truth, Kitty-Come-Here was rather shocked. She had certain rather fixed ideas about the delicacy of cats. It speaks well for her progressive grounding that she did not shout at Gummitch but softly summoned her husband.
By the time Old Horsemeat arrived the young cat had refreshed himself and was coming out of his “well” with a sudden backward undulation. He passed them in the doorway with a single mew and upward look and then made off for the kitchen.
The blue and white room was bright with sunlight. Outside the sky was blue and the leaves were rustling in a stiff breeze. Gummitch looked back once, as if to make sure his human congeners had followed, mewed again, and then advanced briskly toward his little bowl with the air of one who proposes to reveal all mysteries at once.
Kitty-Come-Here had almost outdone herself. She had for the first time poured him the bottled water, and she had floated a few rose petals on the surface.
Gummitch regarded them carefully, sniffed at them, and then proceeded to fish them out one by one and shake them off his paw. Old Horsemeat repressed the urge to say, “I told you so.”

===Chinese===

（她自己就能清楚尝出自来水里的氟化物味，尽管她从未向老马肉提起这一点。）
大泼水之谜期间的另一桩变化是，甘米奇渐渐走出消沉，变得相当快活。他开始晚间在客厅里即兴跳起猫式的斯科蒂舍舞和吉格舞，甚至忘了自己的尊严，欢快地与吸尘器恶龙搏斗——老马肉用一个小附件给他刷毛时，年轻的猫把毛茸茸的圆刷抱在肚皮上，任它呼哧呼哧地威吓，疯狂地用爪子抓挠。就连那个他带着被疯太监抓开的肩膀伤口回家的下午，他也显得出奇地轻快洒脱。
---
谜底在一个晴朗的星期日下午被骤然揭开。猫咪来这儿只穿着长袜走进浴室，看见甘米奇像是要在马桶里淹死自己。他的后腿搭在座圈上，身体其余部分都下到了马桶里。走近些，她看见他的前腿撑在马桶对面内壁上，恰在水面上方，脑袋在双肩之间猛地下探。她能清楚听见有节奏的舔水声。
说实在的，猫咪来这儿相当震惊。她对猫的洁癖持有一些颇为固定的观念。她没有冲甘米奇大喊，而是轻轻唤来丈夫，这份开明的教养倒是可圈可点。
等老马肉赶到时，年轻的猫已经畅饮完毕，正一个向后的波浪式动作从他的“井”里出来。他在门口从两人身边经过，咪了一声，抬眼一望，便径直往厨房去了。
蓝白相间的房间里洒满明亮的阳光。窗外天空湛蓝，树叶在劲风中沙沙作响。甘米奇回头望了一眼，像是确认他的人类同类跟上了没有，又咪了一声，然后神气十足地向他的小碗走去，一副打算一举揭开全部奥秘的架势。
猫咪来这儿这回几乎超常发挥。她头一回给他倒了瓶装水，还在水面上漂了几片玫瑰花瓣。
甘米奇仔细端详它们，嗅了嗅，然后动手把它们一片片捞出来，从爪子上甩掉。老马肉忍住了说“我早说过”的冲动。

===Original===

When the water surface was completely free and winking in the sunlight, Gummitch curved one paw under the side of the bowl and jerked.
Half the water spilled out, gathered itself, and then began to flow across the floor in little rushes, a silver ribbon sparkling with sunlight that divided and subdivided and reunited as it followed the slope. Gummitch crouched to one side, watching it intensely, following its progress inch by inch and foot by foot, almost pouncing on the little temporary pools that formed, but not quite touching them. Twice he mewed faintly in excitement.
---
“He’s *playing* with it,” Old Horsemeat said incredulously.
“No,” Kitty-Come-Here countered wide-eyed, “he’s *creating* something. Silver mice. Water-snakes. Twinkling vines.”
“Good Lord, you’re right,” Old Horsemeat agreed. “It’s a new art form. Would you call it water painting? Or water sculpture? Somehow I think that’s best. As if a sculptor made mobiles out of molten tin.”
“It’s gone so quickly, though,” Kitty-Come-Here objected, a little sadly. “Art ought to last. Look, it’s almost all flowed over to the wall now.”
“Some of the best art forms are completely fugitive,” Old Horsemeat argued. “What about improvisation in music and dancing? What about jam sessions and shadow figures on the wall? Gummitch can always do it again﻿—in fact, he must have been doing it again and again this last month. It’s never exactly the same, like waves or fires. But it’s beautiful.”
“I suppose so,” Kitty-Come-Here said. Then coming to herself, she continued, “But I don’t think it can be healthy for him to go on drinking water out of the toilet. Really.”
Old Horsemeat shrugged. He had an insight about the artistic temperament and the need to dig for inspiration into the smelly fundamentals of life, but it was difficult to express delicately.
Kitty-Come-Here sighed, as if bidding farewell to all her efforts with rose petals and crystalline bottled purity and vanilla extract and the soda water which had amazed Gummitch by faintly spitting and purring at him.
“Oh, well,” she said, “I can scrub it out more often, I suppose.”
Meanwhile, Gummitch had gone back to his bowl and, using both paws, overset it completely. Now, nose a-twitch, he once more pursued the silver streams alive with suns, refreshing his spirit with the sight of them. He was fretted by no problems about what he was doing.

===Chinese===

等水面完全清亮、在阳光下眨眼时，甘米奇把一只爪子勾到碗沿下面，猛地一掀。
半碗水泼了出来，聚拢，然后开始一阵一阵地流过地板，成了一条闪着阳光的银带，顺着坡度时分时合。甘米奇蹲在一旁，全神贯注地盯着它，一寸一寸、一尺一尺地追随它的进程，几乎要向那些形成的小小临时水塘扑去，却始终不碰它们。他兴奋地轻轻咪了两声。
---
“他在玩水，”老马肉难以置信地说。
“不，”猫咪来这儿睁大眼睛反驳，“他在创作。银老鼠。水蛇。闪光的藤蔓。”
“老天，你说得对，”老马肉表示赞同。“这是一种新的艺术形式。该叫水彩画？还是水雕塑？不知怎么，我觉得后者最好。就像雕塑家用熔化的锡做活动雕塑。”
“可它消失得太快了，”猫咪来这儿略带伤感地反对。“艺术应该持久。你看，现在几乎全流到墙边去了。”
“有些最好的艺术形式恰恰是转瞬即逝的，”老马肉争辩道。“音乐和舞蹈里的即兴呢？爵士即兴演奏会和墙上的影子戏呢？甘米奇随时可以重来——事实上，这一个月来他想必已经一次又一次地重来了。它从不完全相同，就像波浪或火焰。但它很美。”
“我想是吧，”猫咪来这儿说。随后她回过神来，接着说：“可我不觉得他一直从马桶里喝水有什么健康的。真的。”
老马肉耸耸肩。他对艺术气质、以及必须向生活发臭的根本处挖掘灵感自有一番洞见，只是很难表达得体面。
猫咪来这儿叹了口气，像是同自己的一切努力道别——玫瑰花瓣、水晶般纯净的瓶装水、香草精，还有那让甘米奇大吃一惊、对他轻轻嘶唾又打呼噜的苏打水。
“哦，好吧，”她说，“我想我可以更勤快地刷洗马桶。”
与此同时，甘米奇已经回到他的碗边，双爪并用，把它整个掀翻。此刻，他又一次抽动着鼻子，追逐那些满载太阳的银流，以这景象滋养自己的精神。他丝毫不为自己在做的事烦心。

===Original===

He had solved them all with one of his characteristically sharp distinctions: there was the *sacred* water, the sparklingly clear water to create with, and there was the water with character, the water to *drink*.

===Chinese===

他已用他特有的一个犀利区分，把所有问题一劳永逸地解决了：有一种是圣水，晶莹剔透、用来创作的水；还有一种是有性情的水，用来喝的水。

## Later Than You Think / 比你想的更晚
===Original===

Obviously the Archeologist’s study belonged to an era vastly distant from today. Familiar similarities here and there only sharpened the feeling of alienage. The sunlight that filtered through the windows in the ceiling had a wan and greenish cast and was augmented by radiation from some luminous material impregnating the walls and floor. Even the wide desk and the commodious hassocks glowed with a restful light. Across the former were scattered metal-backed wax tablets, styluses, and a pair of large and oddly formed spectacles. The crammed bookcases were not particularly unusual, but the books were bound in metal and the script on their spines would have been utterly unfamiliar to the most erudite of modern linguists. One of the books, lying open on a hassock, showed leaves of a thin, flexible, rustless metal covered with luminous characters. Between the bookcases were phosphorescent oil paintings, mainly of sea bottoms, in somber greens and browns. Their style, neither wholly realistic nor abstract, would have baffled the historian of art.
A blackboard with large colored crayons hinted equally at the schoolroom and the studio.
In the center of the room, midway to the ceiling, hung a fish with iridescent scales of breathtaking beauty. So invisible was its means of support that﻿—also taking into account the strange paintings and the greenish light﻿—one would have sworn that the object was to create an underwater scene.
The Explorer made his entrance in a theatrical swirl of movement. He embraced the Archeologist with a warmth calculated to startle that crusty old fellow. Then he settled himself on a hassock, looked up and asked a question in a speech and idiom so different from any we know that it must be called another means of communication rather than another language. The import was, “Well, what about it?”
If the Archeologist were taken aback, he concealed it. His expression showed only pleasure at being reunited with a long-absent friend.
“What about what?” he queried.
“About your discovery!”
“What discovery?” The Archeologist’s incomprehension was playful.
The Explorer threw up his arms. “Why, what else but your discovery, here on Earth, of the remains of an intelligent species? It’s the find of the age! Am I going to have to coax you? Out with it!”
“I didn’t make the discovery,” the other said tranquilly.

===Chinese===

考古学家的书房显然属于一个与今天相距极远的时代。这里那里几点熟悉的相似之处，反而加深了异世之感。从天花板窗户滤进的阳光带着苍白偏绿的色调，又由浸透了墙壁和地板的某种发光材料补足。连宽大的书桌和宽绰舒适的软凳都泛着安宁的光。书桌上散落着金属背板的蜡板、尖笔，还有一副造型奇特的大眼镜。塞得满满的书架并不特别出奇，但那些书都是金属装帧，书脊上的文字，再渊博的现代语言学家也会全然陌生。其中一本摊开在软凳上，露出薄而柔韧、不生锈的金属书页，上面布满发亮的字符。书架之间挂着磷光的油画，多为海底题材，绿色褐色，色调阴沉。它们的风格既非全然写实也非抽象，足以难倒艺术史家。
一块黑板和几支大号彩色蜡笔，既让人想到教室，也让人想到画室。
房间中央，在半天花板的高度，悬着一条鱼，鳞片虹彩流转，美得惊人。它的支撑方式全然隐形——再加上那些奇特的画和偏绿的光——人们几乎要发誓，这一切是为了营造一幅水底场景。
探险家（Explorer）一阵戏剧性的旋风式动作登场。他热烈地拥抱考古学家（Archeologist），那份热情算准了要吓这位脾气乖僻的老家伙一跳。随后他在软凳上坐下，抬头问了个问题，那语言和习语与我们已知的任何语言都如此不同，与其称之为另一种语言，不如称之为另一种交流手段。意思是：“怎么样，到底如何？”
考古学家就算吃了一惊，也藏而不露。他的神情只显示出与久别朋友重逢的喜悦。
“什么到底如何？”他问。
“关于你的发现啊！”
“什么发现？”考古学家的一头雾水是装出来的。
探险家双臂一挥。“嗐，除了你在地球上发现了一个智能物种的遗骸，还能是什么？这可是世纪大发现！难道还要我求你不成？快说！”
“发现不是我做出的，”对方平静地说。

===Original===

“I only supervised the excavations and directed the correlation of material. *You* ought to be doing the talking. *You’re* the one who’s just returned from the stars.”
“Forget that.” The Explorer brushed the question aside. “As soon as our spaceship got within radio range of Earth, they started to send us a continuous newscast covering the period of our absence. One of the items, exasperatingly brief, mentioned your discovery. It captured my imagination. I couldn’t wait to hear the details.” He paused, then confessed, “You get so eager out there in space﻿—a metal-filmed droplet of life lost in immensity. You rediscover your emotions﻿ ﻿…” He changed color, then finished rapidly, “As soon as I could decently get away, I came straight to you. I wanted to hear about it from the best authority﻿—yourself.”
---
The Archeologist regarded him quizzically. “I’m pleased that you should think of me and my work, and I’m very happy to see you again. But admit it now, isn’t there something a bit odd about your getting so worked up over this thing? I can understand that after your long absence from Earth, any news of Earth would seem especially important. But isn’t there an additional reason?”
The Explorer twisted impatiently. “Oh, I suppose there is. Disappointment, for one thing. We were hoping to get in touch with intelligent life out there. We were specially trained in techniques for establishing mental contact with alien intelligent life forms. Well, we found some planets with life upon them, all right. But it was primitive life, not worth bothering about.”
Again he hesitated embarrassedly. “Out there you get to thinking of the preciousness of intelligence. There’s so little of it, and it’s so lonely. And we so greatly need intercourse with another intelligent species to give depth and balance to our thoughts. I suppose I set too much store by my hopes of establishing a contact.” He paused. “At any rate, when I heard that what we were looking for, you had found here at home﻿—even though dead and done for﻿—I felt that at least it was something. I was suddenly very eager. It is odd, I know, to get so worked up about an extinct species﻿—as if my interest could mean anything to them now﻿—but that’s the way it hit me.”
---
Several small shadows crossed the windows overhead. They might have been birds, except they moved too slowly.

===Chinese===

“我只是主持了发掘，指导了材料的汇总整理。该说话的星你才对。你才是刚从星海归来的人。”
“那个就别提了。”探险家把话题挥开。“我们的飞船一进入地球的无线电范围，他们就开始向我们持续播送我们离开期间的新闻。其中一条简短得让人生气，提到了你的发现。它攫住了我的想象力。我等不及要听细节。”他顿了顿，又坦白道，“在外太空那边，人会变得那么急切——一滴包着金属膜的生命之水，迷失在浩渺里。你会重新发现自己的情感……”他变了脸色，随即飞快地说完，“我一能得体脱身，就径直来找你了。我想听最好的权威——你本人——亲口讲讲。”
---
考古学家打趣地看着他。“你惦记我和我的工作，我很高兴；再见到你我也非常开心。不过现在承认吧，你对这件事这么大动干戈，是不是有点反常？我明白，离开地球那么久，任何关于地球的新闻都显得格外重要。可是不是还有一个别的原因？”
探险家不耐烦地扭了扭身子。“哦，就算是吧。一是失望。我们本指望在外太空与智能生命取得联系。我们受过专门训练，懂得如何与异星智能生命形式建立心智接触。结果呢，我们确实发现了一些有生命的行星。但都是原始生命，不值得费神。”
他又一次不好意思地迟疑。“在外太空，你会开始体会智能的珍贵。它那么少，又那么孤独。而我们如此需要与另一个智能物种的交流，来给我们的思想以深度和平衡。我想我对建立接触的期望寄托得太重了。”他停了停。“总之，当我听说我们苦苦寻找的东西，你们竟在家里找到了——虽然已死绝——我觉得至少也算点什么。我一下子热切起来。我知道，为一个灭绝的物种如此激动很奇怪——仿佛我的兴趣如今对他们还有什么意义似的——可我就是这种感觉。”
---
几道小小的影子从头顶的窗前掠过。它们也许是鸟，只是飞得太慢。

===Original===

“I think I understand,” the Archeologist said softly.
“So get on with it and tell me about your discovery!” the Explorer exploded.
“I’ve already told you that it wasn’t my discovery,” the Archeologist reminded him. “A few years after your expedition left, there was begun a detailed resurvey of Earth’s mineral resources. In the course of some deep continental borings, one party discovered a cache﻿—either a very large box or a rather small room﻿—with metallic walls of great strength and toughness. Evidently its makers had intended it for the very purpose of carrying a message down through the ages. It proved to contain artifacts; models of buildings, vehicles, and machines, objects of art, pictures, and books﻿—hundreds of books, along with elaborate pictorial dictionaries for interpreting them. So now we even understand their languages.”
“Languages?” interrupted the Explorer. “That’s queer. Somehow one thinks of an alien species as having just one language.”
“Like our own, this species had several, though there were some words and symbols that were alike in all their languages. These words and symbols seem to have come down unchanged from their most distant prehistory.”
The Explorer burst out, “I am not interested in all that dry stuff! Give me the wet! What were they like? How did they live? What did they create? What did they want?”
The Archeologist gently waved aside the questions. “All in good time. If I am to tell you everything you want to know, I must tell it my own way. Now that you are back on Earth, you will have to reacquire those orderly and composed habits of thought which you have partly lost in the course of your wild interstellar adventurings.”
“Curse you, I think you’re just trying to tantalize me.”
The Archeologist’s expression showed that this was not altogether untrue. He casually fondled an animal that had wriggled up onto his desk, and which looked rather more like an eel than a snake. “Cute little brute, isn’t it?” he remarked. When it became apparent that the Explorer wasn’t to be provoked into another outburst, he continued, “It became my task to interpret the contents of the cache, to reconstruct its makers’ climb from animalism and savagery to civilization, their rather rapid spread across the world’s surface, their first fumbling attempts to escape from the Earth.”
---
“They had spaceships?”
“It’s barely possible.

===Chinese===

“我想我明白，”考古学家轻声说。
“那就快讲，跟我说说你的发现！”探险家爆发了。
“我已经告诉过你，发现不是我做出的，”考古学家提醒他。“你们的探险队离开几年之后，地球开始了一次详细的矿产资源重勘。在一些深入大陆的钻探中，一队人发现了一个秘藏——或者是一只很大的箱子，或者是一间相当小的房间——金属墙壁，坚固而强韧。显然，其制造者正是为了这样一个目的而造它：把一个信息传递下去，穿越世代。里面证实藏有人工制品：建筑、车辆和机器的模型，艺术品，图画，还有书籍——数以百计的书，外加解读它们用的精细图解词典。所以现在我们连他们的语言都懂了。”
“语言？”探险家插话。“这倒怪了。不知怎么，人们总觉得一个外星物种只有一种语言。”
“和我们一样，这个物种有好几种语言，不过有些词和符号在他们所有的语言里都相同。这些词和符号似乎从他们最遥远的史前原封不动地传了下来。”
探险家脱口而出：“这些干巴巴的东西我不感兴趣！给我来点有血肉的！他们长什么样？怎么生活？创造了什么？想要什么？”
考古学家温和地把这些问题挥到一边。“心急吃不了热豆腐。要我把你想知道的都告诉你，我就得按我自己的方式讲。你如今回了地球，就得重新拾起那些有条不紊、从容不迫的思维习惯——你在狂野的星际冒险中已经丢掉一部分了。”
“该死，我看你就是存心吊我胃口。”
考古学家的表情说明这也不全是冤枉他。他随手爱抚一只扭上他书桌的小动物，那东西与其说像蛇，不如说更像鳗鱼。“挺可爱的小家伙，是吧？”他说。眼看探险家不会被激出又一次发作，他继续道：“解读秘藏的内容，就成了我的任务——重构它的制造者如何从兽性与蒙昧爬向文明，如何以相当快的速度蔓延到世界表面，如何做出逃离地球的最初摸索。”
---
“他们有宇宙飞船？”
“不是没有可能。

===Original===

I rather hope they did, since it would mean the chance of a survival elsewhere, though the negative results of your expedition rather lessen that.” He went on, “The cache was laid down when they were first attempting space flight, just after their discovery of atomic power, in the first flush of their youth. It was probably created in a kind of exuberant fancifulness, with no serious belief that it would ever serve the purpose for which it was intended.” He looked at the Explorer strangely. “If I am not mistaken, we have laid down similar caches.”
After a moment the Archeologist continued, “My reconstruction of their history, subsequent to the laying down of the cache, has been largely hypothetical. I can only guess at the reasons for their decline and fall. Supplementary material has been very slow in coming in, though we are still making extensive excavations at widely separated points. Here are the last reports.” He tossed the Explorer a small metal-leaf pamphlet. It flew with a curiously slow motion.
“That’s what struck me so queer right from the start,” the Explorer observed, putting the pamphlet aside after a glance. “If these creatures were relatively advanced, why haven’t we learned about them before? They must have left so many things﻿—buildings, machines, engineering projects, some of them on a large scale. You’d think we’d be turning up traces everywhere.”
“I have four answers to that,” the Archeologist replied. “The first is the most obvious. Time. Geologic ages of it. The second is more subtle. What if we should have been looking in the wrong place? I mean, what if the creatures occupied a very different portion of the Earth than our own? Third, it’s possible that atomic energy, out of control, finished the race and destroyed its traces. The present distribution of radioactive compounds throughout the Earth’s surface lends some support to this theory.
“Fourth,” he went on, “it’s my belief that when an intelligent species begins to retrogress, it tends to destroy, or, rather, debase all the things it has laboriously created. Large buildings are torn down to make smaller ones. Machines are broken up and worked into primitive tools and weapons. There is a kind of unraveling or erasing.

===Chinese===

我还相当希望他们有，因为那意味着在别处幸存的机会，尽管你们探险队的负面结果让这机会减色不少。”他接着说，“秘藏是在他们首次尝试宇航时埋下的，就在他们发现原子能之后，正值青春的最初绽放。它的创建多半出于一种奔放的奇想，并不真信它有朝一日能实现预期的用途。”他古怪地看了探险家一眼。“如果我没弄错，我们也埋下过类似的秘藏。”
过了一会儿，考古学家继续道：“秘藏埋下之后他们的历史，我的重构在很大程度上是假说。他们衰亡的原因，我只能猜测。补充材料的到来一直非常缓慢，尽管我们仍在相距遥远的许多地点进行大规模发掘。这是最近的一批报告。”他把一本小小的金属页小册子抛给探险家。它以一种奇慢的动作飞了过去。
“这正是从一开始就让我觉得怪的地方，”探险家说，扫了一眼便把小册子放到一边。“这些生物如果相当先进，我们以前怎么从来没听说过他们？他们肯定留下了数不清的东西——建筑、机器、工程，有些还是大规模的。你会以为我们到处都该挖出痕迹。”
“对此我有四个回答，”考古学家答道。“第一个最明显：时间。地质纪年那么长的时间。第二个更微妙：要是我们一直找错了地方呢？我是说，要是这些生物占据的地球区域与我们的大不相同呢？第三，原子能失控终结这个种族、抹去其痕迹，也是可能的。目前放射性化合物在整个地球表面的分布，为这个理论提供了若干支持。
“第四，”他继续道，“我的看法是，当一个智能物种开始退化，它便倾向于摧毁——或者不如说贬损——它辛苦创造的一切。大建筑被拆掉改建小建筑。机器被砸碎，改造成原始的工具和武器。有一种解体或抹除在发生。

===Original===

A cultural Second Law of Thermodynamics begins to operate, whereby the intellect and all its works are gradually degraded to the lowest level of meaning and creativity.”
---
“But why?” The Explorer sounded anguished. “Why should any intelligent species end like that? I grant the possibility of atomic power getting out of hand, though one would have thought they’d have taken the greatest precautions. Still, it could happen. But that fourth answer﻿—it’s morbid.”
“Cultures and civilizations die,” said the Archeologist evenly. “That has happened repeatedly in our own history. Why not species? An individual dies﻿—and is there anything intrinsically more terrible in the death of a species than in the death of an individual?”
He paused. “With respect to the members of this one species, I think that a certain temperamental instability hastened their end. Their appetites and emotions were not sufficiently subordinated to their understanding and to their sense of drama﻿—their enjoyment of the comedy and tragedy of existence. They were impatient and easily incapacitated by frustration. They seem to have been singularly guilty in their pleasures, behaving either like gloomy moralists or gluttons.
“Because of taboos and an overgrown possessiveness,” he continued, “each individual tended to limit his affection to a tiny family; in many cases he focused his love on himself alone. They set great store by personal prestige, by the amassing of wealth and the exercise of power. Their notable capacity for thought and manipulative activity was expended on things rather than persons or feelings. Their technology outstripped their psychology. They skimped fatally when it came to hard thinking about the purpose of life and intellectual activity, and the means for preserving them.”
Again the slow shadows drifted overhead.
“And finally,” the Archeologist said, “they were a strangely haunted species. They seem to have been obsessed by the notion that others, greater than themselves, had prospered before them and then died, leaving them to rebuild a civilization from ruins. It was from those others that they thought they derived the few words and symbols common to all their languages.”
“Gods?” mused the Explorer.
The Archeologist shrugged. “Who knows?”
---
The Explorer turned away. His excitement had visibly evaporated, leaving behind a cold and miserable residue of feeling.

===Chinese===

一种文化上的热力学第二定律开始起作用，智能及其一切造物被逐渐降解到意义与创造力的最低水平。”
---
“可为什么？”探险家的声音很痛苦。“为什么任何智能物种会那样收场？原子能失控，我承认有这个可能，虽然按理说他们该有万无一失的防范。但毕竟可能发生。可那第四个回答——太病态了。”
“文化会死，文明会死，”考古学家不动声色地说。“这在我们自己的历史上一再发生。物种为什么就不会？个体会死——一个物种的死，本质上比一个个体之死更可怕吗？”
他停了停。“就这一个物种的成员而言，我认为一种气质性的不稳定加速了他们的终结。他们的欲望和情感没有充分服从于他们的理解力，也没有服从于他们的戏剧感——他们对存在的喜剧与悲剧的欣赏。他们没有耐性，一受挫折便容易一蹶不振。他们在享乐上似乎出奇地负有罪感，行止要么像阴郁的道学家，要么像饕餮之徒。
“由于禁忌和过度膨胀的占有欲，”他继续道，“每个个体都倾向于把感情限制在小小一家之内；许多情况下，他把爱只聚焦在自己身上。他们极看重个人威望，看重财富的积聚与权力的行使。他们思考与动手的非凡能力都耗费在事物上，而不是人或情感上。他们的技术跑到了心理学前头。到了对人生目的、智力活动及其保存手段进行硬性思考时，他们致命地吝啬。”
缓慢的影子又一次从头顶飘过。
“最后，”考古学家说，“他们是一个奇怪地闹鬼的物种。他们似乎着了魔似的认定，另有比他们更伟大的存在，在他们之前兴旺过又死绝了，留下他们从废墟上重建文明。他们以为自己所有语言中那几个共同的词和符号，正是从那些“他人”那里传来的。”
“神明？”探险家若有所思。
考古学家耸耸肩。“谁知道呢？”
---
探险家转过身去。他的兴奋显然已蒸发殆尽，只留下一份又冷又难受的情感残渣。

===Original===

“I am not sure I want to hear much more about them,” he said. “They sound too much like us. Perhaps it was a mistake, my coming here. Pardon me, old friend, but out there in space even *our* emotions become undisciplined. Everything becomes indescribably poignant. Moods are tempestuous. You shift in an instant from zenith to nadir﻿—and remember, out there you can see both.
“I was very eager to hear about this lost species,” he added in a sad voice. “I thought I would feel a kind of fellowship with them across the eons. Instead, I touch only corpses. It reminds me of when, out in space, there looms up before your prow, faint in the starlight, a dead sun. They were a young race. They thought they were getting somewhere. They promised themselves an eternity of effort. And all the while there was wriggling toward them out of that future for which they yearned﻿ ﻿… oh, it’s so completely futile and unfair.”
“I disagree,” the Archeologist said spiritedly. “Really, your absence from Earth has unsettled you even more than I first surmised. Look at the matter squarely. Death comes to everything in the end. Our past is strewn with our dead. That species died, it’s true. But what they achieved, they achieved. What happiness they had, they had. What they did in their short span is as significant as what they might have done had they lived a billion years. The present is always more important than the future. And no creature can have all the future﻿—it must be shared, left to others.”
“Maybe so,” the Explorer said slowly. “Yes, I guess you’re right. But I still feel a horrible wistfulness about them, and I hug to myself the hope that a few of them escaped and set up a colony on some planet we haven’t yet visited.” There was a long silence. Then the Explorer turned back. “You old devil,” he said in a manner that showed his gayer and more boisterous mood had returned, though diminished, “you still haven’t told me anything definite about them.”
“So I haven’t,” replied the Archeologist with guileful innocence. “Well, they were vertebrates.”
“Oh?”
“Yes. What’s more, they were mammals.”
---
“Mammals? I was expecting something different.”
“I thought you were.”
The Explorer shifted. “All this matter of evolutionary categories is pretty cut-and-dried. Even a knowledge of how they looked doesn’t mean much. I’d like to approach them in a more intimate way.

===Chinese===

“我不确定自己还想不想再听更多他们的事，”他说。“他们听起来太像我们了。也许我来这儿是个错误。原谅我，老朋友，在外太空那边，连我们的情感都会变得不守规矩。一切都变得难以言喻地尖锐。心绪狂暴多变。一瞬之间你就从天顶跌到谷底——而且记住，在外太空，天顶和谷底你都看得见。
“我曾那么热切地想听这个失落物种的事，”他忧伤地补充道。“我以为自己会感到一种跨越万古的伙伴之情。可我触摸到的只有尸体。这让我想起，在外太空，船首前方会隐隐浮现一颗死去的太阳，在星光里黯淡模糊。他们是一个年轻的种族。他们以为自己正在步步向前。他们向自己许诺了一个永恒的努力。而与此同时，正从他们渴望的那个未来里，向他们蠕动爬来的却是……哦，这一切如此徒劳，如此不公。”
“我不同意，”考古学家精神抖擞地说。“说真的，你离开地球这段日子让我心神不定的程度，比我起初揣测的还厉害。正视这件事吧。到头来死亡降临于一切。我们的过去布满我们的死者。那个物种死了，没错。但他们成就的，已经成就；他们拥有的幸福，已经拥有。他们在短短一瞬里所做的，与他们倘若活上十亿年可能做的，同样意义重大。现在永远比未来重要。而没有谁能拥有全部的未来——它必须分享，留给别人。”
“也许吧，”探险家缓缓地说。“是的，我想你是对的。可我对他们仍有一种可怕的怅惘，而且我暗自怀抱一个希望：他们中有几只逃了出去，在某个我们还没到过的行星上建立了殖民地。”长久的沉默。然后探险家转回身来。“你这个老滑头，”他说，语气显示他那更快活、更喧闹的情绪回来了，虽然打了折扣，“你到现在还没跟我讲一句他们的准话呢。”
“确实没有，”考古学家带着狡黠的无辜答道。“那么，他们是脊椎动物。”
“哦？”
“是的。而且，他们是哺乳动物。”
---
“哺乳动物？我还以为会是别的样子。”
“我猜也是。”
探险家挪了挪身子。“进化分类这一套太死板了。就算知道他们长什么样，也说明不了什么。我想要更贴近地接近他们。

===Original===

How did they think of themselves? What did they call themselves? I know the word won’t mean anything to me, but it will give me a feeling﻿—of recognition.”
“I can’t say the word,” the Archeologist told him, “because I haven’t the proper vocal equipment. But I know enough of their script to be able to write it for you as they would have written it. Incidentally, it is one of those words common to all their languages, that they attributed to an earlier race of beings.”
The Archeologist extended one of his eight tentacles toward the blackboard. The suckers at its tip firmly grasped a bit of orange crayon. Another of his tentacles took up the spectacles and adjusted them over his three-inch protruding pupils.
The eel-like glittering pet drifted back into the room and nosed curiously about the crayon as it traced:
**rat**

===Chinese===

他们怎么看待自己？他们管自己叫什么？我知道那词对我来说不会有意义，但它会给我一种感觉——一种相认的感觉。”
“我说不出那个词，”考古学家告诉他，“因为我没有相应的发声器官。但我对他们的文字懂得够多，能照他们的写法写给你。顺便说，那正是他们所有语言共有的词之一，被他们归于一个更早的种族。”
考古学家把八条触手中的一条伸向黑板。触手尖端的吸盘牢牢夹起一截橙色蜡笔。另一条触手拿起那副眼镜，架到他三英寸长、凸出的瞳孔前。
那条鳗鱼般闪亮的宠物漂回房间，好奇地凑近蜡笔，看它一笔一画写出：
**鼠**

### I / 一
===Original===

> To bring the dead to life

Is no great magic.

Few are wholly dead:

Blow on a dead man’s embers

And a live flame will start.

===Chinese===

> 让死者复生

并非什么大魔法。

鲜有人彻底死透：

朝死人的余烬吹一口气

便有活的火焰燃起。

===Original===

I dipped through the filmy curtain into the boys’ half of the dressing room and there was Sid sitting at the star’s dressing table in his threadbare yellowed undershirt, the lucky one, not making up yet but staring sternly at himself in the bulb-framed mirror and experimentally working his features a little, as actors will, and kneading the stubble on his fat chin.
I said to him quietly, “Siddy, what are we putting on tonight? Maxwell Anderson’s *Elizabeth the Queen* or Shakespeare’s *Macbeth*? It says *Macbeth* on the callboard, but Miss Nefer’s getting ready for Elizabeth. She just had me go and fetch the red wig.”
He tried out a few eyebrow rears﻿—right, left, both together﻿—then turned to me, sucking in his big gut a little, as he always does when a gal heaves into hailing distance, and said, “Your pardon, sweetling, what sayest thou?”
Sid always uses that kook antique patter backstage, until I sometimes wonder whether I’m in Central Park, New York City, nineteen hundred and three quarters, or somewhere in Southwark, Merry England, fifteen hundred and same. The truth is that although he loves every last fat part in Shakespeare and will play the skinniest one with loyal and inspired affection, he thinks Willy S. penned Falstaff with nobody else in mind but Sidney J. Lessingham. (And no accent on the ham, please.)
I closed my eyes and counted to eight, then repeated my question.
He replied, “Why, the Bard’s tragical history of the bloody Scot, certes.” He waved his hand toward the portrait of Shakespeare that always sits beside his mirror on top of his reserve makeup box. At first that particular picture of the Bard looked too nancy to me﻿—a sort of peeping-tom schoolteacher﻿—but I’ve grown used to it over the months and even palsy-feeling.
He didn’t ask me why I hadn’t asked Miss Nefer my question. Everybody in the company knows she spends the hour before curtain-time getting into character, never parting her lips except for that purpose﻿—or to bite your head off if you try to make the most necessary conversation.
“Aye, ’tiz *Macbeth* tonight,” Sid confirmed, returning to his frowning-practice: left eyebrow up, right down, reverse, repeat, rest. “And I must play the ill-starred Thane of Glamis.”
I said, “That’s fine, Siddy, but where does it leave us with Miss Nefer?

===Chinese===

我掀开薄纱帘钻进化妆室的男半区，西德正坐在明星化妆台前，穿着那件磨得发黄的旧汗衫——今晚的幸运儿。他还没上妆，只对着灯泡镶框的镜子严峻地端详自己，像演员们惯常的那样试着活动面部，又揉捏着肥下巴上的胡茬。
我轻声问他：“西迪，今晚我们演什么？麦克斯韦·安德森的《伊丽莎白女王》，还是莎士比亚的《麦克白》？告示板上写的是《麦克白》，可奈弗小姐却在为伊丽莎白做准备。她刚让我去取红色假发。”
他试了几下挑眉——右、左、双管齐下——然后转向我，稍稍收起他的大肚子，一如住常每次有姑娘进入打招呼距离时那样，说：“恕罪，小甜心，汝有何见教？”
西德在后台总用这套怪老头的古腔古调，弄得我有时犯嘀咕：我到底是在纽约（New York）中央公园、一千九百年又四分之三呢，还是在快活的英格兰萨瑟克、一千五百又四分之三个百年。事实是，虽然他爱莎翁笔下的每一个肥缺，哪怕最瘦的角色他也会怀着忠诚和灵感去演，但他认为威利·S写福斯塔夫时，心里想的就是西德尼·J·莱辛汉姆这个人，别无他人。（还有，姓尾的 ham 请别念重，多谢。）
我闭上眼睛数到八，然后把问题重复了一遍。
他答：“嗨，当然是莎翁那出悲情历史剧，讲那个血腥的苏格兰人的。”他朝莎士比亚画像挥挥手，那画像总摆在他镜子旁、备用化妆箱顶上。起初我觉得莎翁这幅画像女气得可以——像个偷窥狂小学教师——但几个月下来我看惯了，甚至生出些亲切感。
他没有问我为什么不去问奈弗小姐。全团的人都知道，开演前那一小时她都用来进入角色，除了这个目的绝不开口——不然你要是胆敢进行哪怕最必要的交谈，她就咬掉你的头。
“是啊，今晚是《麦克白》，”西德确认道，回到他的皱眉练习：左眉上挑、右眉下压，反向，再来，稍息。“而我必须扮演那位时运不济的葛莱密斯爵士。”
我说：“那敢情好，西迪，可这叫奈弗小姐怎么办？

===Original===

She’s already thinned her eyebrows and beaked out the top of her nose for Queen Liz, though that’s as far as she’s got. A beautiful job, the nose. Anybody else would think it was plastic surgery instead of putty. But it’s going to look kind of funny on the Thaness of Glamis.”
---
Sid hesitated a half second longer than he usually would﻿—I thought, *his timing’s off tonight*﻿—and then he harrumphed and said, “Why, Iris Nefer, decked out as Good Queen Bess, will speak a prologue to the play﻿—a prologue which I have myself but last week writ.” He owled his eyes. “ ’Tis an experiment in the new theater.”
I said, “Siddy, prologues were nothing new to Shakespeare. He had them on half his other plays. Besides, it doesn’t make sense to use Queen Elizabeth. She was dead by the time he whipped up *Macbeth*, which is all about witchcraft and directed at King James.”
He growled a little at me and demanded, “Prithee, how comes it your peewit-brain bears such a ballast of fusty book-knowledge, chit?”
I said softly, “Siddy, you don’t camp in a Shakespearean dressing room for a year, tete-a-teting with some of the wisest actors ever, without learning a little. Sure I’m a mental case, a poor little A & A existing on your sweet charity, and don’t think I don’t appreciate it, but﻿—”
“A-*and*-A, thou sayest?” he frowned. “Methinks the gladsome new forswearers of sack and ale call themselves A.A.”
“Agoraphobe and Amnesiac,” I told him. “But look, Siddy, I was going to sayest that I do know the plays. Having Queen Elizabeth speak a prologue to *Macbeth* is as much an anachronism as if you put her on the gantry of the British moonship, busting a bottle of champagne over its schnozzle.”
“Ha!” he cried as if he’d caught me out. “And saying there’s a new Elizabeth, wouldn’t that be the bravest advertisement ever for the Empire?﻿—perchance rechristening the pilot, copilot and astrogator Drake, Hawkins and Raleigh? And the ship *The Golden Hind*? Tilly fally, lady!”
He went on, “My prologue an anachronism, quotha! The groundlings will never mark it. Think’st thou wisdom came to mankind with the stenchful rocket and the sundered atomy? More, the Bard himself was topfull of anachronism. He put spectacles on King Lear, had clocks tolling the hour in Caesar’s Rome, buried that Roman ‘stead o’ burning him and gave Czechoslovakia a seacoast. Go to, doll.”
“Czechoslovakia, Siddy?”

===Chinese===

她已经为伊丽莎白女王修细了眉毛、垫高了鼻梁，虽然只做到这一步。鼻子修得真漂亮。换了别人会以为那是整形手术而不是油灰。可它长在葛莱密斯夫人的脸上可就有点滑稽了。”
---
西德迟疑了半秒，比平常长——我心想，他今晚的节奏不对——然后他清了清嗓子说：“嗨，艾丽丝·奈弗，装扮成贤明的贝丝女王，将为这出戏念一段开场白——一段我本人就在上礼拜亲笔写下的开场白。”他瞪圆了眼睛。“这是新戏剧的一项实验。”
我说：“西迪，开场白对莎士比亚可不是什么新鲜事。他一半的戏都有。再说，用伊丽莎白女王讲不通。他赶写《麦克白》的时候她已经死了，那戏通篇讲巫术，是冲着詹姆士国王去的。”
他对我低吼一声，质问道：“请问，汝这小鸻鸟脑瓜，何以装载如许陈腐书本压舱之物，丫头？”
我柔声说：“西迪，在莎士比亚的化妆间里露营一年，跟一些世上最有智慧的演员促膝谈心，不学到一点才怪。没错，我是个精神病例，一个靠你甜美慈悲过活的可怜小 A & A，别以为我不领情，可是——”
“A——还——A，汝是说？”他皱眉。“我想，那些新近戒掉萨克酒和麦芽酒的快活人，自称 A.A.。”
“广场恐惧症加失忆症，”我告诉他。“可是你听我说，西迪，我想说的是，我确实懂这些戏。让伊丽莎白女王给《麦克白》念开场白，就像让她站上英国月球飞船的发射架，往它鼻子上砸一瓶香槟一样，是个时代错误。”
“哈！”他叫起来，像是逮住了我的错。“既然说有一位新伊丽莎白，那岂不是帝国最勇敢的广告？——说不定把正驾驶、副驾驶和领航员重新命名为德雷克、霍金斯和罗利？飞船就叫‘金鹿号’？啐啦啦，女士！”
他继续道：“我的开场白是时代错误，瞧您说的！下层观众绝不会察觉。汝以为智慧是随臭烘烘的火箭和裂开的原子才降临人间的吗？再者，莎翁本人就满肚子时代错误。他给李尔王戴眼镜，让时钟在恺撒的罗马报时，把那个罗马人士葬而不火葬，还给捷克斯洛伐克一段海岸线。去你的，洋娃娃。”
“捷克斯洛伐克，西迪？”

===Original===

“Bohemia, then, what skills it? Leave me now, sweet poppet. Go thy ways. I have matters of import to ponder. There’s more to running a repertory company than reading the footnotes to Furness.”
---
Martin had just slouched by calling the Half Hour and looking in his solemnity, sneakers, levis and dirty T-shirt more like an underage refugee from Skid Row than Sid’s newest recruit, assistant stage manager and hardest-worked juvenile﻿—though for once he’d remembered to shave. I was about to ask Sid who was going to play Lady Mack if Miss Nefer wasn’t, or, if she were going to double the roles, shouldn’t I help her with the change? She’s a slow dresser and the Elizabeth costumes are pretty realistically stayed. And she would have trouble getting off that nose, I was sure. But then I saw that Siddy was already slapping on the alboline to keep the grease paint from getting into his pores.
*Greta, you ask too many questions*, I told myself. *You get everybody riled up and you rack your own poor rickety little mind*; and I hied myself off to the costumery to settle my nerves.
The costumery, which occupies the back end of the dressing room, is exactly the right place to settle the nerves and warm the fancies of any child, including an unraveled adult who’s saving what’s left of her sanity by pretending to be one. To begin with there are the regular costumes for Shakespeare’s plays, all jeweled and spangled and brocaded, stage armor, great Roman togas with weights in the borders to make them drape right, velvets of every color to rest your cheek against and dream, and the fantastic costumes for the other plays we favor; Ibsen’s *Peer Gynt*, Shaw’s *Back to Methuselah* and Hilliard’s adaptation of Heinlein’s *Children of Methuselah*, the Capek brothers’ *Insect People*, O’Neill’s *The Fountain*, Flecker’s *Hassan*, *Camino Real*, *Children of the Moon*, *The Beggar’s Opera*, *Mary of Scotland*, *Berkeley Square*, *The Road to Rome*.
There are also the costumes for all the special and variety performances we give of the plays: *Hamlet* in modern dress, *Julius Caesar* set in a dictatorship of the 1920’s, *The Taming of the Shrew* in caveman furs and leopard skins, where Petruchio comes in riding a dinosaur, *The Tempest* set on another planet with a spaceship wreck to start it off *Karrumph!

===Chinese===

“那就算波希米亚，有什么要紧？现在走吧，小宝贝儿。走你的路吧。我有要事要琢磨。经营一个轮演剧团，可不只是读弗内斯的注脚。”
---
马丁刚才懒洋洋地晃过去，喊着“半小时”，他那副严肃劲儿，脚蹬运动鞋、身穿牛仔裤和脏 T 恤，与其说像西德新招的人手——助理舞台监督兼最卖力的少年角色——不如说像个未成年的贫民窟小逃兵，尽管这回他总算记得刮了脸。我正要问西德，如果奈弗小姐不演麦克白夫人，那谁来演；或者她若是一人分饰两角，要不要我帮她赶场换装？她穿衣服慢，而伊丽莎白的戏服束腰束得很写实。还有那个鼻子，我敢肯定她很难弄下来。可随后我就看见西迪已经在往脸上抹阿尔博林油膏，防止油彩渗进毛孔。
格蕾塔，你的问题太多了，我对自己说。你把人人都惹毛，还折磨你自己那可怜摇晃的小脑瓜；于是我溜进服装间去安顿我的神经。
服装间占着化妆室的后半段，对任何一个孩子来说，它都是安顿神经、点燃幻想的绝佳去处——包括一个靠假装成孩子来保住她所剩无几神智的、散了架的大人。首先是莎剧的常规戏服：镶珠嵌宝、亮片锦缎，舞台铠甲，下摆坠着铅块好让褶皱垂得漂亮的罗马大托加，各种颜色任你贴脸做梦的天鹅绒，还有我们爱演的其他剧目的奇幻戏服：易卜生的《培尔·金特》、萧伯纳的《千岁人》、希利亚德改编海因莱因的《梅修撒拉的孩子们》、恰佩克兄弟的《昆虫人》、奥尼尔的《泉》、弗莱克的《哈桑》、《坎米诺·雷亚尔》、《月亮的孩子们》、《乞丐歌剧》、《苏格兰的玛丽》、《伯克利广场》、《罗马之路》。
还有我们为这些戏举办的特别演出和综艺演出的全部戏服：现代装扮的《哈姆雷特》、设在一九二〇年代独裁背景下的《尤利乌斯·恺撒》、穴居人毛皮豹纹的《驯悍记》——那出戏里彼特鲁乔骑着一头恐龙上场——还有设在另一颗行星上的《暴风雨》，开场是一艘飞船失事，咔隆！

===Original===

*﻿—which means a half dozen spacesuits, featherweight but looking ever so practical, and the weirdest sort of extraterrestrial-beast outfits for Ariel and Caliban and the other monsters.
Oh, I tell you the stuff in the costumery ranges over such a sweep of space and time that you sometimes get frightened you’ll be whirled up and spun off just anywhere, so that you have to clutch at something very real to you to keep it from happening and to remind you where you *really* are﻿—as I did now at the subway token on the thin gold chain around my neck (Siddy’s first gift to me that I can remember) and chanted very softly to myself, like a charm or a prayer, closing my eyes and squeezing the holes in the token: “Columbus Circle, Times Square, Penn Station, Christopher Street﻿ ﻿…”
---
But you don’t ever get *really* frightened in the costumery. Not exactly, though your goosehairs get wonderfully realistically tingled and your tummy chilled from time to time﻿—because you know it’s all make-believe, a lifesize doll world, a children’s dress-up world. It gets you thinking of far-off times and scenes as *pleasant* places and not as black hungry mouths that might gobble you up and keep you forever. It’s always safe, always *just in the theatre, just on the stage*, no matter how far it seems to plunge and roam﻿ ﻿… and the best sort of therapy for a potholed mind like mine, with as many gray ruts and curves and gaps as its cerebrum, that can’t remember one single thing before this last year in the dressing room and that can’t ever push its shaking body out of that same motherly fatherly room, except to stand in the wings for a scene or two and watch the play until the fear gets too great and the urge to take just one peek at *the audience* gets too strong﻿ ﻿… and I remember what happened the two times I *did* peek, and I have to come scuttling back.
The costumery’s good occupational therapy for me, too, as my pricked and calloused fingertips testify.

===Chinese===

——这意味着六套太空服，轻若无物却看上去煞有介事，还有给爱丽儿、凯列班和其他怪物穿的最古怪的外星兽戏服。
哦，我告诉你，服装间里的东西跨越的时间和空间如此之广，你有时会害怕自己被卷起来甩到随便什么地方，于是你得紧紧抓住一样对你来说非常真实的东西，好让这事不发生，也好提醒你自己究竟身在何处——就像我现在这样，攥住挂在脖子上细金链的那枚地铁代币（那是西迪送我的头一件礼物，在我记忆里），闭紧眼睛，捏着代币上的孔，对自己非常轻柔地、念咒语似的唱：“哥伦布环岛，时报广场（Times Square），宾州车站，克里斯托弗街……”
---
不过在服装间里你从来不会真的害怕。不至于，虽然你的寒毛会时不时得到妙不可言的真实刺激，肚皮发凉——因为你知道这一切都是假扮，一个真人尺寸的玩偶世界，一个孩子的换装游戏世界。它让你把遥远的时代和场景想象成宜人的地方，而不是可能把你吞下去、永远扣住不还的黑饿大口。它总是安全的，永远只是戏里，只是台上，不管它看上去怎样纵深怎样漫游……而对一颗像我这样坑坑洼洼的脑子——它像我大脑皮层一样布满灰色的车辙、弯道和豁口，记不起过去一年化妆间生活之前的任何一件事，也永远没法把它颤抖的身子挪出那间如母如父的房间，除非到侧台站上一两个场景看戏，直到恐惧太大、想偷看一眼“观众”的冲动太强……而我想起前两次我真的偷看了之后发生的事，我就只好连滚带爬地缩回来。
服装间对我还是很好的作业疗法，我扎出老茧的指尖可以作证。

===Original===

I think I must have stitched up or darned half the costumes in it this last twelvemonth, though there are so many of them that I swear the drawers have accordion pleats and the racks extend into the fourth dimension﻿—not to mention the boxes of props and the shelves of scripts and prompt-copies and other books, including a couple of encyclopedias and the many thick volumes of Furness’s *Variorum Shakespeare*, which as Sid had guessed I’d been boning up on. Oh, and I’ve sponged and pressed enough costumes, too, and even refitted them to newcomers like Martin, ripping up and resewing seams, which can be a punishing job with heavy materials.
In a less sloppily organized company I’d be called wardrobe mistress, I guess. Except that to anyone in show business that suggests a crotchety old dame with lots of authority and scissors hanging around her neck on a string. Although I got my crochets, all right, I’m not that old. Kind of childish, in fact. As for authority, everybody outranks me, even Martin.
Of course to somebody *outside* show business, wardrobe mistress might suggest a yummy gal who spends her time dressing up as Nell Gwyn or Anitra or Mrs. Pinchwife or Cleopatra or even Eve (we got a legal costume for it) and inspiring the boys. I’ve tried that once or twice. But Siddy frowns on it, and if Miss Nefer ever caught me at it I think she’d whang me.
And in a normaller company it would be the wardrobe room, too, but costumery is my infantile name for it and the actors go along with my little whims.
I don’t mean to suggest our company is completely crackers. To get as close to Broadway even as Central Park you got to have something. But in spite of Sid’s whip-cracking there is a comforting looseness about its efficiency﻿—people trade around the parts they play without fuss, the bill may be changed a half hour before curtain without anybody getting hysterics, nobody gets fired for eating garlic and breathing it in the leading lady’s face. In short, we’re a team. Which is funny when you come to think of it, as Sid and Miss Nefer and Bruce and Maudie are British (Miss Nefer with a touch of Eurasian blood, I romance); Martin and Beau and me are American (at least I *think* I am) while the rest come from just everywhere.
---
Besides my costumery work, I fetch things and run inside errands and help the actresses dress and the actors too.

===Chinese===

我想这过去十二个月里，里面的戏服怕是有一半被我缝补过，可戏服实在太多了，我敢发誓那些抽屉带手风琴褶，衣架一路延伸进第四维——更别提一箱箱道具、一架架剧本和提词本及其他书，包括两部百科全书和弗内斯那部《莎士比亚集注》的许多厚册，正如西德猜到的，我一直埋头啃的就是它。哦，我还海绵擦洗、熨烫过够多的戏服，甚至给马丁这样的新人改过衣服，拆线重缝，碰到厚料子可是份苦工。
要是在一个不那么散漫组织的剧团，我大概会被称作服装女管事。只不过在演艺圈人听来，这称呼意味着一个专横的老太太，大权在握，脖子上用绳子挂满剪刀。虽然我的怪念头确实不少，我可没那么老。其实还相当孩子气。至于权威，人人都比我大，连马丁都是。
当然，对演艺圈之外的人来说，服装女管事也许还让人想到一个性感尤物，整天扮成内尔·格温或阿妮特拉或平奇怀夫太太或克娄巴特拉甚至夏娃（我们有一套合法的行头）来鼓舞小伙子们。那样的事我试过一两回。可西迪对此皱眉，而要是被奈弗小姐逮到，我想她会揍我。
在个更正常的剧团，那也该叫服装室，可“服装间”是我孩子气的叫法，演员们也纵容我这点小任性。
我倒不是说我们剧团彻底疯癫。能离百老汇这么近、哪怕只到中央公园，总得有两下子。可尽管西德鞭子甩得响，它的效率里有一种令人安心的松散——大家换戏份不吵不闹，开演前半小时还能换戏码而没人发歇斯底里，没人因为吃大蒜对着女主角的脸呼气而被开除。总之，我们是一个团队。细想起来挺好笑的，因为西德、奈弗小姐、布鲁斯和莫迪是英国人（奈弗小姐带一点欧亚混血，我浪漫地想象）；马丁、博和我是美国人（至少我认为我是），其余的人则来自五湖四海。
---
除了服装间的活计，我还跑腿传物、办室内差事，帮女演员穿衣，也帮男演员。

===Original===

The dressing room’s very coeducational in a halfway respectable way. And every once in a while Martin and I police up the whole place, me skittering about with dustcloth and wastebasket, he wielding the scrub-brush and mop with such silent grim efficiency that it always makes me nervous to get through and duck back into the costumery to collect myself.
Yes, the costumery’s a great place to quiet your nerves or improve your mind or even dream your life away. But this time I couldn’t have been there eight minutes when Miss Nefer’s Elizabeth-angry voice came skirling, “Girl! Girl! Greta, where is my ruff with silver trim?” I laid my hands on it in a flash and loped it to her, because Old Queen Liz was known to slap even her Maids of Honor around a bit now and then and Miss Nefer is a bear on getting into character﻿—a real Paul Muni.
She was all made up now, I was happy to note, at least as far as her face went﻿—I hate to see that spooky eight-spoked faint tattoo on her forehead (I’ve sometimes wondered if she got it acting in India or Egypt maybe).
Yes, she was already all made up. This time she’d been going extra heavy on the burrowing-into-character bit, I could tell right away, even if it was only for a hacked-out anachronistic prologue. She signed to me to help her dress without even looking at me, but as I got busy I looked at *her* eyes. They were so cold and sad and lonely (maybe because they were so far away from her eyebrows and temples and small tight mouth, and so shut away from each other by that ridge of nose) that I got the creeps. Then she began to murmur and sigh, very softly at first, then loudly enough so I got the sense of it.
“Cold, so cold,” she said, still seeing things far away though her hands were working smoothly with mine. “Even a gallop hardly fires my blood. Never was such a Januarius, though there’s no snow. Snow will not come, or tears. Yet my brain burns with the thought of Mary’s death-warrant unsigned. There’s my particular hell!﻿—to doom, perchance, all future queens, or leave a hole for the Spaniard and the Pope to creep like old worms back into the sweet apple of England. Philip’s tall black crooked ships massing like seagoing fortresses south-away﻿—cragged castles set to march into the waves. Parma in the Lowlands!

===Chinese===

这间化妆室在半正经的意义上非常男女同校。每隔一阵，马丁和我会把整个地方打扫一遍，我拎着抹布和废纸篓蹦来蹦去，他抡着刷子和拖把，沉默、狠辣、高效，弄得我总紧张兮兮地干完就溜回服装间定神。
是啊，服装间是个安神、益智、甚至消磨此生的好地方。可这回我进去怕还不到八分钟，奈弗小姐那伊丽莎白式盛怒的嗓音就尖利地刮过来：“丫头！丫头！格蕾塔，我那条镶银边的拉夫领在哪儿？”我一把摸到它，飞跑着送到她那儿，因为老伊丽莎白女王据说连宫廷女侍都时不时赏几个耳光，而奈弗小姐进入角色是动真格的——一个真正的保罗·穆尼。
她已经化好了全妆，我很欣慰地注意到，至少脸是——我不爱看她额头上那个幽灵似的八辐淡纹身（我有时琢磨，那是不是她在印度或埃及演戏得来的）。
是的，她已经全妆完毕。这一次她在“往角色里钻”这件事上格外下功夫，我一眼就看得出来，哪怕只是为了一段胡乱拼凑的时代错乱开场白。她招呼我帮她穿衣，看都不看我一眼，可忙活起来时我看了看她的眼睛。那双眼睛那么冷、那么悲哀、那么孤独（也许因为它离她的眉毛、太阳穴和抿紧的小嘴都那么远，又被那道鼻梁彼此隔开），我起了鸡皮疙瘩。随后她开始低语、叹息，起初很轻，后来响得让我听清了意思。
“冷，真冷，”她说，双手仍在与我流畅地配合，眼睛却望着远处。“连疾驰都难以让我的血液生热。从没有过这样的一月天，虽然没下雪。雪不肯来，眼泪也不肯。可我的头脑却为玛丽那张未签署的死刑状而灼烧。那是我独有的地狱！——判她死，或许殃及后世所有女王；不判，就给西班牙人和教皇留出窟窿，让他们像老蛀虫一样爬回英格兰这只甜苹果。腓力那些高大的黑色歪船正在南边集结，一座座海上的堡垒——准备踏进波涛的峭壁城堡。帕尔马在低地国家！

===Original===

And all the while my bright young idiot gentlemen spurting out my treasure as if it were so much water, as if gold pieces were a glut of summer posies. Oh, alackanight!”
And I thought, *Cry Iced!﻿—that’s sure going to be one tyrannosaur of a prologue. And how you’ll ever shift back to being Lady Mack beats me. Greta, if this is what it takes to do just a bit part, you’d better give up your secret ambition of playing walk-ons some day when your nerves heal.*
---
She was really getting to me, you see, with that characterization. It was as if I’d managed to go out and take a walk and sat down in the park outside and heard the President talking to himself about the chances of war with Russia and realized he’d sat down on a bench with its back to mine and only a bush between. You see, here we were, two females undignifiedly twisted together, at the moment getting her into that crazy crouch-deep bodice that’s like a big icecream cone, and yet here at the same time was Queen Elizabeth the First of England, three hundred and umpty-ump years dead, coming back to life in a Central Park dressing room. It shook me.
She looked so much the part, you see﻿—even without the red wig yet, just powdered pale makeup going back to a quarter of an inch from her own short dark bang combed and netted back tight. The age too. Miss Nefer can’t be a day over forty﻿—well, forty-two at most﻿—but now she looked and talked and felt to my hands dressing her, well, at least a dozen years older. I guess when Miss Nefer gets into character she does it with each molecule.
That age point fascinated me so much that I risked asking her a question. Probably I was figuring that she couldn’t do me much damage because of the positions we happened to be in at the moment. You see, I’d started to lace her up and to do it right I had my knee against the tail of her spine.
“How old, I mean how young might your majesty be?” I asked her, innocently wonderingly like some dumb serving wench.
For a wonder she didn’t somehow swing around and clout me, but only settled into character a little more deeply.
“Fifty-four winters,” she replied dismally. “ ’Tiz Januarius of Our Lord’s year One Thousand and Five Hundred and Eighty and Seven. I sit cold in Greenwich, staring at the table where Mary’s death warrant waits only my sign manual. If I send her to the block, I open the doors to future, less official regicides.

===Chinese===

而我那些明亮年轻的傻瓜绅士们，一路把我的财宝当水泼，金币多得像夏天的野花。哦，呜呼黑夜！”
我心想，老天结冰！——那开场白准是一只霸王龙。而你怎么倒退回麦克白夫人，我可想不通。格蕾塔，如果演这么一小段都要这样，那你最好放弃你那个秘密野心——等神经痊愈了有朝一日去跑龙套。
---
你瞧，她那个角色演绎真的攫住了我。就好像我设法出去散了个步，在公园里坐下，听见总统正自言自语跟俄国开战的可能性，然后意识到他就坐在我背后那张长椅上，中间只隔一丛灌木。你瞧，我们两个女人正不体面地拧作一团，此刻正把她塞进那件深得能蹲下去的疯束胸衣——活像一只大甜筒——而与此同时，英格兰伊丽莎白一世，死了三百好几年的人，正在中央公园的一间化妆室里还魂。这让我发抖。
你瞧，她像角色像到这个地步——尽管还没戴红假发，只扑了粉的苍白妆容，退到离她自己那绺梳拢压紧的深色刘海四分之一英寸处。还有年纪。奈弗小姐顶多四十——嗯，至多四十二——可此刻她看上去、听上去、以及我替她穿衣时手上感到的，起码老了十几岁。我猜奈弗小姐进入角色时，是每个分子一起进的。
年龄这一点让我着迷得不行，竟冒险问她一个问题。大概我盘算着，鉴于我们此刻恰好摆出的姿势，她伤不了我多少。你瞧，我已经开始给她系带子，而要系对，我的膝盖得顶住她脊柱的尾端。
“陛下多大年纪——我是说，多年轻？”我问她，天真烂漫得像个傻丫鬟。
奇怪的是她居然没有抡过来给我一巴掌，只是往角色里又沉了一寸。
“五十四个冬天，”她凄然答道。“现在是主历一千五百八十七年的一月。我坐在格林尼奇的寒冷里，盯着那张桌子，玛丽的死刑状只等我亲笔。我若送她上断头台，就为日后不那么名正言顺的弑君者们敞开大门。

===Original===

But if I doom her not, Philip’s armada will come inching up the Channel in a season, puffing smoke and shot, and my English Catholics, thinking only of Mary Regina, will rise and i’ the end the Spaniard will have all. All history would alter. That must not be, even if I’m damned for it! And yet﻿ ﻿… and yet﻿ ﻿…”
A bright blue fly came buzzing along (the dressing room has *some* insect life) and slowly circled her head rather close, but she didn’t even flicker her eyelids.
“I sit cold in Greenwich, going mad. Each afternoon I ride, praying for some mischance, some prodigy, to wash from my mind away the bloody question for some little space. It skills not what: a fire, a tree a-failing, Davison or e’en Eyes Leicester tumbled with his horse, an assassin’s ball clipping the cold twigs by my ear, a maid crying rape, a wild boar charging with dipping tusks, news of the Spaniard at Thames’ mouth or, more happily, a band of strolling actors setting forth some new comedy to charm the fancy or some great unheard-of tragedy to tear the heart﻿—though that were somewhat much to hope for at this season and place, even if Southwark be close by.”
---
The lacing was done. I stood back from her, and really she looked so much like Elizabeth painted by Gheeraerts or on the Great Seal of Ireland or something﻿—though the ash-colored plush dress trimmed in silver and the little silver-edge ruff and the black-silver tinsel-cloth cloak lined with white plush hanging behind her looked most like a winter riding costume﻿—and her face was such a pale frozen mask of Elizabeth’s inward tortures, that I told myself, *Oh, I got to talk to Siddy again, he’s made some big mistake, the lardy old lackwit. Miss Nefer just can’t be figuring on playing in Macbeth tonight.*
As a matter of fact I was nerving myself to ask *her* all about it direct, though it was going to take some real nerve and maybe be risking broken bones or at least a flayed cheek to break the ice of that characterization, when who should come by calling the Fifteen Minutes but Martin. He looked so downright goofy that it took my mind off Nefer-in-character for all of eight seconds.
His levied bottom half still looked like *The Lower Depths*. Martin is Village Stanislavsky rather than Ye Olde English Stage Traditions. But above that﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

可我若不判她死，腓力的舰队一季之内就会喷着烟雾炮弹，一寸寸爬上海峡，我那些心里只有玛丽女王的英国天主教徒就会起事，到头来西班牙人赢得一切。全部历史都将改写。那绝不可以，哪怕我为此下地狱！可是……可是……”
一只亮蓝的苍蝇嗡嗡飞来（化妆室总算有点昆虫生活），绕着她的头相当近地慢慢兜圈，可她连眼皮都没颤一下。
“我坐在格林尼奇的寒冷里，渐渐发疯。每天下午我骑马外出，祈祷有什么变故、什么奇迹，把那个血淋淋的问题从我心头冲开一小会儿。什么都行：一场火，一棵倒树，戴维森、甚至我的‘眼睛’莱斯特连人带马摔个跟头，刺客的弹丸削过我耳边的冷枝，一个哭喊被强暴的侍女，一头低着獠牙冲来的野猪，西班牙人进了泰晤士河口的消息，或者——更妙的是——一班子流浪戏子，献上一出新喜剧娱人心神，或一出闻所未闻的大悲剧撕人心肝——虽然在此时此地这指望有点过奢，哪怕萨瑟克就在近旁。”
---
系带完毕。我从她身边退开，她看上去真像吉利尔特斯笔下的伊丽莎白，或者爱尔兰大国玺上的那位——虽然那身银饰边的灰鼠绒裙、小小的银边拉夫领、还有身后那件白绒衬里的黑银锦缎斗篷，最像一套冬季骑装——而她的脸是那样一张苍白冻结的面具，映着伊丽莎白内心的煎熬，我对自己说：哦，我得再找西迪谈谈，他犯了个大错，这块老肥油糊涂蛋。奈弗小姐今晚绝不可能还打算演《麦克白》。
其实我正鼓起勇气想直接问她，虽然那需要真正的胆量，说不定要冒断骨头、至少掉层脸皮的风险才能破开她那角色化的冰层——这时恰巧马丁过来喊“十五分钟”。他那副样子实在傻得可以，把我的心思从入戏的奈弗小姐身上引开了整整八秒。
他下半身穿得依旧像《底层》：马丁是格林尼治村的斯坦尼斯拉夫斯基派，不奉老英格兰舞台传统。可是在那以上……

===Original===

well, all it really amounted to was that he was stripped to the waist and had shaved off the small high tuft of chest hair and was wearing a black wig that hung down in front of his shoulders in two big braids heavy with silver hoops and pins. But just the same those simple things, along with his tarpaper-solarium tan and habitual poker expression, made him look so like an American Indian that I thought, *Hey Zeus!﻿—he’s all set to play Hiawatha, or if he’d just cover up that straight-line chest, a frowny Pocahontas.* And I quick ran through what plays with Indian parts we do and could only come up with *The Fountain*.
I mutely goggled my question at him, wiggling my hands like guppy fins, but he brushed me off with a solemn mysterious smile and backed through the curtain. I thought, *nobody can explain this but Siddy*, and I followed Martin.

===Chinese===

呃，实际上无非是他打了个赤膊，剃掉了胸口那一小撮高位胸毛，戴着一顶黑假发，垂在肩膀前面，编成两条缀满银环银钗的粗辫子。可就是这几样简单的东西，配上他沥青棚日光浴晒出的棕黑和一贯的扑克脸，让他活脱脱像个美国印第安人，我心想：嘿-宙斯！——他这就齐活，能演海华沙了；或者只要把那道直线般的胸口一遮，还能演个皱眉的宝嘉康蒂。我飞快把我们演过的带印第安角色的戏码在心里过了一遍，只想得出《泉》。
我无声地瞪着他表达我的疑问，双手像孔雀鱼鱼鳍似的摆动，可他只回我一个庄严而神秘的微笑，转身掀帘出去了。我心想，这事除了西迪没人能解释，于是我跟了出去。

## Pipe Dream / 管道之梦
===Original===

Simon Grue found a two-inch mermaid in his bathtub. It had arms, hips, a finny tail, and (here the real trouble began) a face that reminded him irresistibly of Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich.
It wasn’t until the mermaid turned up in his bathtub that Simon Grue seriously began to wonder what the Russians were doing on the roof next door.
The old house next door together with its spacious tarpapered roof, which held a sort of pent-shack, a cylindrical old water tank, and several chicken-wire enclosures, had always been a focus of curiosity in this region of Greenwich Village, especially to whoever happened to be renting Simon’s studio, the north window-cum-skylight of which looked down upon it﻿—if you were exceptionally tall or if, like Simon, you stood halfway up a stepladder and peered.
During the 1920’s, old-timers told Simon, the house had been owned by a bootlegger, who had installed a costly pipe organ and used the water tank to store hooch. Later there had been a colony of shaven-headed Buddhist monks, who had strolled about the roof in their orange and yellow robes, meditating and eating raw vegetables. There had followed a commedia dell’arte theatrical group, a fencing salon, a school of the organ (the bootlegger’s organ was always one of the prime renting points of the house), an Arabian restaurant, several art schools and silvercraft shops of course, and an Existentialist coffee house.
The last occupants had been two bony-cheeked Swedish blondes who sunbathed interminably and had built the chicken-wire enclosures to cage a large number of sinister smoke-colored dogs﻿—Simon decided they were breeding werewolves, and one of his most successful abstractions, “Gray Hunger,” had been painted to the inspiration of an eldritch howling. The dogs and their owners had departed abruptly one night in a closed van, without any of the dogs ever having been offered for sale or either of the girls having responded with anything more than a raised eyebrow to Simon’s brave greetings of “Skoal!”
The Russians had taken possession about six months ago﻿—four brothers apparently, and one sister, who never stirred from the house but could occasionally be seen peering dreamily from a window. A white card with a boldly-inked “Stulnikov-Gurevich” had been thumbtacked to the peeling green-painted front door.

===Chinese===

西蒙·格鲁（Simon Grue）在他的浴缸里发现了一条两英寸的美人鱼。它有手臂，有臀部，有带鳍的尾巴，还有（真正的麻烦从这里开始）一张让他不由自主想起格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇（Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich）的脸。
直到美人鱼出现在他的浴缸里，西蒙·格鲁才开始认真琢磨：隔壁屋顶上那几个俄国人到底在干什么。
隔壁那座老宅连同它宽敞的沥青纸屋顶——屋顶上有一间坡顶小棚、一只圆筒形旧水箱和几圈铁丝网围栏——一直是格林尼治村这一带的好奇焦点，对碰巧租下西蒙画室的人尤其如此：画室的北窗兼天窗俯瞰着那片屋顶——如果你个子特别高，或者像西蒙那样站在折梯半腰上探头张望的话。
老街坊们告诉西蒙，二十世纪二十年代，这房子归一个私酒贩子所有，那人装了一架昂贵的管风琴，还用水箱存酒。后来住过一窝剃光头的佛教僧侣，他们穿着橙黄袍子在屋顶上漫步、冥想、吃生蔬菜。再后来是一个意大利即兴喜剧剧团、一家击剑沙龙、一所管风琴学校（私酒贩子的管风琴向来是这房子招租的头号卖点）、一家阿拉伯餐馆、当然还有好几所美术学校和银器铺子，以及一家存在主义咖啡馆。
最后一批住户是两个高颊骨的瑞典金发女郎，她们没完没了地晒日光浴，还搭起铁丝网围栏，关了满满一群阴森的烟灰色狗——西蒙断定她们在繁殖狼人，而他最成功的抽象画之一《灰色的饥饿》，就是受一声鬼哭般的嚎叫启发画成的。一天夜里，狗和主人们突然乘一辆厢式货车走了，一条狗也没卖过，西蒙勇敢地向她们道“干杯（Skoal）”时，两个姑娘的回应也从来不超过一条扬起的眉毛。
俄国人大约六个月前住了进来——明摆着是四兄弟加一个妹妹，妹妹从不出门，只是偶尔能看见她在窗前出神地凝望。一张白卡片用图钉钉在绿漆剥落的前门上，上面墨迹遒劲地写着“斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇”。

===Original===

Lafcadio Smits, the interior decorator, told Simon that the newcomers were clearly White Russians; he could tell it by their bushy beards. Lester Phlegius maintained that they were Red Russians passing as White, and talked alarmingly of spying, sabotage and suitcase bombs.
Simon, who had the advantages of living on the spot and having been introduced to one of the brothers﻿—Vasily﻿—at a neighboring art gallery, came to believe that they were both Red and White and something more﻿—solid, complete Slavs in any case, Double Dostoevsky Russians if one may be permitted the expression. They ordered vodka, caviar, and soda crackers by the case. They argued interminably (loudly in Russian, softly in English), they went on mysterious silent errands, they gloomed about on the roof, they made melancholy music with their deep harmonious voices and several large guitars. Once Simon though they even had the bootlegger’s organ going, but there had been a bad storm at the time and he hadn’t been sure.
They were not quite as tight-lipped as the Swedish girls. Gradually a curt front-sidewalk acquaintance developed and Simon came to know their names. There was Vasily, of course, who wore thick glasses, the most scholarly-looking of the lot and certainly the most bibulous﻿—Simon came to think of Vasily as the Vodka Breather. Occasionally he could be glimpsed holding Erlenmayer flasks, trays of culture dishes, and other pieces of biological equipment, or absentmindedly wiping off a glass slide with his beard.
Then there was Ivan, the dourest of the four, though none of them save Vasily seemed very amiable. Simon’s private names for Ivan were the Nihilist and the Bomber, since he sometimes lugged about with him a heavy globular leather case. With it and his beard﻿—a square black one﻿—he had more than once created a mild sensation in the narrow streets of the Village.
Next there was Mikhail, who wore a large crucifix on a silver chain around his neck and looked like a more spiritual Rasputin. However, Simon thought of him less as the Religious than as the Whistler﻿—for his inveterate habit of whistling into his straggly beard a strange tune that obeyed no common harmonic laws.

===Chinese===

室内装潢师拉夫卡迪奥·斯米茨告诉西蒙，新住户显然是白俄；他从那几部大胡子就能看出来。莱斯特·弗莱吉乌斯则坚称他们是冒充白俄的红俄，还煞有介事地大谈间谍、破坏和手提箱炸弹。
西蒙呢，他既有就近居住的便利，又在隔壁一家画廊被人引见给兄弟中的一个——瓦西里——于是渐渐相信他们既是红的又是白的，还是别的什么——总之是结结实实、完完整整的斯拉夫人，如果容许这样说的话，是双倍陀思妥耶夫斯基式的俄国人。他们成箱成箱地订购伏特加、鱼子酱和苏打饼干。他们无休止地争论（俄语大声，英语轻声），出门办神秘的无声差事，在屋顶上郁郁地徘徊，用深沉和谐的嗓音和几把大吉他奏出忧郁的音乐。有一回西蒙以为他们连私酒贩子的管风琴都弄响了，可当时正赶上大风暴，他没敢确定。
他们不像瑞典姑娘们那样守口如瓶。一种简慢的人行道式相识渐渐发展起来，西蒙也知道了他们的名字。当然是瓦西里，戴厚眼镜，一伙人里最像学者，也肯定最能喝——西蒙渐渐把瓦西里想成伏特加呼吸者。偶尔能瞥见他拿着锥形烧瓶、一碟碟培养皿和别的生物器材，或是心不在焉地用胡子擦拭玻璃载片。
然后是伊万，四兄弟里最阴沉的一个——不过除了瓦西里，他们谁也说不上和气。西蒙私下给伊万起了两个外号：虚无主义者和炸弹客，因为他有时拎着一只沉甸甸的球形皮箱到处走。凭这只箱子和他那部方的黑胡子，他不止一次在村子的窄街上制造出小小的轰动。
接下来是米哈伊尔，脖子上银链挂着一枚大十字架，长得像一位更有灵性的拉斯普京。不过西蒙与其把他想成宗教徒，不如想成吹哨人——因为他有个积习，总朝自己蓬乱的胡子里吹一支怪曲子，那曲子不服从任何通行的和声法则。

===Original===

Somehow Mikhail seemed to carry a chilly breeze around with him, a perpetual cold draught, so that Simon had to check himself in order not to clutch together his coat collar whenever he heard the approach of the eerie piping.
Finally there was Lev, beardless, shorter by several inches, and certainly the most elusive of the brothers. He always moved at a scurry, frequently dipping his head, so that it was some time before Simon assured himself that he had the Stulnikov-Gurevich face. He did, unmistakably. Lev seemed to be away on trips a good deal. On his returns he was frequently accompanied by furtive but important-looking men﻿—a different one on each occasion. There would be much bustle at such times﻿—among other things, the shades would be drawn. Then in a few hours Lev would be off again, and his man-about-town companion too.
And of course there was the indoors-keeping sister. Several times Simon had heard one of the brothers calling “Grushenka,” so he assumed that was her name. She had the Stulnikov-Gurevich face too, though on her, almost incredibly, it was strangely attractive. She never ventured on the roof but she often sat in the pent-shack. As far as Simon could make out, she always wore some dark Victorian costume﻿—at least it had a high neck, long sleeves, and puffed shoulders. Pale-faced in the greenish gloom, she would stare for hours out of the pent-shack’s single window, though never in Simon’s direction. Occasionally she would part and close her lips, but not exactly as if she were speaking, at least aloud﻿—he thought of calling her the Bubble Blower. The effect was as odd as Mikhail’s whistling but not as unpleasant. In fact, Simon found himself studying Grushenka for ridiculously long periods of time. His mild obsession began to irk him and one day he decided henceforth to stay away altogether from his north window and the stepladder. As a result he saw little of the alterations the Russians began to make on the roof at this point, though he did notice that they lugged up among other things a length of large-diameter transparent plastic piping.
---
So much for the Russians, now for the mermaid. Late one night Simon started to fill his bathtub with cold water to soak his brushes and rags﻿—he was working with a kind of calcimine at the time, experimenting with portable murals painted on large plaster-faced wooden panels.

===Chinese===

不知怎么，米哈伊尔身上仿佛随身携着一股寒风，一阵永久的冷穿堂风，以至于西蒙一听见那阵诡异的笛音逼近，就得克制自己才不至于一把攥紧衣领。
最后是列夫，没有胡子，矮好几英寸，也肯定是兄弟们里最难见到的一个。他总是疾行，频频低头，所以西蒙过了一阵子才确定自己看清了那张斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇家的脸。确实没错，错不了。列夫似乎常常出门旅行。回来时，常有鬼鬼祟祟却看上去很重要的人陪着——每回都不是同一个人。这种时候家里就会大为忙乱——其中包括把窗帘都拉上。然后几个小时后，列夫又走了，他那位交际场上的同伴也一同消失。
当然还有那位深居简出的妹妹。西蒙有几回听见兄弟中有人喊“格鲁申卡”，便认定那是她的名字。她也有斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇家的脸，只是长在她身上，几乎不可思议地，别有一种奇异的动人。她从不上屋顶，但常坐在坡顶小棚里。就西蒙所能判断的，她总穿着一身维多利亚式的深色衣裙——至少是高领、长袖、泡泡肩。在绿幽幽的暗影里，她脸色苍白，会对着小棚唯一的窗户一连凝望几个小时，却从不朝西蒙的方向看。偶尔她会开合双唇，但不太像在说话，至少不像出声说话——西蒙想管她叫吹泡泡的人。那效果跟米哈伊尔的口哨一样古怪，却没那么讨厌。事实上，西蒙发现自己盯着格鲁申卡一看就是长得离谱的时间。这种温和的痴迷开始折磨他，有一天他决定，从此彻底远离北窗和折梯。结果，俄国人此时开始在屋顶上进行的改造，他看得很少，不过他确实注意到，他们搬上去的东西里有一根大口径透明塑料管。
---
俄国人交代至此，现在说美人鱼。一天深夜，西蒙往浴缸里放冷水，准备泡他的画笔和抹布——他当时在用一种刷墙粉料做试验，试着在大幅石膏面木板上画可搬运的壁画。

===Original===

Heavily laden, he got back to the bathroom just in time to shut off the water﻿—and to see a tiny fish of some sort splashing around in it.
He was not unduly surprised. Fish up to four or five inches in length were not unheard-of apparitions in the cold-water supply of the area, and this specimen looked as if it displaced no more than a teaspoon of water.
He made a lucky grab and the next moment he was holding in his firmly clenched right hand the bottom half of a slim wriggling creature hardly two inches long﻿—and now Simon was surprised indeed.
To begin with, it was not greenish white nor any common fish color, but palely-pinkish, flesh-colored in fact. And it didn’t seem so much a fish as a tadpole﻿—at least its visible half had a slightly oversize head shaped like a bullet that has mushroomed a little, and two tiny writhing arms or appendages of some sort﻿—and it felt as if it had rather large hips for a fish or even a tadpole. Equip a two-months human embryo with a finny tail, give it in addition a precocious feminine sexiness, and you’d get something of the same effect.
But all that was nothing. The trouble was that it had a face﻿—a tiny face, of course, and rather goggly-ghostly like a planarian’s, but a face nevertheless, a human-looking face, and also (here was the real trouble) a face that bore a grotesque but striking resemblance to that of Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich.
Simon’s fingers tightened convulsively. Simultaneously the slippery creature gave a desperate wriggle. It shot into the air in a high curve and fell into the scant inch of space between the bathtub and the wall.
The next half hour was hectic in a groveling sort of way. Retrieving anything from behind Simon’s ancient claw-footed bathtub was a most difficult feat. There was barely space to get an arm under it and at one point the warping of the floor boards prevented even that. Besides, there was the host of dust-shrouded objects it had previously been too much trouble to tease out﻿—an accumulation of decades. At first Simon tried to guide himself by the faint flopping noises along the hidden base of the wall, but these soon ceased.
Being on your knees and your chest with an ear against the floor and an arm strainingly outstretched is probably not the best position to assume while weird trains of thought go hooting through your head, but sometimes it has to happen that way.

===Chinese===

他满载而归回到浴室，正好赶上关水——也正好看见一条什么小鱼在水里扑腾。
他没有过分惊讶。四五英寸长的鱼出现在这一带的冷水供应里，并非闻所未闻的奇观，而这个标本的排水量看上去超不过一茶匙。
他幸运地一抓，下一刻，他紧紧攥拢的右手里已经握着一条扭来扭去的细长生物的下半截，满打满算两英寸长——这下西蒙是真吃惊了。
首先，它不是绿白色，也不是任何常见的鱼色，而是浅粉红色，实际上是肉色的。而且与其说像鱼，不如说像蝌蚪——至少它露出来的这半截，有一颗微微过大的、像略微绽开的子弹头似的脑袋，还有两条扭动的小手臂或什么附肢——而且摸上去，它对一条鱼、甚至一只蝌蚪来说，臀部未免太丰满了。给一个两个月大的人类胚胎装上带鳍的尾巴，再添上一分早熟的女性性感，你就能得到差不多的效果。
可这些都不算什么。麻烦在于它有一张脸——当然是一张小小的脸，而且相当鼓凸虚幻，像涡虫的脸，但终究是一张脸，一张人脸模样的脸，而且（真正的麻烦在这儿）这张脸以一种怪诞却醒目的方式，酷似格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇。
西蒙的手指痉挛般收紧。同时，那滑溜的生物拼命一扭。它划出一道高高的弧线射向空中，落进浴缸和墙壁之间那不到一英寸的缝隙里。
接下来的半小时是一场趴地式的忙乱。要从西蒙那只老式兽爪浴缸后面取回任何东西，都是万分艰难的壮举。缸下勉强容得下一条手臂，有一处地板的翘曲连这个都容不下。何况还有一大堆罩满灰尘的物件，以前都嫌麻烦没去掏——几十年的积攒。起初西蒙还想凭墙根隐约的扑腾声指引自己，可那声音很快就停了。
跪着趴着、耳朵贴地、一条胳膊吃力地伸直，这种姿势大概不是让怪念头的列车在脑中鸣笛疾驰时的最佳选择，可有时偏偏就这样。

===Original===

First came a remembered piece of neighborhood lore that supported the possibility of a connection between the house next door and the tiny pink aquatic creature now suffering minute agonies behind the bathtub. No one knew what ancient and probably larceny-minded amateur plumber was responsible, but the old-timers assured Simon there was a link between the water supply of the Russians’ house with its aerial cistern and that of the building containing Simon’s studio and several smaller apartments; at any rate they maintained that there had been a time during the period when the bootlegger was storing hooch in the water tank that several neighborhood cold-water taps were dispensing a weak but nonetheless authoritative mixture of bourbon and branch water.
So, thought Simon as he groped and strained, if the Russians were somehow responsible for this weird fishlet, there was no insuperable difficulty in understanding how it might have gotten here.
But that was the least of Simon’s preoccupations. He scrabbled wildly and unsuccessfully for several minutes, and then realizing he would never get anywhere in this unsystematic manner, he began to remove the accumulated debris piece by piece: dark cracked ends of soap, washrags dried out in tortured attitudes, innumerable dark-dyed cigarette stumps, several pocket magazines with bleached wrinkled pages, empty and near-empty medicine bottles and pill vials, rusty hairpins, bobby pins, safety pins, crumpled toothpaste tubes (and a couple for oil paint), a gray toothbrush, a fifty-cent piece and several pennies, the mummy of a mouse, a letter from Picasso, and last of all, from the dark corner behind the bathtub’s inside claw, the limp pitiful thing he was seeking.
It was even tinier than he’d thought. He carefully washed the dust and flug off it, but it was clearly dead and its resemblance to Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich had become problematical﻿—indeed, Simon decided that someone seeing it now for the first time would think it a freak minnow or monstrous tadpole and nothing more, though mutation or disease had obviously been at work. The illusion of a miniature mermaid still existed in the tapering tail and armlike appendages, but it was faint. He tried to remember what he knew about salamanders﻿—almost nothing, it turned out. He thought of embryos, but his mind veered away from the subject.

===Chinese===

首先浮起的是一段记得的街坊掌故，它支持了隔壁房子与这只此刻正在浴缸后头受着微型酷刑的粉色小水生生物之间有关联的可能性。没人知道那位古老的、多半心怀盗窃的业余管道工是谁，但老街坊们向西蒙保证，俄国人那座房子连同它的空中水柜，与西蒙画室所在的这栋楼以及几间小公寓，供水是相通的；至少他们坚称，私酒贩子往水箱里存酒的那阵子，附近好几处冷水龙头放出来过一种淡薄的、却不容置疑的波本加自来水的混合物。
于是西蒙一边摸索一边用力想，如果这条怪鱼苗是俄国人弄出来的，那么理解它怎么到这儿来的，并没有什么不可逾越的困难。
可这在他的心事里排位最末。他胡乱抓了几分钟一无所获，随即意识到这样没个章法永远没戏，便开始把积年的垃圾一件件清出来：发黑开裂的肥皂头，拧成痛苦姿势的干抹布，数不清的深色烟头，几本漂白起皱的口袋杂志，空了和快空了的药瓶药管，生锈的发夹、发卡、别针，挤瘪的牙膏皮（还有两管油彩的），一支灰色牙刷，一枚五角硬币和几枚分币，一只老鼠的木乃伊，一封毕加索的来信，最后，从浴缸内侧兽爪后面的暗角里，他找到了那件软塌塌的可怜东西。
它比他想的还要小。他仔细洗去上面的灰尘和绒毛，可它显然死了，它与格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇的相像已经成疑——确实，西蒙断定，此刻头一回见到它的人只会当它是个畸形的米诺鱼或怪物蝌蚪，仅此而已，尽管变异或疾病显然在它身上下过功夫。微型美人鱼的幻象仍存留在那渐细的尾巴和臂状附肢里，但已经很淡了。他试图回忆自己知道的关于蝾螈的知识——结果是几乎一无所知。他想到胚胎，可他的心思从这题目上拐开了。

===Original===

He wandered back into the studio carrying the thing in his hand. He climbed the stepladder by the north window and studied the house next door. What windows he could see were dark. He got a very vague impression that the roof had changed. After he had strained his eyes for some time he fancied he could see a faint path of greenish luminescence streaming between the pent-shack and the water-tank, but it was very faint indeed and might only be his vision swimming.
He climbed down the stepladder and stood for a moment weighing the tiny dead thing in his hand. It occurred to him that one of his friends at the university could dig up a zoologist to pass on his find.
But Simon’s curiosity was more artistic than scientific. In the end he twisted a bit of cellophane around the thing, placed it on the ledge of his easel and went off to bed﻿ ﻿… and to a series of disturbingly erotic dreams.
---
Next day he got up late and, after breakfasting on black coffee, gloomed around the studio for a while, picking things up and putting them down. He glanced frequently at the stepladder, but resisted the temptation to climb up and have another look next door. Sighing, he thumbtacked a sheet of paper to a drawing board and half-heartedly began blocking in a female figure. It was insipid and lifeless. Stabbing irritably at the heavy curve of the figure’s hip, he broke his charcoal. “Damn!” he said, glaring around the room. Abandoning all pretense, he threw the charcoal on the floor and climbed the stepladder. He pressed his nose against the glass.
In daylight, the adjoining roof looked bare and grimy. There was a big transparent pipe running between the water tank and the shack, braced in two places by improvised-looking wooden scaffolding. Listening intently, Simon thought he could hear a motor going in the shack. The water looked sallow green. It reminded Simon of those futuristic algae farms where the stuff is supposed to be pumped through transparent pipes to expose it to sunlight. There seemed to be a transparent top on the water tank too﻿—it was too high for Simon to see, but there was a gleam around the edge. Staring at the pipe again, Simon got the impression there were little things traveling in the water, but he couldn’t make them out.
Climbing down in some excitement, Simon got the twist of cellophane from the ledge of the easel and stared at its contents.

===Chinese===

他手里攥着那东西晃回画室。他爬上北窗边的折梯，端详隔壁的房子。能看见的窗户都黑着。他得到一个非常模糊的印象：屋顶变了样。他眯眼使劲看了半天，才觉得自己隐约看见一道淡绿色的微光在坡顶棚与水箱之间流淌，但实在很淡，也许只是他眼花。
他爬下折梯，站了片刻，掂着手心里那小小的死物。他想起他在大学里的朋友可以给他找个动物学家来鉴定这个发现。
可西蒙的好奇心与其说科学，不如说艺术。最后他扯了一小片玻璃纸把那东西包上，放在画架的搁板上，便上床去了……接着做了一串扰人的春梦。
---
第二天他起晚了，灌下一杯黑咖啡当早餐，在画室里郁郁地转了一阵，拿起这个放下那个。他频频瞟向折梯，又忍住了爬上去再看一眼隔壁的诱惑。叹了口气，他把一张纸钉到画板上，有气无力地开始勾一个女性人体的轮廓。画得寡淡无味，死气沉沉。烦躁地戳向人体那道沉重的臀部曲线时，他的炭条断了。“该死！”他说，环视房间。他索性连装都不装了，把炭条扔到地上，爬上折梯，鼻子贴上玻璃。
白天里，隔壁的屋顶显得光秃而肮脏。一根粗大的透明管子从水箱通到小棚，两处用看着像是临时凑合的木支架撑着。西蒙凝神听去，觉得能听见小棚里有一台马达在响。水是病黄的绿色。它让西蒙想起那些未来主义的海藻农场——据说那里的东西被泵过透明管道，好接受日晒。水箱上似乎也有个透明的顶——太高了，西蒙看不见，但边缘有一圈反光。再盯着管子看，西蒙得到一个印象：水里有些小东西在游动，可他辨不清。
西蒙按捺着兴奋爬下梯子，从画架搁板上取来那包玻璃纸，盯着里面的东西。

===Original===

Wild thoughts were tumbling through his head as he got back up on the stepladder. Sunlight flashed on the greenish water pipe between the tank and the shack, but after the first glance he had no eyes for it. Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich had her face tragically pressed to the window of the shack. She was wearing the black dress with high neck and puffed shoulders. At that moment she looked straight at him. She lifted her hands and seemed to speak imploringly. Then she slowly sank from sight as if, it horridly occurred to Simon, into quicksand.
Simon sprang from his chair, heart beating wildly, and ran down the stairs to the street. Two or three passersby paused to study him as he alternately pounded the flaking green door of the Russians’ house and leaned on the button. Also watching was the shirt-sleeved driver of a moving van, emblazoned “Stulnikov-Gurevich Enterprises,” which almost filled the street in front of the house.
The door opened narrowly. A man with a square black beard frowned out of it. He topped Simon by almost a head.
“Yes?” Ivan the Bomber asked, in a deep, exasperated voice.
“I must see the lady of the house immediately,” Simon cried. “Your sister, I believe. She’s in danger.” He surged forward.
The butt of the Bomber’s right palm took him firmly in the chest and he staggered back. The Bomber said coldly, “My sister is﻿—ha!﻿—taking a bath.”
Simon cried, “In that case she’s drowning!” and surged forward again, but the Bomber’s hand stopped him short. “I’ll call the police!” Simon shouted, flailing his limbs. The hand at his chest suddenly stopped pushing and began to pull. Gripped by the front of his shirt, Simon felt himself being drawn rapidly inside. “Let go! Help, a kidnapping!” he shouted to the inquisitive faces outside, before the door banged shut.
“No police!” rumbled the Bomber, assisting Simon upstairs.
“Now look here,” Simon protested futilely. In the two-story-high living room to his right, the pipes of an organ gleamed golden from the shadows. At the second landing, a disheveled figure met them, glasses twinkling﻿—Vasily the Vodka Breather. He spoke querulously in Russian to Ivan, who replied shortly, then Vasily turned and the three of them crowded up the narrow third flight to the pent-shack.

===Chinese===

重新爬上折梯时，疯狂的念头在他脑子里翻腾。阳光在水箱与小棚之间的绿水管上闪烁，可他扫了一眼之后就再没看它。格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇正把她的脸悲怆地贴在小棚的窗上。她穿着那件高领泡泡肩的黑裙。就在那一刻，她直直地看向他。她举起双手，似乎在恳求着什么。随后她缓缓沉出视野，仿佛——西蒙骇然想到——沉入流沙。
西蒙从椅子上弹起来，心狂跳着，跑下楼梯冲到街上。他一边捶俄国人房子那扇剥落的绿门，一边死按门铃，两三个过路人停下来打量他。同样看着他的是一辆厢式货车的司机，只穿衬衫，车身漆着“斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇企业”，几乎塞满了房子前面的街道。
门开了一条缝。一个方黑胡子男人从里面皱眉望出来。他比西蒙高出几乎一个头。
“何事？”炸弹客伊万用深沉而恼怒的声音问。
“我必须立刻见这家的女主人，”西蒙喊道。“我想是你妹妹。她有危险。”他往前冲。
炸弹客右掌的掌根结结实实抵住他的胸口，他踉跄后退。炸弹客冷冷地说：“我妹妹正在——哈！——洗澡。”
西蒙喊：“那她就是在淹死！”又往前冲，可炸弹客的手牢牢挡住他。“我要叫警察了！”西蒙挥舞着四肢喊。胸口那只手忽然不推了，改成拉。西蒙被揪住衬衫前襟，觉得自己被飞快地拽进屋内。“放开！救命，绑架啦！”他朝外面那些好奇的脸喊，门随即砰地关上。
“不许叫警察！”炸弹客低吼着，把西蒙押上楼。
“你听我说，”西蒙徒劳地抗议。右边两层通高的客厅里，一架管风琴的管子从阴影中泛出金光。到了二楼梯台，一个衣冠不整的身影迎上来，眼镜闪闪——伏特加呼吸者瓦西里。他用俄语冲伊万抱怨地说话，伊万简短地回了几句，然后瓦西里转过身，三个人挤上狭窄的第三段楼梯，上了坡顶棚。

===Original===

This housed a small noisy machine, perhaps an aerator of some sort, for bubbles were streaming into the transparent pipe where it was connected to the machine; and under the pipe, sitting with an idiot smile on a chair of red plush and gilt, was a pale black-mustached man. An empty clear-glass bottle with a red and gold label lay on the floor at his feet. The opposite side of the room was hidden by a heavy plastic shower curtain. Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich was not in view.
Ivan said something explosive, picking up the bottle and staring at it. “Vodka!” he went on. “I have told you not to mix the pipe and the vodka! Now see what you have done!”
“To me it seemed hospitable,” said Vasily with an apologetic gesture. “Besides, only one bottle﻿—”
Ducking under the pipe where it crossed the pent-shack, Ivan picked up the pale man and dumped him crosswise in the chair, with his patent-leather shoes sticking up on one side and his plump hands crossed over his chest. “Let him sleep. First we must take down all the apparatus, before the capitalistic police arrive. Now: what to do with this one?” He looked at Simon, and clenched one large and hairy fist.
“Nyet-nyet-nyet,” said the Vodka Breather, and went to whisper in Ivan’s ear. They both stared at Simon, who felt uncomfortable and began to back toward the door; but Ivan ducked agilely under the pipe and grasped him by the arm, pulling him effortlessly toward the roof exit. “Just come this way if you please, Mr. Gru-*ay*,” said Vasily, hurrying after. As they left the shack, he picked up a kitchen chair.
Crossing the roof, Simon made a sudden effort and wrenched himself free. They caught him again at the edge of the roof, where he had run with nothing clearly in mind, but with his mouth open to yell. Suspended in the grip of the two Russians, with Ivan’s meaty palm over his mouth, Simon had a momentary glimpse of the street below. A third bearded figure, Mikhail the Religious, was staring up at them from the sunny sidewalk. The melancholy face, the deep-socketed tormented eyes, and the narrow beard tangled with the dangling crucifix combined to give the effect of a Tolstoy novel’s dust-jacket. As they hauled Simon away, he had the impression that a chilly breeze had sprung up and the street had darkened. In his ears was Mikhail’s distant, oddly discordant whistling.

===Chinese===

棚子里装着一台吵闹的小机器，大概是某种充氧机，气泡正汩汩涌进机器连接的透明管子里；管子底下，一个苍白的黑胡子男人坐在一把红绒镀金的椅子上，脸上挂着白痴的微笑。他脚边的地板上躺着一只空的透明玻璃瓶，红金标签。房间的另一侧被一幅厚塑料浴帘挡住。格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇不见踪影。
伊万说了句什么爆豆般的话，捡起瓶子盯着看。“伏特加！”他接着说。“我告诉过你别把管子和伏特加掺和！现在看看你干了什么！”
“我觉得这是待客之道，”瓦西里做了个抱歉的手势说。“再说，就一瓶——”
伊万从管子下面钻过去，抄起那苍白的男人，横着堆进椅子里，漆皮鞋翘在一边，胖胖的双手交叠在胸前。“让他睡。我们首先得把全部器材拆下来，趁资本主义警察还没到。现在：这个怎么处置？”他看着西蒙，攥起一只毛茸茸的大拳头。
“不-不-不，”伏特加呼吸者说，凑到伊万耳边嘀咕了几句。两人都盯着西蒙，西蒙浑身不自在，开始朝门口退；可伊万灵巧地从管下钻过，一把攥住他的胳膊，毫不费力地把他拽向屋顶出口。“那就请您这边来，格吕-埃先生，”瓦西里说着，赶紧跟上。出棚子时，他顺手拎了一把厨房椅。
穿过屋顶时，西蒙猛一使劲挣脱了。他们在屋顶边缘又逮住他——他跑向那里时脑中并无明确打算，只是张嘴要喊。悬在两个俄国人的钳制中，嘴被伊万厚实的巴掌捂着，西蒙瞥了一眼楼下的街道。第三个留胡子的人影，宗教徒米哈伊尔，正从洒满阳光的人行道上仰望着他们。那张忧郁的脸、深陷而饱受折磨的眼睛，还有与悬垂十字架缠在一起的窄胡子，合起来造成一种托尔斯泰小说护封的效果。他们把西蒙拖走时，他感觉一阵寒风乍起，街道暗了下来。他耳边是米哈伊尔遥远的、怪得不和谐的哨音。

Grunting, the two brothers set Simon down on the kitchen chair and slid him across the roof until something hard but resilient touched the top of his head. It was the plastic pipe, through which, peering upward, he could see myriads of tiny polliwog-shapes flitting back and forth.
“Do us a kindness not to make noise,” said Ivan, removing his palm. “My brother Vasily will now explain.” He went away.
---
Curiosity as much as shock kept Simon in his chair. Vasily, bobbing his head and smiling, sat down tailor-fashion on the roof in front of him. “First I must tell you, Mr. Gru-*ay*, that I am specialist in biological sciences. Here you see results of my most successful experiment.” He withdrew a round clear-glass bottle from his pocket and unscrewed the top.
“Ah?” said Simon tentatively.
“Indeed yes. In my researches, Mr. Gru-*ay*, I discovered a chemical which will inhibit growth at any level of embryonic development, producing a viable organism at that point. The basic effect of this chemical is always toward survival at whatever level of development﻿—one cell, a blastula, a worm, a fish, a four-legger. This research, which Lysenko scoffed at when I told him of it, I had no trouble in keeping secret, though at the time I was working as the unhappy collaborator of the godless soviets. But perhaps I am being too technical?”
“Not at all,” Simon assured him.
“Good,” Vasily said with simple satisfaction and gulped at his bottle. “Meanwhile my brother Mikhail was a religious brother at a monastery near Mount Athos, my Nihilist brother Ivan was in central Europe, while my third brother Lev, who is of commercial talents, had preceded us to the New World, where we always felt it would some day be our destiny to join one another.
“With the aid of brother Ivan, I and my sister Grushenka escaped from Russia. We picked up Mikhail from his monastery and proceeded here, where Lev had become a capitalist business magnate.
“My brothers, Ivan especially, were interested in my research. He had a theory that we could eventually produce hosts of men in this way, whole armies and political parties, all Nihilist and all of them Stulnikov-Gureviches.

两兄弟哼哧着把西蒙按坐在厨房椅上，推着椅子滑过屋顶，直到一个硬而有弹性的东西碰到他的头顶。那是塑料管，仰头看去，他能看见无数蝌蚪状的小东西在管里来回穿梭。
“劳驾别出声，”伊万拿开巴掌说。“我兄弟瓦西里现在解释。”他走开了。
---
与其说是震惊，不如说是好奇把西蒙钉在椅子上。瓦西里点着头微笑着，在他面前盘腿坐上屋顶。“首先我必须告诉您，格吕-埃先生，我是生物科学专家。您在这儿看到的是我最成功的实验的成果。”他从口袋里掏出一只圆形透明玻璃瓶，拧开盖子。
“啊？”西蒙试探地说。
“可不是。在我的研究中，格吕-埃先生，我发现了一种化学药剂，它能在胚胎发育的任意阶段抑制生长，产出停留在那个阶段的可存活生物体。这药剂的基本效应永远是让生物在无论哪个发育阶段存活——一个细胞、一个囊胚、一条虫、一条鱼、一只四条腿的。这项研究，李森科听我讲起时嗤之以鼻，我藏着它却毫不费事，虽然那时我在给不信神的苏维埃当不情愿的合作者。不过我也许讲得太专业了？”
“一点也不，”西蒙向他保证。
“好，”瓦西里带着单纯的满足说，仰脖灌了一口瓶子里的。“与此同时，我兄弟米哈伊尔在阿索斯山附近一座修道院当修士，我那虚无主义兄弟伊万在中欧，我第三个兄弟列夫，有经商的天分，先我们一步到了新大陆——我们一向觉得，彼此终有一天会团聚。
“在伊万兄弟的帮助下，我和妹妹格鲁申卡逃出了俄国。我们顺路接上米哈伊尔出了修道院，来到这里，列夫已经成了资本家大亨。
“我的兄弟们，尤其是伊万，对我的研究很感兴趣。他有个理论，说我们最终能用这法子造出大批的人，整支整支的军队和政党，全都是虚无主义者，全都是斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇。

I assured him that this was impossible, that I could not play Cadmus, for free-swimming forms are one thing, we have the way to feed them in the aqueous medium; but to make fully developed mammals placental nourishment is necessary﻿—that I cannot provide. Yet to please him I begin with (pardon me!) the egg of my sister, that was as good a beginning as any and perhaps it intrigued my vanity. Ivan dreamed his dreams of a Nihilist Stulnikov-Gurevich humanity﻿—it was harmless, as I told myself.”
