Simon stared at him glassy-eyed. Something rather peculiar was beginning to happen inside his head﻿—about an inch under the point where the cool water-filled plastic pipe pressed down on his scalp. Little ghostly images were darting﻿—delightfully wispy little girl-things, smiling down at him impudently, then flirting away with a quick motion of their mermaid tails.
The sky had been growing steadily darker and now there came the growl of thunder. Against the purple-gray clouds Simon could barely make out the semitransparent shapes of the polliwogs in the pipe over his head; but the images inside his mind were growing clearer by the minute.
“Ah, we have a storm,” Vasily observed as the thunder growled again. “That reminds me of Mikhail, who is much influenced by our Finnish grandmother. He had the belief as a child that he could call up the winds by whistling for them﻿—he even learned special wind musics from her. Later he became a Christian religious﻿—there are great struggles in him. Mikhail objected to my researches when he heard I used the egg of my sister. He said we will produce millions of souls who are not baptized. I asked him how about the water they are in, he replied this is not the same thing, these little swimmers will wriggle in hell eternally. This worried him greatly. We tried to tell him I had not used the egg of my sister, only the egg of a fish.
“But he did not believe this, because my sister changed greatly at the time. She no longer spoke. She put on my mother’s bathing costume (we are a family people) and retired to the bathtub all day long. I accepted this﻿—at least in the water she is not violent. Mikhail said, ‘See, her soul is now split into many unredeemed sub-souls, one each for the little swimmers. There is a sympathy between them﻿—a hypnotic vibration. So long as you keep them near her, in that tank on the roof, this will be.

===Chinese===

我向他保证这不可能，我当不了卡德摩斯，因为自由游泳的形态是一回事，我们有办法在水介质里喂养它们；可要造完全发育的哺乳动物，胎盘营养必不可少——这个我提供不了。不过为了让他高兴，我从（恕我直言！）我妹妹的卵子开始，那是个不坏的开头，也许还满足了点我的虚荣心。伊万做着他那虚无主义的斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇人类的梦——无害的，我这样告诉自己。”

西蒙目光呆滞地盯着他。他脑袋里开始发生一件相当特别的事——就在凉水充盈的塑料管压着他头皮的地方往下约一英寸。小小的幽灵影像在穿梭——一群妙不可言的、雾一样的小小少女，无礼地朝他微笑，然后美人鱼尾巴利落一甩，调情般游走。
天色一直稳定地变暗，这时传来雷的低吼。衬着紫灰色的云，西蒙勉强能辨出头顶管中蝌蚪的半透明影子；可他脑子里的影像却一分钟比一分钟清晰。
“啊，要来暴风雨了，”瓦西里在雷声又响时评论道。“这让我想起米哈伊尔，他深受我们芬兰祖母的影响。他小时候相信，只要吹口哨就能把风召来——他甚至从祖母那儿学了专门的召风曲。后来他成了基督教修士——他内心斗争很激烈。米哈伊尔听说我用了我妹妹的卵子，反对我的研究。他说我们要造出千百万个没受过洗的灵魂。我问他那它们所在的水怎么说，他回答说这不一样，这些小游泳家将在地狱里永远扭动。这让他大为忧心。我们试着告诉他，我没用我妹妹的卵子，只是鱼的卵。
“可他不信，因为我妹妹当时大变。她不再说话。她穿上我母亲的泳衣（我们是重家庭的家族），成天退居浴缸。我接受了——至少在水里她不闹。米哈伊尔说：‘看，她的灵魂如今分裂成许多未得救赎的次灵魂，每条小游泳家一个。它们之间有一种感应——一种催眠的振动。只要你把它们放在她近旁，放在屋顶那只水箱里，就相安无事。

===Original===

If they were gone from there, far from there, the sub-souls would reunite and Grushenka’s soul would be one again.’ He begged me to stop my research, to dump it in the sea, to scatter it away, but Lev and Ivan demand I keep on. Yet Mikhail warned me that works of evil end in the whirlwind. I am torn and undecided.” He gulped at his vodka.
Thunder growled louder. Simon was thinking, dreamily, that if the soul of Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich were split into thousands of sub-souls, vibrating hypnotically in the nearby water tank, with at least one of them escaping as far as his bathtub, then it was no wonder if Grushenka had a strange attraction for him.
“But that is not yet the worst,” Vasily continued. “The hypnotic vibrations of the free-swimming ones in their multitude turn out to have a stimulating effect on any male who is near. Their sub-minds induce dreams of the piquant sort. Lev says that to make money for the work we must sell these dreams to rich men. I protest, but to no avail.”
“Lev is maddened for money. Now besides selling the dreams I find he plans to sell the creatures themselves, sell them one by one, but keep enough to sell the dreams too. It is a madness.”
The darkness had become that of night. The thunder continued to growl and now it seemed to Simon that it had music in it. Visions swam through his mind to its rhythm﻿—hordes of swimming pygmy souls, of unborn water babies, migrations of miniature mermaids. The pipe hanging between water tank and pent-shack became in his imagination a giant umbilicus or a canal for a monstrous multiple birth. Sitting beneath it, helpless to move, he focused his attention with increasing pleasure on the active, supple, ever more human girl-bodies that swam across his mind. Now more mermaid than tadpole, with bright smiling lips and eyes, long Lorelei-hair trailing behind them, they darted and hovered caressingly. In their wide-cheeked oval faces, he discovered without shock, there was a transcendent resemblance to the features of Grushenka Stulnikov-Gurevich﻿—a younger, milk-skinned maiden of the steppes, with challenging eyes and fingers that brushed against him with delightful shocks﻿ ﻿…
“So it is for me the great problem,” Vasily’s distant voice continued. “I see in my work only the pure research, the play of the mind.

一旦它们离开那里，离得远远的，次灵魂就会重新聚合，格鲁申卡的灵魂将复归为一。’他求我停止研究，把它倒进大海，撒向四方，可列夫和伊万要求我继续。但米哈伊尔警告我，恶业终归于旋风。我左右为难，拿不定主意。”他灌了口伏特加。
雷声更响了。西蒙悠悠地想，如果格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇的灵魂分裂成千万个次灵魂，在近旁的水箱里催眠般振动，其中至少一个一直逃到他浴缸里那么远，那么格鲁申卡对他有一股奇异的吸引力，也就不足为怪了。
“可这还不是最糟的，”瓦西里继续道。“那些自由游泳的、数目众多的家伙，它们的催眠振动对附近的任何男性都有刺激效果。它们的次心智引出那种香艳的梦。列夫说，为了给这项工作搞钱，我们必须把这些梦卖给富人。我抗议，但没用。
“列夫为钱疯了。如今除了卖梦，我发现他还打算卖那些生物本身，一只一只地卖，但留够数目好继续卖梦。这是疯狂。”
黑暗已如夜。雷声持续低吼，此刻西蒙觉得雷里有音乐。幻象随着它的节奏在他脑中游动——成群游泳的侏儒灵魂，成群未出生的水婴，迁徙的微型美人鱼。悬在水箱与坡顶棚之间的管子，在他想象中变成一条巨大的脐带，或一条供一场怪异的多胞胎分娩用的运河。坐在管下动弹不得的他，把注意力带着越来越浓的愉悦，聚焦在那些活泼、柔韧、越来越具人形的少女身体上，她们游过他的脑海。此刻她们更像美人鱼而非蝌蚪了，有着明亮的笑唇和眼睛，罗蕾莱般的长发拖在身后，飞窜与悬停都带着爱抚的意味。在她们宽颊的鹅蛋脸上，他毫不惊愕地发现，有一种超凡的与格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇五官的相似——一位更年轻的、牛乳肌肤的草原少女，眼睛带着挑战，指尖擦过他，带着酥麻的电击……
“所以这对我来说是大难题，”瓦西里遥远的声音继续着。“我在我的工作里只看到纯粹的研究，心智的游戏。

===Original===

Lev sees money, Ivan sees dragon teeth﻿—fodder for his political cannon﻿—Mikhail sees unshriven souls, Grushenka sees﻿—who knows?﻿—madness. It is indeed one great problem.”
---
Thunder came again, crashingly this time. The door of the pent-shack opened. Framed in it stood Ivan the Bomber. “Vasily!” he roared. “Do you know what that idiot is doing now?”
As the thunder and his voice trailed off together, Simon became aware at last of the identity of the other sound, which had been growing in volume all the time.
Simultaneously Vasily struggled to his feet.
“The organ!” he cried. “Mikhail is *playing* the Whirlwind Music! We must stop him!” Pausing only for a last pull at the bottle, he charged into the pent-shack, following Ivan.
Wind was shaking the heavy pipe over Simon’s head, tossing him back and forth in the chair. Looking with an effort toward the west, Simon saw the reason: a spinning black pencil of wind that was writing its way toward them in wreckage across the intervening roofs.
The chair fell under him. Stumbling across the roof, he tugged futilely at the door to the pent-shack, then threw himself flat, clawing at the tarpaper.
There was a mounting roar. The top of the water tank went spinning off like a flying saucer. Momentarily, as if it were a giant syringe, the whirlwind dipped into the tank. Simon felt himself sliding across the roof, felt his legs lifting. He fetched up against the roof’s low wall and at that moment the wind let go of him and his legs touched tarpaper again.
Gaining his feet numbly, Simon staggered into the leaning pent-shack. The pale man was nowhere to be seen, the plush chair empty. The curtain at the other side of the room had fallen with its rods, revealing a bathtub more antique than Simon’s. In the tub, under the window, sat Grushenka. The lightning flares showed her with her chin level with the water, her eyes placidly staring, her mouth opening and closing.
Simon found himself putting his arms around the black-clad figure. With a straining effort he lifted her out of the tub, water sloshing all over his legs, and half carried, half slid with her down the stairs.
He fetched up panting and disheveled at the top landing, his attention riveted by the lightning-illuminated scene in the two-story-high living room below.

===Chinese===

列夫看到钱，伊万看到龙牙——给他政治大炮当粮草——米哈伊尔看到未忏悔的灵魂，格鲁申卡看到——谁知道呢？——疯狂。这实在是天大的难题。”
---
雷声又起，这回是炸响。坡顶棚的门开了。炸弹客伊万站在门框里。“瓦西里！”他吼道。“你知道那个白痴现在在干什么吗？”
雷声和他的吼声一同消散，西蒙终于辨认出那另一个一直在增强的声响是什么。
同时瓦西里挣扎着爬起来。
“管风琴！”他喊。“米哈伊尔在弹旋风曲！我们必须阻止他！”他只在瓶子上最后吸了一大口，便冲进坡顶棚，跟上伊万。
风摇晃着西蒙头顶的粗管，把他在椅子里颠来颠去。费力朝西望去，西蒙看清了缘由：一支旋转的黑色风之铅笔，正一路碾过间隔的屋顶、朝着他们书写而来。
椅子在他身下翻倒。他跌跌撞撞横穿屋顶，徒劳地拽坡顶棚的门，随即扑倒在地，抠住沥青纸。
咆哮声越来越大。水箱的顶像飞碟一样旋飞而去。有一瞬，旋风像一支巨大的注射器扎进水箱。西蒙觉得自己在屋顶上滑动，觉得双腿被掀起。他撞上屋顶的矮墙，就在那一刻，风松开了他，他的腿重新触到沥青纸。
西蒙麻木地站起身，踉跄着钻进倾斜的坡顶棚。那苍白的男人不见了，绒椅空着。房间另一侧的浴帘连同帘杆一起倒了，露出一比西蒙那只更古老的浴缸。格鲁申卡坐在缸里，坐在窗下。闪电的光照见她下巴与水面齐平，眼神平静地凝望，嘴一开一合。
西蒙发觉自己张开双臂抱住那个黑衣身影。他奋力把她从缸里抱出来，水浇了他两腿，他半抱半滑地拖着她下了楼。
他气喘吁吁、狼狈不堪地摔在二楼梯台顶，注意力被楼下两层通高客厅里闪电照亮的一幕牢牢吸住。

===Original===

At the far end of it a dark-robed figure crouched at the console of the mighty organ, like a giant bat at the base of the portico of a black and gold temple. In the center of the room Ivan was in the act of heaving above his head his globular leather case.
Mikhail darted a look over his shoulder and sprang to one side. The projectile crashed against the organ. Mikhail picked himself up, tearing something from his neck. Ivan lunged forward with a roar. Mikhail crashed a fist against his jaw. The Bomber went down and didn’t come up. Mikhail unwrapped his crucifix from his fingers and resumed playing.
With a wild cry Simon heaved himself to his feet, stumbled over Grushenka’s sodden garments, and pitched headlong down the stairs.
When he came to, the house was empty and the Stulnikov moving van was gone. At the front door he was met by a poker-faced young man who identified himself as a member of the F.B.I. Simon showed him the globular case Ivan had thrown at the organ. It proved to contain a bowling ball.
The young gentleman listened to his story without changing expression, thanked him warmly, and shooed him out.
The Stulnikov-Gureviches disappeared for good, though not quite without a trace. Simon found this item in the next evening’s paper, the first of many he accumulated yearningly in a scrapbook during the following months:

> *Milford, Pa.*﻿—The “mermaid rain” reported here has been declared a fraud by an eminent European biologist. Vasily Stulnikov-Gurevich, formerly Professor of Genetics at Pire University, Latvia, passing through here on a cross-country trip, declared the miniature “mermaids” were “albino tadpoles, probably scattered about as a hoax by schoolboys.”
> The professor added, “I would like to know where they got them, however. There is clear evidence of mutation, due perhaps to fallout.”
> Dr. Stulnikov directed his party in a brief but intensive search for overlooked specimens. His charming silent sister, Grushenka Stulnikov, wearing a quaint Latvian swimming costume, explored the shallows of the Delaware.
> After collecting as many specimens as possible, the professor and his assistants continued their trip in their unusual camping car. Dr. Stulnikov intends to found a biological research center “in the calm and tolerant atmosphere of the West Coast,” he declared.

===Chinese===

客厅尽头，一个深色袍服的身影伏在雄伟管风琴的琴台上，像黑金神庙门廊底下的一只巨蝠。房间中央，伊万正把他的球形皮箱抡过头顶。
米哈伊尔回头瞥了一眼，闪身一让。那枚投射物砸在管风琴上。米哈伊尔爬起来，从脖子上扯下什么东西。伊万吼着扑上前来。米哈伊尔一拳砸在他下颌上。炸弹客倒了下去，再没起来。米哈伊尔把十字架从手指上解下来，继续弹琴。
西蒙狂喊着撑起身子，绊在格鲁申卡湿透的衣服上，一头栽下楼梯。
等他醒来，房子已经空了，斯图利尼科夫家的厢式货车也不见了。在前门，一位面无表情的年轻人迎上他，自称联邦调查局成员。西蒙把伊万砸向管风琴的那只球形箱子给他看。打开一看，里面是一只保龄球。
年轻的先生听着他的讲述，面不改色，热情地道了谢，把他打发走了。
斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇一家从此消失得无影无踪——不过并非全无痕迹。西蒙在第二天傍晚的报纸上找到这条新闻，此后几个月里，他又如饥似渴地攒了许多条，贴进一本剪贴簿：

> 宾夕法尼亚州米尔福德——此地报告的“美人鱼雨”已被一位著名欧洲生物学家宣布为骗局。拉脱维亚皮雷大学前遗传学教授瓦西里·斯图利尼科夫-古列维奇在横穿全国的旅行途经此地时，宣称这些微型“美人鱼”是“白化蝌蚪，多半是男学生们的恶作剧，撒得到处都是”。
> 教授补充道：“不过我倒想知道他们从哪儿弄来的。有明显的变异迹象，也许要归因于放射性尘埃。”
> 斯图利尼科夫博士指挥他的队伍对被遗漏的标本进行了短暂而密集的搜索。他那位迷人的沉默的妹妹格鲁申卡·斯图利尼科夫，穿着一件古色古香的拉脱维亚泳衣，探索了特拉华河的浅滩。
> 收集了尽可能多的标本之后，教授和他的助手们乘坐他们那辆与众不同的野营车继续赶路。斯图利尼科夫博士宣布，他打算“在西海岸平静而宽容的氛围中”建立一所生物研究中心。

## The Big Engine / 大引擎
===Original===

There are all sorts of screwy theories (the Professor said) of what makes the wheels of the world go round. There’s a boy in Chicago who thinks we’re all of us just the thoughts of a green cat; when the green cat dies we’ll all puff to nothing like smoke. There’s a man in the west who thinks all women are witches and run the world by conjure magic. There’s a man in the east who believes all rich people belong to a secret society that’s a lot tighter and tougher than the Mafia and that has a monopoly of power-secrets and pleasure-secrets other people don’t dream exist.
Me, I think the wheels of the world just go. I decided that forty years ago and I’ve never since seen or heard or read anything to make me change my mind.
I was a stoker on a lake boat then (the Professor continued, delicately sipping smoke from his long thin cigarette). I was as stupid as they make them, but I liked to think. Whenever I’d get a chance I’d go to one of the big libraries and make them get me all sorts of books. That was how guys started calling me the Professor. I’d get books on philosophy, metaphysics, science, even religion. I’d read them and try to figure out the world. What was it all about, anyway? Why was I here? What was the point in the whole business of getting born and working and dying? What was the use of it? Why’d it have to go on and on?
And why’d it have to be so complicated?
Why all the building and tearing down? Why’d there have to be cities, with crowded streets and horse cars and cable cars and electric cars and big openwork steel boxes built to the sky to be hung with stone and wood﻿—my closest friend got killed falling off one of those steel boxkites. Shouldn’t there be some simpler way of doing it all? Why did things have to be so mixed up that a man like myself couldn’t have a single clear decent thought?
More than that, why weren’t people a real part of the world? Why didn’t they show more honest-to-God response? When you slept with a woman, why was it something you had and she didn’t? Why, when you went to a prize fight, were the bruisers only so much meat, and the crowd a lot of little screaming popinjays? Why was a war nothing but blather and blowup and bother? Why’d everybody have to go through their whole lives so dead, doing everything so methodical and prissy like a Sunday School picnic or an orphan’s parade?

===Chinese===

关于是什么让世界的轮子转动，有各式各样的歪理论（教授说）。芝加哥有个小子认为，我们全都不过是一只绿猫的念头；绿猫一死，我们就会像烟一样噗地散成乌有。西部有个人认为，所有女人都是女巫，靠咒术运转着世界。东部有个人相信，所有富人都属于一个秘密会社，比黑手党严密冷酷得多，垄断着旁人做梦也想不到其存在的权力秘诀和享乐秘诀。
至于我，我认为世界的轮子只是转着而已。四十年前我就这么定了论，从那以后再没见过、听过、读到过任何让我改主意的东西。
那时我在一条湖轮上当司炉（教授继续说，从他那支细长的香烟里优雅地啜着烟）。我蠢得冒尖，可我喜欢琢磨。一有机会，我就去一家大图书馆，让他们给我弄来各种各样的书。伙计们就是这么开始叫我教授的。我借哲学书、玄学书、科学书，甚至宗教书。我读它们，试图把这个世界琢磨明白。这一切到底是怎么回事？我为什么在这儿？出生、干活、死去，这整桩买卖的意义何在？有什么用？为什么得没完没了地进行下去？
还有，为什么得这么复杂？
为什么全是建了又拆？为什么非得有城市，有拥挤的街道、马车、缆车、电车，还有一座座搭到天上去、好挂上石头木头的大孔钢架——我最要好的朋友就是从那样一只钢风筝上摔下来死的。难道就没有更简单的法子把这一切办了？为什么事情得搅得这么乱，乱得像我这样的人连一个清清楚楚、体体面面的念头都容不下？
再说，人为什么不是世界真正的一部分？为什么他们显不出一点真真正正的反应？你跟一个女人睡了，为什么那是你有而她没有的东西？为什么去看拳击赛时，打手们只是那么一堆肉，观众只是一群尖叫的花花公子？为什么一场战争不过是空谈、爆破和瞎折腾？为什么人人都得一辈子那么死气沉沉地过，干什么都那么按部就班、规行矩步，像主日学校的野餐，像孤儿的游行？

===Original===

---
And then, when I was reading one of the science books, it came to me. The answer was all there, printed out plain to see, only nobody saw it. It was just this: Nobody was really alive.
Back of other people’s foreheads there weren’t any real thoughts or minds, or love or fear, to explain things. The whole universe﻿—stars and men and dirt and worms and atoms, the whole shooting match﻿—was just one great big engine. It didn’t take mind or life or anything else to run the engine. It just ran.
Now one thing about science. It doesn’t lie. Those men who wrote those science books that showed me the answer, they had no more minds than anybody else. Just darkness in their brains, but because they were machines built to use science, they couldn’t help but get the right answers. They were like the electric brains they’ve got now, but hadn’t then, that give out the right answer when you feed in the question. I’d like to feed in the question, “What’s Life?” to one of those machines and see what came out. Just figures, I suppose. I read somewhere that if a billion monkeys had typewriters and kept pecking away at them they’d eventually turn out all the Encyclopedia Brittanica in trillions and trillions of years. Well, they’ve done it all right, and in jig time.
They’re doing it now.
A lot of philosophy and psychology books I worked through really fit in beautifully. There was Watson’s *Behaviorism* telling how we needn’t even assume that people are conscious to explain their actions. There was Leibitz’s *Monadology*, with its theory that we’re all of us lonely atoms that are completely out of touch and don’t effect each other in the slightest, but only seem to﻿ ﻿… because all our little clockwork motors were started at the same time in pre-established harmony. We *seem* to be responding to each other, but actually we’re just a bunch of wooden-minded puppets. Jerk one puppet up into the flies and the others go on acting as if exactly nothing at all had happened.
So there it was all laid out for me (the Professor went on, carefully pinching out the end of his cigarette). That was why there was no honest-to-God response in people. They were machines.
The fighters were machines made for fighting. The people that watched them were machines for stamping and screaming and swearing. The bankers had banking cogs in their bellies, the crooks had crooked cams.

===Chinese===

---
后来，我在读一本科学书的时候，一下子明白了。答案全在那儿，白纸黑字印着，只是没人看见。就是这么回事：没有一个人真正活着。
别人的额头背后，没有任何真正的思想或心智，没有爱或恐惧，可以用来解释事情。整个宇宙——恒星、人、泥土、虫子、原子，全套家当——只是一台硕大无朋的引擎。运转这台引擎不需要心智、生命或任何别的东西。它只是转。
科学有个好处：它不说谎。那些写出向我展示答案的科学书的人，他们跟别人一样没有心智。脑子里只是一团黑暗，但因为他们是为使用科学而造的机器，他们不由自主地得出正确的答案。他们就像如今那些电动脑——那时候还没有——你把问题喂进去，它就吐出正确答案。我想把“什么是生命？”这个问题喂进那样一台机器，看看出来什么。我猜，只是数字。我在哪儿读到过，如果十亿只猴子有打字机，不停地啄，它们最终会在亿万万年后打出一整套《大英百科全书》。嗯，它们还真打出来了，而且快得很。
它们现在就在打。
我啃过的许多哲学书心理学书，其实全都严丝合缝地扣上了。有华生的《行为主义》，说我们甚至不必假设人有意识，就能解释他们的行为。有莱布尼茨的《单子论》，理论说我们全都是孤零零的原子，彼此彻底隔绝，丝毫不相互影响，只是看上去如此……因为我们的小小发条马达是在“前定和谐”里同时上好的。我们似乎在彼此回应，实际上只是一群木头脑袋的傀儡。把一个傀儡猛吊上天桥，其余的照样演戏，仿佛什么都没发生过。
于是这一切就这样摊开在我面前（教授继续说，仔细地掐灭烟头）。这就是为什么人身上没有真真正正的反应。他们是机器。
拳击手是为拳击而造的机器。看他们的人是负责跺脚、尖叫、叫骂的机器。银行家肚子里装着银行齿轮，骗子装着骗人的凸轮。

===Original===

A woman was just a loving machine, all nicely adjusted to give you a good time (sometimes!) but the farthest star was nearer to you than the mind behind that mouth you kissed.
See what I mean? People just machines, set to do a certain job and then quietly rust away. If you kept on being the machine you were supposed to be, well and good. Then your actions fitted with other people’s. But if you didn’t, if you started doing something else, then the others didn’t respond. They just went on doing what was called for.
It wouldn’t matter what you did, they’d just go on making the motions they were set to make. They might be set to make love, and you might decide you wanted to fight. They’d go on making love while you fought them. Or it might happen the other way﻿—seems to, more often!
Or somebody might be talking about Edison. And you’d happen to say something about Ingersoll. But he’d just go on talking about Edison.
You were all alone.
---
Except for a few others﻿—not more than one in a hundred thousand, I guess﻿—who wake up and figure things out. And they mostly go crazy and run themselves to death, or else turn mean. Mostly they turn mean. They get a cheap little kick out of pushing things around that can’t push back. All over the world you find them﻿—little gangs of three or four, half a dozen﻿—who’ve waked up, but just to their cheap kicks. Maybe it’s a couple of coppers in ’Frisco, a schoolteacher in K.C., some artists in New York, some rich kids in Florida, some undertakers in London﻿—who’ve found that all the people walking around are just dead folk and to be treated no decenter, who see how bad things are and get their fun out of making it a little worse. Just a mean *little* bit worse. They don’t dare to destroy in a big way, because they know the machine feeds them and tends them, and because they’re always scared they’d be noticed by gangs like themselves and wiped out. They haven’t the guts to really wreck the whole shebang. But they get a kick out of scribbling their dirty pictures on it, out of meddling and messing with it.
I’ve seen some of their fun, as they call it, sometimes hidden away, sometimes in the open streets.
You’ve seen a clerk dressing a figure in a store window? Well, suppose he slapped its face. Suppose a kid stuck pins in a calico pussycat, or threw pepper in the eyes of a doll.

===Chinese===

一个女人只是一台做爱的机器，调校得妥妥帖帖好让你快活（偶尔！），但你亲吻的那张嘴背后的心智，比最远的恒星离你还远。
明白我的意思吗？人只是机器，设定好干某件活，然后悄悄锈掉。你要是继续当你该当的那台机器，很好。那你的动作就跟别人的合榫。可你要是不，你要是开始干点别的，别人就不回应了。他们只是继续干设定里要求的。
不管你干什么都不要紧，他们只会继续做他们被设定要做的动作。他们也许被设定做爱，而你决定想打架。你打他们，他们继续做爱。也可能反过来——而且好像反过来的时候更多！
或者，也许有人正聊着爱迪生，你碰巧说了句英格索尔。他呢，只管继续聊爱迪生。
你孤身一人。
---
除了另外少数几个——我估摸十万里挑不出一个——他们醒了过来，把事情想明白了。他们多半发了疯，把自己跑死，要不然就变坏。多数变坏。从推搡那些推不回来的东西里，他们捞一点廉价的乐子。全世界你都能找到他们——三四个、五六个一小伙——醒了，但只醒到他们那点廉价乐子的份上。也许是旧金山的一对警察，堪萨斯城的一个教员，纽约（New York）的几个艺术家，佛罗里达的几个富家子，伦敦的几个殡葬工——他们发现所有走来走去的人只是死人，不必待得更体面，他们看出事情有多糟，便从把事情弄糟一点点里取乐。就恶劣那么小小的一点。他们不敢大肆破坏，因为他们知道机器喂养他们、照料他们，也因为总害怕被同类团伙察觉而被清除。他们没有胆量真把整台机器砸个稀烂。但他们乐于在上面涂他们的脏画，乐于对它捅捅戳戳、搅和糟蹋。
我见过他们的一些乐子——他们这么称呼——有时藏在暗处，有时就在光天化日的大街上。
你见过店员在橱窗里给模特穿衣裳吧？好，设想他扇它耳光。设想一个小孩往印花布猫身上钉大头针，或者往娃娃眼睛里撒胡椒。

===Original===

No decent live man would have anything to do with nickel sadism or dime paranoia like that. He’d either go back to his place in the machine and act out the part set for him, or else he’d hide away like me and live as quiet as he could, not stirring things up. Like a mouse in a dynamo or an ant in an atomics plant.
(The Professor went to the window and opened it, letting the sour old smoke out and the noises of the city in.)
---
Listen (he said), listen to the great mechanical symphony, the big black combo. The airplanes are the double bass. Have you noticed how you can always hear one nowadays? When one walks out of the sky another walks in.
Presses and pumps round out the bass section. Listen to them rumble and thump! Tonight they’ve got an old steam locomotive helping. Maybe they’re giving a benefit show for the old duffer.
Cars and traffic﻿—they’re the strings. Mostly cellos and violas. They purr and wail and whine and keep trying to get out of their section.
Brasses? To me the steel-on-steel of streetcars and El trains always sounds like trumpets and cornets. Strident, metallic, fiery cold.
Hear that siren way off? It’s a clarinet. The ship horns are tubas, the diesel horn’s an oboe. And that lovely dreadful french horn is an electric saw cutting down the last tree.
But what a percussion section they’ve got! The big stuff, like streetcar bells jangling, is easy to catch, but you have to really listen to get the subtleties﻿—the buzz of a defective neon sign, the click of a stoplight changing.
Sometimes you do get human voices, I’ll admit, but they’re not like they are in Beethoven’s “Ninth” or Holst’s “Planets.”
*There’s* the real sound of the universe (the Professor concluded, shutting the window). That’s your heavenly choir. That’s the music of the spheres the old alchemists kept listening for﻿—if they’d just stayed around a little longer they’d all have been deafened by it. Oh, to think that Schopenhauer was bothered by the crack of carters’ whips!
And now it’s time for this mouse to tuck himself in his nest in the dynamo. Good night, gentlemen!

===Chinese===

一个体面的活人不会沾那种五分钱的虐待狂、一毛钱的偏执狂的边。他要么回到机器里自己的位置上，演好分派给他的角色，要么就像我这样躲起来，尽量安静地活着，不搅动任何东西。像发电机里的一只老鼠，原子能厂里的一只蚂蚁。
（教授走到窗前，推开它，放走陈年的烟雾，放进城市的喧嚣。）
---
听（他说），听这部伟大的机械交响曲，这支黑色大乐队。飞机是低音提琴。你注意到没有，如今你总能听见一架？一架走出天空，另一架就走进来。
印刷机和泵充实了低音部。听它们轰隆、砰砰！今晚他们还拉来一台老蒸汽机车帮忙。没准是给那老家伙办一场义演。
汽车和车流——它们是弦乐。多半是大提琴和中提琴。它们呜呜、哀号、尖叫，一个劲想窜出自己的声部。
铜管？在我听来，街车和高架火车那钢铁碾钢铁的声响，向来像小号和短号。尖锐、金属、灼热的冷。
听见远处那声汽笛了吗？那是单簧管。船笛是大号，柴油喇叭是双簧管。而那支可爱又可怕的圆号，是一把电锯，正在放倒最后一棵树。
可他们的打击乐部多棒！大动静，像街车铃铛的叮当，容易听出来，可那些精微处你就得真听了——一块霓虹灯牌的嗡嗡杂音，红绿灯变换的一声咔喍。
有时确实也有人声，我承认，可它们不像贝多芬《第九》或霍尔斯特《行星组曲》里那样。
那才是宇宙真正的声音（教授总结道，关上窗）。那是你的天使唱诗班。那是老炼金术士们一直侧耳守候的天体音乐——他们只要多待上一小会儿，就全得被它震聋。哦，想想吧，叔本华竟被车夫的鞭哨声烦扰！
现在，这只老鼠该回他在发电机里的窝里睡觉了。晚安，先生们！

### I / 一
===Original===

“Come on, Gussy,” Fay prodded quietly, “quit stalking around like a neurotic bear and suggest something for my invention team to work on. I enjoy visiting you and Daisy, but I can’t stay aboveground all night.”
“If being outside the shelters makes you nervous, don’t come around any more,” Gusterson told him, continuing to stalk. “Why doesn’t your invention team think of something to invent? Why don’t you? Hah!” In the “Hah!” lay triumphant condemnation of a whole way of life.
“We do,” Fay responded imperturbably, “but a fresh viewpoint sometimes helps.”
“I’ll say it does! Fay, you burglar, I’ll bet you’ve got twenty people like myself you milk for free ideas. First you irritate their bark and then you make the rounds every so often to draw off the latex or the maple gloop.”
Fay smiled. “It ought to please you that society still has a use for you outré inner-directed types. It takes something to make a junior executive stay aboveground after dark, when the missiles are on the prowl.”
“Society can’t have much use for us or it’d pay us something,” Gusterson sourly asserted, staring blankly at the tankless TV and kicking it lightly as he passed on.
“No, you’re wrong about that, Gussy. Money’s not the key goad with you inner-directeds. I got that straight from our Motivations chief.”
“Did he tell you what we should use instead to pay the grocer? A deep inner sense of achievement, maybe? Fay, why should I do any free thinking for Micro Systems?”
“I’ll tell you why, Gussy. Simply because you get a kick out of insulting us with sardonic ideas. If we take one of them seriously, you think we’re degrading ourselves, and that pleases you even more. Like making someone laugh at a lousy pun.”
---
Gusterson held still in his roaming and grinned. “That the reason, huh? I suppose my suggestions would have to be something in the line of ultra-subminiaturized computers, where one sinister fine-etched molecule does the work of three big bumbling brain cells?”
“Not necessarily. Micro Systems is branching out. Wheel as free as a rogue star. But I’ll pass along to Promotion your one molecule-three brain cell sparkler. It’s a slight exaggeration, but it’s catchy.”
“I’ll have my kids watch your ads to see if you use it and then I’ll sue the whole underworld.” Gusterson frowned as he resumed his stalking. He stared puzzledly at the antique TV.

===Chinese===

“来吧，加西，”费伊轻声催促，“别再像头神经质的熊一样踱来踱去了，给我的发明团队想点东西出来。我喜欢来看你和黛西，可我不能在地面待一整夜。”
“要是待在避难所外面让你紧张，那就别再来了，”古斯特森（Gusterson）告诉他，继续踱。“你那发明团队怎么不自己想点什么来发明？你自己怎么不？哈！”这一声“哈”里，含着对一整套生活方式的胜利的谴责。
“我们想，”费伊不动声色地应道，“不过新鲜的视角有时有帮助。”
“可不是嘛！费伊，你这窃贼，我打赌你豢养着二十个像我这样的人，挤我们的免费点子。先磨破他们的树皮，然后隔三差五巡回一圈，抽取乳胶或者枫糖浆。”
费伊笑了。“社会对你这种另类的、内倾型的人物还有用处，你应该高兴。能让一个初级主管在导弹出没的入夜之后还待在地面，可不是小事。”
“社会对我们不可能有多大用处，不然会付我们点什么，”古斯特森酸酸地断言，茫然地盯着那台没有显像管的电视，路过时轻轻踢了它一脚。
“不，这你可说错了，加西。钱时你们内倾型的人不是关键的鞭子。这是我们动机部头头亲口说的。”
“他有没有告诉你，我们该拿什么别的去付杂货店？大概是一种深刻的内在成就感？费伊，我凭什么要为微系统公司白动脑子？”
“我告诉你为什么，加西。就因为你能从用挖苦的点子羞辱我们当中取乐。我们要是把其中一个当了真，你就觉得我们自贬了身份，那让你更开心。就像让人被一个烂双关逗笑一样。”
---
古斯特森踱到一半停住，咧嘴笑了。“是这么个缘故，嗯？我猜我的建议得是超超小型计算机那一类的，一个阴森森的精细蚀刻分子，顶三个笨手笨脚的大脑细胞？”
“不一定。微系统公司正在扩张业务。放心像颗流浪恒星那样自由驰骋。不过你那个‘一分子三脑细胞’的俏皮话，我会转给推广部。有点夸张，但上口。”
“我会让我家孩子们盯着你们的广告，看你们用没用它，然后告你们整个地下世界。”古斯特森皱着眉继续踱。他困惑地盯着那台老式电视。

===Original===

“How about inventing a plutonium termite?” he said suddenly. “It would get rid of those stockpiles that are worrying you moles to death.”
Fay grimaced noncommittally and cocked his head.
“Well, then, how about a beauty mask? How about that, hey? I don’t mean one to repair a woman’s complexion, but one she’d wear all the time that’d make her look like a 17-year-old sexpot. That’d end *her* worries.”
“Hey, that’s for me,” Daisy called from the kitchen. “I’ll make Gusterson suffer. I’ll make him crawl around on his hands and knees begging my immature favors.”
“No, you won’t,” Gusterson called back. “You having a face like that would scare the kids. Better cancel that one, Fay. Half the adult race looking like Vina Vidarsson is too awful a thought.”
“Yah, you’re just scared of making a million dollars,” Daisy jeered.
“I sure am,” Gusterson said solemnly, scanning the fuzzy floor from one murky glass wall to the other, hesitating at the TV. “How about something homey now, like a flock of little prickly cylinders that roll around the floor collecting lint and flub? They’d work by electricity, or at a pinch cats could bat ’em around. Every so often they’d be automatically herded together and the lint cleaned off the bristles.”
“No good,” Fay said. “There’s no lint underground and cats are verboten. And the aboveground market doesn’t amount to more moneywise than the state of Southern Illinois. Keep it grander, Gussy, and more impractical﻿—you can’t sell people merely useful ideas.” From his hassock in the center of the room he looked uneasily around. “Say, did that violet tone in the glass come from the high Cleveland hydrogen bomb or is it just age and ultraviolet, like desert glass?”
---
“No, somebody’s grandfather liked it that color,” Gusterson informed him with happy bitterness. “I like it too﻿—the glass, I mean, not the tint. People who live in glass houses can see the stars﻿—especially when there’s a window-washing streak in their germ-plasm.”
“Gussy, why don’t you move underground?” Fay asked, his voice taking on a missionary note. “It’s a lot easier living in one room, believe me. You don’t have to tramp from room to room hunting things.”
“I like the exercise,” Gusterson said stoutly.
“But I bet Daisy’d prefer it underground. And your kids wouldn’t have to explain why their father lives like a Red Indian.

“发明一只钚白蚁怎么样？”他突然说。“正好把那些愁得你们这些鼹鼠要死的库存给处理了。”
费伊不置可否地做了个鬼脸，歪着头。
“那么，美容面罩怎么样？那个如何，嘿？我不是说修女人脸色那种，而是她成天戴着、让她看起来像个十七岁性感尤物的那种。那就了结‘她’的烦恼了。”
“嘿，那个我要，”黛西从厨房喊。“我要让古斯特森吃苦头。我要他手脚并用地爬着，哀求我不成熟的恩宠。”
“不，你不会，”古斯特森回喊。“你长那样一张脸会吓坏孩子们。还是取消那个吧，费伊。半个成年人类都长得像薇娜·维达森，这想法太可怕了。”
“哟，你只是害怕赚一百万美元罢了，”黛西讥笑道。
“我当然是，”古斯特森郑重地说，目光扫过模糊的地板，从一面昏暗的玻璃墙扫到另一面，在电视机前停了停。“来点家常的怎么样，比如一小群带刺的小圆筒，在地板上滚来滚去收集绒毛和碎絮？用电力驱动，急了也可以让猫拍着它们玩。隔一阵自动把它们赶撞到一起，把鬃毛上的绒毛清理掉。”
“不行，”费伊说。“地下没有绒毛，猫也禁养。而地面市场按钱算，抵不过南伊利诺伊一个州。点子再宏大点，加西，再不切实际点——光是管用的点子是卖不出去的。”他从房间中央的软凳上不安地环顾四周。“我说，玻璃里那道紫色调是克利夫兰氢弹那一下子弄出来的，还是只是年头和紫外线，像沙漠玻璃？”
---
“不，是哪家的祖父喜欢那个颜色，”古斯特森带着快活的刻薄告诉他。“我也喜欢——我是说玻璃，不是那色调。住在玻璃房子里的人看得见星星——尤其当他们的种质里带一条擦窗染色体。”
“加西，你们怎么不搬到地下去？”费伊问，嗓音里带上了传教的调子。“住一间房轻松多了，相信我。你不用从一个房间跋涉到另一个房间找东西。”
“我喜欢这运动量，”古斯特森昂然说。
“可我打赌黛西更愿意住地下。而且你的孩子们也不用解释，他们父亲为什么过得像个印第安红人。”

Not to mention the safety factor and insurance savings and a crypt church within easy slidewalk distance. Incidentally, we see the stars all the time, better than you do﻿—by repeater.”
“Stars by repeater,” Gusterson murmured to the ceiling, pausing for God to comment. Then, “No, Fay, even if I could afford it﻿—and stand it﻿—I’m such a bad-luck Harry that just when I got us all safely stowed at the N minus 1 sublevel, the Soviets would discover an earthquake bomb that struck from below, and I’d have to follow everybody back to the treetops. *Hey! How about bubble homes in orbit around earth?* Micro Systems could subdivide the world’s most spacious suburb and all you moles could go ellipsing. Space is as safe as there is: no air, no shock waves. Free fall’s the ultimate in restfulness﻿—great health benefits. Commute by rocket﻿—or better yet stay home and do all your business by TV-telephone, or by waldo if it were that sort of thing. Even pet your girl by remote control﻿—she in her bubble, you in yours, whizzing through vacuum. Oh, damn-damn-*damn*-*damn*-**damn!**”
He was glaring at the blank screen of the TV, his big hands clenching and unclenching.
“Don’t let Fay give you apoplexy﻿—he’s not worth it,” Daisy said, sticking her trim head in from the kitchen, while Fay inquired anxiously, “Gussy, what’s the matter?”
“Nothing, you worm!” Gusterson roared, “Except that an hour ago I forgot to tune in on the only TV program I’ve wanted to hear this year﻿—*Finnegans Wake* scored for English, Gaelic and brogue. Oh, damn-*damn*-**damn!**”
“Too bad,” Fay said lightly. “I didn’t know they were releasing it on flat TV too.”
---
“Well, they were! Some things are too damn big to keep completely underground. And I had to forget! I’m always doing it﻿—I miss everything! Look here, you rat,” he blatted suddenly at Fay, shaking his finger under the latter’s chin, “I’ll tell you what you can have that ignorant team of yours invent. They can fix me up a mechanical secretary that I can feed orders into and that’ll remind me when the exact moment comes to listen to TV or phone somebody or mail in a story or write a letter or pick up a magazine or look at an eclipse or a new orbiting station or fetch the kids from school or buy Daisy a bunch of flowers or whatever it is.

更不用说安全系数、保险折扣，还有一部自动步道直达的地下教堂。顺便说，星星我们随时都看，比你们看得还清楚——靠中继。”
“靠中继看星星，”古斯特森对天花板咕哄，停下来等上帝评论。然后，“不，费伊，就算我住得起——也受得了——我是个这么倒霉的家伙，等我们全家刚在地下第 N 减 1 层安稳住下，苏联人就会发明出从底下往上打的地震炸弹，我就只好跟着所有人回树梢。嘿！绕地轨道上的泡泡住宅怎么样？微系统公司可以把全世界最宽敞的郊区细分出售，你们全体鼹鼠可以去画椭圆。太空是有史以来最安全的地方：没有空气，没有冲击波。自由落体是休憩的极致——健康福利大大的。通勤坐火箭——或者干脆待在家里，一切事务用电视电话办理，是那类事的话就用瓦尔多遥控。甚至可以遥控爱抚你的姑娘——她在她的泡泡里，你在你的泡泡里，臉臊地穿过真空。哦，该-该-该-真该死！”
他瞪着电视空白的屏幕，大手一攥一松。
“别让费伊给你气出中风——他不配，”黛西从厨房探进她利落的脑袋说，费伊则焦急地问：“加西，怎么了？”
“没什么，你这虫子！”古斯特森吼道，“只不过一小时前我忘了调台，收听我今年唯一想听的电视节目——《芬尼根的守灵夜》，配英语、盖尔语和土腔的演播。哦，该-真-该死！”
“可惜，”费伊轻快地说。“我不知道他们也用平面电视播。”
---
“他们在播！有些东西太大了，没法完全憋在地下。而我偏偏忘了！我老是这样——我什么都错过！听着，你这耗子，”他突然冲着费伊咶咶叫，手指头在后者下巴底下晃，“我告诉你让你那个无知的团队能发明什么。他们可以给我捣鼓一个机械秘书，我可以往里面喂指令，到了确切时刻它会提醒我：听电视、给谁打电话、寄一篇小说、写一封信、拿一本杂志、看日食或一个新的轨道站、接孩子放学、给黛西买一束花，或者别的什么。

===Original===

It’s got to be something that’s always with me, not something I have to go and consult or that I can get sick of and put down somewhere. And it’s got to remind me forcibly enough so that I take notice and don’t just shrug it aside, like I sometimes do even when Daisy reminds me of things. That’s what your stupid team can invent for me! If they do a good job, I’ll pay ’em as much as fifty dollars!”
“That doesn’t sound like anything so very original to me,” Fay commented coolly, leaning back from the wagging finger. “I think all senior executives have something of that sort. At least, their secretary keeps some kind of file﻿ ﻿…”
“I’m not looking for something with spiked falsies and nylons up to the neck,” interjected Gusterson, whose ideas about secretaries were a trifle lurid. “I just want a mech reminder﻿—that’s all!”
“Well, I’ll keep the idea in mind,” Fay assured him, “along with the bubble homes and beauty masks. If we ever develop anything along those lines, I’ll let you know. If it’s a beauty mask, I’ll bring Daisy a pilot model﻿—to use to scare strange kids.” He put his watch to his ear. “Good lord, I’m going to have to cut to make it underground before the main doors close. Just ten minutes to Second Curfew! ’By, Gus. ’By, Daze.”
Two minutes later, living room lights out, they watched Fay’s foreshortened antlike figure scurrying across the balding ill-lit park toward the nearest escalator.
Gusterson said, “Weird to think of that big bright space-poor glamor basement stretching around everywhere underneath. Did you remind Smitty to put a new bulb in the elevator?”
“The Smiths moved out this morning,” Daisy said tonelessly. “They went underneath.”
“Like cockroaches,” Gusterson said. “Cockroaches leavin’ a sinkin’ apartment building. Next the ghosts’ll be retreatin’ to the shelters.”
“Anyhow, from now on we’re our own janitors,” Daisy said.
He nodded. “Just leaves three families besides us loyal to this glass death trap. Not countin’ ghosts.” He sighed. Then, “You like to move below, Daisy?” he asked softly, putting his arm lightly across her shoulders. “Get a woozy eyeful of the bright lights and all for a change? Be a rat for a while? Maybe we’re getting too old to be bats. I could scrounge me a company job and have a thinking closet all to myself and two secretaries with stainless steel breasts. Life’d be easier for you and a lot cleaner.

===Chinese===

它必须是一直跟着我的东西，不是那种我得专门跑去查的，也不是那种我会厌倦、随手擱在什么地方的东西。而且它提醒得够用力，让我注意到、而不是耸耸肩丢到一边——就连黛西提醒我时我有时都这样。让你那蠢团队给我发明这个！他们干好了，我出他们整整五十美元！”
“这听起来没什么特别原创的，”费伊冷静地评论，从那根晃着的手指后面往后仰。“我想所有的高级主管都有类似的东西。至少，他们的秘书会保存某种档案……”
“我不要那种海绵垫假胸、尼龙袜一直拉到脖子的，”古斯特森插嘴，他对秘书的想象有点耸人听闻。“我只想要个机械提醒器——就这样！”
“好吧，我会把这主意记在心里，”费伊向他保证，“连同泡泡住宅和美容面罩。我们哪天真朝哪个方向开发了，我通知你。要是美容面罩，我给黛西带个试用型号——用来吓別家小孩。”他把手表贴到耳边。“天哪，我得赶紧了，得赶在大门关上前下到地下。离二级宵禁只剩十分钟了！回见，加西。回见，黛子。”
两分钟后，客厅灯灭了，他们看着费伊那前缩变形、蚂蚁似的人影，匆匆穿过那座秃顶般、灯光昏暗的公园，奔向最近的自动扶梯。
古斯特森说：“一想到那个巨大、明亮、缺空间的魅力地下室就在脚下四面八方延伸，就发毛。你提醒史密蒂给电梯换灯泡了吗？”
“史密斯家今早搬走了，”黛西平平淡淡地说。“他们下去了。”
“像蟑螂，”古斯特森说。“蟑螂离开一栋正在下沉的公寓楼。接下来连鬼都要往避难所撤退了。”
“总之，从现在起我们自己当自己的门房，”黛西说。
他点点头。“只剩三家，加上我们，对这座玻璃死亡陷阱忠心耿耿。不算鬼的话。”他叹了口气。然后，“你想搬下去吗，黛西？”他轻声问，把胳膊轻轻搭上她的肩。“换个花样，晕乎乎地看个够那片亮灯光？当一阵子老鼠？也许我们当蝙蝠当得太老了。我可以去公司讨份差事，有一间专用的思想小间，两个不锈钢乳房的秘书。你的日子会轻松些，也干净些。

===Original===

And you’d sleep safer.”
“That’s true,” she answered and paused. She ran her fingertip slowly across the murky glass, its violet tint barely perceptible against a cold dim light across the park. “But somehow,” she said, snaking her arm around his waist, “I don’t think I’d sleep happier﻿—or one bit excited.”

===Chinese===

你也会睡得安全些。”
“那倒是，”她答道，顿了顿。她的指尖缓缓划过昏暗的玻璃，在公园对面清冷的微光映衬下，那道紫色调几乎看不出来。“可是不知怎么，”她说着，胳膊蛇一般绕上他的腰，“我不觉得我会睡得更快乐——或者有一丁点兴奋。”

## The Last Letter / 最后一封信
===Original===

On Tenthmonth 1, 2457 AD, at exactly 9 a.m. Planetary Federation Time﻿—but with a permissible error of a millionth of a second either way﻿—in the fifth sublevel of NewNew York Robot Postal Station 68, Black Sorter gulped down ten thousand pieces of first-class mail.
This breakfast tidbit did not agree with the mail-sorting machine. It was as if a robust dog had been fed a large chunk of good red meat with a strychnine pill in it. Black Sorter’s innards went *whirr-klunk*, a blue electric glow enveloped him, and he began to shake as if he might break loose from the concrete.
He desperately spat back over his shoulder a single envelope, gave a great *huff* and blew out toward the sorting tubes a medium-size snowstorm consisting of the other nine thousand, nine hundred and ninety-nine pieces of first-class mail chewed to confetti. Then, still convulsed, he snapped up a fresh ten thousand and proceeded to chomp and grind on them. Black Sorter was rugged.
The rejected envelope was tongued up by Red Subsorter, who growled deep in his throat, said a very bad word, and passed it to Yellow Rerouter, who passed it to Green Rerouter, who passed it to Brown Study, who passed it to Pink Wastebasket.
Unlike Black Sorter, Pink Wastebasket was very delicate, though highly intuitive﻿—the machine equivalent of a White Russian countess. She was designed to scan in 3,137 codes, route special-delivery spacemail to interplanetary liners by messenger rocket, and distinguish 9s from upside-down 6s.
Pink Wastebasket haughtily inhaled the offending envelope and almost instantly turned a bright crimson and began to tremble. After a few minutes, small atomic flames started to flicker from her midsection.
White Nursemaid Seven and Greasy Joe both received Pink Wastebasket’s distress signal and got there as fast as their wheels would roll them, but the highborn machine’s malady was beyond their simple skills of oilcan and electroshock.
---
They summoned other machine-tending-and-repairing machines, ones far more expert than themselves, but all were baffled. It was clear that Pink Wastebasket, who continued to tremble and flicker uncontrollably, was suffering from the equivalent of a major psychosis with severe psychosomatic symptoms. She spat a stream of filthy ions at Gray Psychiatrist, not recognizing her old friend.

===Chinese===

公元2457年十月初一，行星联邦时间上午九点整——允许正负各百万分之一秒的误差——在新新纽约第68机器人邮政站的地下第五层，黑分拣机（Black Sorter）一口吞下了一万件一级邮件。
这份早餐点心不合这台邮件分拣机的胃口。就像一条壮实的狗被喂了一大块好红肉，肉里却藏了一粒马钱子碱药丸。黑分拣机的内脏发出“呜——哐”的声响，一团蓝色的电光笼罩了它，它开始发抖，仿佛要从混凝土里挣脱出去。
它拼命地从肩后喀出一封值封，然后长长“呼”了一口气，朝分拣管吹出一场中等规模的暴风雪——由其余九千九百九十九件被嚼成纸屑的一级邮件组成。然后，它还在抽搐，又猛地吞下新的一万件，继续大嚼特嚼。黑分拣机皮实得很。
那封被拒收的信封被红副分拣机用舌尖卷了起来，它喉咙深处发出低吼，骂了一句很脏的话，把它传给黄改寄机，黄改寄机传给绿改寄机，绿改寄机传给棕沉思机，棕沉思机传给粉红废纸篓。
与黑分拣机不同，粉红废纸篓非常娇气，虽然直觉极强——相当于机器里的白俄伯爵夫人。她被设计来扫描3137种编码、用信使火箭把特快太空邮件发往行星班机，以及把9和倒过来的6区分开。
粉红废纸篓傲慢地把那封闯祸的信封吸了进去，几乎立刻变得通红，开始发抖。几分钟后，小小的原子火焰开始从她的中段噴嚼罽起。
白保姆七号和油腻乔都收到了粉红废纸篓的求救信号，轮子能滚多快就滚多快地赶了过来，但这位出身高贵的机器的病症，超出了他们油壶加电击的简单手艺。
---
他们召来了别的机器保养维修机器，比自己内行得多的那些，可全都束手无策。显然，粉红废纸篓——她还在不由自主地颤抖、闪烁——患的是相当于重度精神病加重度身心症的东西。她朝灰精神病医师喷出一串污秽的离子，没认出她的老朋友。

===Original===

Meanwhile, the paper blizzard from Black Sorter was piling up in great drifts between the dark pillars of the sublevel, and flurries had reached Pink Wastebasket’s aristocratic area. An expedition of sturdy machines, headed by two hastily summoned snowplows, was dispatched to immobilize Black Sorter at all costs.
Pink Wastebasket, quivering like a demented hula dancer, was clearly approaching a crisis. Finally Gray Psychiatrist﻿—after consulting with Green Surgeon, and even then with an irritated reluctance, as if he were calling in a witch-doctor﻿—summoned a human being.
The human being walked respectfully around Pink Wastebasket several times and then gave her a nervous little poke with a rubber-handled probe.
Pink Wastebasket gently regurgitated her last snack, turned dead white, gave a last flicker and shake, and expired. Black Coroner recorded the immediate cause of death as tinkering by a human being.
The human being, a bald and scrawny one named Potshelter, picked up the envelope responsible for all the trouble, stared at it incredulously, opened it with trembling fingers, scanned the contents briefly, gave a great shriek and ran off at top speed, forgetting to hop on his perambulator, which followed him making anxious clucking noises.
The nearest human representative of the Solar Bureau of Investigation, a rather wooden-looking man named Krumbine, also bald, recognized Potshelter as soon as the latter burst gasping into his office, squeezing through the door while it was still dilating. The human beings whose work took them among the Top Brass, as the upper-echelon machines were sometimes referred to, formed a kind of human elite, just one big nervous family.
“Sit down, Potshelter,” the S.B.I. Man said. “Hold still a second so the chair can grab you. Hitch onto the hookah and choose a tranquilizer from the tray at your elbow. Whatever deviation you’ve uncovered can’t be that much of a danger to the planets. I imagine that when you leave this office, the Solar Battle Fleet will still be orbiting peacefully around Luna.”
“I seriously doubt that.”
---
Potshelter gulped a large lavender pill and took a deep breath. “Krumbine, a letter turned up in the first-class mail this morning.”
“Great Scott!”
“It is a letter from one person to another person.”
“Good Lord!”
“The flow of advertising has been seriously interfered with.

===Chinese===

与此同时，黑分拣机喷出的纸暴风雪在地下层的黑暗立柱之间堆成了一座座雪堆，飞雪已经飘到了粉红废纸篓的贵族地界。一队结实的机器，由两台匆匆召来的扫雪机打头，被派去不惜一切代价制服黑分拣机。
粉红废纸篓颤抖得像个发疯的草裙舞女，显然正在逼近危象。最后，灰精神病医师——在咨询过绿外科医师之后，即便那时也带着恼怒的不情愿，仿佛在请巫医——召来了一个人。
那个人恭恭敬敬地绕着粉红废纸篓走了几圈，然后用一根橡胶柄探针紧张地捅了她一下。
粉红废纸篓温柔地吐出她最后一份点心，变成惨白色，最后闪了一下、抖了一下，断了气。黑验尸官将直接死因记录为：遭人类捅弄。
那个人叫波特谢尔特，秃顶、瘦削。他捾起那封闯下全部大祸的信封，难以置信地盯着它，用颤抖的手指拆开，粗粗扫了一眼内容，发出一声尖叫，拔腿就跑，忘了跳上他的轮椅——那车跟在后面发出焦急的咯咯声。
太阳系调查局离得最近的人类代表，一个模样有点木、名叫克鲁姆拜恩的人，也是秃顶，在后者挤进门缝、喘着气冲进办公室的那一刻就认出了他——门还在扩张中他就挤了进来。在“高层铜管”们——那些上层机器有时被这么称呼——中间工作的人类，形成了一种人类精英，一个神经质的大家族。
“坐下，波特谢尔特，”调查局的人说。“先别动，好让椅子抓住你。勾住水烟筒，从你手边的托盘里挑一颗镇静剂。你揭发的不管什么偏差行为，不至于对各大行星构成那么大的威胁。我料想等你离开这间办公室时，太阳系战斗舰队还在绕着月亮安静地转呢。”
“我可严重怀疑。”
---
波特谢尔特吞下一颗大大的淡紫色药丸，深吸一口气。“克鲁姆拜恩，今天早上一级邮件里冒出来一封信。”
“老天爷！”
“是一封从一个人寄给另一个人的信。”
“上帝啊！”
“广告流受到了严重干扰。

===Original===

At a modest estimate, three hundred million pieces of expensive first-class advertising have already been chewed to rags and I’m not sure the Steel Helms﻿—God bless ’em!﻿—have the trouble in hand yet.”
“Judas Priest!”
“Naturally the poor machines weren’t able to cope with the letter. It was utterly outside their experience, beyond the furthest reach of their programming. It threw them into a terrible spasm. Pink Wastebasket is dead and at this very instant, if we’re lucky, three police machines of the toughest blued steel are holding down Black Sorter and putting a muzzle on him.”
“Great Scott! It’s incredible, Potshelter. And Pink Wastebasket dead? Take another tranquilizer, Potshelter, and hand over the tray.”
Krumbine received it with trembling fingers, started to pick up a big pink pill but drew back his hand from it in sudden revulsion at its color and swallowed two blue oval ones instead. The man was obviously fighting to control himself.
He said unsteadily, “I almost never take doubles, but this news you bring﻿—Good Lord! I seem to recall a case where someone tried to send a sound-tape through the mails, but that was before my time. Incidentally, is there any possibility that this is a letter sent by one *group* of persons to another group? A hive or a therapy group or a social club? That would be bad enough, of course, but﻿—”
“No, just one single person sending to another.” Potshelter’s expression set in grimly solicitous lines. “I can see you don’t quite understand, Krumbine. This is not a sound-tape, but a letter written in letters. You know, letters, characters﻿—like books.”
“Don’t mention books in this office!” Krumbine drew himself up angrily and then slumped back. “Excuse me, Potshelter, but I find this very difficult to face squarely. Do I understand you to say that one person has tried to use the mails to send a printed sheet of some sort to another?”
“Worse than that. A written letter.”
“Written? I don’t recognize the word.”
“It’s a way of making characters, of forming visual equivalents of sound, without using electricity. The writer, as he’s called, employs a black liquid and a pointed stick called a pen. I know about this because one hobby of mine is ancient means of communication.”
---
Krumbine frowned and shook his head. “Communication is a dangerous business, Potshelter, especially at the personal level.

===Chinese===

保守估计，已有三亿件昂贵的一级广告被嚼成了碎布，而且我不敢肯定钢盔们——上帝保佑他们！——已经控制住局势。”
“我的天！”
“那些可怜的机器自然应付不了这封信。它完全在他们的经验之外，超出了他们程序设计最远的边界。它把他们扔进了可怕的痉挛。粉红废纸篓死了，而此时此刻，如果我们走运，三台最硬的蓝钢警察机器正按住黑分拣机，给它戴口罩。”
“老天爷！难以置信，波特谢尔特。粉红废纸篓死了？再吃一颗镇静剂，波特谢尔特，把托盘递过来。”
克鲁姆拜恩用颤抖的手指接过托盘，想去拿一颗粉色大药丸，却突然厌恶那颜色地缩回手，改吞了两颗蓝色的椭圆药丸。这人显然在努力控制自己。
他摇摇晃晃地说：“我几乎从不吃双份，可你带来的这消息——上帝啊！我似乎记得有过一个案子，有人想通过邮局寄一盘录音带，但那是在我任职之前。顺便问一句，有没有可能是某一‘群’人寄给另一群人的信？一个蜂巢、一个治疗小组，或者一个社交俱乐部？那当然也够糟，不过——”
“不，就只是一个单独的人寄给另一个。”波特谢尔特的表情凝成严峻关切的线条。“我看你还没完全明白，克鲁姆拜恩。这不是录音带，而是一封用字母写成的信。你知道的，字母，字符——像书一样。”
“别在这间办公室里提书！”克鲁姆拜恩愤怒地挺直身子，随即又塌回椅子里。“原谅我，波特谢尔特，可我发现自己很难直面这件事。我是否可以理解为，有一个人试图利用邮局，把某种印出来的纸片寄给另一个人？”
“比那还糟。一封手写的信。”
“手写？我不认识这个词。”
“那是一种制造字符的办法，不用电，造出声音的视觉对应物。书写者——他们是这么叫的——使用一种黑色液体和一支叫笔的尖头小棍。我之所以知道，是因为我的爱好之一就是古代通讯手段。”
---
克鲁姆拜恩皱起眉，摇头。“通讯是危险的行当，波特谢尔特，尤其是个人层面的。

===Original===

With you and me, it’s all right, because we know what we’re doing.”
He picked up a third blue tranquilizer. “But with most of the hive-folk, person-to-person communication is only a morbid form of advertising, a dangerous travesty of normal newscasting﻿—catharsis without the analyst, recitation without the teacher﻿—a perversion of promotion employed in betraying and subverting.”
The frown deepened as he put the blue pill in his mouth and chewed it. “But about this pen﻿—do you mean the fellow glues the pointed stick to his tongue and then speaks, and the black liquid traces the vibrations on the paper? A primitive non-electrical oscilloscope? Sloppy but conceivable, and producing a record of sorts of the spoken word.”
“No, no, Krumbine.” Potshelter nervously popped a square orange tablet into his mouth. “It’s a handwritten letter.”
Krumbine watched him. “I never mix tranquilizers,” he boasted absently. “Handwritten, eh? You mean that the message was imprinted on a hand? And the skin or the entire hand afterward detached and sent through the mails in the fashion of a Martian reproach? A grisly find indeed, Potshelter.”
“You still don’t quite grasp it, Krumbine. The fingers of the hand move the stick that applies the ink, producing a crude imitation of the printed word.”
“Diabolical!” Krumbine smashed his fist down on the desk so that the four phones and twoscore microphones rattled. “I tell you, Potshelter, the S.B.I. is ready to cope with the subtlest modern deceptions, but when fiends search out and revive tricks from the pre-Atomic Cave Era, it’s almost too much. But, Great Scott, I dally while the planets are in danger. What’s the sender’s code on this hellish letter?”
“No code,” Potshelter said darkly, proferring the envelope. “The return address is﻿—handwritten.”
Krumbine blanched as his eyes slowly traced the uneven lines in the upper left-hand corner:

> *from* Richard Rowe

215 West 10th St. (horizontal)

2837 Rocket Court (vertical)

Hive 37, NewNew York 319, N.Y.

Columbia, Terra

在你我之间没问题，因为我们知道自己在干什么。”
他拈起第三颗蓝色镇静剂。“可对大多数蜂巢民众来说，人对人的通讯只是一种病态形式的广告，是对正常新闻广播的危险戏仿——没有分析师的宣泄，没有教师的背诵——一种被用于背叛和颠覆的促销手法的堕落。”
他把蓝药丸放进嘴里嚼着，眉头皱得更深了。“不过说这支笔——你是说那家伙把尖头小棍粘在舌头上说话，黑液体就把振动描在纸上？一台原始的非电示波器？邋遢，但可以想象，还能造出某种口语的记录。”
“不，不，克鲁姆拜恩。”波特谢尔特紧张地往嘴里弹了一片方形橙色药片。“那是一封手写的信。”
克鲁姆拜恩看着他。“我从不混吃镇静剂，”他心不在焉地夸口道。“手写，嗯？你是说讯息是印在一只手上的？然后把皮肤或者整只手切下来，按火星人的羞辱方式寄过邮局？确实是个毛骨悚然的发现，波特谢尔特。”
“你还是没太抓住要领，克鲁姆拜恩。是手指移动小棍涂上墨水，造出印刷文字的粗糙仿品。”
“恶魔般的行为！”克鲁姆拜恩一拳砸在桌上，震得四部电话和四十来个麦克风哗啦作响。“我告诉你，波特谢尔特，太阳系调查局有能力对付最精妙的现代骗局，可当恶魔们挖出并复活原子洞穴时代的伎俩，就几乎叫人无法招架了。可是，老天爷，我在耽搁，行星们正处于危险中。这封地狱来信的寄件人代码是什么？”
“没有代码，”波特谢尔特阴沉地说，递上信封。“回信地址是——手写的。”
克鲁姆拜恩的脸白了，他的眼睛缓缓描摹着左上角那些歪歪扭扭的行：

> 西十街215号（横写）

> 火箭巷2837号（竖写）

“Ugh!” Krumbine said, shivering. “Those crawling characters, those letters, as you call them, those *things* barely enough like print to be readable﻿—they seem to be on the verge of awakening all sorts of horrid racial memories. I find myself thinking of fur-clad witch-doctors dipping long pointed sticks in bubbling black cauldrons. No wonder Pink Wastebasket couldn’t take it, brave girl.”
---
Firming himself behind his desk, he pushed a number of buttons and spoke long numbers and meaningful alphabetical syllables into several microphones. Banks of colored lights around the desk began to blink like a theatre marquee sending Morse Code, while phosphorescent arrows crawled purposefully across maps and space-charts and through three-dimensional street diagrams.
“There!” he said at last. “The sender of the letter is being apprehended and will be brought directly here. We’ll see what sort of man this Richard Rowe is﻿—if we can assume he’s human. Seven precautionary cordons are being drawn around his population station: three composed of machines, two of S.B.I. agents, and two consisting of human and mechanical medical-combat teams. Same goes for the intended recipient of the letter. Meanwhile, a destroyer squadron of the Solar Fleet has been detached to orbit over NewNew York.”
“In case it becomes necessary to Z-Bomb?” Potshelter asked grimly.
Krumbine nodded. “With all those villains lurking just outside the Solar System in their invisible black ships, with planeticide in their hearts, we can’t be too careful. One word transmitted from one spy to another and anything may happen. And we must bomb before they do, so as to contain our losses. Better one city destroyed than a traitor on the loose who may destroy many cities. One hundred years ago, three person-to-person postcards went through the mails﻿—just three postcards, Potshelter!﻿—and *pft* went Schenectady, Hoboken, Cicero, and Walla Walla. Here, as long as you’re mixing them, try one of these oval blues﻿—I find them best for steady swallowing.”
Bells jangled. Krumbine grabbed up two phones, holding one to each ear. Potshelter automatically picked up a third. The ringing continued. Krumbine started to wedge one of his phones under his chin, nodded sharply at Potshelter and then toward a cluster of microphones at the end of the table. Potshelter picked up a fourth phone from behind them. The ringing stopped.

他在办公桌后挺直身子，按下一串按钮，对着几个麦克风报出一长串数字和意味深长的字母音节。桌子周围一排排彩灯开始闪烁，像剧院招牌在发摩尔斯电码，同时磷光箭头在地图、太空图和三维街道图上有目的地爬行。
“好了！”他最后说。“信的寄件人正在被捕，将被直接带到这儿。我们会看看这个理查德·罗是何等人物——如果我们能假设他是人类的话。七道预防性警戒圈正围着他的居民站收拢：三道由机器组成，两道由调查局探员组成，还有两道由人类加机械的医疗卫生战斗组组成。信件的原定收件人也同样办理。与此同时，太阳系舰队的一支驱逐舰中队已分遣出来，在新新纽约上空进入轨道。”
“万一需要扔 Z 弹？”波特谢尔特冷冷地问。
克鲁姆拜恩点头。“有那帮恶棍潜伏在太阳系外，坐在他们看不见的黑船上，心里揣着穿行星的念头，我们再怎么小心也不为过。一个间谍向另一个传出一个词，什么事都可能发生。而且我们必须抢在他们之前轰炸，好控制损失。毁掉一座城，总好过放跑一个可能毁掉许多城的叛徒。一百年前，三张人对人的明信片过了邮局——就三张明信片，波特谢尔特！——嘩，斯克内克塔迪、霍博肯、西塞罗和沃拉沃拉就没了。来，既然你在混着吃，试试这种蓝色椭圆的——我发现它们最适合连续吞服。”
铃声大作。克鲁姆拜恩抓起两部电话，一边耳朵一部。波特谢尔特自动拿起第三部。铃声还在响。克鲁姆拜恩把一部电话往下巴底下楔，朝波特谢尔特、又朝桌子那头的一簇麦克风利落地一点头。波特谢尔特从它们后面拿起第四部。铃声停了。

The two men listened, looking doped, Krumbine with an eye fixed on the sweep second hand of the large wall clock. When it had made one revolution, he cradled his phones. Potshelter followed suit.

The wall opened to a small heart shape toward the center and a sheaf of pale yellow envelopes arced out and plopped on the middle of the desk. Krumbine started to leaf through them, scanning the little transparent windows.
“Hm, Electronic Soap﻿ ﻿… Better Homes and Landing Platforms﻿ ﻿… Psycho-Blinkers﻿ ﻿… Your Girl Next Door﻿ ﻿… Poppy-Woppies﻿ ﻿… Poopsy-Woopsies﻿ ﻿…”
He started to open an envelope, then, after a quick look around and an apologetic smile at Potshelter, dumped them all on the disposal hopper, which gargled briefly.
“After all, there *is* a crisis this morning,” he said in a defensive voice.
Potshelter nodded absently. “I can remember back before personalized delivery and rhyming robots,” he observed. “But how I’d miss them now﻿—so much more distingué than the hives with their non-personalized radio, TV and stereo advertising. For that matter, I believe there are some backward areas on Terra where the great advertising potential of telephones and telegrams hasn’t been fully realized and they are still used in part for personal communication. Now me, I’ve never in my life sent or received a message except on my walky-talky.” He patted his breast pocket.
Krumbine nodded, but he was a trifle shocked and inclined to revise his estimate of Potshelter’s social status. Krumbine conducted his own social correspondence solely by telepathy. He shared with three other S.B.I. officials a private telepath﻿—a charming albino girl named Agnes.
“Yes, and it’s a very handsome walky-talky,” he assured Potshelter a little falsely. “Suits you. I like the upswept antenna.” He drummed on the desk and swallowed another blue tranquilizer. “Dammit, what’s happened to those machines? They ought to have the two spies here by now. Did you notice that the second﻿—the intended recipient of the letter, I mean﻿—seems to be female? Another good Terran name, too, Jane Dough. Hive in Upper Manhattan.” He began to tap the envelope sharply against the desk. “Dammit, where *are* they?”
“Excuse me,” Potshelter said hesitantly, “but I’m wondering why you haven’t read the message inside the envelope.”
---
Krumbine looked at him blankly. “Great Scott, I assumed that at least *it* was in some secret code, of course. Normally I’d have asked you to have Pink Wastebasket try her skill on it, but﻿ ﻿…” His eyes widened and his voice sank.

===Chinese===

墙中央张开一个心形小口，一沓淡黄色信封划着弧线飞出，噼啪落在桌子中央。克鲁姆拜恩开始翻检，扫视那些透明小窗口。
“嗯，电子肥皂……更美好的家园与降落平台……心理眨灯器……您的邻家女孩……泡泡沃皮……普茜沃茜……”
他刚要拆一封，随即迅速环顾一圈，朝波特谢尔特抱歉地笑笑，把它们全倒进了处置漏斗，漏斗短暂地咕噜了一阵。
“毕竟，今天早上确实有危机，”他用辩解的口气说。
波特谢尔特心不在焉地点头。“我还记得个性化投递和押韵机器人出现之前的日子，”他说。“可如今我会多么想念它们——比那些只有非个性化广播、电视和立体声广告的蜂巢高雅多了。说起来，我相信地球上还有几个落后地区，电话和电报的巨大广告潜力还没充分实现，它们还在一定程度上被用于个人通讯。至于我，我这辈子除了用步谈机，从没发过或收过任何讯息。”他拍拍胸前口袋。
克鲁姆拜恩点头，但他有点小小的震惊，倾向于修改对波特谢尔特社会地位的评估。克鲁姆拜恩自己的社交通讯完全靠心灵感应进行。他和另外三名调查局官员合用一位私人灵媒——一位迷人的白化病姑娘，名叫艾格尼丝。
“是啊，而且那是台非常漂亮的步谈机，”他有点言不由衷地向波特谢尔特保证。“配你正合适。我喜欢那根上翘的天线。”他敲着桌子，又吞下一颗蓝色镇静剂。“见鬼，那些机器怎么了？他们早该把两个间谍带到这儿了。你注意到没有，第二个——我是说信的原定收件人——好像是个女的？也是个地道的地球名字，简·多。上曼哈顿的蜂巢。”他开始拿信封尖锐地敲桌子。“见鬼，他们到底在哪儿？”
“恕我冒昧，”波特谢尔特迟疑地说，“可我在想，您为什么还没读信封里面的讯息。”

> Dear Jane,
> It must surprise you that I know your name, for our hives are widely separated. Do you recall day before yesterday when your guided tour of Grand Central Spaceport got stalled because the guide blew a fuse? I was the young man with hair in the tour behind yours. So I am writing you a love-letter.

> *Do not be frightened of me, Jane. I am no caveman except for my hair. I am not insane. I am emotionally disturbed, but in a way that no machine has ever described to me. I want only your happiness.*

> 亲爱的简：

Krumbine slumped back in his chair, which braced itself manfully against him, and looked long and thoughtfully at Potshelter. “Well, if that’s a code, it’s certainly a fiendishly subtle one. You’d think he was talking to his Girl Next Door.”
Potshelter nodded wonderingly. “I only read as far as where they were planning to blow up Grand Central Spaceport and all the guides in it.”
“Judas Priest, I think I have it!” Krumbine shot up. “It’s a pilot advertisement﻿—Boy Next Door or﻿—that kind of thing﻿—printed to look like hand-writtening, which would make all the difference. And the pilot copy got mailed by accident﻿—which would mean there is no real Richard Rowe.”
At that instant, the door dilated and two blue detective engines hustled a struggling young man into the office. He was slim, rather handsome, had a bushy head of hair that had somehow survived evolution and radioactive fallout, and across the chest and back of his paper singlet was neatly stamped: “**Richard Rowe**.”
When he saw the two men, he stopped struggling and straightened up. “Excuse me, gentlemen,” he said, “but these police machines must have made a mistake. I’ve committed no crime.”
Then his gaze fell on the hand-addressed envelope on Krumbine’s desk and he turned pale.
---
Krumbine laughed harshly. “No crime! No, not at all. Merely using the mails to communicate. Ha!”
The young man shrank back. “I’m sorry, sir.”
“Sorry, he says! Do you realize that your insane prank has resulted in the destruction of perhaps a half-billion pieces of first-class advertising?﻿—in the strangulation of a postal station and the paralysis of Lower Manhattan?﻿—in the mobilization of S.B.I. reserves, the de-mothballing of two divisions of G.I. machines and the redeployment of the Solar Battle Fleet? Good Lord, boy, why did you do it?”
---
Richard Rowe continued to shrink but he squared his shoulders. “I’m sorry, sir, but I just had to. I just had to get in touch with Jane Dough.”
“A girl from another hive? A girl you’d merely gazed at because a guide happened to blow a fuse?” Krumbine stood up, shaking an angry finger. “Great Scott, boy, where was Your Girl Next Door?”
Richard Rowe stared bravely at the finger, which made him look a trifle cross-eyed. “She died, sir, both of them.”
“But there should be at least six.”

Just then, the door re-dilated and a blue matron machine conducted a young woman into the office. Rowe, there’s a sky-pilot two levels up﻿—look for the usual white collar just below the photocells. Marry this girl and take her home to your hive. If your Queen Mother objects refer her to﻿—er﻿—Potshelter here.”
He cut short the young people’s thanks. “Just one thing,” he said, wagging a finger at Rowe. “Don’t written any more letters.”

But we’re going to have to issue recommendations for new procedures and safeguards that will reduce the possibilities to one in a trillion trillion. It will undoubtedly up the Terran income tax a healthy percentage, but we can’t have something like this happening again. Every boy must marry the Girl Next Door! And the first-class mails must not be interfered with! The advertising must go through!”
“I’d almost like to see it happen again,” Potshelter murmured dreamily, “if there were another Jane Dough in it.”
---
Outside, Richard and Jane had halted to allow a small cortege of machines to pass. First came a squad of police machines with Black Sorter in their midst, unmuzzled and docile enough, though still gnashing his teeth softly. Then﻿—stretched out horizontally and borne on the shoulders of Gray Psychiatrist, Black Coroner, White Nursemaid Seven and Greasy Joe﻿—there passed the slim form of Pink Wastebasket, snow-white in death. The machines were keening softly, mournfully.
Round about the black pillars, little mecho-mops were scurrying like mice, cleaning up the last of the first-class-mail bits of confetti.
Richard winced at this evidence of his aberration, but Jane squeezed his hand comfortingly, which produced in him a truly amazing sensation that changed his whole appearance.
“I know how you feel, darling,” she told him. “But don’t worry about it.

他打断年轻人的道谢。“就一件事，”他朝罗晃手指。“别再手写信了。”

“这个，我不知道——”克鲁姆拜恩开口。
“哦，求求您，先生！”

收缩的门把这对年轻人关出视野时，他转回身来。

“我倒几乎愿意再看它发生一次，”波特谢尔特梦呓般低语，“只要里面还有另一个简·多。”

外面，理查德和简停下脚步，让一小队机器通过。先是警察机器一队，中间是黑分拣机，已经摘了口套，相当驯顺，只是还在轻轻磨牙。随后——平平伸展着，由灰精神病医师、黑验尸官、白保姆七号和油腻乔抬着——粉红废纸篓纤细的躯体经过，死时雪白。机器们在低声哀鸣。
黑色的立柱四周，小小的机械拖把像老鼠一样窜来窜去，清理着一级邮件纸屑的最后残余。
理查德对自己的反常行为的这份证据缩了一下，可简安慰地捏捏他的手，这在他身上引发了一种真正惊人的感觉，改变了他整个人的模样。
“我知道你的感受，亲爱的，”她对他说。“可别担心。

Just think, dear, I’ll always be able to tell your friends’ wives something no other woman in the world can boast of: that my husband once wrote me a letter!”

===Chinese===

想想吧，亲爱的，我永远能告诉你朋友们的太太一件世上没有别的女人能夸口的事：我丈夫曾经给我写过一封信！”

## The Moon Is Green / 月亮是绿的
===Original===

“Effie! What the devil are you up to?”
Her husband’s voice, chopping through her mood of terrified rapture, made her heart jump like a startled cat, yet by some miracle of feminine self-control her body did not show a tremor.
*Dear God*, she thought, *he mustn’t see it. It’s so beautiful, and he always kills beauty.*
“I’m just looking at the Moon,” she said listlessly. “It’s green.”
*Mustn’t, mustn’t see it.* And now, with luck, he wouldn’t. For the face, as if it also heard and sensed the menace in the voice, was moving back from the window’s glow into the outside dark, but slowly, reluctantly, and still faunlike, pleading, cajoling, tempting, and incredibly beautiful.
“Close the shutters at once, you little fool, and come away from the window!”
“Green as a beer bottle,” she went on dreamily, “green as emeralds, green as leaves with sunshine striking through them and green grass to lie on.” She couldn’t help saying those last words. They were her token to the face, even though it couldn’t hear.
“Effie!”
She knew what that last tone meant. Wearily she swung shut the ponderous lead inner shutters and drove home the heavy bolts. That hurt her fingers; it always did, but he mustn’t know that.
“You know that those shutters are not to be touched! Not for five more years at least!”
“I only wanted to look at the Moon,” she said, turning around, and then it was all gone﻿—the face, the night, the Moon, the magic﻿—and she was back in the grubby, stale little hole, facing an angry, stale little man. It was then that the eternal thud of the air-conditioning fans and the crackle of the electrostatic precipitators that sieved out the dust reached her consciousness again like the bite of a dentist’s drill.
“Only wanted to look at the Moon!” he mimicked her in falsetto. “Only wanted to die like a little fool and make me that much more ashamed of you!” Then his voice went gruff and professional. “Here, count yourself.”
She silently took the Geiger counter he held at arm’s length, waited until it settled down to a steady ticking slower than a clock﻿—due only to cosmic rays and indicating nothing dangerous﻿—and then began to comb her body with the instrument. First her head and shoulders, then out along her arms and back along their under side. There was something oddly voluptuous about her movements, although her features were gray and sagging.

===Chinese===

“埃菲！你到底在搞什么鬼？”
丈夫的声音劈开她那又惊又喜的心境，吓得她的心像受惊的猫一样一跳，但凭着女性自制力的某种奇迹，她的身体没有露出半点颤抖。
亲爱的上帝，她想，绝不能让他看见。它那么美，而他总要扼杀美。
“我只是在看月亮，”她恹恹地说。“它是绿的。”
不能，绝不能让他看见。而现在，运气好的话，他不会看见了。因为那张脸，仿佛也听见、也感到了那声音里的威胁，正从窗口的亮光里退回外面的黑暗中，但退得缓慢、依恋，依然带着小鹿般的神情，恳求着，哄诱着，招引着，美得不可思议。
“马上关上百叶窗，小傻瓜，从窗边过来！”
“绿得像啤酒瓶，”她梦吃般继续道，“绿得像祖母绿，绿得像阳光穿透的树叶，还有可以躺卧的绿草。”最后几个字她忍不住说了出来。那是她给那张脸的信物，尽管它听不见。
“埃菲！”
她知道最后这个声调意味着什么。她疲惫地推开沉重的铅制内百叶窗，把粗重的门闩推到底。这弄疼了她的手指；一向如此，可他绝不能知道。
“你知道那些百叶窗碰不得！至少五年内碰不得！”
“我只是想看看月亮，”她说着转过身，随后那一切都不见了——那张脸，夜色，月亮，魔法——她回到了这个油腻腻、空气腐闷的小洞里，面对一个愤怒的、腐闷的小个子男人。就在那时，空调风扇永恒的砰砰声和静电除尘器的噼啪声——它们筛除灰尘——再度抵达她的意识，像牙医钻头的咬嘴。
“只是想看看月亮！”他捏着嗓子学她。“只是想像个小傻瓜一样死掉，让我更丢你的脸！”随后他的嗓音转为粗声粗气的公事腔。“来，自己测量。”
她默默接过他隔臂递来的盖革计数器，等它安定到比钟慢的稳定滴答——只由宇宙线引起，不指示任何危险——然后开始用仪器梳理自己的身体。先是头和肩，再沿两臂外侧下去，又沿内侧回来。她的动作里有一种奇异的肉感，尽管她的面容灰败松弛。

===Original===

The ticking did not change its tempo until she came to her waist. Then it suddenly spurted, clicking faster and faster. Her husband gave an excited grunt, took a quick step forward, froze. She goggled for a moment in fear, then grinned foolishly, dug in the pocket of her grimy apron and guiltily pulled out a wristwatch.
He grabbed it as it dangled from her fingers, saw that it had a radium dial, cursed, heaved it up as if to smash it on the floor, but instead put it carefully on the table.
“You imbecile, you incredible imbecile,” he softly chanted to himself through clenched teeth, with eyes half closed.
She shrugged faintly, put the Geiger counter on the table, and stood there slumped.
He waited until the chanting had soothed his anger, before speaking again. He said quietly, “I do suppose you still realize the sort of world you’re living in?”
---
She nodded slowly, staring at nothingness. Oh, she realized, all right, realized only too well. It was the world that hadn’t realized. The world that had gone on stockpiling hydrogen bombs. The world that had put those bombs in cobalt shells, although it had promised it wouldn’t, because the cobalt made them much more terrible and cost no more. The world that had started throwing those bombs, always telling itself that it hadn’t thrown enough of them yet to make the air really dangerous with the deadly radioactive dust that came from the cobalt. Thrown them and kept on throwing until the danger point, where air and ground would become fatal to all human life, was approached.
Then, for about a month, the two great enemy groups had hesitated. And then each, unknown to the other, had decided it could risk one last gigantic and decisive attack without exceeding the danger point. It had been planned to strip off the cobalt cases, but someone forgot and then there wasn’t time. Besides, the military scientists of each group were confident that the lands of the other had got the most dust. The two attacks came within an hour of each other.
After that, the Fury. The Fury of doomed men who think only of taking with them as many as possible of the enemy, and in this case﻿—they hoped﻿—all. The Fury of suicides who know they have botched up life for good.

===Chinese===

滴答声的节奏没有变化，直到她的腰部。随后它突然加速，越点越快。她丈夫兴奋地哼了一声，抢前一步，僵住。她惊恐地瞪了片刻，随即傻笑起来，在肮脏的围裙口袋里掏了掏，内疚地掏出一只手表。
他一把抓过它——它还挂在她指间晃——看见它是镭表盘，咒骂一声，把它高高抡起似乎要砸到地板上，却转而小心地把它放在桌上。
“你这低能儿，你这不可思议的低能儿，”他咬紧牙关、半闭着眼，对自己轻声念叨。
她微微耸肩，把盖革计数器放上桌子，垂头丧气地站在那里。
他等到那念叨平息了他的怒气才再开口。他平静地说：“我想你总还知道自己活在怎样一个世界里吧？”
---
她缓缓点头，凝望虚空。哦，她知道，太知道了。不知道的是这个世界自己。那个一个劲囤积氢弹的世界。那个把氢弹装进钴壳的世界——尽管它承诺过不装——因为钴让炸弹可怕得多，成本却不多一文。那个开始扔那些炸弹的世界，一边扔一边告诉自己，扔下的还不足以让钴产生的致命放射性尘埃真正毒化空气。扔了又扔，一直扔到逼近那个危险点——空气和地面将对一切人类生命致命的临界。
随后，大约一个月里，两大敌对阵营迟疑了。然后各自背着他方，认定自己能冒险发动最后一场巨大的决定性攻击而不越过危险点。原计划是剥掉钴壳，可有人忘了，随后就来不及了。再说，双方的军事科学家都确信，落进对方国土的灰尘最多。两场攻击前后相隔不到一小时。
那之后，是狂怒。必死之人只想着尽量多地带走敌人——这一回，他们希望，是全部——的狂怒。知道自己已经把生命永久搞砸的自杀者的狂怒。

===Original===

The Fury of cocksure men who realize they have been outsmarted by fate, the enemy, and themselves, and know that they will never be able to improvise a defense when arraigned before the high court of history﻿—and whose unadmitted hope is that there will be no high court of history left to arraign them. More cobalt bombs were dropped during the Fury than in all the preceding years of the war.
After the Fury, the Terror. Men and women with death sifting into their bones through their nostrils and skin, fighting for bare survival under a dust-hazed sky that played fantastic tricks with the light of Sun and Moon, like the dust from Krakatoa that drifted around the world for years. Cities, countryside, and air were alike poisoned, alive with deadly radiation.
The only realistic chance for continued existence was to retire, for the five or ten years the radiation would remain deadly, to some well-sealed and radiation-shielded place that must also be copiously supplied with food, water, power, and a means of air-conditioning.
Such places were prepared by the farseeing, seized by the stronger, defended by them in turn against the desperate hordes of the dying﻿ ﻿… until there were no more of those.
After that, only the waiting, the enduring. A mole’s existence, without beauty or tenderness, but with fear and guilt as constant companions. Never to see the Sun, to walk among the trees﻿—or even know if there were still trees.
Oh, yes, she realized what the world was like.
---
“You understand, too, I suppose, that we were allowed to reclaim this ground-level apartment only because the Committee believed us to be responsible people, and because I’ve been making a damn good showing lately?”
“Yes, Hank.”
“I thought you were eager for privacy. You want to go back to the basement tenements?”
*God, no! Anything rather than that fetid huddling, that shameless communal sprawl. And yet, was this so much better? The nearness to the surface was meaningless; it only tantalized. And the privacy magnified Hank.*
She shook her head dutifully and said, “No, Hank.”
“Then why aren’t you careful? I’ve told you a million times, Effie, that glass is no protection against the dust that’s outside that window. The lead shutter must never be touched! If you make one single slip like that and it gets around, the Committee will send us back to the lower levels without blinking an eye.

===Chinese===

自以为是的男人们意识到自己被命运、敌人和自己联合愚弄了的狂怒，他们知道自己站在历史最高法庭的被告席上将再也编不出辩词——而他们不敢承认的希望是，历史最高法庭已不复存在，无从提审。狂怒期间投下的钴弹，比此前整个战争年代加起来还多。
狂怒之后，是恐怖。男男女女，死亡透过鼻孔和皮肤筛进骨头，在一片尘霾的天空下为活命而争抢——那天空让日月之光变出光怪陆离的把戏，像喀拉喀托的火山灰那样在世界各地飘了多年。城市、乡村和空气一概中了毒，活跃着致命的辐射。
继续存在下去唯一现实的指望，是在辐射仍将致命的那五年或十年里，退守到某个密封良好、屏蔽辐射的地方，那地方还必须储备充裕的食物、水、动力和一套空气调节手段。
这样的地方由远见者预备，被强者夺取，又被他们轮番守卫，抗击垂死者的绝望蜂拥……直到再没有那样的蜂拥。
那以后，只剩等待，熬煎。一种鼴鼠的生存，没有美也没有温柔，只有恐惧和负罪作伴。永远见不到太阳，走不进树林——甚至不知道还有没有树林。
哦，是啊，她知道自己活在怎样的世界里。
---
“我想你也明白，我们获准收回这间地面公寓，只因委员会相信我们是负责任的人，而且我最近干得相当漂亮？”
“是的，汉克。”
“我还以为你渴望隐私呢。你想回地下层的集体宿舍？”
上帝，不！宁可怎样都行，也别回那恶臭的蜗居、那无耻的大杂烩。可这又好到哪里去？贴近地面毫无意义；它只是撩拨人。而隐私放大了汉克。
她本分地摇头说：“不想，汉克。”
“那你为什么不小心点？我跟你说过一百万次了，埃菲，玻璃挡不住那扇窗外的尘。铅百叶窗绝不能碰！你要是再这样失手一次，传出去，委员会眼都不眨就把我们遣回下层。

===Original===

And they’ll think twice before trusting me with any important jobs.”
“I’m sorry, Hank.”
“Sorry? What’s the good of being sorry? The only thing that counts is never to make a slip! Why the devil do you do such things, Effie? What drives you to it?”
She swallowed. “It’s just that it’s so dreadful being cooped up like this,” she said hesitatingly, “shut away from the sky and the Sun. I’m just hungry for a little beauty.”
“And do you suppose I’m not?” he demanded. “Don’t you suppose I want to get outside, too, and be carefree and have a good time? But I’m not so damn selfish about it. I want my children to enjoy the Sun, and my children’s children. Don’t you see that that’s the all-important thing and that we have to behave like mature adults and make sacrifices for it?”
“Yes, Hank.”
He surveyed her slumped figure, her lined and listless face. “You’re a fine one to talk about hunger for beauty,” he told her. Then his voice grew softer, more deliberate. “You haven’t forgotten, have you, Effie, that until last month the Committee was so concerned about your sterility? That they were about to enter my name on the list of those waiting to be allotted a free woman? Very high on the list, too!”
She could nod even at that one, but not while looking at him. She turned away. She knew very well that the Committee was justified in worrying about the birth rate. When the community finally moved back to the surface again, each additional healthy young person would be an asset, not only in the struggle for bare survival, but in the resumed war against Communism which some of the Committee members still counted on.
It was natural that they should view a sterile woman with disfavor, and not only because of the waste of her husband’s germ-plasm, but because sterility might indicate that she had suffered more than the average from radiation. In that case, if she did bear children later on, they would be more apt to carry a defective heredity, producing an undue number of monsters and freaks in future generations, and so contaminating the race.
Of course she understood it. She could hardly remember the time when she didn’t. Years ago? Centuries? There wasn’t much difference in a place where time was endless.
---
His lecture finished, her husband smiled and grew almost cheerful.
“Now that you’re going to have a child, that’s all in the background again.

===Chinese===

而且他们把重要工作交给我之前会三思。”
“对不起，汉克。”
“对不起？对不起有什么用？要紧的只有一件事：永远不失手！你到底为什么干这种事，埃菲？什么东西驱使你？”
她咽了口唾沫。“只是，这样关着实在太可怕了，”她迟疑地说，“与天空和太阳隔绝。我只是渴求一点点美。”
“你以为我就不渴？”他质问。“你以为我不想也到外面去，无忧无虑，痛快玩一场？可我没那么自私。我要我的孩子们享受太阳，我孩子的孩子。你难道不明白这才是头等重要的事，明白我们得表现得像成熟的成年人、为它做出牺牲？”
“是的，汉克。”
他打量她萎靡的身形、她皱纹密布、了无生气的脸。“你倒有脸谈渴求美，”他对她说。随后他的嗓音放软，更有心机了。“你没忘吧，埃菲，直到上个月委员会还那么担心你的不育？他们差一点就要把我的名字登上等候分配自由妇女的名单？还是排得很前面的！”
连这一条她也能点头，只是不能看着他。她转过脸去。她非常清楚，委员会担心出生率是有道理的。等社群最终重返地面，每一个额外的健康年轻人都是一笔资产——不仅在赤裸裸的求生挣扎中，也在某些委员仍然指望的、重启的对共之战中。
他们自然看不惯一个不育的女人，不只因为浪费她丈夫的种质，还因为不育可能表明她遭受的辐射超过平均。那样的话，她日后若真生育，孩子更可能携带有缺陷的遗传，在未来世代里产出过多的怪物和怪胎，污染种族。
她当然懂。她几乎想不起自己什么时候不懂。几年前？几世纪前？在一个时间无边无尽的地方，两者没多大差别。

训话完毕，她丈夫笑了，几乎快活起来。
“现在你有了孩子，那一页又翻过去了。

===Original===

Do you know, Effie, that when I first came in, I had some very good news for you? I’m to become a member of the Junior Committee and the announcement will be made at the banquet tonight.” He cut short her mumbled congratulations. “So brighten yourself up and put on your best dress. I want the other Juniors to see what a handsome wife the new member has got.” He paused. “Well, get a move on!”
She spoke with difficulty, still not looking at him. “I’m terribly sorry, Hank, but you’ll have to go alone. I’m not well.”
He straightened up with an indignant jerk. “There you go again! First that infantile, inexcusable business of the shutters, and now this! No feeling for my reputation at all. Don’t be ridiculous, Effie. You’re coming!”
“Terribly sorry,” she repeated blindly, “but I really can’t. I’d just be sick. I wouldn’t make you proud of me at all.”
“Of course you won’t,” he retorted sharply. “As it is, I have to spend half my energy running around making excuses for you﻿—why you’re so odd, why you always seem to be ailing, why you’re always stupid and snobbish and say the wrong thing. But tonight’s really important, Effie. It will cause a lot of bad comment if the new member’s wife isn’t present. You know how just a hint of sickness starts the old radiation-disease rumor going. You’ve *got* to come, Effie.”
She shook her head helplessly.
“Oh, for heaven’s sake, come on!” he shouted, advancing on her. “This is just a silly mood. As soon as you get going, you’ll snap out of it. There’s nothing really wrong with you at all.”
He put his hand on her shoulder to turn her around, and at his touch her face suddenly grew so desperate and gray that for a moment he was alarmed in spite of himself.
“Really?” he asked, almost with a note of concern.
She nodded miserably.
“Hmm!” He stepped back and strode about irresolutely. “Well, of course, if that’s the way it is﻿ ﻿…” He checked himself and a sad smile crossed his face. “So you don’t care enough about your old husband’s success to make one supreme effort in spite of feeling bad?”
Again the helpless headshake. “I just can’t go out tonight, under any circumstances.” And her gaze stole toward the lead shutters.
He was about to say something when he caught the direction of her gaze. His eyebrows jumped.

===Chinese===

你知道吗，埃菲，我刚进门时，本来给你带了很好的消息？我要成为初级委员会的成员了，今晚的宴会上宣布。”他打断她含混的道贺。“所以打起精神来，穿上你最好的裙子。我要让其他初级委员看看，新成员娶了多标致的太太。”他顿了顿。“嗯，动起来呀！”
她艰难开口，仍不看他。“非常抱歉，汉克，可你得一个人去。我不舒服。”
他愤怒地一挺身。“又来了！先是百叶窗那档子幼稚、不可原谅的事，现在又来这个！对我的名声一点感觉都没有。别荒唐了，埃菲。你必须来！”
“非常抱歉，”她盲然重复，“可我真的不行。我会吐的。我只会让你丢脸。”
“你当然会，”他尖锐地反驳。“照现在这样，我一半的精力都花在到处替你打圆场上——你为什么这么怪，为什么总像有病，为什么又蠢又势利，老说错话。可今晚真的很重要，埃菲。新成员的夫人不到场，会引起很多闲话。你知道一点点病态的暗示就会让老的辐射病谣言传起来。你必须来，埃菲。”
她无助地摇头。
“哦，看在老天的份上，走吧！”他喊道，逼近她。“这只是犯傻气。你一动身就会缓过来的。你根本没什么毛病。”
他把手放上她的肩要扳她转身，一触之下，她的脸突然变得那样绝望灰败，一时间连他也不由得慌了。
“真的？”他问，几乎带着一点关切。
她凄惨地点头。
“嗯！”他退后一步，踌躇地踱来踱去。“好吧，当然，既然是这样……”他打住，一丝悲哀的笑掠过脸庞。“这么说，你对老丈夫的成功还不够上心，不肯哪怕感觉糟糕也做一次超人的努力？”
又是无助的摇头。“我今晚就是不能出去，无论如何不能。”她的目光偷偷溜向铅百叶窗。
他正要说什么，突然捉住了她目光的方向。他的眉毛跳了起来。

===Original===

For seconds he stared at her incredulously, as if some completely new and almost unbelievable possibility had popped into his mind. The look of incredulity slowly faded, to be replaced by a harder, more calculating expression. But when he spoke again, his voice was shockingly bright and kind.
“Well, it can’t be helped naturally, and I certainly wouldn’t want you to go if you weren’t able to enjoy it. So you hop right into bed and get a good rest. I’ll run over to the men’s dorm to freshen up. No, really, I don’t want you to have to make any effort at all. Incidentally, Jim Barnes isn’t going to be able to come to the banquet either﻿—touch of the old flu, he tells me, of all things.”
He watched her closely as he mentioned the other man’s name, but she didn’t react noticeably. In fact, she hardly seemed to be hearing his chatter.
“I got a bit sharp with you, I’m afraid, Effie,” he continued contritely. “I’m sorry about that. I was excited about my new job and I guess that was why things upset me. Made me feel let down when I found you weren’t feeling as good as I was. Selfish of me. Now you get into bed right away and get well. Don’t worry about me a bit. I know you’d come if you possibly could. And I know you’ll be thinking about me. Well, I must be off now.”
He started toward her, as if to embrace her, then seemed to think better of it. He turned back at the doorway and said, emphasizing the words, “You’ll be completely alone for the next four hours.” He waited for her nod, then bounced out.
---
She stood still until his footsteps died away. Then she straightened up, walked over to where he’d put down the wristwatch, picked it up and smashed it hard on the floor. The crystal shattered, the case flew apart, and something went *zing*!
She stood there breathing heavily. Slowly her sagged features lifted, formed themselves into the beginning of a smile. She stole another look at the shutters. The smile became more definite. She felt her hair, wet her fingers and ran them along her hairline and back over her ears. After wiping her hands on her apron, she took it off. She straightened her dress, lifted her head with a little flourish, and stepped smartly toward the window.
Then her face went miserable again and her steps slowed.
No, it couldn’t be, and it won’t be, she told herself.

===Chinese===

他难以置信地瞪了她好几秒，仿佛某个全新得近乎不可思议的可能蹦进了他的脑子。难以置信的神色缓缓褪去，换上一种更冷硬、更精算的表情。可他再开口时，嗓音亮得惊人，而且和气。
“唉，那自然没法子，你要是没法尽兴，我当然不想让你去。快上床好好休息吧。我到男生宿舍去梳洗一下。不，真的，我一点也不想让你费一丝力气。顺便说一句，吉姆·巴恩斯今晚也去不了宴会——老流感，他跟我说，偏偏是这个。”
提到另一个男人的名字时他紧盯着她，可她没有明显反应。事实上，她几乎没在听他絮叨。
“我刚才对你凶了点，恐怕是，埃菲，”他悔疚地继续。“对不住。我为新职位兴奋，我想这就是事情让我上火的原因。发现你不像我那么舒坦，我有点泄气。我自私了。现在快上床，养好病。一点也别为我操心。我知道只要有一点可能你就会来的。我知道你会想着我。好，我得走了。”
他朝她走过去，像要拥抱她，随即又觉得不妥。他在门口回身说，一字一顿：“接下来的四个小时，你会完全一个人。”他等她点头，然后一弹而出。
---
她站着不动，直到他的脚步声消逝。随后她挺直身子，走到他放手表的地方，捾起它，狠狠砸在地板上。表蒙子碎了，表壳迸开，什么东西“嘣”地一响！
她站在那里喘粗气。她松垂的面容缓缓抬起，渐渐聚成一个微笑的开端。她又偷看了一眼百叶窗。微笑变得确凿了。她摸摸头发，汮湿手指，沿发际线抹过，再抹过耳后。她在围裙上擦了手，把它解下来。她抚平裙子，头微微一扬，轻快地迈向窗户。
随后她的脸又凄苦起来，脚步慢了。
不，不可能是，也绝不会是，她对自己说。

===Original===

It had been just an illusion, a silly romantic dream that she had somehow projected out of her beauty-starved mind and given a moment’s false reality. There couldn’t be anything alive outside. There hadn’t been for two whole years.
And if there conceivably were, it would be something altogether horrible. She remembered some of the pariahs﻿—hairless, witless creatures, with radiation welts crawling over their bodies like worms, who had come begging for succor during the last months of the Terror﻿—and been shot down. How they must have hated the people in refuges!
But even as she was thinking these things, her fingers were caressing the bolts, gingerly drawing them, and she was opening the shutters gently, apprehensively.
No, there couldn’t be anything outside, she assured herself wryly, peering out into the green night. Even her fears had been groundless.
But the face came floating up toward the window. She started back in terror, then checked herself.
